### [1] [Unveiling Public Opinion: A Study of Sentiment Analysis Using LSTM and Traditional Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07772)
*Atiq Ur Rehman*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper evaluates machine learning and deep learning models for Twitter sentiment analysis, comparing logistic regression, random forest, naïve Bayes, gradient boosting, and LSTM networks. The LSTM model achieved the best performance with 90.98% training accuracy, 80.00% testing accuracy, and a 0.92 micro-average ROC-AUC score, outperforming conventional approaches in capturing contextual and sequential dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The massive influx of real-time user-generated content on social media platforms like Twitter has made automated sentiment analysis indispensable for extracting actionable insights from public opinion. The authors aim to identify the optimal algorithmic approach for classifying tweet sentiments into positive, negative, or neutral categories.

Method: The study preprocesses a Kaggle Twitter dataset using tokenization, lemmatization, and stopword elimination. It then benchmarks multiple algorithms — logistic regression, random forest, naïve Bayes, gradient boosting, and LSTM networks — evaluating them on training accuracy, testing accuracy, and micro-average ROC-AUC score.

Result: The LSTM network outperformed all other models, achieving a training accuracy of 90.98%, testing accuracy of 80.00%, and a micro-average ROC-AUC score of 0.92. Conventional machine learning models were less effective at capturing contextual and sequential textual features.

Conclusion: Deep learning approaches, specifically LSTM networks, are superior to traditional machine learning methods for Twitter sentiment analysis due to their ability to model sequential and contextual information in text, leading to better generalization and more robust sentiment classification.

Abstract: In this age of social media, sites like Twitter have become meeting places for people to share their views and feelings on a wide range of issues and current events as they unfold in real time. Sentiment analysis, a critical application of NLP, has become indispensable due to the massive influx of user-generated content, enabling the extraction of meaningful insights from the opinions and emotions expressed in textual data. Sentiment analysis on Twitter employs sophisticated computational techniques to categorize tweets into positive, negative, or neutral sentiments. This method not only examines individual expressions but also analyzes vast databases related to specific subjects or events. By spotting these emotions, machine learning models help improve public opinion interpretation and trend forecasting. This paper examines the effectiveness of various machine learning and deep learning approaches. Designed for this use, the system evaluates logistic regression, random forest, naïve bayes, gradient boosting, and LSTM networks, among other algorithms applied in sentiment classification. This work identifies the optimal sentiment analysis model using a Kaggle Twitter dataset that has been preprocessed through tokenization, lemmatization, and stopword elimination. Emphasizing the better performance of the LSTM approach, the model attained a training accuracy of 90.98%, a testing accuracy of 80.00%, and a micro-average ROC- AUC score of 0.92. These results show that the model outperforms conventional machine learning techniques in capturing contextual and sequential textual aspects.

</details>


### [2] [From Solvers to Research: Large Language Model-Driven Formal Mathematics at the Research Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07779)
*Eric Jiang,Xiao Liang,Yikai Zhang,Yingjia Wan,Mengting Li,Haikang Deng,Alexander K. Taylor,Justin Baker,Rushil Raghavan,Junyi Zhang,Ying Nian Wu,Andrea L. Bertozzi,Kai-Wei Chang,Raghu Meka,Matthew Sottile,Nanyun Peng,Amit Sahai,Terence Tao,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This position paper argues that current LLM-driven theorem provers excel at formal proof generation for well-defined problems but fundamentally cannot tackle frontier mathematics (open conjectures, new theorem discovery). The authors survey the AI4Math field and identify core limitations in datasets, relational structure, mathematical exploration, tool ecosystems, and human-AI collaboration, proposing a strategic roadmap to shift from problem-solvers toward true mathematical research agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI4Math systems, despite success in formal proof generation via ITP languages, are limited to well-defined, closed-form problems and cannot address frontier research mathematics — which is open-ended, under-specified, and multi-layered. The authors argue a paradigm shift is needed from predefined problem-solvers to autonomous research agents capable of rigorous formal reasoning for discovering new theorems and resolving open conjectures.

Method: This is a position paper that conducts a systematic review of the AI4Math landscape, covering three key areas: datasets for formal mathematics, auto-formalization (translating informal math into formal languages), and proof synthesis. The authors then critically analyze limitations across five dimensions — datasets, relational structure, mathematical exploration, tool ecosystem, and human-AI collaboration — and synthesize these into a strategic roadmap for future development.

Result: The analysis identifies that current systems suffer from: inadequate datasets lacking relational and hierarchical structure; insufficient support for mathematical exploration beyond proof verification; fragmented tool ecosystems; and underdeveloped human-AI collaboration paradigms. These limitations collectively prevent AI systems from functioning as genuine mathematical research agents.

Conclusion: The next leap in AI4Math requires a decisive shift from problem-solvers to research agents, demanding advances in structured datasets, exploration-oriented frameworks, integrated tool ecosystems, and collaborative human-AI workflows to enable rigorous formal reasoning on frontier mathematical challenges.

Abstract: Recent developments in AI for Mathematics (AI4Math), especially Large Language Model (LLM)-driven theorem provers, has achieved remarkable success in formal proof generation for well-defined mathematical problems through Interactive Theorem Proving (ITP) languages. However, current systems remain fundamentally limited in tackling frontier research mathematics, such as discovering new theorems or resolving open conjectures, which are often open-ended, under-specified, and involve multiple layers of abstraction. We argue that the next leap in AI4Math systems requires a decisive shift from predefined problem-solvers to research agents that can address frontier mathematical challenges with rigorous formal mathematical reasoning. In this position paper, we provide a systematic review of the field, covering datasets, auto-formalization, and proof synthesis. More importantly, we identify core limitations of existing systems in serving as mathematical research agents, examining issues across datasets, relational structure, mathematical exploration, tool ecosystem, and human-AI collaboration, outlining a strategic road-map for the future of AI4Math.

</details>


### [3] [DeepSearch-World: Self-Distillation for Deep Search Agents in a Verifiable Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07820)
*Xinyu Geng,Xuanhua He,Sixiang Chen,Yanjing Xiao,Fan Zhang,Shijue Huang,Haitao Mi,Zhenwen Liang,Tianqing Fang,Yi R. Fung*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DeepSearch-Evolve is a self-distillation framework that trains web agents through iterative trajectory generation, filtering, and fine-tuning within DeepSearch-World, a deterministic 420K-task environment. Without teacher distillation, the 9B model achieves 31.2% on BrowseComp, 61.5% on GAIA, and 93.4% on HotpotQA, demonstrating that verifiable environments enable scalable self-evolution for long-horizon agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to training tool-use agents suffer from two key limitations: supervised fine-tuning relies on static teacher-distilled trajectories that limit exploration, while sparse-reward reinforcement learning provides insufficient supervision for long-horizon interactions. A scalable self-improvement paradigm is needed that allows agents to learn from their own experience without external teacher signals.

Method: The authors introduce DeepSearch-World, a deterministic, verifiable environment with reproducible search and page-reading tools containing 420K multi-hop QA tasks built from entity-level random walks. Within this environment, the DeepSearch-Evolve framework iteratively executes four steps: (1) trajectory generation by the agent, (2) filtering based on verifiable outcomes and cognitive behaviors (progress verification, grounded reflection, failure recovery), (3) data mixing to balance successful and corrective trajectories, and (4) fine-tuning to produce progressively stronger agents — a self-distillation loop without reliance on more capable teacher models.

Result: DeepSearch-World-9B achieves competitive results against open-source agents: 31.2% on BrowseComp, 61.5% on GAIA, and 93.4% on HotpotQA. These results are attained purely through self-evolution without distillation from stronger models.

Conclusion: Verifiable, deterministic environments with rich cognitive behavior support can enable scalable self-evolution for long-horizon web agents, eliminating dependence on teacher models. The authors commit to releasing the environment, training pool, validation set, model, and code.

Abstract: Training tool-use agents to improve from their own experience remains challenging, as supervised fine-tuning relies on fixed teacher-distilled trajectories, while sparse-reward reinforcement learning provides weak supervision for long-horizon interactions. We present DeepSearch-Evolve, a self-distillation framework for web agents built on DeepSearch-World, a deterministic and verifiable environment with reproducible search and page-reading tools. DeepSearch-World contains 420K multi-hop QA tasks constructed from entity-level random walks and supports key agentic cognitive behaviors useful for self-evolving, including progress verification, grounded reflection, and failure recovery. DeepSearch-Evolve iteratively performs trajectory generation, filtering, data mixing, and fine-tuning to train stronger agents. Without distillation from more capable models, DeepSearch-World-9B achieves competitive performance compared with open-source agents, reaching 31.2% on BrowseComp, 61.5% on GAIA, and 93.4% on HotpotQA, showing that verifiable environments enable scalable self-evolution for long-horizon web agents. We will release the environment, 420K training pool, validation set, model, and code to facilitate future research on self-improving deep search agents.

</details>


### [4] [How Do I Know What to Say Next? Barenholtz's Autogenerative Theory as an Enrichment of Harrisean Integrationism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07891)
*J. Mark Bishop,Stephen J. Cowley*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a synthesis of Roy Harris's Integrationist linguistics and Elan Barenholtz's autogenerative theory, arguing the latter fills explanatory gaps in the former — specifically regarding the structural mechanism of prospective openness, semiotic continuity between linguistic and non-linguistic activity, and the nature of the accumulated archive of past integrations — while also offering NLP/LLM researchers a principled account of what statistical structure in language models actually represents and what it inherently lacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrationist linguistics (Harris) rightly critiques computational referentialism — the view that language is a code mapping onto a pre-given world — but leaves three explanatory gaps: (1) no structural mechanism for how signs sustain prospective openness, (2) undertheorised continuity between linguistic and non-linguistic semiosis, and (3) no detailed account of the structural properties of the archive of past integrations. The paper aims to fill these gaps without undermining Integrationism's core commitments, while also clarifying for NLP practitioners what LLMs' statistical structure actually captures and what they fundamentally miss.

Method: The paper proceeds through theoretical synthesis. It maps Barenholtz's autogenerative theory — developed from observing LLM behaviour — onto Harris's Integrationist framework, demonstrating point-by-point correspondence: the autogenerative model supplies a structural mechanism for prospective openness, provides a computational correlate for semiotic continuity, and offers a theory of the archive as accumulated residue. The synthesis is argued to preserve Harris's ontological primacy of the situated integrative act while adding explanatory content Integrationism itself lacks.

Result: The synthesis yields three contributions: (1) a structural mechanism for prospective openness in bipartite communication; (2) a computational correlate for Harris's thesis of semiotic continuity between language and other sign-making; and (3) a theory of the archive — what accumulated past integrations look like structurally and how new participants draw upon it. For NLP/LLM research, it provides a principled account distinguishing what statistical structure LLMs exploit from what they inherently cannot provide (situated, prospective joint action).

Conclusion: Barenholtz's autogenerative theory can enrich Harris's Integrationism without violating its core commitments, filling critical explanatory gaps regarding prospective openness, semiotic continuity, and the archive. This synthesis carries significant implications for NLP/LLM research, offering a theoretically grounded understanding of both the nature and the fundamental limitations of the statistical patterns large language models exploit.

Abstract: Roy Harris's Integrationist linguistics offers a compelling critique of the referentialist tradition embedded deep at the heart of computational approaches to language, arguing that language is not a code that maps onto a pre-given world but a situated, bipartite activity oriented toward prospective joint action. Yet Integrationism leaves certain explanatory gaps: it does not fully account for the structural mechanism by which signs sustain prospective openness, it undertheorises the continuity between linguistic and non-linguistic semiotic activity, and it offers no detailed account of the structural properties of the accumulated archive of past integrations. This paper argues that Elan Barenholtz's autogenerative theory of language, developed in response to the behaviour of Large Language Models (LLMs), can fill precisely these gaps, enriching Integrationism without undermining any of its core commitments. Specifically, the autogenerative account provides: a structural mechanism for the prospective openness that Harris identifies as central to bipartite communication; a computational correlate for Harris's thesis of semiotic continuity between language and other sign-making activity; and a theory of the archive: what the accumulated residue of past integrations looks like and how new participants draw upon it. The synthesis preserves Harris's ontological primacy of the situated integrative act while adding explanatory content that Integrationism itself does not supply. For practitioners and researchers in natural language processing and large language model design, the argument offers a principled account of what the statistical structure that LLMs so effectively exploit actually is, and of what it cannot, by its nature, provide.

</details>


### [5] [Scalable and Culturally Specific Stereotype Dataset Construction via Human-LLM Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07895)
*Weicheng Ma,John Guerrerio,Soroush Vosoughi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a cost-efficient human-LLM collaborative framework to build EspanStereo, a Spanish-language stereotype dataset covering multiple Spanish-speaking countries. Evaluation with this dataset reveals significant cross-country variation in LLM stereotypical behavior, highlighting the need for culturally grounded bias assessment beyond English-centric benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing stereotype research in LLMs is heavily concentrated on English-speaking contexts due to a lack of multilingual datasets and the prohibitive cost of manual annotation in underrepresented cultures. There is a pressing need for scalable, culturally grounded stereotype evaluation across diverse linguistic and regional contexts.

Method: The authors propose a human-LLM collaborative annotation framework: LLMs generate candidate stereotypes, and in-culture human annotators validate them. This framework is applied to construct EspanStereo, a stereotype dataset spanning multiple Spanish-speaking countries across Europe and Latin America, capturing both well-documented stereotypes and region-specific biases.

Result: The framework effectively identifies nuanced, region-specific biases. Evaluation of Spanish-supporting LLMs using EspanStereo reveals significant variation in stereotypical behavior across different Spanish-speaking countries, demonstrating that monolithic language-level evaluation obscures important cultural differences.

Conclusion: The collaborative annotation framework is adaptable to other languages and regions, offering a scalable path toward multilingual stereotype benchmarks. This work broadens the scope of stereotype analysis in LLMs and lays the groundwork for comprehensive cross-cultural bias evaluation.

Abstract: Research on stereotypes in large language models (LLMs) has largely focused on English-speaking contexts, due to the lack of datasets in other languages and the high cost of manual annotation in underrepresented cultures. To address this gap, we introduce a cost-efficient human-LLM collaborative annotation framework and apply it to construct EspanStereo, a Spanish-language stereotype dataset spanning multiple Spanish-speaking countries across Europe and Latin America. EspanStereo captures both well-documented stereotypes from prior literature and culturally specific biases absent from English-centric resources. Using LLMs to generate candidate stereotypes and in-culture annotators to validate them, we demonstrate the framework's effectiveness in identifying nuanced, region-specific biases. Our evaluation of Spanish-supporting LLMs using EspanStereo reveals significant variation in stereotypical behavior across countries, highlighting the need for more culturally grounded assessments. Beyond Spanish, our framework is adaptable to other languages and regions, offering a scalable path toward multilingual stereotype benchmarks. This work broadens the scope of stereotype analysis in LLMs and lays the groundwork for comprehensive cross-cultural bias evaluation.

</details>


### [6] [When Debiasing Backfires: Counterintuitive Side Effects of Preprocessing-Based Stereotype Mitigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07937)
*Yahan Zheng,John Guerrerio,Soroush Vosoughi,Weicheng Ma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Preprocessing-based stereotype mitigation methods in NLP often introduce unintended side effects, increasing stereotyping or counter-stereotyping for non-targeted demographics. These shifts are missed by standard benchmarks and are not explained by attention flow changes, calling for side-effect-aware evaluation and mitigation practices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing preprocessing-based stereotype mitigation methods (e.g., debiased corpora, group mention removal, group reference swapping) are evaluated only on targeted groups. The authors identify a critical blind spot: these methods may inadvertently worsen stereotyping for other demographics, including unrelated ones, and standard benchmarks fail to detect these side effects.

Method: The authors systematically study side effects across two model families (encoder-only and decoder-only), multiple preprocessing strategies (removing stereotypical sentences, removing group mentions, swapping group references), and both pre- and post-training at varying data scales on Wikipedia. They evaluate stereotype shifts relative to neutral baselines for both targeted and non-targeted demographics, and employ attention-rollout analysis to probe mechanistic explanations.

Result: All tested preprocessing strategies induce measurable side effects, increasing stereotyping or counter-stereotyping for non-targeted demographics, including across unrelated demographic categories. Standard benchmarks consistently miss these shifts. Attention-rollout analysis reveals that side effects are not accompanied by large changes in attention flow, making mechanistic interpretation difficult.

Conclusion: Side-effect-aware, transparent mitigation practices are necessary. The authors provide actionable diagnostics for detecting unintended shifts and argue that the NLP community must adopt more comprehensive evaluation protocols that go beyond targeted-group metrics when assessing debiasing interventions.

Abstract: Preprocessing-based methods for stereotype mitigation, such as pre-/post-training on debiased corpora, are widely used in NLP. While these approaches reduce measurable stereotypes for targeted groups, we find they often induce unintended shifts-side effects, where stereotyping or counter-stereotyping can increase relative to neutral baselines for other demographics, including across unrelated demographic categories. We demonstrate these side effects across two model families (encoder-only and decoder-only), multiple preprocessing strategies (removing stereotypical sentences, removing group mentions, and swapping group references), and both pre- and post-training at different data scales on Wikipedia. Standard benchmarks frequently miss these shifts. Using attention-rollout analysis, we observe that such side effects are not accompanied by large changes in attention flow, complicating mechanistic explanations. We discuss implications for evaluation, provide actionable diagnostics, and argue for side-effect-aware, transparent mitigation practices.

</details>


### [7] [WebSwarm: Recursive Multi-Agent Orchestration for Deep-and-Wide Web Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08662)
*Xiaoshuai Song,Liancheng Zhang,Kangzhi Zhao,Yutao Zhu,Zhongyuan Wang,Guanting Dong,Jinghan Yang,Han Li,Kun Gai,Ji-Rong Wen,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: WebSwarm is a progressive recursive delegation framework for LLM-based web search agents that dynamically builds task decomposition, recursive expansion, and agent collaboration during inference, outperforming single-agent and multi-agent baselines on deep, wide, and interleaved search tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single ReAct-style LLM agents are limited by one long trajectory and constrained context, struggling to handle depth and coverage simultaneously. Existing multi-agent systems improve coverage via parallel execution but lack recursive depth, collaboration adaptability, and evidence-grounded expansion.

Method: WebSwarm introduces a progressive recursive delegation framework where agentic search nodes are dynamically instantiated, each coupling a local objective with a search mode. Nodes can either solve objectives directly or delegate to child nodes; results flow upward for further expansion, revision, or aggregation. The framework first probes how task-relevant information is organized on the web to ground node expansion and reuses process-level experience across homogeneous sibling nodes.

Result: Experiments on BrowseComp-Plus, WideSearch, DeepWideSearch, and GISA benchmarks show WebSwarm consistently outperforms single-agent and multi-agent baselines on deep, wide, and interleaved deep-and-wide tasks.

Conclusion: WebSwarm's recursive delegation approach effectively addresses the depth-coverage trade-off in web search agents, with ablation studies, task difficulty analysis, web tool efficiency evaluation, and model generalization experiments providing insights for future multi-agent search system design.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based web search agents are transforming information seeking from simple factoid question answering into complex, deep-and-wide search and research-oriented tasks. A single ReAct-style agent is constrained by one long trajectory and limited context, making it difficult to handle depth and coverage simultaneously. Existing multi-agent systems improve search coverage through parallel execution and aggregation, but still exhibit clear limitations in recursive depth, collaboration adaptability, and evidence-grounded expansion. We propose WebSwarm, a progressive recursive delegation framework that jointly constructs task decomposition, recursive expansion, and agent collaboration during inference. WebSwarm dynamically instantiates agentic search nodes, each coupling a local objective with a search mode that specifies how the node should organize search and collaboration. Each node can either solve its objective itself or further delegate child nodes; after solving, it returns evidence and results upward, enabling parent nodes to further expand, revise, or aggregate the search process. To guide this process, WebSwarm first probes how task-relevant information is organized on the web to ground subsequent node expansion, and reuses process-level experience across homogeneous sibling nodes. Experiments on BrowseComp-Plus, WideSearch, DeepWideSearch, and GISA show that WebSwarm consistently outperforms single-agent and multi-agent baselines on deep, wide, and interleaved deep-and-wide tasks. Further analyses of ablation, task difficulty, web tool efficiency, and model generalization explain WebSwarm's effectiveness and provide insights for multi-agent search systems.

</details>


### [8] [A Multi-cluster Boundary Learning Method for Out-of-Scope Intent Detection via MiniLM Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07974)
*Yihong Xu,Mingyu Kang,Linyuan Lü*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes a multi-cluster boundary learning method using MiniLM embeddings in a one-class classification framework for out-of-scope intent detection, achieving SOTA performance on CLINC150, StackOverflow, and Banking77 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing OOS intent detection methods face two key limitations: (1) multi-class classification approaches degrade in accuracy as the number of known intent classes grows, and (2) LLM-embedding methods are parameter-heavy, making training and deployment difficult.

Method: A multi-cluster boundary learning approach that uses all-MiniLM-L6-v2 embeddings within a one-class classification workflow. The method learns decision boundaries from multi-cluster embeddings derived from training utterances and rejects out-of-domain utterances as OOS intents.

Result: The proposed method achieves state-of-the-art OOS intent detection performance compared to other baselines on CLINC150, StackOverflow, and Banking77 datasets. Ablation studies confirm that MiniLM adapts well to the workflow and embedding requirements.

Conclusion: The multi-cluster boundary learning method with MiniLM embeddings effectively addresses OOS intent detection challenges, offering a lightweight yet accurate alternative to both traditional multi-class and large LLM-embedding approaches.

Abstract: Intent detection is a critical task that bridges human intents and system actions in human-machine interaction systems. However, there still exist challenges for detecting out-of-scope (OOS) intents. (i) The traditional methods view the OOS intent detection as a multi-class classification, then the detection accuracy decreases as the class number of the known intents increases; (ii) LLM-embedding methods require large parameters, that makes them difficult to train and practically deploy. Thus, this work proposes a multi-cluster boundary learning method to detect OOS intents via MiniLM embedding (i.e., all-MiniLM-L6-v2) in an one-class classification workflow. The method learns the boundaries of multi-cluster embeddings generated by MiniLM from the training utterances, and then rejects the out-of-domain utterances as OOS intents. Experiments are conducted on public CLINC150, StackOverflow and Banking77 datasets. The results show that the method achieves the state-of-the-art OOS intent detection performance compared the other baselines. Ablation studies are also conducted and the results show that the used MiniLM can better adapt to the workflow and utterance embedding requirements. The code is available at supplementary materials.

</details>


### [9] [When Implausible Tokens Get Reinforced: Tail-Aware Credit Calibration for LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07976)
*Xiuyi Lou,Zicheng Xu,Yu-Neng Chuang,Hoang Anh Duy Le,Zhaozhuo Xu,Guanchu Wang,Vladimir Braverman*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies "Positive-Credit Contamination" in critic-free RL for LLMs, where erroneous low-probability tokens receive the same positive credit as plausible ones. The authors propose TACO (Tail-Aware Credit CalibratiOn), which computes tail-risk scores to dampen positive credit for risky tokens while preserving useful rare patterns, achieving consistent improvements over GRPO-style baselines across multiple LLMs and benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critic-free RL methods (e.g., GRPO) uniformly broadcast identical advantage signals to all tokens in a trajectory, ignoring token-level differences in correctness. This causes low-probability "tail" tokens that are contextually wrong to be reinforced indiscriminately alongside valid tokens — a phenomenon the authors term "Positive-Credit Contamination." This undermines reasoning quality and training stability, especially in long-horizon RL.

Method: TACO introduces a two-stage calibration mechanism: (1) A tail-risk score is computed for each token using local generation context, distinguishing tokens that fall into the unreliable tail (unexpected rarity) from those arising from uncertainty-driven exploration. (2) This score is used to scale down positive credit for high-risk tokens — attenuating rather than zeroing out gradients — so that incidental noise is progressively dampened while recurring useful rare patterns can still accumulate reinforcement over time.

Result: Experiments across three LLMs and eight benchmarks demonstrate that TACO consistently outperforms GRPO-style baselines. TACO also improves training stability, enabling sustained performance gains in long-horizon RL settings.

Conclusion: Uniform credit assignment in critic-free RL is fundamentally flawed due to Positive-Credit Contamination. TACO effectively addresses this by context-aware, soft calibration of token-level credit, leading to more stable training and superior reasoning performance. The method is practical and the code is open-sourced.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has achieved remarkable success in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, widely used critic-free RL methods rely on uniform credit assignment, broadcasting the same advantage to all tokens regardless of their differences. We identify a critical failure mode of this design, which we refer to as Positive-Credit Contamination: low-probability tail tokens that are contextually erroneous receive identical positive credit to plausible ones within the same trajectory, resulting in the indiscriminate reinforcement of flawed reasoning behavior. To mitigate this issue, we propose Tail-Aware Credit calibratiOn (TACO), a method that calibrates uniform credit assignment to suppress undesirable positive updates. TACO first computes a tail-risk score that incorporates the local generation context to assess each token's risk of falling into the unreliable tail, distinguishing unexpected rarity from uncertainty-driven exploration. TACO then uses this score to tune positive credit for risky tokens without removing their gradients entirely, so that recurring useful rare patterns can accumulate reinforcement while incidental noise is progressively dampened. Experimental results across three LLMs and eight benchmarks show that TACO consistently outperforms GRPO-style baselines. Notably, TACO improves training stability, supporting sustained performance gains in long-horizon RL. The source code is available at: https://github.com/xiuyilou/TACO.

</details>


### [10] [ICDAR 2026 HIPE-OCRepair Competition on LLM-Assisted OCR Post-Correction for Historical Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08143)
*Maud Ehrmann,Emanuela Boros,Juri Opitz,Andrianos Michail,Florian Wagner,Simon Clematide*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HIPE-OCRepair-2026 is an ICDAR competition evaluating LLM-assisted OCR post-correction on historical documents across English, French, and German. Four teams submitted systems ranging from zero-shot to fine-tuning. Results show LLMs significantly improve OCR quality but exhibit performance variance across languages/noise levels, with over-correction on low-noise inputs as a key challenge. All resources are publicly released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OCR post-correction is a persistent challenge in digital heritage: legacy OCR errors plague large-scale digitized collections, and re-digitization is impractical. LLMs present a promising opportunity to revisit this problem, but their effectiveness across languages, document types, and noise conditions—as well as their hallucination tendencies—remains poorly understood.

Method: The competition provides the HIPE-OCRepair-2026 dataset, a harmonized multilingual resource of historical newspapers and printed works (17th–20th c.) in English, French, and German. Participants correct noisy OCR transcripts at paragraph/article level without source images. Evaluation uses a retrieval-oriented scoring approach rather than diplomatic transcription metrics. Four teams employed strategies from zero-shot prompting to continued pre-training and fine-tuning of LLMs.

Result: LLM-assisted systems significantly improve OCR quality overall, but performance varies across datasets, languages, and noise levels. A recurring issue is over-correction on low-noise inputs. The retrieval-oriented evaluation reveals limitations not captured by character error rate reduction alone.

Conclusion: Modern LLMs offer substantial gains for OCR post-correction in historical documents, but deployment requires careful handling of language- and noise-specific variability and mitigation of over-correction tendencies. The publicly released dataset, scorer, and pipeline establish a reproducible benchmark for future research.

Abstract: We present the results of HIPE-OCRepair-2026, an ICDAR competition on LLM-assisted OCR post-correction of historical documents. OCR post-correction remains a long-standing challenge in digital heritage: large-scale collections of digitized documents are affected by legacy OCR errors, while re-digitization at scale remains impractical. Large language models (LLMs) offers a major opportunity to revisit this challenge, yet their effectiveness across languages, document types, and noise conditions - and their tendency to hallucinate - remains insufficiently understood. HIPE-OCRepair-2026 pursues two objectives: (i) to evaluate the capabilities of modern OCR post-correction systems, and (ii) to provide a reproducible evaluation framework anchored in the HIPE-OCRepair-2026 dataset, a harmonized multilingual resource consolidating existing and newly curated historical datasets. Participants were tasked with correcting noisy OCR transcripts from historical newspapers and printed works in English, French, and German (17th-20th century), working at the level of coherent transcription units (paragraphs or articles) without access to source images. The evaluation adopts a retrieval-oriented rather than diplomatic scoring approach, reflecting the practical use case of search and access over digitized collections. Four teams submitted systems ranging from zero-shot prompting to continued pre-training and fine-tuning, offering insights into the merits of different adaptation strategies. Results show that modern LLM-assisted systems can significantly improve OCR quality, but performance varies across datasets, languages, and noise levels. Over-correction on low-noise inputs emerges as a recurring challenge, highlighting the importance of evaluation beyond character error reduction. The dataset, scorer, and evaluation pipeline are publicly released to support future research.

</details>


### [11] [A Reliability Assessment of LALM Audio Judges for Full-Duplex Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07985)
*A. Sayyad,J. Emmons,S. Jones,T. Lin,H. Krishnan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Gemini 2.5 Flash can serve as a reliable audio judge (LALM) for scoring full-duplex agent conversations directly from stereo waveforms, matching human raters on most production dimensions at ~100x lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating full-duplex spoken dialogue agents is expensive and slow when relying solely on human raters. The authors aim to validate whether Gemini audio models can act as reliable, cost-effective automated judges ("LALMs") that score conversations directly from raw audio, potentially substituting or supplementing human evaluation.

Method: The study tests three Gemini audio models (2.5 Flash, 3.5 Flash, 3.1 Pro) as zero-shot judges on 209 stereo sessions scored across 8 production dimensions. Gemini 2.5 Flash is validated as the ground-truth model against three calibrated human raters using: (i) Spearman rank correlation comparison between LALM-human and human-human pairs with bootstrap confidence intervals, (ii) exact-agreement analysis with the three-rater human mean, and (iii) sensitivity to 57 adversarial defect-injected clips assessed via Newcombe-Wilson confidence intervals. The dataset spans 152 natural full-duplex conversations across 13 accent-and-condition strata.

Result: For Gemini 2.5 Flash: (i) on 5/8 dimensions, LALM-human Spearman ρ differs from human-human ρ by ≤0.07, with 7/8 dimensions showing overlapping 95% bootstrap CIs; (ii) LALM agrees within ±1 point of the human mean on 60–92% of sessions for 6/8 dimensions; (iii) on 45/48 defect-dimension cells, LALM sensitivity equals or exceeds humans. Gemini 3.5 Flash improves agreement to 8/8 dimensions, while 3.1 Pro exhibits calibration drift (lower absolute scores despite comparable rank correlation). Human rating costs ~100× more than LALM inference.

Conclusion: Gemini 2.5 Flash is empirically defensible as a substitute or fourth rater on most evaluated dimensions. However, model swaps require re-validation focused on calibration, not just rank correlation. Four deployment caveats are identified, and the massive cost advantage makes LALM-based evaluation operationally compelling.

Abstract: We report the empirical reliability of Gemini models as audio judges that score full-duplex agent conversations directly from the raw stereo waveform, tested across three models in the Gemini family: 2.5 Flash, 3.5 Flash, and 3.1 Pro. Our primary evidence base uses Gemini 2.5 Flash as the ground-truth model, validated against three calibrated human raters on 209 stereo sessions, scored on 8 production dimensions: 152 full-duplex conversations across 13 accent-and-condition strata, together with 57 adversarial defect-injected clips. The evidence for Gemini 2.5 Flash is consistent across three tests. (i) On 5 of 8 dimensions the LALM-human Spearman rho departs from the pairwise human-human rho by at most 0.07, and on 7 of 8 dimensions the two quantities 95 percent bootstrap confidence intervals overlap. (ii) The LALM agrees with the three-rater human mean within 1 point on 60 to 92 percent of sessions on 6 of 8 dimensions. (iii) On 45 of 48 (defect, dimension) cells the LALM is as sensitive as humans or better under Newcombe-Wilson 95 percent confidence intervals, though most of these are underpowered nulls rather than demonstrated parity. Rank-ordering ability transfers across the Gemini family: 3.5 Flash improves simple agreement to 8 of 8 dimensions, while 3.1 Pro rates several dimensions markedly lower than humans despite comparable rank correlation. A model swap should be re-validated on calibration specifically, not assumed from rank-correlation alone. We identify four areas where deployment requires care, and we estimate that human rating alone for our current evaluation cadence costs roughly two orders of magnitude more than the equivalent LALM workload. The data presented here provides a defensible empirical basis for deploying the LALM as a substitute or fourth rater on the dimensions where the evidence supports it.

</details>


### [12] [LEXIC: Lightweight Eye-tracking eXtension via Injected Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08152)
*Sumin Lee,Kyeonghun Kim,Subeen Lee,Jiwon Yang,Tien Nguyen,Ken Ying-Kai Liao,Nam-Joon Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LEXIC-Base extends a gaze-only model with word-level difficulty signals (GPT-2 surprisal, frequency, length) via concatenation or residual conditioning, yielding statistically significant AUROC gains (+1.8–2.9 pp) on the EyeBench OneStop reading comprehension benchmark over a language-model-free baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On the EyeBench benchmark, text-aware models using pretrained LMs achieve 56–63% AUROC for predicting reading comprehension from eye movements, while gaze-only models perform at chance. The authors investigate how far a purely gaze-only model can be pushed without any language model, using only lightweight, word-level difficulty signals to narrow this gap.

Method: Building on the EyeBench AhnCNN baseline (LEXIC-Base), the authors propose two conditioning mechanisms to inject three precomputed word-level difficulty signals — GPT-2 surprisal, word frequency, and word length — into per-fixation inputs: (1) LEXIC-Concat: direct concatenation of difficulty features to fixation embeddings; (2) LEXIC-Res: a residual mechanism where a small prediction head estimates typical-reader gaze response, and the encoder is conditioned on the deviation between predicted and observed gaze. Evaluation uses K=5 seed-ensemble training across ten folds on the OneStop reading comprehension task.

Result: Both LEXIC-Concat and LEXIC-Res yield statistically consistent AUROC gains on Unseen Text of +1.8 to +2.2 percentage points (Wilcoxon p ≤ 0.065). LEXIC-Concat additionally improves Unseen Reader by +2.9 pp (p = 0.010). LEXIC-Res shows a weaker gain on Unseen Reader (+1.8 pp, p = 0.19), attributed to the prediction head being calibrated to training readers and transferring poorly to out-of-distribution readers.

Conclusion: Lightweight, language-model-free conditioning with word-level difficulty signals yields modest but statistically meaningful gains for gaze-only reading comprehension prediction. The residual approach reveals a key architectural limitation: calibration to training-reader gaze distributions does not generalize well to unseen readers, identifying an important boundary for future gaze-only modeling.

Abstract: On the recent EyeBench benchmark, predicting reading comprehension from eye movements exposes a stark gap: text-aware models using pretrained language models reach 56--63% AUROC, while gaze-only models operate at chance. We ask how far a gaze-only model can be pushed by lightweight, language-model-free conditioning. Building on the EyeBench AhnCNN baseline, LEXIC-Base, we propose two mechanisms to inject three precomputed word-level difficulty signals, GPT-2 surprisal, word frequency, and word length, into the per-fixation input: direct concatenation, LEXIC-Concat, and a residual mechanism, LEXIC-Res, where a small head predicts typical-reader gaze response and the encoder is conditioned on the deviation. On the OneStop reading comprehension task, with K=5 seed-ensemble training across ten folds, both mechanisms produce statistically consistent AUROC gains on Unseen Text, +1.8 to +2.2 percentage points, Wilcoxon p <= 0.065. LEXIC-Concat additionally lifts Unseen Reader by +2.9 percentage points, p = 0.010. We trace an architectural boundary in LEXIC-Res on Unseen Reader, +1.8 percentage points, p = 0.19, to the prediction head being calibrated to training readers, transferring imperfectly to out-of-distribution readers.

</details>


### [13] [Hallucination Self-Play: Bootstrapping Reinforced Detector via Evolved Generator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07993)
*Shiping Yang,Shining Liang,Weihao Liu,Wenbiao Ding,Linjun Shou,Lu Cheng,Angel X. Chang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Hallucination Self-Play (HSP) is a framework where a detector and generator, initialized from the same base model, iteratively improve each other — the detector trains the generator via RLAIF to produce harder hallucinations, and the generator's outputs further fine-tune the detector, enabling small LLMs to match or surpass larger models on faithfulness detection without external supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for detecting faithfulness hallucinations rely on static generators to synthesize training data, which limits iterative improvement of the detector. There is a need for a dynamic, co-evolving framework that can bootstrap hallucination detection without requiring large-scale human annotation.

Method: HSP initializes both a detector and a generator from the same base LLM. The detector is first fine-tuned on human-labeled data, then used as a reward model to train the generator via reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF) to produce increasingly hard-to-detect hallucinations. The evolved generator then synthesizes hallucination data, which is used to further optimize the detector through rule-based reinforcement learning. This self-play loop continues iteratively.

Result: Experiments on the RAGTruth benchmark with two model families show that HSP progressively enhances a small LLM to match or even outperform advanced LLMs in faithfulness hallucination detection, all without external supervision beyond the initial human-labeled fine-tuning set.

Conclusion: HSP demonstrates that self-play between a generator and detector can effectively bootstrap hallucination detection capabilities in small models, eliminating the reliance on static generators or external supervision from larger LLMs.

Abstract: Identifying faithfulness hallucinations in LLM-generated outputs remains challenging due to the scarcity of high-quality annotated data. Recent work relies on advanced LLMs to synthesize training data, including rationales, labels, and hallucinated claims. However, these methods treat the generator as a static component, limiting iterative improvement of the detector. To address this limitation, we introduce Hallucination Self-Play (HSP), a novel framework that enables the detector to bootstrap with an evolved generator. HSP involves two roles initialized from the same base model, a detector that assesses the faithfulness of model outputs, and a generator that produces increasingly hard-to-detect hallucinated responses. Specifically, the detector is first fine-tuned on human-labeled data and then employed as a reward model to train the generator via reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF). In turn, the evolved generator synthesizes hallucination data to further optimize the detector through rule-based reinforcement learning. Experiments on RAGTruth benchmark and two model families demonstrate that the proposed framework can progressively enhance a small LLM to match or even outperform advanced LLMs without external supervision. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Hallucination-Self-Play-50B5 .

</details>


### [14] [From Execution to Education: A Bloom-Aligned Framework for Measuring Educational Control in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08009)
*Yi Zhang,Julia Rayz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Bloom's Taxonomy-aligned framework to measure LLMs' ability to control the cognitive demand of programming tasks. Testing Qwen3-Next models across 2,520 tasks reveals a directional asymmetry: models can increase cognitive demand reliably but struggle to decrease it. Probing analyses show that general-purpose and code-specialized models exhibit distinct internal representations of educational control, and strong coding performance does not guarantee Bloom-aligned educational adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between LLMs' ability to solve programming tasks and their capacity to adapt those tasks for learners at different cognitive levels, using revised Bloom's Taxonomy as an operational framework for cognitive demand in CS education.

Method: A Bloom-aligned framework is applied to two intervention settings: (1) general difficulty control (harder/easier) and (2) Bloom's control (targeting higher/lower Bloom's levels). A matched model pair — Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Instruct and Qwen3-Coder-Next — is evaluated on 2,520 tasks from three benchmarks. Analysis uses semantic-delta clustering and layer-wise Fisher's Discriminant Ratio probing to characterize internal representations.

Result: Both models exhibit robust directional asymmetry: they reliably increase cognitive demand but fail to lower it effectively. The general instruct model shows clearer middle-layer separability for both difficulty and Bloom-control contrasts; the coder model shows weaker separability for difficulty and a deeper peak for Bloom-control contrasts. Strong execution performance does not translate to strong educational control.

Conclusion: Strong coding performance does not automatically entail Bloom-aligned educational control. The framework provides a principled way to evaluate and compare LLMs on their capacity to adapt instructional materials along pedagogically meaningful cognitive dimensions.

Abstract: We introduce a Bloom-aligned framework for measuring educational control in Large Language Models (LLMs): the ability to preserve a task's instructional intent while shifting its cognitive demand toward specified learning objectives. We apply this framework to programming tasks in computer science education to study the gap between solving tasks and adapting them for learners. Using revised Bloom's Taxonomy as an operational scale of cognitive demand, we evaluate two intervention settings: general difficulty control, where models are asked to make tasks harder or easier, and Bloom's control, where models are asked to target higher or lower Bloom's levels. We evaluate a matched Qwen3-Next model pair, comparing Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Instruct with Qwen3-Coder-Next across 2,520 tasks from three benchmarks. The framework reveals a robust directional asymmetry: both models reliably increase cognitive demand, but struggle to lower it. We further characterize these outcomes with semantic-delta clustering and layer-wise Fisher's Discriminant Ratio probing. Within this controlled comparison, the general model shows clearer middle-layer separability for both general difficulty and Bloom-control contrasts, whereas the coder model shows weaker separability for general difficulty and a deeper peak for Bloom-control contrasts. These results show that strong execution performance does not automatically entail Bloom-aligned educational control.

</details>


### [15] [Tool-Making and Self-Evolving LLM Agents in Low-Latency Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08010)
*Kalle Kujanpää,Ning Liu,Shahnawaz Alam,Yeshwanth Reddy Sura,Tianyu Yang,Kristina Klinkner,Shervin Malmasi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes replacing the repetitive inference-time code generation loop in production LLM agents with a tool-making pipeline that compiles repeated SOP steps into validated, versioned tools, reducing latency by 42% and error rate by up to 53% in a real-world fulfillment center alarm-triage system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production LLM agents waste latency and reliability by regenerating code for identical procedural steps across every request. This redundant inference is costly, introduces run-to-run variance, and complicates auditing. The authors aim to eliminate this waste by extracting and crystallizing repeated standard operating procedure (SOP) steps into reusable, verified tools.

Method: The approach introduces an agentic tool-making pipeline that operates before deployment. It collects execution traces from a live environment, observes backend schemas and values, generates candidate tools grounded in these observations, and repairs them against labeled test cases. The pipeline produces validated, versioned tools. At runtime, the production agent directly calls these pre-compiled tools, falling back to code generation only when a tool is unavailable or inapplicable. The system is deployed in a Fulfillment Center alarm-triage agent operating over a 44-node SOP with heterogeneous metric backends.

Result: In production, tool calls reduced p50 latency by 42%. On 1,500 historical alarms, end-to-end error rate dropped by up to 53% by suppressing run-to-run variance. Because tools return compact structured verdicts, a controlled ablation using a direct-call architecture further reduced p50 latency by 62%. Versioned tools also improved auditability and surfaced specification gaps and upstream data drift.

Conclusion: Self-evolving agents that compile repeated procedural knowledge into validated, versioned tools can make industrial LLM systems significantly faster, more reliable, and easier to operate, with additional benefits for auditability and drift detection.

Abstract: Production LLM agents often waste latency and reliability by regenerating code for the same procedural steps on every request. We replace this inference-time coding loop with an agentic tool-making pipeline that compiles repeated SOP steps into validated, versioned tools before deployment. The tool-maker grounds synthesis in the live environment as it collects execution traces, observes backend schemas and values, generates candidate tools, and repairs them against labeled cases. At runtime, the production agent calls these tools directly and falls back to code generation only when needed. We deploy the approach in a Fulfillment Center alarm-triage system, where an agent diagnoses alarms against a 44-node SOP over heterogeneous metric backends. In production, tool calls reduce p50 latency by 42%. On 1,500 historical alarms, they reduce end-to-end error rate by up to 53% by suppressing run-to-run variance in repeated steps. Because tools return compact structured verdicts, they also enable a simpler direct-call architecture, reducing p50 latency by a further 62% in a controlled ablation. Versioned tools also improve auditability and expose specification gaps and upstream data drift. Our results show that self-evolving agents can make industrial LLM systems faster, more reliable, and easier to operate.

</details>


### [16] [Large-Language-Models-as-a-Judge in Theory-Agnostic Adaptive Metric-Alignment for Prototypical Networks in Personality Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08374)
*Jing Jie Tan,Ban-Hoe Kwan,Danny Wee-Kiat Ng,Yan-Chai Hum,Shih-Yu Lo,Po-An Chen,Noriyuki Kawarazaki,Kosuke Takano,Anissa Mokraoui*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: JAM is a theory-agnostic framework for personality recognition that learns unified latent pseudo-facets from text without relying on predefined psychological taxonomies. It uses an Attention-Pooled Graph Prototypical Network, Cross-Theory Harmonization, and an LLM-as-a-Judge mechanism to improve cross-framework generalization and support low-resource personality theories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional personality recognition is constrained by theory-dependent formulations that fit models to predefined psychological taxonomies, limiting generalization. Since personality is theory-invariant while existing annotations reflect only partial and inconsistent views of the same latent traits, a theory-agnostic approach is needed to uncover shared underlying behavioral structure.

Method: JAM employs three core components: (1) an Attention-Pooled Graph Prototypical Network that learns structured representations via clustering in embedding space; (2) Cross-Theory Harmonization (CTH) integrating Human-Guided Linkage and Machine-Induced Consensus to unify heterogeneous datasets without predefined labels; and (3) an LLM-as-a-Judge mechanism in two configurations (LLM-before-the-loop and LLM-in-the-loop) that identifies ambiguous samples to guide adaptive metric learning.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that JAM improves cross-framework generalization and performance, establishing a strong step toward theory-agnostic personality inference and supporting low-resource personality theories.

Conclusion: JAM successfully shifts personality recognition from theory-dependent to theory-agnostic learning by discovering unified latent pseudo-facets. The framework's ability to infer psychological profiles directly from text without theory-specific labels, combined with improved cross-framework generalization, represents significant progress toward more flexible and generalizable personality inference.

Abstract: Personality recognition has traditionally been constrained by theory-dependent formulations, where models are trained to fit predefined psychological taxonomies rather than uncovering shared underlying behavioral structure. This limits generalization, as personality itself is better understood as theory-invariant, while existing annotations reflect only partial and sometimes inconsistent views of the same latent traits. In this work, we introduce JAM ((J)udge for (A)daptive (M)etric-Alignment), a theory-agnostic framework that shifts learning from adapting to predefined personality theories toward discovering unified latent pseudo-facets that capture shared psychological structure. Rather than constraining the model to any personality taxonomy during training or inference, the framework learns generalizable psychological representations and can infer an individual's latent psychological profile directly from the textual samples, without requiring theory-specific labels. JAM achieves this through an Attention-Pooled Graph Prototypical Network that learns structured representations via clustering in embedding space, together with a Cross-Theory Harmonization (CTH) approach that integrates (i) Human-Guided Linkage and (ii) Machine-Induced Consensus to unify heterogeneous datasets without relying on predefined labels. To further improve robustness and data quality, we incorporate an LLM-as-a-Judge mechanism operating in two configurations, (i) LLM-before-the-loop and (ii) LLM-in-the-loop which identifies ambiguous samples to guide adaptive metric learning. Experiments show that JAM improves cross-framework generalization and performance, establishing a strong step toward theory-agnostic personality inference and supporting low-resource personality theories. The related code repository, model weights, and artifacts are available at https://research.jingjietan.com/JAM

</details>


### [17] [Can We Trust LLM's Logic? Quantifying Uncertainty, Coherence, and Robustness via a Graph-Based Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08017)
*Riccardo Revalor,Jalees Rehman,Debjit Pal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GRAPHEVAL introduces a graph-based framework for evaluating LLM reasoning fidelity, proposing the Graph Reasoning Coherence Score (GRCS) as a novel uncertainty metric that captures semantic-structural consensus and detects confident hallucinations. It also introduces Graph Self-Consistency (GSC), a medoid-based decoding strategy that prioritizes reasoning faithfulness over nominal accuracy, and demonstrates through adversarial ablation that GSC-selected reasoning paths are structurally critical.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs exhibit flawed, unfaithful reasoning that decoding strategies like Self-Consistency fail to detect because they only assess final-answer agreement, ignoring intermediate logical validity. This motivates the need for reliable uncertainty quantification that accounts for semantic and structural coherence in reasoning, selection of faithful reasoning chains, and understanding reasoning robustness under adversarial conditions.

Method: The authors propose GRAPHEVAL, which reframes UQ as a reasoning fidelity problem using graph-based representations of the reasoning space. The core metric, GRCS, quantifies semantic-structural consensus across reasoning paths. GSC is introduced as a medoid-based decoding strategy that selects the most structurally central reasoning chain. Adversarial medoid ablation is used to test whether removing the GSC-selected path degrades faithfulness and accuracy.

Result: GRCS is the only metric consistently negatively correlated with reasoning faithfulness across both large and small models. GSC exposes that Self-Consistency accuracy in smaller models is inflated by unfaithful lucky guesses, while preserving or improving accuracy in more capable models. Adversarial medoid ablation shows the GSC-selected path is a "load-bearing path" — forcing models away from it degrades reasoning faithfulness and, in targeted cases, accuracy.

Conclusion: Quantifying reasoning uncertainty requires going beyond final-answer agreement to assess semantic-structural coherence. GRCS reliably captures reasoning fidelity, and GSC offers a decoding strategy that prioritizes faithful reasoning. The structural centrality of GSC-selected paths reveals that reasoning topology is fragile under adversarial perturbation, underscoring the importance of graph-aware evaluation for trustworthy LLM reasoning.

Abstract: Large-Language Models (LLMs) can be prone to flawed and unfaithful reasoning that decoding strategies like Self-Consistency (SC) fail to detect as they evaluate only final-answer agreement while ignoring the logical validity of intermediate steps. This raises three fundamental questions: How can we reliably quantify uncertainty in LLM reasoning? Can semantic, structural, and causal awareness select more faithful reasoning compared to naïve majority voting? and How robust is reasoning topology under adversarial conditions? To address these questions, we introduce GRAPHEVAL, a graph-based reasoning framework that re-frames uncertainty quantification (UQ) as a holistic reasoning fidelity problem. We propose a novel UQ metric, Graph Reasoning Coherence Score (GRCS), that quantifies semantic-structural consensus of the reasoning space and captures pathological mode collapse and confident hallucinations. We find that GRCS is the only metric that is consistently negatively correlated with reasoning faithfulness across both more capable and smaller models. Additionally, we introduce Graph Self-Consistency (GSC), a medoid-based decoding strategy that trades nominal accuracy for reasoning fidelity, exposing the degree to which SC is inflated by unfaithful lucky guesses in smaller models, while preserving or improving accuracy in more capable ones. Finally, through adversarial medoid ablation, we demonstrate that the GSC-selected path acts as a "load-bearing path" and forcing models away from it degrades reasoning faithfulness and, in targeted cases, causes drops in accuracy.

</details>


### [18] [Structured Pruning of Large Language Models via Power Transformation and Sign-Preserving Score Aggregation with Adaptive Feature Retention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08027)
*Ryota Kobayashi,Tsubasa Hirakawa,Takayoshi Yamashita,Hironobu Fujiyoshi,Yasunori Ishii,Tomoyuki Okuno,Kazuki Kozuka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes an improved structured pruning method for LLMs that adapts Adaptive Feature Retention (AFR) from unstructured to structured pruning by solving three issues: distribution mismatch via power transformation, sign-preserving score aggregation, and percentile-based outlier removal. Demonstrates accuracy comparable to unstructured AFR pruning on Llama-3-8B, Vicuna-v1.5-13B, and LLaVA-v1.5-13B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unstructured pruning methods like AFR achieve strong accuracy but lack practical hardware speedup. Directly adapting AFR to structured pruning introduces three problems: (1) heterogeneous pruning score distributions across layers/heads, (2) loss of sign information that indicates optimization direction consistency, and (3) sensitivity to outliers in pruning scores. The paper aims to bridge the gap between unstructured pruning accuracy and structured pruning efficiency.

Method: A three-component unified framework: (1) Power transformation to nonlinearly align heterogeneous pruning score distributions across different layers and attention heads into a comparable space; (2) Sign-preserving score aggregation that retains the sign direction (positive/negative) of individual pruning scores when combining them, preserving optimization consistency signals; (3) Percentile-based outlier removal to filter extreme pruning score values that would otherwise distort the pruning decision. These are integrated into a structured pruning pipeline based on AFR.

Result: The method achieves accuracy comparable to unstructured AFR pruning on Llama-3-8B, Vicuna-v1.5-13B (language models), and LLaVA-v1.5-13B (vision-language model), while delivering practical inference speedup through structured pruning, which unstructured methods cannot provide.

Conclusion: The proposed three techniques successfully bridge unstructured-to-structured pruning adaptation for AFR, enabling structured pruning that retains unstructured-level accuracy with real-world inference efficiency gains. This demonstrates that careful handling of score distribution alignment, sign information, and outliers is critical for transferring unstructured pruning criteria to structured settings.

Abstract: This paper proposes an improved structured pruning method for large language models (LLMs) that addresses key challenges in adapting Adaptive Feature Retention (AFR), an unstructured pruning technique, to structured pruning. When applying AFR to structured pruning, three major problems arise: distribution mismatch between heterogeneous pruning scores, loss of sign information indicating optimization direction consistency, and influence of outliers. To address these issues, we propose a unified approach combining power transformation for nonlinear distribution alignment, sign-preserving score aggregation, and percentile-based outlier removal. Experiments on Llama-3-8B, Vicuna-v1.5-13B, and LLaVA-v1.5-13B demonstrate that our method maintains accuracy comparable to unstructured pruning while achieving practical inference speedup through structured pruning.

</details>


### [19] [PLURAL: A Global Dataset for Value Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08034)
*Dhruv Agarwal,Anya Shukla,Tanya Goyal,Aditya Vashistha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PLURAL is a large-scale, value-focused preference dataset (~500K triplets across 20 countries) built from the Integrated Values Survey. It enables training LLMs for pluralistic alignment, reducing value representation error by up to 27.7% and passing blind human evaluation across India, Brazil, and Japan.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs predominantly reflect Western values, failing to represent the diverse value systems of global users. There is a need for scalable resources that enable pluralistic alignment — steering models to respect culturally distinct normative preferences across countries.

Method: The authors ground their work in the Integrated Values Survey (IVS), a nationally representative survey across 92 countries. They employ a two-stage synthetic generation pipeline: first, survey responses are mapped to value dimensions; second, these signals are transformed into realistic preference triplets (scenario, preferred response, dispreferred response) that preserve normative value structures. The initial PLURAL release covers 20 diverse countries with ~500K triplets. Evaluation is three-pronged: (i) dataset-level validation of cross-country differences and within-country diversity, (ii) automated evaluation of alignment improvement (mean absolute error reduction), and (iii) blind human evaluation with 176 evaluators from India, Brazil, and Japan.

Result: (i) PLURAL faithfully preserves both cross-country value variation and within-country diversity from IVS. (ii) Training on PLURAL improves value alignment with target countries, reducing mean absolute error by up to 27.7% over strong baselines. (iii) Blind human evaluators in India, Brazil, and Japan judged PLURAL-aligned responses as significantly more representative of their national values.

Conclusion: PLURAL provides a learnable, scalable signal for value steering in LLMs, offering a practical resource for pluralistic alignment. It demonstrates that synthetic preference data grounded in representative survey instruments can effectively encode culturally diverse normative preferences.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are used worldwide, yet disproportionately reflect Western values, limiting their ability to represent diverse value systems. We introduce PLURAL, a large-scale, value-focused preference dataset grounded in the Integrated Values Survey (IVS), a nationally representative survey spanning 92 countries. Using a two-stage generation pipeline, we transform survey responses into synthetic preference triplets that preserve normative value signals while producing realistic scenarios. We release an initial version of PLURAL containing ~500,000 preference triplets representing people in 20 diverse countries. We evaluate PLURAL in three ways: (i) dataset-level validation showing that it preserves both cross-country value differences and within-country diversity from the original survey; (ii) automated evaluation showing that training on PLURAL improves alignment with target countries' cultural profiles, reducing mean absolute error by up to 27.7% relative to strong baselines; and (iii) blind human evaluation with 176 evaluators in India, Brazil, and Japan, who judge PLURAL-aligned responses as more representative of their national values. Together, these results show that PLURAL contains learnable signal for value steering, offering a scalable resource for pluralistic alignment. Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/agdhruv/plural-alignment

</details>


### [20] [What LLM Forecasters Know but Don't Say: Probing Internal Representations for Calibration and Faithfulness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08046)
*Raphaël Sarfati,Pratyush Ranjan Tiwari,Siddharth Boppana,Christopher J. Earls,Srikar Varadaraj,Eric Ho*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Probes trained on internal LLM activations outperform chain-of-thought reasoning in calibration and faithfully track forecast changes, even when CoT is misleading. Pre-reasoning representations largely fix final answers, enabling 30–47% token savings via early routing without accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuned LLM forecasters suffer from poor calibration and unfaithful chain-of-thought reasoning — the CoT trace often fails to reflect the true evidential basis of a forecast. The authors investigate whether internal model representations (activations) provide a more reliable window into both calibration quality and reasoning faithfulness.

Method: Using Eternis-Forecaster 8B (with generalization checks on GLM-4.7-Flash and GLM-4.5-Air) on the OpenForesight dataset, the authors: (1) train representation-pooling probes on intermediate activations to evaluate calibration; (2) assess CoT faithfulness via evidence ablation (removing influential sources) and diversionary injection, comparing behavioral shifts against probe activations; (3) test whether a single pre-reasoning forward pass can recover the final answer, and route questions based on the spread of this pre-set distribution to save tokens.

Result: Probes achieve substantially better calibration than the model's own output. Under evidence ablation, forecast behavior changes while CoT traces often remain unchanged; probes function as "lie detectors," tracking behavioral shifts and predicting direction of change in 84% of cases — including when CoT conceals the perturbation. A single pre-reasoning pass recovers committed answers and confidence, and routing by pre-set answer distribution spread saves 30–47% of generated tokens with no accuracy loss.

Conclusion: Internal representation probing is a practical, effective tool for calibrating, auditing faithfulness, and efficiently triaging LLM forecasters and reasoning models, offering reliability where surface-level CoT reasoning falls short.

Abstract: Large language models fine-tuned for forecasting can be accurate yet poorly calibrated, and their chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning may not faithfully reflect the evidence behind a forecast. We ask whether internal representations offer a more direct window into both. Working with Eternis-Forecaster 8B on OpenForesight, we train representation-pooling probes on intermediate activations and find they achieve substantially better calibration; a result that also holds for GLM-4.7-Flash and GLM-4.5-Air. We then assess CoT faithfulness through evidence ablation and diversionary injection: removing an influential source in the prompt often changes the model's forecast while leaving the reasoning trace untouched. The same probes function as lie detectors: their activations track behavioral shifts far better than the reasoning trace does, and they also predict the direction of change in 84% of cases, including when the CoT conceals the perturbation's influence. Finally, forced answering reveals that forecasts are largely fixed before reasoning begins: a single pre-reasoning pass recovers the committed answer and confidence, and routing questions by the spread of this pre-set answer distribution saves 30-47% of generated tokens, with no loss of accuracy. Together, these results establish probing internal representations as a practical tool for calibrating, auditing, and triaging language model forecasters and reasoning models more broadly.

</details>


### [21] [Holographic Neural PCFG for Unsupervised Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08063)
*Ryosuke Yamaki,Daichi Mochihashi,Nobutaka Shimada,Tadahiro Taniguchi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Hol-PCFG replaces black-box neural rule scoring with closed-form holographic embeddings (circular correlation over torus-constrained embeddings), achieving state-of-the-art unsupervised constituency parsing across six languages while reducing parameters by 99.94% and enabling character-level Japanese parsing without segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Neural PCFGs rely on high-capacity black-box networks for rule scoring, leaving probabilities without interpretable mathematical form. The authors seek to give PCFG rules a closed-form, algebraically principled scoring mechanism that preserves or improves performance.

Method: Hol-PCFG adapts Holographic Embeddings (circular correlation from knowledge-graph triple scoring) to model PCFG's three relation types — left-child, right-child, and lexical emission — over embeddings constrained to a torus (complex unit circle). This yields closed-form rule probabilities that intrinsically encode grammar-rule structure.

Result: State-of-the-art unsupervised parsing in six languages; 99.94% reduction in rule-scoring parameters vs. baseline; more stable training; character-level Japanese parsing with near-identical morpheme-level performance, obviating morphological segmentation.

Conclusion: Algebraically structured scoring via holographic embeddings can match or surpass black-box neural parameterizations for PCFG parsing, offering a compact, interpretable, and stable alternative that also enables segmentation-free parsing for morphologically rich languages like Japanese.

Abstract: Unsupervised constituency parsing aims to accurately induce latent tree structures from raw text alone. Recent neural parameterizations of PCFGs achieve strong performance in both supervised and unsupervised parsing, yet rely on high-capacity black-box networks for rule scoring -- as exemplified by the Neural PCFG family -- leaving rule probabilities without an interpretable mathematical form. In this paper, we propose Holographic Neural PCFG (Hol-PCFG), which recasts PCFG rule scoring as algebraic relation modeling among grammar-symbol embeddings. Hol-PCFG adapts Holographic Embeddings (Nickel et al., 2016), which scores knowledge-graph triples via circular correlation, to the left-child, right-child, and lexical-emission relations over torus-constrained embeddings, giving every rule probability a closed form that carries the intrinsic structure of grammar rules by construction. Hol-PCFG achieves state-of-the-art parsing performance in six languages while cutting rule-scoring parameters by 99.94% relative to the baseline model and training more stably. Additionally, we demonstrate that Hol-PCFG can parse Japanese directly from characters without any morphological segmentation, retaining nearly the same morpheme-level performance.

</details>


### [22] [COBART: Controlled, Optimized, Bidirectional and Auto-Regressive Transformer for Ad Headline Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08071)
*Yashal Shakti Kanungo,Gyanendra Das,Pooja A,Sumit Negi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A prefix-controlled BART fine-tuning method for ad headline generation that achieves 25.82% Rouge-L improvement and 5.82% CTR gain over baselines, with user-controllable output length for diverse ad formats.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing headline generation methods fail to adapt to evolving ad formats and changing creative requirements. There is a need for a method that simultaneously optimizes CTR and headline quality while allowing customization (e.g., length control) for different ad formats.

Method: Prefix control tokens are integrated with BART (Bidirectional and Auto-Regressive Transformer) fine-tuning. These control tokens enable users to specify desired headline length, allowing the model to generate optimized headlines tailored to different ad formats. The approach is architecture-agnostic and adaptable to various creative requirements and optimization criteria.

Result: The proposed method achieves a 25.82% increase in Rouge-L score and a 5.82% increase in estimated CTR over a previously published strong ad headline generation baseline.

Conclusion: Prefix-controlled BART fine-tuning is an effective, flexible approach for generating optimized ad headlines that meet diverse format requirements while improving both quality and CTR performance.

Abstract: Online ads are essential to all businesses and ad headlines are one of their core creative component. Existing methods can generate headlines automatically and also optimize their click-through-rate (CTR) and quality. However, evolving ad formats and changing creative requirements make it difficult to generate optimized & customized headlines.
