We propose a novel method that uses prefix control tokens along with BART fine-tuning. It yields the highest CTR and also allows users to control the length of generated headlines for use across different ad formats. The method is also flexible and can easily be adapted to other architectures, creative requirements and optimization criteria. Our experiments demonstrate a 25.82% increment in Rouge-L and a 5.82% increment in estimated CTR over previously published strong ad headline generation baseline.

</details>


### [23] [MASTE: A Multi-Agent Pipeline for Zero-Shot Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08080)
*Ao Hong,Lehang Wang,Zhirun Yue,Mingxin Wang,Zihan Wang,Houde Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MASTE proposes a multi-agent pipeline that decomposes zero-shot Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction into four sequential subtasks handled by specialized agents, substantially outperforming existing zero-shot and chain-of-thought LLM baselines without using any labeled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLMs struggle with zero-shot ASTE because single-pass generation forces simultaneous resolution of span boundaries, opinion grouping, and sentiment polarity. Few-shot ICL and chain-of-thought prompting provide only marginal gains and depend on labeled in-domain demonstrations or hand-crafted reasoning prompts unavailable in zero-shot scenarios.

Method: MASTE uses a multi-agent pipeline with four sequential stages: (1) aspect term extraction, (2) opinion term extraction conditioned on aspects, (3) aspect-opinion pairing conditioned on both, and (4) sentiment classification conditioned on the paired triplets. Each stage is handled by a specialized agent with explicit conditioning on prior outputs, enabling training-free zero-shot operation across different LLM backbones.

Result: Extensive experiments on four ASTE benchmarks demonstrate that MASTE substantially outperforms zero-shot and chain-of-thought LLM baselines under the same backbone, significantly narrowing the performance gap to fully supervised methods without using any labeled triplets.

Conclusion: Decomposing complex structured prediction tasks like ASTE into a multi-agent pipeline with sequential, explicitly conditioned subtasks is an effective strategy for boosting zero-shot LLM performance, approaching supervised results without training data.

Abstract: Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction (ASTE) requires jointly identifying (aspect, opinion, sentiment) triples from a given review sentence. While large language models (LLMs) achieve strong zero-shot performance on many NLP benchmarks, their effectiveness on ASTE remains limited, as single-pass generation forces the model to determine span boundaries, opinion grouping, and sentiment polarity in a single decoding step. Common remedies, such as few-shot in-context learning and chain-of-thought prompting, offer only marginal improvements and rely heavily on either in-domain demonstrations sampled from labeled training data or carefully engineered reasoning prompts, neither of which is broadly available in zero-shot deployment. Inspired by the classical agent paradigm, we propose MASTE, a multi-agent pipeline for zero-shot Aspect Sentiment Triplet Extraction. MASTE decomposes ASTE into four sequential stages, where specialized agents handle different compositional subtasks with explicit conditioning on prior outputs. This design enables entirely training-free zero-shot ASTE and generalizes across different backbones and datasets. Extensive experiments on four ASTE benchmarks show that MASTE substantially outperforms zero-shot and chain-of-thought LLM baselines under the same backbone, narrowing the gap to fully supervised methods without using any labeled triplets. Code is available at https://github.com/Hankerlove/MASTE.

</details>


### [24] [COALA: Robust Contextualized Speech-augmented Language Modeling for ASR via Contrastive Regularizer and Biasing Score Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08117)
*Jhih-Rong Guo,Bi-Cheng Yan,Tien-Hong Lo,Berlin Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: COALA proposes a framework that maps speech-augmented language model latent representations into a discriminative space to score audio-entity matching, addressing SLM context-window limitations and training collapse in multi-target scenarios for improved contextual biasing in ASR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speech-augmented language models (SLMs) have inherent context-window limitations that make it difficult to identify relevant target entities from large-scale biasing lists, especially in multi-entity scenarios where multiple rare words co-occur. Prior approaches also suffer from training collapse under such multi-target conditions.

Method: COALA maps SLM latent representations into a specialized discriminative space to quantify the matching intensity between audio segments and candidate entities. This scoring mechanism operates alongside the SLM to select relevant targets from the biasing list, and the framework addresses training instability in multi-target utterances.

Result: Experiments on the LibriSpeech benchmark show that COALA consistently achieves superior contextual biasing performance across various biasing list scales.

Conclusion: COALA provides a robust solution for integrating external knowledge into SLM-based ASR systems, effectively handling both single and multi-entity contextual biasing scenarios while overcoming context-window and training stability limitations.

Abstract: Contextual biasing seeks to integrate external knowledge into automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems to accurately recognize domain-specific entities. In this paper, we propose COALA (Contextualized ASR Leveraging Biasing Scoring), a robust framework designed to enhance speech-augmented language models (SLMs) in complex multi-entity scenarios. Considering the inherent context-window limitations of SLMs, identifying relevant target entities from a large-scale biasing list is crucial for effective recognition. To this end, COALA maps SLM latent representations into a specialized discriminative space to quantify the matching intensity between audio segments and candidate entities. Furthermore, we address the training collapse in prior study when handling multi-target utterances-where multiple rare words co-occur. Experimental results on the LibriSpeech benchmark demonstrate that COALA consistently achieves superior contextual biasing performance across various biasing list scales.

</details>


### [25] [SQuaD-SQL: Efficient Text-to-SQL with Small Language Models via LLM-Guided Knowledge Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08161)
*Wangyu Wu,Xiaojian Lin,Rong Fu,Zaiyang Yu,Xuhang Chen,Wenjun Yu,Zhenhong Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SQuaD-SQL uses knowledge distillation and synthetic data generation to train small language models (SLMs) for Text-to-SQL, achieving 86.9% execution accuracy on WikiSQL while being significantly more efficient than LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs perform well on Text-to-SQL but are computationally prohibitive for resource-constrained deployments. The authors aim to bridge this gap by enabling SLMs to approach LLM-level performance on Text-to-SQL through targeted training strategies.

Method: SQuaD-SQL combines three components: (1) LLM-based synthetic data generation using carefully designed prompts to extract structured knowledge; (2) parameter-efficient fine-tuning that allows full model training on a single consumer GPU; and (3) domain-adaptive fine-tuning with domain-specific synthetic data for targeted performance gains.

Result: SQuaD-SQL attains 86.9% execution accuracy on the WikiSQL test set, approaching LLM-level performance while delivering faster inference and lower memory usage.

Conclusion: With appropriate training strategies, SLMs can serve as practical, efficient alternatives to LLMs for Text-to-SQL in resource-limited environments.

Abstract: Text-to-SQL is a fundamental task in natural language processing that enables users to interact with structured databases using natural language. While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance on this task, their substantial computational requirements hinder deployment in resource-constrained settings. In this paper, we introduce SQuaD-SQL (Small-Qualified and Distilled for SQL), a novel approach that empowers small language models (SLMs) to approach the performance of LLMs on the Text-to-SQL task while significantly improving efficiency through knowledge distillation and synthetic data generation. Our method comprises three key components: (1) LLM-based synthetic data generation, where structured knowledge is extracted from LLMs via carefully designed prompting strategies; (2) parameter-efficient fine-tuning, enabling full model training on a single consumer-grade GPU; and (3) domain-adaptive fine-tuning, where domain-specific synthetic data further enhances performance in targeted domains. Experiments on the WikiSQL dataset demonstrate that SQuaD-SQL achieves an execution accuracy of 86.9% on the test set, approaching the performance of LLMs while offering faster inference and lower memory usage. These results suggest that, with proper training strategies, SLMs can serve as practical and efficient alternatives for Text-to-SQL applications in resource-limited environments.

</details>


### [26] [Hidden Decoding at Scale: Latent Computation Scaling for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08186)
*Aiwei Liu,Cheng Shi,Chuhan Wu,Ci Lei,Di Lu,Donald He,Fan Zhang,Fanhao Kong,Feifei Zhang,Guan Wang,Haicheng Wang,Haoyu Liu,Houjin Yu,Jiachen Ding,Jiayi Feng,Jie Zhou,Jijun Chi,Jindi Shi,Jing Lei,Junjie Zhang,Laiyi Li,Le Tian,Linhao Zhang,Miao Fan,Sijun Zhang,Wei Jia,Weiwei Shi,Wenhan Li,Wentao Zhao,Wenteng Liang,Xiao Zhou,Xiaojin Zhou,Xihuai Wang,Xinyu Gao,Xuanliang Wang,Xuyang Ao,Yang Yu,Yangxiu You,Yinuo Zhao,Yufei Kuang,Yufei Wang,Yuan Liu,Yuan Liu,Yuwen Chen,Zhencong Tian,Zhongyin Zhao,Zilin Yu,Zitao Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Hidden Decoding proposes scaling LLMs by expanding tokens into multiple independent streams (adding computation along the sequence-length dimension) rather than enlarging the Transformer backbone. It enables fixed-backbone continued pretraining with Stream-Factorized Attention to keep costs manageable, demonstrating gains at 100B+ MoE scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling LLMs typically requires enlarging the Transformer backbone and costly full pretraining. The authors seek an alternative: improving an already-strong model by allocating more computation per token while keeping the backbone fixed. Existing depth-recurrent (looped) approaches conflict with pipeline parallelism, motivating a sequence-length-based scaling approach compatible with standard large-model training infrastructure.

Method: Hidden Decoding expands each token into n independent streams, each with its own embedding table. The intermediate streams' key-value caches serve as context, enabling richer internal computation without adding or widening Transformer layers. To reduce the quadratic attention cost, Stream-Factorized Attention restricts cross-stream mixing to only a few layers; most layers attend only within their own stream, making the cost roughly linear in n.

Result: At frontier scale, WeLM-HD4-80B and WeLM-HD4-617B (n=4) outperform matched non-HD baselines, marking the first demonstrated sequence-length scaling method at 100B+ MoE scale. Gains increase with larger expansion factors n, validating sequence-length expansion as a practical scaling path.

Conclusion: Sequence-length expansion via Hidden Decoding is a viable and practical fixed-backbone scaling approach for frontier-scale LLMs, achieving meaningful improvements without the cost of full backbone retraining, and scales efficiently using Stream-Factorized Attention.

Abstract: Scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) has been driven mainly by enlarging the Transformer backbone, but for an already-strong model this requires another round of costly pretraining. We study whether an existing backbone can keep improving by allocating more computation to each token while leaving the Transformer backbone fixed. Depth-recurrent (looped) Transformers pursue this goal but are hard to scale, because looped computation does not fit naturally with the pipeline parallelism used to train the largest models. We add computation along the sequence-length dimension, where the extra computation is simply a longer input and stays compatible with standard large-model training. We propose Hidden Decoding, a sequence-length scaling method applied during continued pretraining (CPT). It expands each token into n streams with independent embedding tables and keeps the intermediate streams' key-value cache as context, so each token performs more internal computation without adding or widening Transformer layers. To keep this affordable at scale, we introduce Stream-Factorized Attention, in which most layers attend only within each stream and only a few layers mix across streams, reducing the attention cost from quadratic to roughly linear in n. Experiments support two scaling results. At frontier scale, we train WeLM-HD4-80B and WeLM-HD4-617B at n=4 and improve their matched non-HD baselines, making Hidden Decoding the first demonstrated sequence-length scaling method at the 100B+ MoE scale. Across expansion factors, the gains grow as n increases, showing that sequence-length expansion is a practical fixed-backbone scaling path for frontier-scale LLMs.

</details>


### [27] [Diarization-Guided Qwen-ASR Adaptation for Multilingual Two-Speaker Conversational Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08208)
*Hao Wu,RongQi Han,Zhen Wang,Wei Liang,Wei Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A modular speaker diarization + adapted Qwen3-ASR-1.7B system achieves 17.97 tcpMER on the MLC-SLM 2026 Challenge eval set, with supervised fine-tuning providing the largest gain and synthetic-speech LoRA + GRPO reinforcement learning yielding further improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a robust multilingual two-speaker conversational speech recognition system for the MLC-SLM 2026 Challenge, improving upon the baseline Qwen-ASR-1.7B model through modular diarization and multi-stage ASR adaptation.

Method: A modular diarization front end (VAD → subsegment generation → CAMPPlus speaker embeddings → two-speaker spectral clustering → RTTM-based segmentation) feeds speaker-attributed segments grouped by language/region to an adapted Qwen3-ASR-1.7B model. ASR adaptation proceeds in three stages: (1) supervised full fine-tuning on official training data, (2) LoRA fine-tuning with TTS-based synthetic speech augmentation using a three-pipeline framework, and (3) GRPO reinforcement learning with WER/CER rewards and penalties for hallucination, repetition, and length deviation.

Result: On the development set, the system achieves 23.70 average tcpMER (6.83 absolute improvement over the baseline). On the final evaluation set, it achieves 17.97 average tcpMER. Ablation confirms supervised fine-tuning yields the largest gain, with LoRA and RL providing additional robustness.

Conclusion: A carefully staged ASR adaptation pipeline — combining full fine-tuning, synthetic-speech LoRA, and GRPO-based RL — significantly improves multilingual conversational speech recognition when paired with a modular speaker diarization front end.

Abstract: This paper describes our self-designed system for Task 1 of the MLC-SLM 2026 Challenge for multilingual two-speaker conversational speech. The system combines a modular speaker diarization front end with a challenge-adapted Qwen3-ASR-1.7B recognizer. The diarization front end performs voice activity detection, subsegment generation, CAMPPlus speaker embedding extraction, two-speaker spectral clustering, and RTTM-based audio segmentation. The resulting speaker-attributed segments are grouped by language or region and decoded by the adapted ASR model. For ASR adaptation, we first perform supervised full fine-tuning on the official training data, then apply LoRA fine-tuning with synthetic speech generated by a three-pipeline TTS-based synthetic speech augmentation framework, and finally refine the model using GRPO reinforcement learning with rewards based on WER/CER and penalties for hallucination, repetition, and length deviation. On the official development set, the full system achieves an average tcpMER of 23.70, reducing the error rate by 6.83 absolute points relative to the released Qwen-ASR-1.7B performance. On the final evaluation set, the system achieves an average tcpMER of 17.97. Ablation results show that supervised fine-tuning provides the largest gain, while synthetic-speech LoRA adaptation and reinforcement learning further improve robustness.

</details>


### [28] [Best-of-$N$ TTS Evaluation is Confounded by ASR Family Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08256)
*Taehyung Yu,Seongjae Kang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Best-of-N inference for TTS improves with ASR-based verification, but verifier rankings are highly dependent on which ASR family is used for evaluation. Cross-family rank ensembles mitigate this coupling and achieve the lowest WER across evaluators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors identify a critical evaluation confound in Best-of-N (BoN) inference for zero-shot TTS: a verifier's apparent quality depends heavily on which ASR family is used as the evaluator. Same-family verifier-evaluator pairs inflate apparent performance, creating misleading rankings that do not generalize across ASR architectures.

Method: The paper systematically evaluates BoN inference using F5-TTS on LibriSpeech-PC test-clean with three ASR families as verifiers/evaluators (Whisper, wav2vec 2.0, HuBERT). It measures verifier-evaluator coupling via linear CKA similarity and oracle headroom recovery. Two cross-family rank ensemble strategies are proposed: rank-averaging and conjunctive max-rank, which aggregate rankings across heterogeneous verifiers to reduce family-specific bias.

Result: Verifier rankings reverse across ASR families, and same-family pairs recover 2-3× more oracle headroom than cross-family pairs despite near-identical representations (linear CKA 0.978). The proposed cross-family rank ensembles achieve 1.61% WER at N=10 (−12% relative to F5-TTS baseline) with no degradation under SIM-o/UTMOS. The best single verifier achieves 1.72% WER (−16.5%) under the official F5-TTS evaluator.

Conclusion: The coupling between verifier and evaluator ASR families is a significant, underexplored confound in BoN-TTS evaluation that cannot be explained by representational similarity alone. Cross-evaluator triangulation using rank ensembles should be adopted as default reporting practice to ensure robust, generalizable results.

Abstract: Best-of-$N$ (BoN) inference improves content consistency in zero-shot text-to-speech by selecting from $N$ candidates with an automatic speech recognition (ASR) verifier. We identify an underexplored evaluation confound: a verifier's apparent quality depends strongly on which ASR family judges it. On LibriSpeech-PC test-clean~\citep{librispeechpc} with F5-TTS~\citep{f5tts}, verifier rankings reverse across Whisper, wav2vec~2.0, and HuBERT evaluators, and same-family verifier-evaluator pairs recover 2-3$\times$ more oracle headroom than cross-family pairs despite near-identical representations (linear CKA $0.978$) -- a pattern consistent with identity- or lineage-level coupling rather than representational overlap. We propose two \textbf{cross-family rank ensembles} (rank-averaging and conjunctive max-rank) that attain the lowest mean WER across three independent evaluators -- $1.61\%$ at $N{=}10$ ($-12\%$ relative to F5-TTS) -- with no measurable degradation under automatic SIM-o/UTMOS metrics; the best single verifier drives WER from $2.06\%$ to $1.72\%$ ($-16.5\%$) under the official F5-TTS evaluator. We recommend cross-evaluator triangulation as default reporting practice.

</details>


### [29] [XALPHA: A Memory-Driven AI Quant Researcher for Hypothesis-to-Code Alpha Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08332)
*Fengyuan Liu,Yuchen Fu,Yuqi Wang,Qi Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: XAlpha is a memory-driven AI quant researcher that transforms alpha discovery from isolated factor generation into a continuous closed-loop research process. It uses three specialized components — Macro Brain, Micro Brain, and Cross Brain — integrated with a multi-source research memory system to read, hypothesize, implement, validate, reflect, and evolve trading signals, achieving superior performance on the CSI300 benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial markets are noisy, non-stationary, and high-dimensional, making predictive alpha discovery difficult. While alpha discovery has progressed from manual factor design to ML, evolutionary search, and LLM-based frameworks, existing methods only automate isolated steps rather than functioning as end-to-end quant researchers capable of absorbing external knowledge, closing the hypothesis-to-code validation loop, and learning from accumulated feedback.

Method: XAlpha employs a memory-driven architecture with three specialized brains: (1) a Macro Brain that plans research themes and selects archetypes guided by a multi-source memory system integrating report-grounded financial knowledge with prior discovery feedback; (2) a Micro Brain that transforms hypotheses into executable factor code and verifies ex-ante tri-alignment among hypothesis idea, code logic, and financial plausibility; (3) a Cross Brain that consolidates empirical outcomes into generation-level feedback, cycle-level summaries, and archetype-level research cues. This memory system enables continuous reading, hypothesizing, implementation, validation, reflection, and evolution.

Result: Experiments on the CSI300 index demonstrate that XAlpha achieves stronger overall alpha discovery performance compared to representative baseline methods.

Conclusion: XAlpha successfully shifts the paradigm of alpha mining from isolated factor generation to a closed-loop, memory-driven research process that continuously evolves. The integration of external financial knowledge with iterative discovery feedback enables more robust and predictive trading signal generation in complex, non-stationary financial markets.

Abstract: Financial markets are noisy, non-stationary, and high-dimensional, making it difficult to discover predictive and robust trading signals. Alpha discovery has evolved from manual factor design to machine learning, evolutionary search, and recent LLM-based frameworks, improving the efficiency of factor generation, search, and evaluation. However, existing methods still mostly automate isolated steps, rather than functioning as end-to-end quant researchers that can absorb external knowledge, close the hypothesis-to-code validation loop, and learn from accumulated discovery feedback. To fill this gap, we introduce XAlpha, a memory-driven AI Quant Researcher for continuous hypothesis-to-code alpha discovery. XAlpha maintains a multi-source research memory system that integrates report-grounded financial knowledge with discovery feedback from prior generations and research cycles. Guided by this memory system, a Macro Brain plans research themes and selects suitable Archetypes; a Micro Brain transforms the planned hypothesis pool into executable factor code and verifies ex-ante tri-alignment among the hypothesis idea, code logic, and financial plausibility; and a Cross Brain consolidates empirical outcomes into generation-level feedback, cycle-level summaries, and archetype-level research cues for future exploration. In this way, XAlpha turns alpha mining from isolated factor generation into a closed-loop research process that continuously reads, hypothesizes, implements, validates, reflects, and evolves. Experiments on CSI300 show that XAlpha achieves stronger overall alpha discovery performance than representative baselines.

</details>


### [30] [TypeProbe: Recovering Type Representations from Hidden States of Pre-trained Code Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08339)
*Giuliano Gorgone,Fausto Carcassi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether pretrained code models internally encode type information, finding that cross-lingual type representations emerge even from untyped code, and that hidden states linearly encode result types in ways robust to lexical and syntactic perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While state-of-the-art code models perform well, it is unclear whether they internally represent formal type semantics. Prior interpretability work on code models has not directly targeted type representations or cross-lingual type structure.

Method: The authors probe the residual streams of pretrained code models using a parallel dataset of Java (typed) and Python (untyped) code examples. They train linear probes on one language's hidden states to predict argument and result types, then test cross-lingual transfer. They also evaluate robustness to lexical perturbations and cross-language syntactic variations.

Result: Cross-lingual type representations emerge even from untyped Python code. Hidden states linearly encode the result type implied by typed function application, and probes trained on one language can infer types in the other. This structure shows partial robustness to lexical perturbations and syntactic variations across languages.

Conclusion: Pretrained code models develop internal, cross-lingual representations of type semantics, even when trained on untyped code, suggesting that type-level abstractions are implicitly learned during pretraining.

Abstract: State-of-the-art code models achieve impressive performance, yet the extent to which they internally encode type information remains poorly understood. We probe the residual streams of pretrained code models for internal type representations using a parallel dataset of Java and Python code examples. Our results show that cross-lingual type representations emerge even from untyped code. Moreover, we test whether hidden states linearly encode the result type implied by typed function application by training probes on one language to infer argument and result types in the other. Finally, we find that this structure is partly robust to lexical perturbations and cross-language syntactic variations. To the best of our knowledge, prior work on interpretability of code models has not directly targeted formal type semantics or cross-lingual type representations. We release our code and datasets.

</details>


### [31] [Grounded Event Extraction from SEC 8-K Filings with a Fine-Grained Taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08346)
*Rian Dolphin,Joe Dursun,Jarrett Blankenship,Katie Adams,Quinton Pike*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A two-stage LLM system tags 8-K filings with a fine-grained 119-event taxonomy, anchoring every label to a verbatim quote and assigning quality scores. Tag precision rises from 12% to 96% with higher scores, and the taxonomy separates economically distinct events that share the same SEC item code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SEC-mandated 8-K item codes are too coarse to distinguish economically meaningful events—routine updates and CEO departures share codes, and major market-moving disclosures are lumped into a catch-all. Fine-grained labeling at corpus scale requires LLMs, but only if labels are traceable to source text and verifiably reliable, motivating a system with grounded, quality-scored event tags.

Method: The authors develop a two-stage pipeline. Stage 1 uses constrained generation to map 8-K text to a three-tier, 119-type event taxonomy, anchoring each tag to a verbatim source quote validated via fuzzy n-gram matching. Stage 2 independently re-grades each quote-category pair, producing a quality score. The system is applied to 292,984 filings (2022–2026), yielding 601,088 grounded tags. Evaluation uses an LLM judge on 5,125 stratified tags, plus an event study measuring unsigned abnormal returns to confirm economic distinctiveness.

Result: Precision rises monotonically with quality score: from 12% at the lowest tier to 96% at the highest. Unsupported (hallucinated) tags drop from ~8% to near zero. Ablation confirms the quality score is calibrated only when assigned in a dedicated second pass rather than jointly. The event study shows the taxonomy cleanly separates economically distinct events even within the same SEC item code, without using any language model in the return analysis.

Conclusion: Grounded, quality-scored LLM annotation at scale makes fine-grained 8-K event classification both feasible and trustworthy. The released dataset of 601,088 tags enables more precise corporate event research than raw item codes permit.

Abstract: Form 8-K filings are the primary channel through which U.S. public companies disclose material events, but the SEC item codes attached to them are coarse: a single item spans routine administrative changes and chief executive departures, and many of the most market-moving disclosures fall into a catch-all item. Large language models make fine-grained labelling feasible at corpus scale, but only if the labels can be traced to the source text and shown to be reliable. We present a two-stage system that tags 8-K disclosures against a three-tier taxonomy of 119 event types. The first stage constrains output to valid taxonomy entries and anchors every tag to a verbatim quote via fuzzy n-gram validation; the second re-grades each cited quote against the category definition to produce a quality score. Applying the system to 292,984 filings from 2022 to 2026 yields 601,088 grounded event tags, which we release. Over 5,125 stratified tags, an LLM judge finds precision rises monotonically with the quality score, from 12% to 96%, while unsupported tags fall from 8% to near zero. Ablation shows the score is calibrated only when assigned in a dedicated second pass. An event study on unsigned abnormal returns confirms, without any language model, that the taxonomy separates economically distinct events sharing an item code.

</details>


### [32] [Echoes Across Vietnam's Highlands, Delta, and Coast: A Multilingual Corpus for Cham, Khmer, and Tay-Nung](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08362)
*Anh Trac Duc Dinh,Khang Nhat Hoang Vo,Vinh Cong Doan,Tai Tien Ta,Khoa Duc Anh Lam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CKTN introduces the first NLP corpus/benchmark for Cham, Khmer, and Tay-Nung (44K documents, 24M subword tokens). The authors show that multilingual encoders fragment these underrepresented languages and that standard adaptation metrics (LM loss, lexical-overlap retrieval) mislead evaluation. They propose a script-aware adaptation recipe — vocabulary augmentation + calibrated replaced-token pretraining — that reduces fragmentation and yields superior classification performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vietnam's ethnic minority languages (Cham, Khmer, Tay-Nung) are nearly absent from NLP. Beyond data scarcity, these languages differ sharply in script, degree of Vietnamese contact, and standardization level — conditions under which standard multilingual adaptation methods can learn spurious signals (e.g., trivial script cues) rather than genuine semantic representations, leading to fragmented embedding spaces and misleading evaluation outcomes.

Method: The authors construct CKTN, a corpus and benchmark spanning continued pretraining, category classification, and summary-document retrieval. They then propose a script-aware adaptation recipe consisting of (1) vocabulary augmentation to better cover the target scripts, combined with (2) calibrated replaced-token pretraining, which prevents the discriminator from exploiting trivial script mismatches and forces the model to learn deeper linguistic signals.

Result: Existing multilingual encoders exhibit severe fragmentation on these languages. Standard adaptation metrics prove misleading: models can achieve lower language-modeling loss or high lexical-overlap retrieval scores while failing at cross-document semantic generalization. The proposed script-aware recipe produces an encoder with substantially reduced fragmentation and the strongest classification performance, while exposing the limits of lexical-overlap retrieval as an evaluation signal.

Conclusion: For low-resource languages with heterogeneous scripts and varying degrees of language contact, script-aware adaptation is critical. Common metrics like LM perplexity and lexical-overlap retrieval can mask genuine semantic failures, and evaluation protocols must account for this. The CKTN benchmark provides a foundation for more principled multilingual NLP research on Vietnam's minority languages.

Abstract: Vietnam's ethnic minority languages are almost absent from the field of Natural Language Processing (NLP), and the challenge goes beyond data scarcity: Cham, Khmer, and Tay-Nung differ sharply in script, Vietnamese contact, and standardization, conditions under which standard multilingual adaptation can learn the wrong signals. We introduce CKTN, the first corpus and benchmark for these languages (44,367 documents, 24M subword tokens), spanning continued pretraining, category classification, and summary-document retrieval. We show that existing multilingual encoders severely fragment these languages, and that common adaptation metrics can mislead: models may lower language-modeling loss or excel at lexical-overlap retrieval while still failing at semantic generalization across documents. We address this with a script-aware adaptation recipe - vocabulary augmentation combined with calibrated replaced-token pretraining - that prevents the discriminator from exploiting trivial script mismatches. The result is an encoder with substantially less fragmentation and the strongest classification performance among evaluated models, exposing the limits of lexical-overlap retrieval as an evaluation signal.

</details>


### [33] [Prompt Compression via Activation Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08399)
*Thibaud Ardoin,Semira Einsele,Evis Bregu,Gerhard Wunder*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Researchers demonstrate that task-relevant information from an LLM instruction prompt can be compressed into a single activation vector (a learned weighted sum of mid-layer activations) and re-injected at an early layer, replacing the original token sequence with under 2% accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether the semantic and task-relevant information contained in a full instruction prompt can be condensed into a compact activation representation, thereby reducing per-query computational cost for fixed prompts without re-processing the entire token sequence each time.

Method: Extract activations at an intermediate layer of the target LLM during prompt processing; learn a weighted sum of these activations to form a single compressed vector; inject this vector into an early layer of the model, bypassing the original token-level prompt processing. Evaluate accuracy retention compared to full prompt baselines.

Result: The compressed activation vector preserves task-relevant information with less than 2% accuracy degradation relative to full prompt processing. The analysis further reveals: (i) mid-layer representations transfer meaningfully to early layers (cross-layer compatibility); (ii) a single vector encodes quantifiable semantic information; (iii) weighted-sum activation pooling is a robust compression mechanism.

Conclusion: LLM activations exhibit structured, compressible representations where task-relevant prompt information can be distilled into a single vector and re-injected across layers. This enables computational savings for fixed-instruction scenarios and advances understanding of how information is encoded in transformer activation space.

Abstract: Large language models process prompts by propagating activations through dozens of layers before generating a response. We ask whether the task-relevant information contained in an instruction prompt can be compressed into a single activation vector and re-injected into the model, replacing the original token sequence? We show this is achievable using a learned weighted sum of activations extracted at an intermediate layer and injected at an early layer of the target LLM. The compressed vector preserves task-relevant information, incurring an accuracy drop of under $2\%$ relative to full prompt processing. Beyond its practical implications, including reducing per-query computation for fixed instruction prompts without reprocessing the original token sequence, our analysis reveals structure in the activation space of LLMs: (i) mid-layer representations transfer meaningfully to early layers, suggesting a degree of cross-layer compatibility in how information is encoded; (ii) a single activation vector encodes a quantifiable and recoverable amount of semantic information; (iii) a weighted sum of activations is a robust representation compressor.

</details>


### [34] [When Synthetic Speech Is All You Have: Better Call GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08409)
*Shashi Kumar,Yanis Labrak,Hasindri Watawana,Sergio Burdisso,Esaú Villatoro-Tello,Kadri Hacioğlu,Petr Motlicek,Andreas Stolcke*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GRPO, a critic-free reinforcement learning method, substantially outperforms supervised fine-tuning for adapting LLM-based ASR to regulated domains using synthetic speech, achieving a 40% relative WER reduction by improving stopping calibration and attention alignment without altering early-layer representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting LLM-based ASR to privacy-regulated domains like banking is constrained by limited access to real speech data. While synthetic TTS data is a workable substitute, it suffers from acoustic mismatch with real recordings. Prior work addressing this gap has relied solely on supervised fine-tuning (SFT), leaving unexplored the potential of reinforcement learning to extract more value from synthetic speech.

Method: The authors apply Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a critic-free RL method that rewards low word error rate (WER) hypotheses, to adapt an LLM-based ASR model using only synthetic TTS data. They compare pure GRPO adaptation, SFT-only adaptation, and an SFT-then-GRPO combined pipeline. Analysis traces performance gains to behavioral improvements (stopping calibration and attention anchoring) rather than representational changes.

Result: GRPO-only adaptation reduces WER by 40% relative to SFT (36.71% → 22.09%). Combining SFT followed by GRPO yields a 45% relative reduction. The gains stem from reduced insertion errors via better stopping calibration and improved speech-to-text alignment through stronger attention anchoring to audio, while early-layer representations remain unchanged.

Conclusion: When synthetic speech is the primary adaptation resource, reinforcement learning—specifically GRPO—should be preferred over supervised fine-tuning for LLM-based ASR, as it more effectively addresses the acoustic mismatch problem through behavioral optimization.

Abstract: LLM-based ASR adapted to regulated domains such as banking is bottlenecked by privacy: real speech is costly and legally constrained to collect, making synthetic text-to-speech (TTS) an attractive substitute. Yet synthetic speech stays acoustically mismatched with real recordings, and work on this gap has stayed within supervised fine-tuning (SFT). We instead turn to reinforcement learning, and show that Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) extracts far more from the same synthetic speech than SFT. Synthetic-only adaptation of the model with GRPO, a critic-free method rewarding low-WER hypotheses, reduces WER by 40\% relative to SFT (36.71\%$\to$22.09\%), and an SFT-then-GRPO combination pushes this further to 45\%. We trace the gain to behavior rather than representation: GRPO reduces insertion errors by improving stopping calibration and speech-to-text alignment by better anchoring attention to audio, leaving early-layer representations intact. When synthetic speech is the main resource, reinforcement learning should be preferred over supervised fine-tuning.

</details>


### [35] [Detecting Ladder Logic Bombs in IEC 61131-3 PLC Programs using ESBMC-PLC+: A Formal Verification Approach with Trigger Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08417)
*Pierre Dantas,Lucas Cordeiro,Waldir Junior*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ESBMC-LLB is a formal verification tool that detects Ladder Logic Bombs (LLBs) in PLC programs by exposing function-block logic to model checking. It uses k-induction for unbounded proofs of bomb absence and bounded model checking to recover triggers. It detected 30/30 bombs on the Iacobelli 2024 dataset (including adaptive triggers) and 149/150 on the SWaT corpus, with semantic model checking and CFG-triage identified as complementary approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ladder-diagram verifiers drop function-block bodies from their intermediate representation (IR), making malicious logic bombs invisible to provers. Furthermore, current detectors (signature, anomaly, CFG-triage) lack guarantees about trigger behavior and cannot reliably detect adaptive triggers (computed, opaque-arithmetic, multi-scan). A verification approach with formal guarantees is needed.

Method: ESBMC-LLB builds on the ESBMC-PLC+ verification engine and adds a modeling layer that (1) exposes function-block logic previously dropped from IRs, and (2) recasts bomb detection as formal verification. A scan-watchdog mechanism exposes non-termination payloads, and output wiring exposes actuator-forgery payloads as safety violations. k-induction provides unbounded proofs of bomb absence across all scans, while bounded model checking returns counterexamples that serve as recovered triggers.

Result: On the Iacobelli 2024 dataset: 30/30 bombs detected, all triggers recovered, including adaptive triggers (computed, opaque-arithmetic, multi-scan) that evade CFG-triage. On PLC-Defuser's SWaT corpus v1.0.0: 149/150 bombs (99%) detected with zero false positives and full trigger recovery. On a later SWaT version with nonlinear non-termination bombs, detection dropped to 49% due to SMT solver timeouts.

Conclusion: Semantic model checking and CFG-triage are complementary: semantic model checking excels at unbounded proofs, adaptive-trigger robustness, and Boolean/integer/linear analog logic; CFG-triage handles nonlinear analog non-termination cases where SMT solvers time out. The authors delineate where each approach is superior.

Abstract: A Ladder Logic Bomb (LLB) is malicious control logic in a Programmable Logic Controller (PLC) program that lies dormant until a trigger activates a payload to manipulate actuators, forge sensor readings, or deny operator control. We observe that real malicious logic hides inside function-block bodies, which existing ladder-diagram verifiers drop from their intermediate representation (IR), making bombs invisible to provers. We present ESBMC-LLB, which uses ESBMC-PLC+ as its verification engine and adds a modeling layer that exposes function-block logic and recasts bomb detection as a formal verification problem: a scan-watchdog exposes non-termination payloads, and output wiring exposes actuator-forgery payloads as safety violations. k-induction gives an unbounded proof of bomb-absence across all scans, and the bounded model checker returns a counterexample that is the trigger - guarantees that signature, anomaly, and CFG-triage detectors lack. On the public Iacobelli 2024 dataset, ESBMC-LLB detects all 30 bombs and recovers every trigger; it also detects adaptive triggers (computed, opaque-arithmetic, multi-scan) that evade CFG-triage. We also report the first semantic model-checker evaluation on PLC-Defuser's SWaT corpus: our analog extension makes the full corpus parseable; on v1.0.0, it detects 149/150 bombs (99%) with zero false positives, recovering each trigger; on a later version with nonlinear non-termination bombs, detection drops to 49% as the SMT solver times out. We conclude that semantic model checking and CFG-triage are complementary - the former gives unbounded proofs, adaptive-trigger robustness, and handles Boolean/integer and linear analog logic; the latter leads to nonlinear analog non-termination, and we delineate where each wins.

</details>


### [36] [Two Axes of LLM Abstention: Answer Correctness and Question Answerability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08456)
*Benedikt J. Wagner*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs conflate answer correctness and question answerability under a single confidence score. This paper shows these are separable axes: answer-confidence tracks correctness but not answerability, while a linear probe on hidden states tracks answerability. The probe detects false-premise questions that the model otherwise fails to report, enabling a two-threshold policy that jointly certifies both error budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current refusal mechanisms rely on thresholding a single confidence score, which cannot distinguish between "the model would answer incorrectly" and "the question should not be answered" (unanswerable/false-premise questions). This conflation leads to poor calibration and unverifiable safety guarantees, especially on naturally occurring false-premise questions.

Method: The authors evaluate five instruction-tuned models (2B–14B, three families) on two axes: (1) answer-confidence (whether the answer is correct) and (2) answerability (whether the question is valid). They use linear probes on hidden states to extract answerability signals and compare against surface-level metrics like P(IK), P(True), and direct premise-checking prompts. They then construct a calibrated two-threshold policy that requires both an answerability score and a correctness score to independently clear their respective thresholds before answering, providing separate certifiable budgets for unanswerable-answer rate and wrong-answer rate.

Result: Answer-confidence is nearly blind to answerability, while hidden-state probes achieve 0.69–0.77 AUROC on CREPE false-premise questions. Instructing the model to check premises backfires (57% false challenge rate). The probe-routed instruction roughly triples challenge precision. The two-threshold policy certifies both error budgets at 0.75 coverage of correct answers (vs. 0.31 for single-threshold) and is the only policy that certifies at all at 14B scale.

Conclusion: Answer correctness and question answerability are separate representational axes in LLMs, with the latter accessible only through hidden-state probes rather than output-level signals. This separation persists across scales and enables a principled, certifiable refusal policy that dominates single-threshold approaches.

Abstract: A model should refuse two different things: answers it would get wrong, and questions it should not answer at all, such as unanswerable ones or ones resting on a false premise. The usual recipe thresholds a single confidence score, which cannot tell these apart. Across five instruction-tuned models from three families (2B to 14B), we find they are separate axes. Ordinary answer-confidence tracks whether an answer is right but is nearly blind to whether the question is answerable; a linear probe on hidden states does the reverse. The blind spot does not shrink with scale. It is worst on naturally occurring false-premise questions (CREPE). There, answer-confidence, P(IK), P(True), and even asking the model outright whether a premise is false all stay near chance, while a hidden-state probe reaches 0.69 to 0.77 AUROC: the model represents a problem it will not report. This turns out to be fixable. Instructing a model to check premises backfires, because it then disputes sound and false premises alike (57% false challenges), unable to tell them apart; routing the same instruction with the probe roughly triples challenge precision. We turn the two axes into a calibrated policy that answers only when an answerability score and a correctness score each clear a separately certifies behave differently: the unanswerable-answer rate is controllable at every scale, while the wrong-answer rate is capped by model accuracy, so the guarantee tightens as threshold policy certifies both budgets at 0.75 coverage of correct answers, against 0.31 for a single threshold; at 14B it is the only policy that certifies at all.

</details>


### [37] [Cross-seed explainability using Procrustes-conditioned Joint End-to-end Top-K Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08499)
*Bendegúz Váradi,Zoltán Kmetty*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Procrustes-conditioned joint SAE that aligns activation spaces of independently trained BERT models before dictionary learning, combined with Top-K sparsity and end-to-end optimization, to extract cross-seed universal features. It demonstrates superior feature universality (Pearson r ≥ 0.70) over post-hoc baselines across three NLP benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Independently trained neural networks with identical architectures learn misaligned feature spaces due to the non-convex nature of dictionary learning and random initialization. This makes cross-model feature comparison and universality claims difficult in mechanistic interpretability. Existing post-hoc alignment methods are insufficient; a principled approach to learn inherently aligned, universal features during training is needed.

Method: The authors first compute an orthogonal Procrustes rotation between the activation spaces of two independently trained (different seed) BERT models. They then jointly train a Top-K Sparse Autoencoder on the Procrustes-aligned activations from both seeds simultaneously. The training incorporates end-to-end downstream task optimization and an auxiliary dead-feature revival loss. Feature universality is measured via Pearson correlation between corresponding latent activations across seeds. Evaluation is performed on five independent seed pairs (10 BERT models) across SST-2, Stanford Politeness, and TweetEval Emotion datasets.

Result: The full Procrustes-conditioned joint SAE pipeline achieves Pearson r ≥ 0.70 for cross-seed feature universality on all three benchmark datasets, outperforming post-hoc alignment baselines. Qualitative analysis confirms that high-universality features encode interpretable sociolinguistic patterns.

Conclusion: Combining Procrustes-based activation alignment with joint sparse autoencoder training effectively yields cross-seed universal features, advancing mechanistic interpretability by enabling reliable feature comparison across independently trained models.

Abstract: We present a Procrustes-conditioned Joint End-to-end Top-K Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) for extracting cross-seed universal features from independently trained BERT models. Cross-seed feature universality is a fundamental challenge in mechanistic interpretability: because dictionary learning is non-convex, independently trained networks learn misaligned feature spaces, so apparently identical features may differ by random initialization. We address this by computing an orthogonal Procrustes rotation between seeds' activation spaces before joint SAE training, combining Top-K sparsity, end-to-end downstream optimization, and an auxiliary dead-feature revival loss based on previous SAE literature. Evaluating on five independent seed pairs (ten BERT models) across three benchmark datasets (SST-2, Stanford Politeness, TweetEval Emotion), our full pipeline produces more universal features (Pearson r $\geq$ 0.70 across seeds) than post-hoc alignment baselines on all three datasets. A minimal qualitative analysis confirms that high-universality features encode interpretable sociolinguistic patterns.

</details>


### [38] [When the Judge Changes, So Does the Measurement: Auditing LLM-as-Judge Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08535)
*Zongyou Yang,Yinghan Hou,Xiaokun Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM judge upgrades are not interchangeable — scaling parameters yields different validity gains than API version changes. Only Qwen3 1.7B→4B shows robust adjacent improvement; stronger judges reduce but don't eliminate position/verbosity biases. The paper argues for standardized reporting: dataset slices, bias probes, error-dependence estimates, and protocol audit trails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-judge evaluation scores can shift when the evaluator model is replaced, even if candidate responses remain unchanged. This evaluator-replacement ambiguity threatens measurement validity, yet the community lacks systematic understanding of how different judge upgrade paths (dense scaling vs. API releases) affect judgment consistency and bias.

Method: The authors compare two judge-upgrade paths across four judgment datasets: (1) scaling Qwen3 dense models from 1.7B to 32B parameters, and (2) moving across MiniMax M2 to M2.7 API releases. They examine adjacent-version gains, position bias, verbosity bias, repeated-sample jury reliability, and structured debate effects. Error-dependence and deliberation attribution are also analyzed.

Result: Only Qwen3 1.7B→4B yields a robust adjacent gain; MiniMax adjacent releases do not. Stronger judges reduce but fail to eliminate position and verbosity biases. Repeated-sample juries provide minimal benefit when errors are correlated across samples. Structured debate can substantially shift decisions, but without parser and fallback logs, these shifts cannot be definitively attributed to deliberation.

Conclusion: LLM-as-judge evaluation reports should be augmented with dataset slices, bias probes, error-dependence estimates, and protocol audit trails to ensure measurement validity across evaluator upgrades. Judge upgrades are not interchangeable, and naive replacement undermines the reliability of comparative evaluation pipelines.

Abstract: An LLM-as-judge score can move even when the candidate responses stay fixed, simply because the evaluator has changed. We treat this evaluator-replacement ambiguity as a measurement-validity problem. Across four judgment datasets, we compare two upgrade paths available in practice: scaling Qwen3 dense judges from 1.7B to 32B parameters and moving across MiniMax M2-M2.7 released APIs. The main pattern is that judge upgrades are not interchangeable: only Qwen3 1.7B to 4B gives a robust adjacent gain, while MiniMax adjacent releases do not. Stronger judges reduce but do not remove position and verbosity bias. Repeated-sample juries add little when errors are correlated. Structured debate can move decisions substantially, but without parser and fallback logs those shifts cannot be attributed to deliberation. We argue that LLM-as-judge reports should include dataset slices, bias probes, error-dependence estimates, and protocol audit trails.

</details>


### [39] [It Takes a MAESTRO To Prune Bad Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08601)
*Palaash Goel,Ayush Maheshwari,Tanmoy Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MAESTRO is a structured pruning framework for MoE language models that uses Markov chain modeling of expert activation trajectories to derive globally-aware importance scores, outperforming local heuristics by up to 10.61% under 50% compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sparsely-activated MoE models achieve inference efficiency by activating few parameters per token, but all experts must reside in memory, creating prohibitive deployment costs. Existing structured pruning methods use locally-derived heuristics that ignore the interdependent, cross-layer nature of MoE routing decisions.

Method: MAESTRO models autoregressive expert activation trajectories across transformer layers as Ergodic Markov chains. The stationary distribution of each chain encodes cross-layer routing dependencies, yielding a globally-aware importance heuristic for expert pruning. This approach captures the routing-congruent, interdependent nature of MoE expert selection.

Result: Evaluated across five domains (Safety, Bias, Ethics, and others), MAESTRO outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 10.61% in average performance retention under 50% compression, with substantially lower cross-task variance.

Conclusion: Global, routing-congruent pruning informed by Markov chain modeling produces MoE models that generalize more consistently across heterogeneous tasks, demonstrating the value of modeling cross-layer expert dependencies rather than relying on layer-local importance heuristics.

Abstract: Sparsely-activated Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models achieve remarkable inference efficiency by activating only a small fraction of parameters per token, yet their full expert banks reside in memory at all times, creating a prohibitive deployment bottleneck. Existing structured pruning methods, largely designed for dense transformers, assess expert importance using locally derived heuristics that are blind to the interdependent nature of MoE routing. We introduce MAESTRO (Markov-chain Approximated Expert Sparsification via Transition-based ROuting), a structured pruning framework designed for MoE architectures that models autoregressive expert activation trajectories as Ergodic Markov chains whose stationary distributions encode cross-layer dependencies, yielding a globally aware importance heuristic. Evaluated across five diverse domains including Safety, Bias, and Ethics, MAESTRO outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 10.61% in average performance retention under a strict 50% compression regime, while exhibiting substantially lower cross-task variance, indicating that global, routing-congruent pruning produces models that generalize more consistently across heterogeneous tasks.

</details>


### [40] [DominoTree: Conditional Tree-Structured Drafting with Domino for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08642)
*Saw S. Lin,Jyh-Shing Roger Jang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DominoTree introduces a training-free best-first draft tree mechanism that leverages Domino's conditional, non-factorized correction along root-to-node paths, achieving state-of-the-art speculative decoding speedups (up to 6.6× over autoregressive) and the highest mean acceptance lengths across multiple benchmarks and temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing block-diffusion drafters (e.g., DFlash) generate draft blocks with only per-position marginals, while best-first tree methods (e.g., DDTree) expand candidate trees from those marginals. Domino adds a GRU-based causal correction making draft token distributions path-dependent, but DDTree's factorized formulation cannot represent this structure. The gap motivates a tree-based drafter that fully exploits Domino's conditional, non-factorized corrections.

Method: DominoTree constructs a best-first draft tree scored by Domino's conditional, non-factorized correction along each root-to-node path. To make this practical, per-node correction is restricted to a candidate top-M. Tree construction uses a GPU-native CUDA-graph builder that is bit-identical to a reference Python implementation, ensuring unchanged acceptance while keeping per-round construction cheap.

Result: On Qwen3-4B across eight benchmarks, DominoTree achieves up to 6.6× speedup over autoregressive decoding and the highest mean accept length (up to 10.7 tokens/round) at all tested temperatures. It wins throughput over the released Domino decoder at every temperature (9–10% overall, up to +22% on Alpaca) and over DDTree/CaDDTree at every temperature. On Qwen3-8B, it maintains the highest accepted length at all temperatures with a +24% throughput win over DDTree at T=0; at higher temperatures the edge narrows but overall aggregate wins over DFlash and Domino persist.

Conclusion: DominoTree effectively bridges the gap between Domino's path-dependent corrections and tree-based speculative decoding, delivering superior throughput and acceptance lengths across models, benchmarks, and temperatures without requiring additional training.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by drafting several tokens and verifying them in parallel. Block-diffusion drafters such as DFlash produce
  a draft block in one pass but model only per-position marginals; best-first tree methods such as DDTree expand candidate trees from those marginals. The
  released Domino drafter adds a GRU-based causal correction that makes each draft token's distribution path-dependent, a structure DDTree's factorized
  formulation cannot represent. We introduce DominoTree, a training-free best-first draft tree scored by Domino's conditional, non-factorized correction
  along each root-to-node path, made practical by restricting the per-node correction to a candidate top-M. On Qwen3-4B across eight benchmarks, DominoTree
  reaches up to 6.6x speedup over autoregressive decoding and the highest mean accept length of any evaluated method, up to 10.7 tokens per round, at every
  temperature we test. DominoTree constructs its tree with a GPU-native, CUDA-graph builder that is bit-identical to a reference Python implementation, so
  acceptance is unchanged, while keeping per-round tree construction cheap. With this builder as default, DominoTree wins throughput over the released
  Domino decoder at every temperature, 9-10% overall on Qwen3-4B and up to +22% on Alpaca, and over DDTree/CaDDTree at every temperature we test. On Qwen3-
  8B, DominoTree keeps the highest accepted length at every temperature and adds a decisive throughput win at T=0, +24% over DDTree; at higher temperature
  that edge over DDTree/CaDDTree narrows to a tie and a small loss, while its Overall aggregate wins over DFlash and Domino persist.

</details>


### [41] [UltraX: Refining Pre-Training Data at Scale with Adaptive Programmatic Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08646)
*Xinlong Zhao,Dongsheng Liu,Hengyu Zhao,Zixuan Fu,Zheng Wang,Jie Cai,Jie Zhou,Qiang Ma,Xuanhe Zhou,Xu Han,Yudong Wang,Zhiyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: UltraX introduces a function-calling refinement framework for large-scale pre-training data that extends the editing space with insertion (beyond deletion/modification), employs program-supervision generation with dataset-adaptive prompting, and uses sliding-window prediction with global aggregation to achieve superior data efficiency and refinement reliability compared to existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As Scaling Laws yield diminishing returns due to physical data limits, improving LLMs requires higher-quality data utilization. However, existing refinement methods are insufficient: rule-based methods lack instance-level adaptability, while LLM-based methods suffer from poor efficiency and reliability at scale.

Method: UltraX is a function-calling refinement framework that: (1) expands the editing function space to include insertion alongside deletion and modification for fine-grained instance-level editing; (2) builds a reliable program-supervision generation pipeline using dataset-adaptive prompt optimization, Line Alignment Mapping, and Dynamic Context Replacement to convert original-refined text pairs into structured program supervision; (3) improves supervision quality via low-confidence example filtering and ratio-controlled sampling by operation combination; and (4) ensures reliable large-scale execution through sliding-window prediction, global operation aggregation, and systematic post-processing.

Result: UltraX achieves the highest average performance across all evaluated corpora and matches or surpasses baselines while using fewer training tokens, demonstrating stronger data efficiency and refinement reliability.

Conclusion: UltraX provides an effective solution for large-scale pre-training data refinement by combining expanded editing operations with reliable program supervision and robust inference mechanisms, offering a viable path to improve LLM training when data expansion is no longer feasible.

Abstract: As available training data approaches its physical limit, gains from Scaling Laws have begun to diminish. Consequently, improving Large Language Models (LLMs) now depends less on data expansion and more on higher-quality data utilization. However, in the context of large-scale corpora, existing refinement methodologies face significant limitations in quality, efficiency, and reliability: Rule-based approaches are constrained by fixed heuristics and struggle with instance-level variations; LLM-based approaches improve quality but fail to meet the efficiency and reliability requirements of large-scale data processing. To address these challenges, we propose UltraX, a function-calling refinement framework for large-scale pre-training data that completes the editing function space by introducing insertion in addition to deletion and modification, enabling fine-grained instance-level editing. Specifically, UltraX builds a reliable program-supervision generation pipeline. In this pipeline, dataset-adaptive prompt optimization first guides an expert LLM to produce high-quality end-to-end refined texts, and Line Alignment Mapping and Dynamic Context Replacement then convert original-refined text pairs into structured program supervision. Meanwhile, UltraX improves supervision quality and stabilizes the training distribution with low-confidence example filtering and ratio-controlled sampling by operation combination. During inference and execution, it normalizes and validates model outputs through sliding-window prediction, global operation aggregation, and systematic post-processing, improving the stability and reliability of large-scale execution. Experiments show that UltraX achieves the highest average performance across all corpora and also matches or surpasses baselines with fewer training tokens, demonstrating stronger data efficiency and refinement reliability.

</details>


### [42] [Do You Need a Frontier Model as a Citation Verifier? Benchmarking Rubric LLMs for Deep-Research Source Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08700)
*Ethan Leung,Elias Lumer,Corey Feld,Austin Huber,Vamse Kumar Subbiah,Kevin Paul*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates 8 LLM judges on citation quality across two dimensions (source relevance and factual support), finding that cheaper models like GPT-5-mini perform competitively with more expensive ones. However, judges with comparable F1 scores exhibit significant differences in directional bias (pass-rate drift, false positive/negative rates), which would be amplified in RL training — making judge calibration essential before using citation rubrics as reward signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges are increasingly used as reward models in reinforcement learning for rubric-based evaluation, but their reliability depends on understanding both their capability and their biases. Without calibration, a biased judge could reinforce undesirable behaviors during RL training. This study investigates this calibration problem specifically for citation quality in deep-research systems.

Method: The authors construct an adversarial long-form benchmark with 1,248 rubric decisions scored against gold labels, all human-reviewed, with 378 hard cases drawn from judge disagreements. They evaluate 8 off-the-shelf LLM judges from 3 model families on two citation-quality dimensions — source relevance and factual support — measuring F1, Cohen's κ, pass-rate drift, false positive rate, and false negative rate.

Result: GPT-5-mini achieved the highest source-relevance F1 (0.908, κ=0.636). On factual support, all judges were statistically indistinguishable (overlapping confidence intervals). However, judges with comparable F1 scores showed substantial divergence in pass-rate drift, FPR, and FNR — revealing that scalar F1 masks directional bias that RL training would amplify.

Conclusion: Calibrating the LLM judge is a prerequisite for using citation rubrics as reward signals in RL, but this calibration does not require the most expensive model — cheaper judges can suffice.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning increasingly relies on an LLM judge to score each rubric criterion, and that judge acts as the reward model during training. Before such a signal can be trusted, we need to know how capable the judge must be and how biased it is. We study this calibration question for citation quality in deep-research systems, where a search-grounded LLM must support each claim it writes with a cited source. Citation quality is a structured rubric task in which each attribution-citation pair is judged along two dimensions that require an LLM, source relevance and factual support. On an adversarial long-form benchmark, we score 8 off-the-shelf LLM judges from 3 model families against gold labels over 1,248 rubric decisions, all of which were human-reviewed and 378 of which were hard cases adjudicated from judge disagreements. Cheaper judges remain competitive across both dimensions, with GPT-5-mini attaining the strongest source-relevance pass-class F1 at 0.908 ($κ$=0.636), while on factual support the judges are statistically indistinguishable (overlapping confidence intervals), so no single model dominates. At comparable F1, the judges still differ substantially in pass-rate drift, false positive rate, and false negative rate. Scalar F1 obscures this directional bias, yet it is exactly what a downstream reinforcement learning loop would reinforce. Calibrating the judge is therefore a prerequisite for using citation rubrics as reward signals, and our results show that this calibration does not require the most expensive available model.

</details>


### [43] [Validity of LLMs as data annotators: AMALIA on authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08731)
*Manuel Pita*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A publicly funded 9B Portuguese LLM (AMALIA) matches much larger models in coding moral foundations, but when prompts are decomposed to test theoretical fidelity, it fails — relying on surface shortcuts like outrage cues rather than genuine construct understanding. This "recovery gap" metric exposes validity failures that raw agreement scores miss, arguing that sovereign-LLM benchmarks must test evidential routes, not just human coder agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: National/small-language LLMs are evaluated on agreement with human coders for content analysis tasks, but agreement (reliability) does not equal validity. The authors argue that models may achieve high agreement via correlated surface shortcuts rather than following the theoretical structure of the construct being measured. A method is needed to distinguish valid measurement from spurious pattern-matching.

Method: The "recovery gap" method: take a holistic coding prompt (e.g., "does this text invoke the moral foundation of authority?") and decompose it into the atomic clauses from the construct's codebook. Each clause is coded separately, then recombined using the theory's explicit decision rule. The performance drop from holistic to decomposed coding is the recovery gap. If the model truly follows the construct's theory, calibration should close this gap. The authors apply this to AMALIA-9B coding Moral Foundations Theory's authority dimension on European Portuguese text, and compare against multilingual open LLMs.

Result: AMALIA shows a large recovery gap: decomposition recovers only ~50% of holistic performance. Error analysis reveals reliance on surface correlates, particularly moral outrage language near authority figures. An open multilingual LLM tested on the same Portuguese corpus under identical instructions closes the gap, ruling out the corpus as the primary explanation. The gap indicates AMALIA uses spurious shortcuts rather than the construct's theoretical structure.

Conclusion: This is a single counterexample, not a condemnation of national LLMs. The key argument is methodological: sovereign-LLM benchmark suites should include tests of evidential warranting (how the model reaches its coding) alongside traditional agreement metrics. For constructs requiring inference beyond surface features, the recovery gap offers one such diagnostic.

Abstract: A national language model offers a linguistic community its own instrument for measuring what its citizens say and value. Portugal's AMALIA, a publicly funded 9B-parameter model for European Portuguese, appears competitive on agreement alone: asked to code the moral foundation of authority, it agrees with trained human coders to within six F1 points of open models eight to thirteen times its size. Yet agreement is reliability, not validity. For theoretical constructs that must be inferred rather than read from surface features, the question is whether the model follows the construct's theory or reaches the right code by correlated shortcuts. We test this with the recovery gap: the loss in performance when a holistic prompt is decomposed into the codebook's atomic clauses and recombined by the theory's explicit rule. If calibration closes that gap, some portability should survive across models and languages; where it does not, the construct-model instrument is the likely locus of failure. We ask whether a calibrated English instrument transfers to AMALIA-9B and to European Portuguese. For one construct and one corpus, it does not. Decomposition recovers only about half of AMALIA's holistic performance, and error analysis suggests reliance on surface correlates, especially moral outrage near authority figures. An open multilingual LLM closes the gap on the same Portuguese corpus under the same instructions, pointing away from the corpus as the main explanation. AMALIA can still screen and pre-code at scale, but it cannot yet measure this construct well enough to stand alone. The study is a single counterexample, not a verdict on national models; it argues that sovereign-LLM benchmark batteries should test not only agreement with human coders, but the evidential route by which that agreement is warranted.

</details>


### [44] [UniClawBench: A Universal Benchmark for Proactive Agents on Real-World Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08768)
*Zhekai Chen,Chengqi Duan,Kaiyue Sun,Bohao Li,Yuqing Wang,Manyuan Zhang,Xihui Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: UniClawBench is a capability-driven benchmark that evaluates proactive AI agents in live Docker containers across 400 bilingual real-world tasks, organized around five foundational model capabilities, using a closed-loop multi-agent evaluation strategy to disentangle base model and framework performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks are inadequate for evaluating proactive agents because they rely on sandboxed environments and single-turn evaluation, and their scenario-based taxonomies conflate multiple capabilities within single task categories, obscuring the root causes of agent failures.

Method: UniClawBench organizes 400 bilingual real-world tasks around five foundational capabilities: Skill Usage, Exploration, Long-Context Reasoning, Multimodal Understanding, and Cross-Platform Coordination. Evaluation occurs in live Docker containers with step-by-step completion checkpoints. A closed-loop evaluation strategy employs three agents — an executor agent, a hidden supervisor agent, and a user agent — to simulate realistic multi-turn human feedback without exposing grading criteria. SOTA models are tested under multiple agent frameworks to isolate base model capabilities from framework design choices.

Result: Comprehensive comparisons across both models and frameworks reveal how base model capabilities and agent framework designs jointly shape real-world agent performance.

Conclusion: UniClawBench provides a more granular, ecologically valid evaluation paradigm for proactive agents, and the benchmark with code is publicly released to facilitate future research.

Abstract: The rapid development of large language models and multimodal large language models has accelerated the emergence of proactive agents capable of operating everyday tools and assisting users in real-world environments. However, existing benchmarks struggle to evaluate such agents effectively, as they often rely on sandboxed environments and single-turn evaluation paradigms. Moreover, their scenario-based task taxonomies mix multiple model capabilities within the same task category, making it difficult to identify the root causes of agent failures. To address these limitations, we introduce UniClawBench, the first capability-driven benchmark designed to evaluate proactive agents in dynamic, real-world settings. UniClawBench is built around five foundational model capabilities: Skill Usage, Exploration, Long-Context Reasoning, Multimodal Understanding, and Cross-Platform Coordination. Based on these capabilities, we design 400 bilingual real-world tasks. Unlike previous benchmarks that rely on static, pre-recorded answers, our benchmark evaluates agents in live Docker containers using fine-grained, step-by-step completion checkpoints. Furthermore, we design a closed-loop evaluation strategy comprising an executor agent, a hidden supervisor agent, and a user agent to simulate realistic multi-turn human feedback without leaking grading criteria. To disentangle base model capabilities from framework-level design choices, we evaluate state-of-the-art models under multiple agent frameworks. Through comprehensive comparisons across both models and frameworks, we show how base model capabilities and agent framework designs jointly shape performance in real-world environments. To facilitate future research, we make our benchmark and code publicly available at https://github.com/HKU-MMLab/UniClawBench.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [45] [Controllability-Aware Adversarial Examples Against LLM-Based Network Traffic Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07739)
*Zhenpeng Li*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper investigates the adversarial robustness of LLMs used as network intrusion detection classifiers. By designing a controllability-aware black-box attack that restricts perturbations to only directly controllable traffic features, the authors evaluate transfer-based attacks across seven LLMs and two ML baselines on five IDS datasets. They find that LLM vulnerability is substantial but varies by dataset and comparator, with consistent cross-architecture transfer hierarchies observed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly applied to network intrusion detection, yet their adversarial robustness under realistic attacker constraints—where only certain traffic features can be manipulated in practice—remains poorly understood. Prior work lacks a systematic evaluation that respects network communication semantics in attack design.

Method: A controllability-aware black-box transfer framework is proposed. Flow features are partitioned into directly controllable (DC), indirectly controllable (IC), and uncontrollable (UC) groups based on network semantics.
