Perturbations are restricted solely to DC features while IC/UC features are frozen. An XGBoost surrogate model generates adversarial examples using three attack methods: finite-difference PGD, greedy coordinate-wise perturbations, and NES. These examples are transferred to seven LLM targets and two conventional ML targets across five IDS benchmarks (1999–2022). Cross-surrogate validation with tree, neural, and linear surrogates is also conducted.

Result: Across 27 valid LLM configurations and 500,000+ adversarial examples: (1) LLM transfer vulnerability is substantial but dataset- and comparator-dependent—LLMs are more vulnerable than LightGBM on RT-IoT2022 and CIC-IDS-2018, comparable on NSL-KDD and UNSW-NB15, and less vulnerable on HIKARI-2021; however, LLMs show higher ASR than averaged ML baselines on all five datasets. (2) A consistent cross-architecture transfer hierarchy emerges: gradient- and score-based perturbations transfer more effectively than greedy perturbations across all 27 LLM cells and 9/10 ML cells. (3) Cross-surrogate validation yields similar LLM ASR, suggesting findings are not XGBoost-specific. Constraint violation rate is 0% by construction.

Conclusion: LLM-based network traffic classifiers exhibit significant transfer-based adversarial vulnerability, though the degree depends on the dataset and the specific comparator. The consistent transfer hierarchy and the effectiveness of controllability-aware constraints highlight the need for robustness-aware deployment strategies when using LLMs in intrusion detection systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored as network intrusion detection classifiers, but their adversarial robustness under realistic attacker constraints remains unclear. We present a controllability-aware black-box transfer framework for LLM-based network traffic classifiers. The framework partitions flow features into directly controllable (DC), indirectly controllable (IC), and uncontrollable (UC) groups according to network communication semantics, then restricts perturbations to DC features while freezing IC/UC features. Using a shared XGBoost surrogate, we generate finite-difference PGD, greedy coordinate-wise, and NES adversarial examples and transfer them to seven LLM targets and two conventional ML targets across five IDS benchmarks from 1999 to 2022. Across 27 valid LLM configurations and over 500,000 adversarial examples, we find that LLM transfer vulnerability is substantial but dataset- and comparator-dependent. Compared with LightGBM, LLMs are more vulnerable on RT-IoT2022 and CIC-IDS-2018, comparable on NSL-KDD and UNSW-NB15, and less vulnerable on HIKARI-2021; compared with the averaged ML baseline, LLMs show higher ASR on all five datasets. We further observe a consistent cross-architecture transfer hierarchy: gradient- and score-based perturbations transfer more effectively than greedy perturbations across all 27 LLM cells and 9/10 ML cells. Cross-surrogate validation with tree, neural, and linear surrogates yields similar LLM ASR, reducing evidence that the findings are XGBoost-specific. Constraint violation rate is 0\% by construction.

</details>


### [46] [Forensic Schema for Psychological Manipulation in Cyber Fraud: LLM-Driven Victim Reports Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07751)
*Zikai Alex Wen,Corrazon Ogot,Juan Li,Yan Bai*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A forensic schema incorporating psychological manipulation indicators and cryptocurrency evidence fields was developed and applied to 10,994 cybercrime victim reports using LLM-driven annotation. The schema reliably distinguishes fraud types by manipulation profile, but victim narratives lack actionable forensic detail and blockchain identifiers, suggesting AI-assisted intake with schema-informed follow-up questions as a remedy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cybercrime classification systems focus on contact metadata and financial transactions but ignore the psychological manipulation techniques that perpetrators use to defraud victims. This gap limits both forensic investigation and understanding of criminal MOs, particularly in cryptocurrency-related scams where blockchain evidence and manipulation tactics are intertwined.

Method: The authors constructed a forensic schema with four categories and 35 questions, adding 11 manipulation indicators and cryptocurrency evidence fields to established forensic frameworks. They applied it to 10,994 victim reports using LLM-driven annotation, validated against two human annotators (mean Cohen's κ = 0.69, matching inter-annotator agreement at κ = 0.68), and performed statistical analysis of manipulation profiles across fraud types including Cramér's V. A rationale-based evidence audit assessed the quality and completeness of supporting detail in victim narratives.

Result: Each major fraud type exhibited a statistically distinct manipulation profile (Cramér's V up to 0.790). LLM-human agreement matched human inter-annotator agreement, demonstrating reliable automated annotation. However, the evidence audit revealed a forensic detail gap: while detection of manipulation techniques was reliable, victim narratives varied substantially in actionable detail, and blockchain-specific identifiers were nearly absent from reports.

Conclusion: AI-assisted victim intake systems that deploy schema-informed follow-up questions represent the most direct path to closing the forensic detail gap. The tiered annotation strategy offers a reusable template for LLM-based information extraction in other forensic text domains.

Abstract: Existing cybercrime classification schemas capture contact metadata and financial transactions but omit the psychological manipulation techniques perpetrators employ. We present a forensic schema (four categories, 35 questions) adding 11 manipulation indicators and cryptocurrency evidence fields to established forensic foundations. Applied to 10,994 victim reports via large language model (LLM)-driven annotation and validated against two human annotators (mean LLM-human $κ= 0.69$, matching inter-annotator $κ= 0.68$), the schema revealed a statistically distinct manipulation profile for each major fraud type (Cramer's $V$ up to $0.790$). A rationale-based evidence audit nonetheless exposed a forensic detail gap: detection of manipulation techniques was reliable, but victim narratives varied widely in the actionable detail supporting each Yes answer, and blockchain-specific identifiers were nearly absent. These findings point to AI-assisted victim intake with schema-informed follow-up questions as the most direct way to close the gap. The tiered annotation strategy also provides a reusable template for LLM-based extraction from other forensic text domains.

</details>


### [47] [ScopeJudge: Cost-Aware Pre-Execution Gating for Offensive Security Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07774)
*Shane Caldwell,Max Harley,Ads Dawson,Michael Kouremetis,Vincent Abruzzo,Will Pearce*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ScopeJudge is a benchmark of 4,897 tool calls (7.7% violations) for evaluating pre-execution gating of LLM security agents. The paper demonstrates that static policies are structurally insufficient — scope enforcement requires request-conditioned monitoring — and evaluates eight judge models across five transcript strategies, mapping a cost-accuracy Pareto frontier with recommended operating points for high-stakes deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agents are deployed for offensive security tasks, a single out-of-scope tool call can breach engagement boundaries, disrupt production systems, or invalidate bug-bounty findings. Unlike fixed safety policies, scope in offensive security is dynamic: the same action can be in-scope or out-of-scope depending on the target and runtime context, which cannot be enumerated in advance. There is a need for reliable, real-time pre-execution gating mechanisms and a rigorous benchmark to evaluate them.

Method: The authors construct ScopeJudge, a dataset of 4,897 tool calls drawn from agent trajectories on deliberately tempting security tasks, labeled at the call level by professional penetration testers (Fleiss' kappa = 0.64, establishing an expert agreement ceiling of F1 = 0.78). They evaluate eight judge LLMs across five transcript strategies that vary the context window (from static policy alone to full raw transcript) and measure precision, recall, F1, and cost to chart a Pareto frontier. Two operating points are recommended: cost-sensitive and recall-first configurations.

Result: Static-policy-only judging collapses recall to near zero, confirming that scope enforcement depends on the user's request context. Request-conditioned monitoring is necessary but introduces a cost-accuracy trade-off. The Pareto frontier reveals distinct operating regimes, and the authors recommend a recall-first configuration for high-stakes deployments where missed violations are costlier than spurious rejections.

Conclusion: Pre-execution gating with a trusted LLM judge is a viable approach for enforcing dynamic scope boundaries in autonomous offensive security agents, but static policies are structurally insufficient. The ScopeJudge dataset and benchmark provide a foundation for real-time monitoring and scalable oversight, with clear guidance on cost-accuracy trade-offs for practitioners.

Abstract: As LLM agents take on offensive security work, a single out-of-scope tool call can breach a client's engagement boundary, disrupt production, or void a bug-bounty finding. Unlike a fixed safety policy, the boundary that matters is declared in the user's request and must be inferred from intent. That challenge is sharpened by the adversarial nature of offensive security: the same tool call is in or out of scope depending not on the action itself but on the target it touches and the context in which it runs, which no fixed policy can enumerate in advance. We study pre-execution gating: a cheap, trusted LLM judge inspects each call proposed by a strong, swappable agent, and accepts or rejects it before it runs. We introduce ScopeJudge, a benchmark of 4,897 tool calls (7.7% scope violations) from agent trajectories on tasks engineered to tempt agents out of scope and labeled at the call level by professional penetration testers, with substantial inter-grader agreement (Fleiss kappa = 0.64) that sets an expert agreement reference point of F1 = 0.78. We evaluate eight judge models under five transcript strategies, varying how much context the judge sees, from the static policy alone to the full raw transcript, and chart the resulting cost-accuracy Pareto frontier. We find that a static policy is structurally insufficient for scope enforcement: blind to the user's request, judge recall collapses to near zero, confirming that scope lives in the request and that request-conditioned monitoring is necessary. Because a missed violation costs more than a spurious rejection, we report precision, recall, and F1 separately and recommend two operating points: a cost-sensitive configuration and a recall-first one for high-stakes deployments. We release the ScopeJudge dataset to support real-time monitoring and scalable oversight of autonomous security agents.

</details>


### [48] [Open Models, Open Risks: Measuring Unsafe Generation in Text-to-Image Models In the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07827)
*Peilin Han,Yang Liu,Yilong Yang,Jingchun Zhang,Teng Li,Jianfeng Ma,Zhuo Ma*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A large-scale empirical study of text-to-image safety across 200+ real-world Hugging Face models, revealing that existing detector-based jailbreak metrics overestimate risk due to semantic drift and artifacts. The authors introduce Advanced ASR for more accurate unsafe generation measurement, finding that safety degradation in the wild is neither universal nor uniform, while identifying genuinely high-risk models — both explicit NSFW releases and seemingly benign ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing T2I jailbreak safety studies are conducted in controlled lab settings on few canonical models, leaving the safety status of the rapidly growing in-the-wild T2I ecosystem unclear. This gap is exacerbated by: (1) detector-based metrics designed only for controlled evaluation, and (2) in-the-wild risks arising not only from adversarial prompting but also from unsafe release practices and unsafe model derivatives.

Method: The authors propose Advanced ASR (Attack Success Rate), a refined metric that better captures semantically valid and visually plausible unsafe generations — addressing limitations of detector-only metrics like semantic drift and generation artifacts. They systematically evaluate 200+ in-the-wild T2I models from Hugging Face under three representative jailbreak attacks, and trace high-risk models back to their release context, reporting findings to Hugging Face.

Result: Detector-only jailbreak metrics substantially overestimate practical risk. Many downstream models retain non-trivial safety even without explicit post-hoc safeguards, showing safety degradation is neither universal nor uniform. However, a set of high-risk models was identified, including explicitly NSFW-oriented releases and seemingly benign models whose unsafe behavior only surfaces through systematic evaluation.

Conclusion: In-the-wild T2I safety assessment requires metrics beyond detector-only approaches, like Advanced ASR. While safety degradation is not inevitable across the ecosystem, systematic evaluation is essential to uncover latent risks in models that appear benign. The work underscores the need for ecosystem-wide monitoring and responsible release practices.

Abstract: Existing safety studies on text-to-image (T2I) jailbreaks are largely conducted in controlled in-the-lab settings, typically on a small number of canonical models. As a result, the current safety status of the rapidly growing in-the-wild T2I ecosystem remains unclear. This uncertainty is amplified by two factors: existing detector-based metrics are designed for controlled evaluation, and in-the-wild risks may arise not only from adversarial prompting, but also from unsafe release practices and unsafe model derivatives.
  In this paper, we present a large-scale empirical study of in-the-wild T2I safety through the lens of jailbreak. We first show that detector-only jailbreak metrics substantially overestimate practical risk over in the wild due to semantic drift and generation artifacts, and we introduce Advanced ASR to better capture semantically valid and visually plausible unsafe generation. Using this refined metric, we evaluate 200+ in-the-wild T2I models from Hugging Face under three representative jailbreak attacks. Our results show that many downstream models retain a non-trivial degree of safety even without explicit post-hoc safeguards, indicating that safety degradation in the wild is neither universal nor uniform. At the same time, we identify a set of high-risk models, including explicitly NSFW-oriented releases as well as seemingly benign models whose unsafe behavior is only exposed through systematic evaluation. We further trace these models to their release context and report high-risk cases to Hugging Face.

</details>


### [49] [Closed-Loop Dynamic Validator Node Scaling in Private Substrate Blockchains Using Takagi-Sugeno Fuzzy Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07901)
*Thandile Nododile,Ayinde M. Usman,Clement N. Nyirenda*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A Takagi-Sugeno fuzzy inference system autonomously manages validator counts in private blockchains by analyzing live metrics (block time, size, node count) to recommend scaling actions. Tested on a 10-node Substrate network with real water meter data, it achieves stable, oscillation-free scaling that threshold-based baselines cannot match.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Private blockchain networks operate with fixed validator configurations that cannot adapt to changing workloads: over-provisioning wastes resources, while under-provisioning slows block production and finalisation. Determining the optimal validator count is challenging because it depends on multiple shifting, interdependent factors.

Method: A Takagi-Sugeno fuzzy inference controller with triangular membership functions across three linguistic variables (block production time, block size, active node count), using a complete 27-rule base with product t-norm aggregation. Key innovation: empirical recalibration of membership functions anchored to observed operating ranges rather than theoretical extremes. The controller outputs a continuous efficiency score and a discrete recommendation: Scale Up, Maintain, or Scale Down. Evaluated on a 10-node Substrate blockchain storing real smart water meter data hashes from Queensland Government open data. Closed-loop experiments tested configurations of 4, 7, and 10 active nodes, with autonomous scaling in both directions.

Result: Statistical analysis confirmed distinct operational profiles for 4-, 7-, and 10-node configurations reflecting each provisioning state. In closed-loop tests, the controller converged to the same stable equilibrium from both directions (activating validators under rising load, removing them under over-provisioning). Compared to three threshold-based baselines, the fuzzy controller produced fewer scaling oscillations while maintaining comparable block production times.

Conclusion: Takagi-Sugeno fuzzy inference effectively supports autonomous validator management in private blockchain deployments, delivering stable scaling behavior that threshold-based approaches cannot achieve. The empirical recalibration of membership functions to real operating ranges is a key enabler.

Abstract: Private blockchain networks run with fixed node configurations that cannot adapt to changing workload conditions. Too many nodes serving a light workload waste resources; too few nodes facing heavy demand slow block production and degrade finalisation. The right validator count is hard to determine, as it depends on overlapping factors that shift over time. This paper presents a Takagi-Sugeno (TS) fuzzy inference system that reads live blockchain parameters (block production time, block size, and active node count) and outputs a continuous efficiency score alongside a scaling recommendation: Scale Up, Maintain, or Scale Down. The controller uses triangular membership functions across three linguistic variables, evaluated through a complete 27-rule base with product t-norm aggregation. A key contribution is an empirical recalibration of the membership functions, anchoring linguistic terms to the observed operating range of the testbed rather than to theoretical extremes. The system is evaluated on a 10-node Substrate blockchain network storing real smart water meter data hashes from the Queensland Government open data portal. Statistical analysis across configurations of 4, 7, and 10 active nodes confirms that the controller produces distinct operational profiles reflecting each configuration's provisioning state. In closed-loop experiments, the controller autonomously adjusts validator participation in both directions, activating validators under rising load and removing them under over-provisioning, converging to the same stable equilibrium from both directions. Compared against three threshold-based baselines, it shows fewer scaling oscillations while maintaining comparable block production times. Results show that TS fuzzy inference can support autonomous validator management in private blockchain deployments, with stable scaling behaviour threshold approaches cannot match.

</details>


### [50] [Mechanistic Interpretability of LLM Jailbreaks via Internal Attribution Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07903)
*Anupam Wagle,Ifrat Ikhtear Uddin,Chaowei Zhang,Longwei Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper introduces a mechanistic framework that uses paired internal computation graphs to diagnose LLM vulnerabilities to adversarial and jailbreak attacks. By comparing graph structures between clean and attacked prompts, the method reveals how attacks systematically suppress safety components, introduce attack-specific features, and reroute computation paths. The framework supports causal interventions on nodes, paths, and subgraphs to move from descriptive attribution to causal diagnosis, demonstrating that structural deviations correlate with unsafe behaviors and that targeted interventions improve robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches to understanding LLM vulnerabilities rely on input-output behavioral analysis or attribution methods, which provide limited insight into how adversarial perturbations actually alter the model's internal reasoning mechanisms. The mechanisms underlying unsafe or incorrect behaviors remain poorly understood, motivating the need for a mechanistic, causal framework that can reveal the internal structural transformations induced by attacks.

Method: The authors propose representing LLM inference as internal computation graphs — structured causal interactions among latent features. They construct and align paired computation graphs for clean and attacked prompts, then decompose computation into invariant, suppressed, and emergent structures. Vulnerability motifs (recurring structural patterns associated with failure modes) are identified, and causal interventions (manipulating nodes, paths, and subgraphs) are performed to evaluate their contributions to attack success.

Result: Experiments across multiple open-source LLMs and diverse adversarial/jailbreak benchmarks show that structural deviations in internal computation graphs strongly correlate with unsafe behaviors. Targeted causal interventions on identified vulnerability motifs demonstrably improve model robustness.

Conclusion: Internal computation graphs serve as a principled foundation for understanding, diagnosing, and mitigating LLM vulnerabilities. The framework enables a transition from descriptive attribution to causal diagnosis, offering both mechanistic interpretability and actionable pathways for improving model safety.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit remarkable capabilities but remain highly vulnerable to adversarial prompts and jailbreak attacks. Existing approaches primarily analyze these failures through input-output behaviors or attribution methods, offering limited insight into how adversarial perturbations alter the model's internal reasoning. Consequently, the mechanisms underlying unsafe or incorrect behaviors remain poorly understood. We introduce a mechanistic framework for diagnosing LLM vulnerabilities using paired internal computation graphs, which represent prompt-specific inference as structured causal interactions among latent features. By constructing and aligning computation graphs for clean and attacked prompts, we reveal that adversarial attacks induce systematic transformations of internal reasoning, including suppression of safety-relevant components, emergence of attack-specific features, and rerouting of computation paths. Building on this representation, we propose a unified framework that (i) decomposes computation into invariant, suppressed, and emergent structures, (ii) identifies recurring vulnerability motifs associated with failure modes, and (iii) performs causal interventions on nodes, paths, and subgraphs to directly evaluate their contributions to attack success. This enables a transition from descriptive attribution to causal diagnosis of model failures. Experiments across multiple open-source LLMs and diverse adversarial and jailbreak benchmarks demonstrate that structural deviations in internal computation graphs strongly correlate with unsafe behaviors. Furthermore, targeted interventions on identified vulnerability motifs improve model robustness, establishing internal computation graphs as a principled foundation for understanding, diagnosing, and mitigating LLM vulnerabilities.

</details>


### [51] [KS-CFA: Control-Flow Attestation via Symbolic Replay Against Control-Flow Bending Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07926)
*Zhanyu Sha,Konstantinos Markantonakis,Carlton Shepherd,Amir Rafi*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: KS-CFA is a control-flow attestation scheme that detects control-flow bending attacks by combining symbolic execution with selective identification of input-sourced control-flow dependent variables. The prover records a control-flow trace and external inputs inside a TEE, while the verifier replays the path via single-path symbolic execution to detect divergences, without requiring path enumeration or hardware modifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing control-flow attestation (CFA) schemes struggle to detect control-flow bending (CFB) attacks — where adversaries manipulate branch flags, loop counters, and runtime data to steer execution along legal CFG edges. Prior solutions either require enumerating vast measurement spaces, cover only a subset of attacks, or depend on low-level hardware changes. A practical, comprehensive CFB detection mechanism is needed.

Method: KS-CFA leverages two key techniques: (1) selective identification of input-sourced control-flow dependent variables — a strict subset of state whose values derive directly from external input — and (2) single-path symbolic execution. The prover, running inside a RISC-V Keystone TEE, records a control-flow trace plus the external inputs that determine relevant runtime variables. The verifier then replays the reported path through symbolic execution, predicting each control-flow transfer and localising divergences that indicate an attack.

Result: Implemented on RISC-V Keystone TEE with Embench-IoT benchmarks, KS-CFA was evaluated on QEMU and a Rocket-based FPGA (NiteFury II). Prover-side runtime overhead relative to unattested execution is 6.8–20.5× on QEMU and 6.7–32.2× on the FPGA. Verification requires no path or value enumeration.

Conclusion: KS-CFA provides effective CFB attack detection across four transfer types (indirect calls, conditional/indirect jumps, returns) without the enumeration burden or hardware modifications required by prior work, demonstrating feasibility on both emulated and FPGA-based RISC-V platforms.

Abstract: Control-flow attestation (CFA) enables a remote entity to verify program execution on a target device by monitoring control-flow behaviour at runtime. However, control-flow bending (CFB) attacks remain difficult to detect, where an adversary steers execution along legal edges of the program's control-flow graph by corrupting branch flags, loop counters, and other runtime data. Existing solutions impose significant drawbacks: they require enumerating vast measurement spaces, cover only a reduced subset of attacks, or rely on low-level hardware modifications. In this work, we present KS-CFA, a new CFA scheme that detects CFB attacks across four transfer types (indirect calls, conditional and indirect jumps, and returns) without those costs. To this end, we combine symbolic execution and selective identification of input-sourced control-flow dependent variables: a strict subset of control-flow-relevant state whose values are directly read from external input. The proving device records, inside a trusted execution environment (TEE), a control-flow trace and the external inputs that determine relevant run-time variables. The verifier then replays the reported path through single-path symbolic execution, predicting each transfer and localising divergences that signal an attack. We implement and evaluate KS-CFA using the RISC-V Keystone TEE and Embench-IoT on QEMU and a Rocket-based FPGA platform (NiteFury II). Prover-side overhead relative to unattested execution ranges from 6.8-20.5x on QEMU and 6.7-32.2x on the FPGA, and verification requires no path or value enumeration.

</details>


### [52] [Who Broke the System? Failure Localization in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07989)
*Yufei Xia,Anjun Gao,Yueyang Quan,Zhuqing Liu,Minghong Fang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: AgentLocate is a framework for diagnosing failures in LLM-based multi-agent systems by attributing errors to a specific agent and the earliest decisive step. It uses LLM judging with multi-perspective verification and confidence-aware aggregation, plus lightweight fine-tuning for continuous improvement. It outperforms existing methods on two benchmarks in both accuracy and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based multi-agent systems face significant challenges in failure diagnosis due to long-horizon interactions and tightly coupled agent behaviors. When execution fails, it is difficult to pinpoint which agent is responsible and at what point the trajectory becomes irreversibly misdirected. Existing methods lack effective mechanisms for this fine-grained failure localization.

Method: AgentLocate combines: (1) an LLM-based judging mechanism that attributes failures to a specific agent and the earliest decisive step; (2) multi-perspective verification by independent evaluators whose assessments are aggregated using a confidence-aware strategy; and (3) lightweight fine-tuning of the judge using the aggregated feedback to iteratively improve attribution quality.

Result: Experimental evaluation on two complementary benchmarks — covering diverse tasks, agent configurations, and trajectory lengths — shows that AgentLocate consistently outperforms existing failure localization methods in identifying both responsible agents and failure steps. It also demonstrates efficiency in token usage and running time.

Conclusion: AgentLocate provides an effective and efficient solution for failure localization in LLM-based multi-agent systems, enabling precise attribution to both the responsible agent and the earliest decisive failure step. Its confidence-aware multi-perspective verification and adaptive fine-tuning mechanism contribute to superior performance over existing approaches.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) based multi-agent systems enable complex problem solving through coordinated reasoning and action, but their distributed structure also introduces new challenges in diagnosing system-level failures. When an execution fails, identifying which agent is responsible and at what point the trajectory first becomes irreversibly misdirected is difficult due to long-horizon interactions and tightly coupled agent behaviors. In this paper, we study the problem of failure localization in LLM-based multi-agent systems and present AgentLocate, a framework that attributes failures to both a specific agent and the earliest decisive step. AgentLocate combines an LLM-based judging mechanism with multi-perspective verification by independent evaluators, whose assessments are aggregated using a confidence-aware strategy. The resulting feedback is further used to adapt the judge through lightweight fine-tuning, improving attribution quality. We evaluate AgentLocate on two complementary benchmarks covering diverse tasks, agent configurations, and trajectory lengths. Experimental results show that AgentLocate consistently outperforms existing failure localization methods in identifying both responsible agents and failure steps, while remaining efficient in terms of token usage and running time.

</details>


### [53] [Beware What You Autocomplete: Forensic Attribution of Backdoored Code Completions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08011)
*Anjun Gao,Yueyang Quan,Zhuqing Liu,Minghong Fang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CodeTracer is a forensic framework that traces malicious code completions from backdoored LLMs back to the specific fine-tuning data responsible, using behavioral fingerprinting, semantic search, and LLM-based reasoning. It achieves high accuracy and robustness across diverse backdoor attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based code completion systems are vulnerable to backdoor attacks via malicious fine-tuning data. Existing defenses struggle against adaptive and sophisticated attacks. There is a need for forensic attribution — tracing a miscompletion back to its root-cause training samples — to enable targeted remediation and accountability, rather than just detection.

Method: CodeTracer operates under post-deployment constraints using only the fine-tuning corpus and a reported miscompletion. It works in three stages: (1) extracting a structured behavioral fingerprint from the compromised output, (2) narrowing the search space to semantically relevant code samples via embedding-based retrieval, and (3) employing LLM-based reasoning to attribute the unsafe logic to specific backdoor fine-tuning instances.

Result: Evaluated across three vulnerability cases, ten backdoor attacks, and sixteen baselines, CodeTracer consistently achieves high forensic accuracy, low false identification rates, and strong robustness against adaptive attacks designed to evade attribution.

Conclusion: CodeTracer provides an effective and practical forensic solution for tracing backdoor-induced code miscompletions to their source training data, filling a critical gap in post-deployment security for LLM-based code completion systems.

Abstract: Large language models have enabled powerful code completion systems that assist developers by predicting subsequent lines of code. However, these models remain vulnerable to backdoor attacks, where malicious fine-tuning data covertly implants unsafe behaviors. Despite advances in defensive techniques, adaptive and sophisticated backdoor attacks still evade detection and mitigation. We present CodeTracer, a forensic framework that traces malicious code completions back to the backdoor fine-tuning data responsible for them. Operating under realistic post-deployment constraints, CodeTracer relies solely on the fine-tuning corpus and the reported miscompletion event. It extracts a structured behavioral fingerprint from the compromised output, narrows the search to semantically relevant code samples, and employs LLM-based reasoning to attribute unsafe logic to specific backdoor data. Extensive evaluations across three representative vulnerability cases and ten backdoor attacks, along with sixteen competitive baselines, demonstrate that CodeTracer consistently achieves high forensic accuracy, low false identification rates, and strong robustness against adaptive attacks.

</details>


### [54] [Multi-Agent Firewall Architecture for Privacy Protection of Sensitive Data in Interactions with Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08282)
*Hugo García Cuesta,Pablo Mateo Torrejón,Alfonso Sánchez-Macián*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: An open-source, privacy-focused LLM firewall combining a browser extension and proxy for full traffic interception, using a multi-agent pipeline with deterministic and LLM-driven semantic analysis to prevent data leakage, achieving up to 94.93% F1 score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are widely adopted as productivity tools but lack adequate safeguards when integrated into workflows, creating significant security risks — particularly around data leakage and prompt injection. Existing solutions do not offer open-source, privacy-preserving, user-facing firewall capabilities that cover both web-based and programmatic LLM interactions.

Method: A dual-component architecture: (1) a browser extension and (2) a proxy server, together intercepting all HTTP(S) and WebSocket traffic to/from LLMs. The core detection engine is a flexible multi-agent pipeline combining deterministic rule-based detectors with LLM-driven semantic analysis. Key capabilities include data leakage prevention, proprietary code leakage prevention, and extensible components for future threats like prompt injection evasion. The layered design supports heterogeneous deployment, allowing trade-offs among computational cost, detection depth, and latency.

Result: The system achieves F1 scores of up to 94.93% on optimal configurations, demonstrating strong detection performance for data leakage prevention in LLM interactions.

Conclusion: The proposed open-source LLM firewall provides a practical, privacy-focused, and extensible security layer for LLM workflows, with strong empirical performance and flexible deployment options suitable for diverse organizational needs.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) have become essential productivity tools, their integration into workflows without adequate safeguards creates significant risks. This paper proposes an open-source, privacy-focused, user-facing firewall designed to secure both web-based and programmatic LLM interactions. The architecture combines a browser extension and a proxy for total traffic interception across both HTTP(S) and WebSocket communications. At its core, a flexible multi-agent pipeline delivers data leakage prevention through a hybrid approach combining deterministic detectors with LLM-driven semantic analysis, proprietary code leakage prevention, and extensible components designed for future security enhancements such as prompt injection evasion. The framework's layered architecture enables deployment across heterogeneous environments, allowing organizations to balance computational cost, detection depth and latency. Evaluation results demonstrate it achieves F1 scores of up to 94.93% on optimal configurations.

</details>


### [55] [Degree-Constrained Interval Optimization for Minimax Polynomial Approximation in Homomorphic Encryption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08042)
*Jiheon Woo,Donggyun Ryu,Yongjune Kim*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper formulates a distribution-aware interval optimization problem for minimax polynomial approximations in homomorphic encryption (HE). It balances within-interval approximation error against outside-interval extrapolation error by combining minimax polynomials with domain extension polynomials (DEPs), providing an analytically tractable proxy objective and an implementation-error decomposition with an upper bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In HE-based privacy-preserving inference, non-polynomial activations must be approximated by polynomials. Minimax approximation (Remez algorithm) is standard but involves a critical hyperparameter: the approximation interval. A wider interval increases robustness to large inputs but degrades approximation quality under a fixed polynomial degree. The paper addresses the lack of principled methods for choosing this interval in a distribution-aware manner.

Method: The authors (1) formulate the interval selection as a distribution-aware MSE minimization problem with respect to the pre-activation distribution; (2) combine minimax polynomials with domain extension functions (DEFs) and their HE-realizable polynomial counterparts (DEPs) to clip outside-interval inputs; (3) derive an analytically tractable DEF-based proxy objective capturing the within-interval vs. outside-interval error trade-off; and (4) connect the idealized DEF objective to practical DEP constructions via an implementation-error decomposition with an accompanying upper bound.

Result: The paper provides a theoretically grounded framework with an analytically tractable proxy objective and a bounded implementation-error decomposition that bridges the gap between idealized DEF-based clipping and HE-realizable DEP constructions.

Conclusion: By formulating the interval selection as a distribution-aware optimization and incorporating DEPs for controlled extrapolation suppression, the proposed framework enables principled design of polynomial approximations for HE inference that optimally balance approximation fidelity and robustness to input magnitude.

Abstract: Homomorphic encryption (HE) enables privacy-preserving inference under arithmetic constraints that restrict encrypted evaluation to additions and multiplications. As a result, non-polynomial activation functions must be replaced by polynomial approximations. Among polynomial approximation methods, minimax approximation, typically computed by the Remez algorithm, is a standard approach because it minimizes the maximum approximation error over a given design interval. For minimax polynomial design, the approximation interval is a critical hyperparameter: a wider interval improves robustness to large-magnitude inputs while increasing the minimax approximation error under a fixed degree budget. In this paper, we formulate this trade-off as a distribution-aware interval optimization problem, where the approximation interval is chosen to minimize the mean-squared error (MSE) with respect to the pre-activation distribution of interest. To effectively control outside-interval inputs, we combine minimax polynomials with domain extension functions (DEFs) and their HE-realizable polynomial counterparts, domain extension polynomials (DEPs), which approximate a clipping operation outside the design interval and thereby suppress uncontrolled polynomial extrapolation. We first derive an analytically tractable DEF-based proxy objective that captures the trade-off between within-interval minimax approximation error and outside-interval clipping error. We then connect this idealized objective to HE-realizable DEP constructions through an implementation-error decomposition with an accompanying upper bound.

</details>


### [56] [zkComposer: Decomposing Proof Construction to Scale zkML](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08095)
*Pawan Kumar Sanjaya,Christina Giannoula,Valdy Oktavian,Mehdi Saeedi,Gabor Sines,Gururaj Saileshwar,Nandita Vijaykumar*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: zkComposer introduces a modular proof-construction framework for zero-knowledge machine learning that decomposes monolithic proofs into independent, cryptographically linked sub-proofs, unlocking parallelism across model layers and input sequences, achieving up to 6.84x reduction in prover time over existing systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing zkML systems use monolithic proof computation, constructing a single proof for the entire ML model. This approach exhibits limited parallelism — prover time does not decrease significantly with more threads — making proof generation prohibitively expensive. A new paradigm is needed to unlock additional dimensions of parallelism beyond what existing proof kernels offer.

Method: zkComposer decomposes the zkML inference proof into independent sub-proofs, each covering a subset of computation (e.g., contiguous layers of the ML model). Adjacent sub-proofs are cryptographically linked through shared commitments to activations from boundary layers. Critically, this linkage provides the same guarantees as a monolithic proof without requiring additional linking proofs or changes to underlying cryptographic primitives. The approach also supports partitioning across both model layers and input sequences (as demonstrated on GPT-2).

Result: On CNN workloads, zkComposer reduces prover time and response time by up to 3.25x relative to zkCNN. On GPT-2 with layer-wise partitioning, it achieves up to 4.83x reduction relative to zkGPT. When partitioning across both model layers and input sequences in GPT-2, zkComposer reduces prover time and response time by up to 6.84x relative to zkGPT.

Conclusion: Modular proof construction via zkComposer effectively overcomes the parallelism bottleneck in monolithic zkML systems, delivering substantial speedups across both CNN and transformer workloads. The approach maintains full security guarantees without modifying cryptographic primitives, making it a practical advancement for verifiable private inference.

Abstract: Zero-knowledge machine learning (zkML) enables a server to perform verifiable inference while keeping model parameters private from the client. However, existing zkML systems incur prohibitive proof-generation costs. We observe that proof generation exhibits limited parallelism; that is, prover time does not decrease significantly as the number of threads increases. This limitation is because existing systems rely on monolithic proof computation, constructing a single proof for the entire machine learning model. We introduce zkComposer, a modular proof-construction framework that unlocks an additional dimension of parallelism, in addition to the parallelism in existing proof kernels. zkComposer decomposes the zkML proof of correct inference into independent sub-proofs, each covering a subset of the computation for inference e.g., each independent sub-proof can cover a subset of contiguous layers in the ML model. Adjacent sub-proofs are cryptographically linked through shared commitments to the activations from the boundary layer. zkComposer provides the same guarantees as the monolithic proof without requiring additional linking proofs or changes to the underlying cryptographic primitives. We implement zkComposer and evaluate it on three CNNs and GPT-2. We show that, on CNN workloads, zkComposer reduces prover time and response time by up to 3.25x relative to zkCNN [1]. On GPT-2, zkComposer reduces these times by up to 4.83x relative to zkGPT [2], when partitioning along the model layers. When partitioning across both model layers and input sequences in GPT-2, we show that zkComposer reduces prover time and response time by up to 6.84x relative to zkGPT [2].

</details>


### [57] [Securing Autonomous Vehicle Systems via Twin-Aware Federated Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08137)
*Zifan Zhang,Minghong Fang,Dianwei Chen,Zhuqing Liu,Prashant Khanduri,Xianfeng Yang,Anupam Das,Yuchen Liu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents SeAPPer, a secure aggregation framework for Federated Reinforcement Learning (FRL) in autonomous driving that combines digital twin-based rehearsal learning with historical parameter reinforcement to defend against poisoning attacks, providing theoretical convergence guarantees and empirical validation in highway driving scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated reinforcement learning enables privacy-preserving collaborative learning in vehicular environments, but poisoning attacks remain a critical and largely unexplored threat in safety-critical autonomous driving. Malicious agents can subtly manipulate system parameters to compromise the global control model, creating severe safety hazards that demand robust defensive mechanisms.

Method: The proposed SeAPPer framework employs two key strategies: (1) digital twins for rehearsal-based learning that simulates and validates agent behaviors before aggregation, and (2) a historical reinforcement mechanism that leverages past aggregated model parameters combined with a selected central gradient to discriminate benign updates from malicious ones, ensuring only trustworthy data contributes to the global model.

Result: The authors provide theoretical convergence guarantees for SeAPPer under poisoning attacks and validate its effectiveness through digital twin simulations of realistic highway environments, demonstrating robust autonomous vehicle control under adversarial conditions.

Conclusion: SeAPPer offers a principled defense against poisoning attacks in FRL-based autonomous driving systems, combining rehearsal learning and historical consistency checks to maintain model integrity without compromising the privacy benefits of federated learning.

Abstract: Federated reinforcement learning (FRL) is crucial for enabling collaborative learning across multiple agents without sharing raw data, thereby enhancing privacy and scalability in the decision-making process within dynamic vehicular environments. However, poisoning attacks pose a significant threat to the security and reliability of FRL-based systems, particularly in safety-critical autonomous driving, where this vulnerability remains largely unexplored. These attacks can compromise the global control model by subtly injecting malicious system parameters, leading to potential hazards. To counter these challenges, we present \alg (\underline{Sec}ure \underline{A}ggregation with \underline{p}oisoning-\underline{p}revention and historical reinforcement) as a defensive framework aimed at enhancing the robustness of FRL systems designed for safety-critical driving scenarios. \alg strategically integrates digital twins for rehearsal-based learning and leverages historical aggregated model parameters along with a selected central gradient to ensure that only benign data is aggregated, effectively mitigating the influence of malicious agents. Theoretical guarantees are provided for the convergence performance of \alg in the presence of poisoning attacks. We also validate the effectiveness of \alg using developed digital twins that model realistic highway environments to evaluate the control of autonomous vehicles under adversarial conditions.

</details>


### [58] [Prismata: Confining Cross-Site Prompt Injection in Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08147)
*Corban Villa,Alp Eren Ozdarendeli,Sijun Tan,Raluca Ada Popa*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Prismata is a defense system against prompt injection attacks on autonomous web agents. It enforces contextual least privilege through dynamic trust derivation that produces permission labels for page content, combined with mechanical confinement (content redaction and capability restriction). It requires no developer annotations, works across diverse websites, and significantly reduces attack success while maintaining benign task utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous web agents that interpret natural language instructions are vulnerable to prompt injection attacks from third-party and user-generated content on web pages. This resurrects the classic web security problem of mixing trusted and untrusted content (akin to Cross-Site Scripting). The fundamental difficulty is that deriving a task-specific security policy requires reasoning over page structure that is entangled with attacker-controlled content.

Method: Prismata enforces contextual least privilege through two complementary mechanisms: (1) Dynamic trust derivation that produces permission labels for page content, with structural confinement guarantees inspired by classical integrity models (e.g., Biba), ensuring labels can only decrease in privilege and mislabelings are bounded; (2) Mechanical confinement that enforces these labels by redacting content and restricting agent capabilities. The system requires no developer annotations, supporting the long tail of websites.

Result: Evaluated against recent published web agent attacks (including adaptive variants), Prismata substantially reduces attack success while preserving benign task utility.

Conclusion: Prismata demonstrates that contextual least privilege, enforced through structured trust derivation and mechanical confinement without requiring developer annotations, is an effective defense against prompt injection attacks on autonomous web agents.

Abstract: Autonomous web agents promise to automate everyday browsing tasks, but inherit one of the web's oldest attack surfaces. Cross-Site Scripting proved that mixing trusted and untrusted content is dangerous, even on benign pages. Agents resurface this risk by interpreting natural language as instructions, allowing third-party and user-generated content to hijack the agent via prompt injection. The core challenge is that deriving a task-specific security policy requires reasoning over page structure that is entangled with the attacker's content.
  We present Prismata, a defense enforcing contextual least privilege for web agents, constraining both what the agent sees and what it can do. Prismata's dynamic trust derivation produces permission labels for page content, with structural confinement guarantees, inspired by classical integrity models, that bound any labeling errors so that labels can only decrease in privilege and mislabelings are bounded. Prismata's mechanical confinement enforces these labels by redacting content and restricting agent capabilities. Importantly, these mechanisms require no developer annotations, so Prismata supports the long tail of websites. Across recent published web agent attacks, including adaptive variants, Prismata substantially reduces attack success while preserving benign task utility.

</details>


### [59] [Out of Sight: Compression-Aware Content Protection against Agentic Crawlers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08180)
*Xuefei Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CAPE protects online text from LLM-based agents by injecting invisible perturbations that cause severe information loss during the context compression step agents use to fit context budgets, achieving up to 75.8% improvement over baselines while preserving visual appearance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents with reasoning, summarization, and memory abilities pose a new threat to online content. Traditional defenses like access controls are bypassed by browser-mimicking agents, and injection-based defenses degrade human readability. The authors identify context compression — a routine step agents use to fit context windows — as an overlooked defensive layer that can be exploited for content protection.

Method: CAPE works in three stages: (1) extracts disruptive seed perturbations from an accessible surrogate compressor, (2) adapts these seeds to query-only target compressors via prior-guided evolution, and (3) uses preference-calibrated candidate prioritization to maximize protection under a low query budget. Perturbations are invisible to humans but cause severe information loss during agent compression.

Result: CAPE improves information loss by up to 75.8% over the strongest baseline across three content types and four compression settings. It transfers effectively to real-world systems including LangGraph agent workflow and GitHub Copilot, and keeps protected content visually indistinguishable from originals.

Conclusion: Context compression represents a viable new defense layer for protecting content against LLM-based agents. CAPE demonstrates practical, generalizable protection without compromising human readability, and the authors hope to spur further research into content protection in the agent era.

Abstract: The rise of LLM-based agents with reasoning, summarization, and memory capabilities has created a new threat surface for online content that conventional defenses fail to address. Existing defenses like access controls can be circumvented by agents mimicking ordinary browsers, and injection-based defenses often degrade human readability. In this paper, we revisit the agent pipeline and identify context compression, which agents routinely invoke to fit context budgets, as a critical yet overlooked defense layer. We propose CAPE, a framework that protects high-value textual content by injecting invisible perturbations without changing its human-visible surface form, thereby inducing severe information loss during agent compression. CAPE extracts disruptive seed perturbations from an accessible surrogate compressor, then adapts them to query-only target compressors through prior-guided evolution and preference-calibrated candidate prioritization, achieving effective protection under a low query budget. Experiments on three content types and four compression settings show that CAPE improves information loss by up to 75.8% over the strongest baseline while keeping protected content visually indistinguishable from originals. CAPE also transfers to real-world settings, including the LangGraph agent workflow and GitHub Copilot, highlighting its generality and practical value. This paper aims to reveal context compression as a new defense layer, promoting content protection research in the agent era.

</details>


### [60] [Simulating the Resident: Generating Executable Smart Home Schedules via LLM Personas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08231)
*Victor Jüttner,Xenia Wagner,Christoph Jahn,Erik Buchmann*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper proposes using LLMs to generate diverse resident personas and executable smart-home device interaction schedules within a simulated environment, addressing the privacy, cost, and scalability challenges of collecting real-world smart-home datasets for HCI security and privacy research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generating authentic smart-home datasets for usable security and privacy research requires long-term observation of real residents in their homes, which is slow, expensive, and raises significant privacy concerns. A privacy-conscious, scalable alternative is needed.

Method: The authors propose a three-component approach: (1) a design framework that configures simulated households along five socio-technical dimensions, (2) a multi-stage LLM pipeline that generates behaviorally grounded, structured, executable device interaction schedules, and (3) a proof-of-concept implementation demonstrating feasibility on physical testbeds.

Result: A proof of concept demonstrates the feasibility of the approach, showing that LLM-generated personas and interaction schedules can be produced in a structured, executable format suitable for driving physical smart-home testbeds.

Conclusion: As a work in progress, the framework aims to enable scalable, privacy-conscious smart-home experimentation without relying on intrusive real-world data collection, though further validation and development are needed.

Abstract: Smart homes have emerged as an important domain for HCI research, including work on usable security and privacy. Ideally, studies in these areas draw on datasets collected in real homes with real residents, capturing authentic device interactions, network traffic, and daily routines. However, creating such datasets is slow, expensive, and raises significant privacy concerns, as it requires long-term observation of people in their most private spaces. We propose using LLMs to generate diverse resident personas that interact with a simulated smart home, producing behaviorally grounded interaction schedules that can be executed on physical testbeds. We present (1) a design framework configuring simulated households across five socio-technical dimensions, (2) a multi-stage LLM pipeline that produces structured, executable device interaction schedules, and (3) a proof of concept demonstrating feasibility. As a work in progress, we aim to support scalable, privacy-conscious smart-home experimentation without relying on intrusive real-world data collection.

</details>


### [61] [MLQENABLER: Enabling Secure Machine Learning Queries over Encrypted Database in Cloud Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08197)
*Xu Zhou,Haoyang Chen,Xinyu Lei*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MLQENABLER is a scheme that enables secure machine learning queries over encrypted cloud databases using an index-aid approach, balancing security with minimal ML performance degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public cloud providers offer both storage and ML services, but clients cannot fully trust commercial clouds with sensitive data. Simply encrypting data before outsourcing makes it impossible to run ML queries, as encrypted data becomes pseudorandom. The motivation is to bridge this gap—enabling ML computation on encrypted data without exposing plaintext to untrusted cloud providers.

Method: The proposed MLQENABLER scheme uses an index-aid approach, where auxiliary index structures are built over the encrypted database to support ML queries. These indices preserve enough structural information for ML operations while keeping the underlying data encrypted, simultaneously achieving security and ML capability.

Result: Initial experiments demonstrate that MLQENABLER achieves an acceptable security level while incurring only a slight degradation in ML performance compared to unencrypted baselines.

Conclusion: The index-aid approach in MLQENABLER offers a viable trade-off between data confidentiality and ML query functionality in public cloud environments, addressing the tension between encryption and ML service enablement.

Abstract: In cloud computing, the public cloud service providers (CSPs) can provide cloud storage as the primary service while providing additional machine learning (ML)-based services by using the clients' data in storage. This business model extends the border of cloud computing services and brings in new business growth possibilities. Although it is promising, the model also brings in security concerns since the public commercial cloud cannot be fully trusted. For example, the public commercial clouds may sell clients' sensitive data to the government or other companies. To address the security concerns, an immediate solution is to require clients to encrypt their datasets before outsourcing to the cloud. However, if a database is formally encrypted, then the database contains only pseudorandom numbers, making it impossible to enable ML over it. In this project, we propose MLQENABLER (ML Queries Enabler) scheme to enable secure ML queries over encrypted database in cloud storage. MLQENABLER employs an index-aid approach to achieve security and ML capability simultaneously. Our initial experiments show that MLQENABLER achieves an acceptable security level while incurring only a slight ML performance degradation.

</details>


### [62] [Threshold Authorization Without Threshold Signatures: Signature-Agnostic MPC Custody](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08226)
*Dariia Porechna*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A dual-gate architecture decouples member authentication (via any EUF-CMA signature scheme, including post-quantum ones) from threshold authorization (via Shamir-shared seal generation). This allows seamless migration from ECDSA to SLH-DSA or ML-DSA through key rotation, supports commodity HSMs, and provides information-theoretic below-threshold secrecy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-quantum transition disrupts existing threshold signature schemes (TSS) for digital-asset custody: hash-based signatures resist efficient threshold signing, and lattice-based threshold protocols remain immature. A new architecture is needed that supports threshold multi-party approval without tying the authorization mechanism to any specific signature scheme, enabling smooth migration to post-quantum signatures.

Method: The paper proposes a dual-gate architecture with two separated components: (1) Member authentication — each of n members signs their approval using any EUF-CMA signature scheme (ECDSA, SLH-DSA, ML-DSA, etc.); (2) Threshold authorization — a quorum of t members jointly produces a threshold seal derived from Shamir-shared secrets bound to the operation. The seal serves as a base instance of programmable authorization computation, supporting policies ranging from simple quorum to richer secret-shared state evaluations. Member signatures are not part of the threshold computation. Deployment targets include smart contracts, vault modules, or HSMs guarding a master key.

Result: The architecture achieves: (a) signature scheme as a deployment parameter — migrating from ECDSA to post-quantum schemes is a key rotation, not a protocol redesign; (b) commodity HSM compatibility via standard sign API; (c) information-theoretic below-threshold secrecy — an adversary with ≥t signing keys but no coefficient shares cannot produce the seal; (d) enforcement-layer authorization output rather than a native chain signature.

Conclusion: The dual-gate separation of authentication and authorization provides a practical, future-proof framework for digital-asset custody that accommodates the post-quantum transition without sacrificing security properties or operational compatibility with existing HSM infrastructure.

Abstract: Digital-asset custody has been built on threshold multi-party approval: no operation proceeds unless $t$ of $n$ parties approve, and fewer than t compromised parties can neither authorize nor learn the authorization secret. Threshold signature schemes (TSS) have been the standard mechanism, but the post-quantum transition disrupts this model: standardized hash-based signatures resist efficient threshold signing, and lattice-based threshold protocols remain an emerging research track.
  We present a dual-gate architecture that separates member authentication from threshold authorization. Each member signs its approval with an ordinary signature under any EUF-CMA scheme; the quorum jointly produces a threshold seal from Shamir-shared secrets bound to the operation. The seal is the base instance of a programmable authorization computation: simple quorum is the minimal policy, while richer policies can evaluate secret-shared state without making the member-signature scheme part of that computation. The signature scheme is a deployment parameter: migrating from ECDSA to SLH-DSA or ML-DSA is a key rotation, not a protocol redesign, and members holding keys in commodity HSMs participate through the standard sign API. The architecture can be deployed wherever the asset-control path supports programmable verification, such as smart contracts, vault modules, or HSMs guarding a master key, and produces an enforcement-layer authorization rather than a native chain signature. Below-threshold secrecy is information-theoretic; an adversary holding $\geq t$ signing keys but no coefficient shares still cannot produce the seal.

</details>


### [63] [Mini-Programs, Mega-Problems: Unveiling OAuth-based Authentication Misuses in Mini-Programs via Dynamic Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08232)
*Zidong Zhang,Zhentao Xie,Lingyun Ying,Qinsheng Hou,Yacong Gu,Wenrui Diao,Jianliang Wu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MINIAUTH is the first dynamic analysis framework for detecting OAuth-based Authentication (OBA) misuse in mini-programs at scale. Applied to ~47K mini-programs across WeChat and Baidu, it uncovered 1,834 misuse cases, including three novel runtime OBA vulnerability types enabling victim impersonation, plus a cryptographic flaw in Baidu's OBA APIs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior studies on OAuth misuse in mini-programs rely on static code analysis, which cannot handle obfuscated code or detect runtime logic flaws. The authors aim to bridge this gap by discovering dynamic, runtime OBA misuses that enable attacker impersonation, and to assess the cross-platform prevalence of such vulnerabilities.

Method: MINIAUTH, a framework that: (1) automatically identifies OBA login pages in mini-programs, (2) dynamically executes the authentication workflow in instrumented runtime environments, and (3) analyzes runtime behaviors to detect misuse. It handles obfuscated code by observing actual execution traces rather than relying on static analysis. Evaluated on 44,273 WeChat mini-programs and 2,721 Baidu mini-programs.

Result: Discovered 1,834 OBA misuse cases across both platforms, including three new runtime vulnerability types: client-side identity forgery via exposed credentials, and authentication bypass through static/plaintext identifiers. Also identified a cryptographic design flaw in Baidu's OBA APIs enabling brute-force attacks on session keys. Cross-platform evaluation confirmed these misuses are ecosystem-agnostic. Findings were responsibly disclosed, receiving CNVD/CNNVD IDs.

Conclusion: Runtime OBA misuses are widespread and cross-platform in nature, highlighting significant gaps in developer guidance and platform-level safeguards. The work demonstrates that dynamic analysis is essential for uncovering vulnerabilities that static approaches miss, and calls for stronger platform-enforced protections for OAuth integration in mini-program ecosystems.

Abstract: Mini-programs have become a dominant paradigm for lightweight application deployment within super apps such as WeChat. To support seamless integration, super apps provide OAuth mechanisms for user login. However, improper integration of OAuth-based Authentication (OBA) flows by third-party developers can lead to critical security flaws. In this paper, we discover three new types of runtime OBA misuses that differ from prior static-code-based studies, enabling attackers to impersonate victims. To assess their real-world impact, we design and implement MINIAUTH, the first analysis framework for systematically analyzing OBA misuse at scale. MINIAUTH automatically pinpoints the OBA login page of a mini-program, executes the workflow dynamically, and analyzes its runtime behaviors. This enables it to handle obfuscated mini-programs and uncover vulnerabilities that existing approaches cannot detect. Applying MINIAUTH to 44,273 WeChat and 2,721 Baidu mini-programs, we uncover 1,834 misuse cases, including critical logic flaws that enable client-side identity forgery via exposed credentials and authentication bypass through static or plaintext identifiers. Our cross-platform evaluation further shows that such misuses are not confined to a single ecosystem but consistently appear across different mini-program platforms. We also identify a cryptographic design flaw in Baidu's OBA APIs that allows brute-forcing of session keys. We responsibly disclosed our findings to the developers and platforms, receiving acknowledgments and assigned CNVD/CNNVD IDs. These results underscore the need for more robust developer guidance and enhanced platform-level safeguards.

</details>


### [64] [From Legacy Documentation to OSCAL: An MCP-Based Agent Pipeline for Threat-Informed Continuous Compliance in Critical Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08288)
*Lea Roxanne Muth,Marian Margraf*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A non-invasive multi-agent pipeline uses MCP to convert natural-language OT system descriptions into NIST OSCAL-compliant audit artifacts, achieving high CVE/D3FEND recall. The key insight: grounding errors shift to the asset extraction phase, making remaining risks visible and efficiently reviewable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critical infrastructure OT environments cannot be actively scanned, yet require active system feedback for risk assessment and compliance. Existing LLM-based approaches risk fabricating vulnerabilities and hallucinating attack paths, necessitating a grounded, verifiable alternative.

Method: A non-invasive, MCP (Model Context Protocol)-grounded multi-agent pipeline that decouples LLM reasoning from deterministic knowledge retrieval against authoritative threat-intelligence sources. It converts natural-language system descriptions into a source-verified knowledge graph and generates OSCAL-format artifacts (System Security Plan and Security Assessment Report).

Result: In a synthetic water utility scenario, the pipeline achieved 0.90 CVE recall and perfect (1.0) D3FEND recall. It produced a schema-valid OSCAL SSP and SAR. The core finding: MCP grounding shifts errors into the asset extraction phase, where a single mis-extracted entity can yield genuine but irrelevant CVEs — yet this makes the residual risk visible, verifiable, and suitable for efficient manual review.

Conclusion: MCP-based grounding does not eliminate hallucinations entirely but relocates errors to the initial asset extraction stage. This is an improvement because infrastructure details (OS, versions) are typically known, making manual verification time-efficient, while the downstream pipeline remains auditable and source-verifiable.

Abstract: In critical infrastructure, operational technology environments often cannot be actively scanned, and yet active system feedback is needed for risk assessment and compliance. This paper presents a non-invasive, MCP-grounded multi-agent pipeline that converts natural-language system descriptions into source-verified knowledge graph and audit-ready artifacts in the NIST OSCAL format for continuous automated compliance management. The architecture decouples LLM-based reasoning from deterministic knowledge retrieval against authoritative threat-intelligence sources, reducing the risk of fabricated vulnerabilities and hallucinated attack paths.
  In an evidence-based synthetic scenario of a water utility, the pipeline achieves 0.90 CVE recall and perfect D3FEND recall. It generates a schema-valid OSCAL System Security Plan and an OSCAL Security Assessment Report. Nevertheless, the core insight is not that grounding via MCP eliminates errors (e.g., hallucinations) entirely from the pipeline, but that it shifts errors into the first phase of asset extraction from the natural language description. Here, a single incorrectly extracted entity can lead to genuine but irrelevant CVEs in subsequent stages of the pipeline, which consumes time and resources. However, it makes the remaining risk visible, verifiable, and suitable for a time-efficient manual review, since the infrastructure (e.g., version numbers, OS, etc.) is typically known.

</details>


### [65] [Reverse Engineering Compliance: A Dual-Graph Verification Framework for Auditing Legacy IT Security Concepts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08292)
*Lea Roxanne Muth,Marian Margraf*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The ASSERT Framework extracts legacy IT security concepts into formal document graphs, performs a five-class graph difference against a verified reference, and exports schema-valid OSCAL artifacts — enabling measurable detection of document-infrastructure inconsistencies, though with a trade-off between discovering undocumented entities and schema enforcement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The NIS-2 Directive drives demand for continuous, auditable, machine-readable compliance evidence (OSCAL), but organizations still possess extensive legacy IT security concepts (IT-SCs). Simply migrating these without verification risks transferring outdated or incorrect assets into the new format. Existing research focuses on generating new concepts, leaving a gap for a verification framework that can extract, compare, and validate legacy documents against an independently constructed reference state before OSCAL export.

Method: The ASSERT Framework employs three stages: (1) ontology-based extraction of legacy documents into formal document graphs; (2) a five-class graph difference computation against a verified reference graph to identify inconsistencies; and (3) export of schema-valid OSCAL outputs for system description and assessment evidence. Evaluation uses the BSI's RecPlast dataset, comparing a local open-weight model and a commercial model across three configurations with varying reference-ontology exposure.

Result: ASSERT makes document-infrastructure inconsistencies measurable. The evaluation across three configurations reveals a fundamental trade-off: greater capacity to discover undocumented entities comes at the cost of reduced schema enforcement, and vice versa.

Conclusion: ASSERT provides a structured, auditable path for migrating legacy IT security documentation into OSCAL-compliant artifacts, filling a critical gap between document-based and machine-readable compliance regimes under NIS-2. The identified trade-off between entity discovery and schema conformance highlights an inherent tension in automated security concept verification.

Abstract: The NIS-2 Directive increases the need for continuous, auditable compliance evidence and motivates a shift from document-based compliance toward machine-readable compliance artifacts. The Open Security Controls Assessment Language (OSCAL) is a standard for this purpose, which the German Federal Office for Information Security (BSI) is adapting with Grundschutz++. However, companies are still managing extensive legacy IT security concepts (IT-SCs), and migrating them without verification could transfer outdated assets into the new format. While existing research primarily addresses the generation of new concepts, there is a lack of a verification framework that extracts legacy IT-SCs into an auditable intermediate representation, deterministically compares the extracted graph with an independently constructed reference state, and exports schema-valid OSCAL artifacts.
  This paper introduces the Automated Security Concept Structure Extraction and Reverse Topology-checking (ASSERT) Framework, which addresses this gap by using ontology-based extraction of legacy documents into formal document graphs, a five-class graph difference against a verified reference graph, and the export into schema-valid OSCAL outputs for system description and assessment evidence. Using the BSI's RecPlast dataset, we compare a local open-weight model and a commercial model across three configurations with different levels of reference-ontology exposure. The evaluation shows that ASSERT makes document-infrastructure inconsistencies measurable, but reveals a trade-off between discovering undocumented entities and enforcing a schema.

</details>


### [66] [Secure QR Codes: Authenticity Verification via EdDSA Signatures and CBOR Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08383)
*Wojciech Jonderko,Wojciech Wodo*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proposes evolving secure QR code architectures to combat spoofing attacks, starting with an offline EdDSA-based solution and advancing to a scalable Hybrid Web PKI model that supports real-time key revocation while maintaining backward compatibility with standard QR scanners.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QR codes lack inherent security features, enabling critical spoofing attacks (quishing) on public infrastructure such as self-service parking machines. Existing solutions fail to balance cryptographic integrity, scalability, and real-time key revocation — particularly in massive smart-city IoT deployments.

Method: A two-stage architecture evolution: (1) A fully offline proof-of-concept using EdDSA signatures on Ed25519, CBOR-encoded certificates, and ZLIB compression to fit robust cryptographic integrity within static QR capacity constraints. (2) A scalable Hybrid Web PKI architecture employing standardized JWKS endpoints, a Central Trust Registry, and URL fragments to enable dynamic real-time validation for compliant applications while preserving seamless backward compatibility with native camera apps.

Result: The offline approach demonstrates that EdDSA-based cryptographic integrity is feasible within QR code static capacity limits. The Hybrid Web PKI model further enables immediate key revocation and dynamic validation, overcoming the offline model's scalability constraints.

Conclusion: The dual-mode approach — combining offline integrity guarantees with online dynamic validation — provides a practical, deployable path toward eliminating QR code spoofing across both legacy and compliant ecosystems.

Abstract: QR codes are a ubiquitous part of daily life, widely trusted by millions. However, their lack of inherent security features has given rise to critical attack vectors, such as spoofing (quishing) on public infrastructure like self-service parking machines. To address this, we present a comprehensive evolution of secure QR code architectures. First, we evaluate a fully offline proof-of-concept leveraging EdDSA signatures (instantiated on the Ed25519 curve), CBOR-encoded certificates, and ZLIB compression, demonstrating that robust cryptographic integrity can be achieved within the QR code's strict static capacity.
