However, recognizing the scalability limitations of fully offline models-specifically the inability to perform immediate key revocation in massive smart-city IoT deployments-we subsequently propose a scalable Hybrid Web PKI architecture. This forward-looking model utilizes standardized JWKS endpoints, a Central Trust Registry, and URL fragments to ensure seamless backward compatibility with standard native cameras while providing dynamic, real-time validation for compliant applications. This dual-mode approach offers a practical, deployable path toward eliminating QR spoofing.

</details>


### [67] [Token-Flow Firewall: Semantic Runtime Auditing for Persistent AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08395)
*Puji Wang,Yingchen Zhang,Ruqing Zhang,Jiafeng Guo,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: TokenWall is a runtime semantic firewall for persistent AI agents that intercepts unsafe natural-language token flows before they reach privileged sinks, achieving a 12.5% attack success rate with 97.4% benign pass rate and only 0.69s added latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent AI agents expose a significantly larger attack surface than single-turn chatbots because unsafe content can propagate through persistent state, reusable skills, and tool interactions. Most security-critical interactions travel via natural-language token flows (memory, tool arguments, files, inter-component communication), creating an opportunity for unified interception, but existing defenses rely on sparse auditing or costly remote oversight.

Method: TokenWall operates as a semantic firewall over agent token flows with three components: (1) boundary-aware semantic auditing that constructs structured source-sink audit records across all token flows; (2) lightweight local inspection executed before any privileged operation; and (3) selective escalation of ambiguous high-risk cases to stronger remote arbitration modules, enabling full-coverage pre-execution mediation without blanket remote calls.

Result: On the CIK-Bench benchmark, TokenWall reduces attack success rate to 12.5% while maintaining a 97.4% benign executable pass rate without human confirmation. It introduces only 0.69 seconds of additional latency on benign cases.

Conclusion: Semantic runtime containment via token-flow firewalling achieves a practical security-utility trade-off for persistent AI agents, demonstrating that full-coverage pre-execution mediation with selective escalation is both effective and efficient.

Abstract: Persistent AI agents extend large language models (LLMs) beyond single-turn interaction into long-lived software systems. Unlike traditional chat assistants, unsafe content in these agents can propagate through persistent state, reusable skills, and tool-mediated interactions, creating a substantially larger semantic attack surface. We observe that most security-critical interactions in such agents are transmitted through natural-language token flows, including memory updates, tool arguments, retrieved files, and inter-component communications. This observation enables a new security formulation: unsafe behavior can be intercepted as risky semantic flows before reaching privileged runtime sinks. Based on this insight, we propose TokenWall, a runtime defense framework that acts as a semantic firewall over agent token flows. TokenWall performs boundary-aware semantic auditing over these flows, constructing structured source-sink audit records, applying lightweight local inspection before execution, and selectively escalating ambiguous high-risk cases to stronger arbitration modules. Unlike prior approaches that rely on sparse auditing or remote large-model oversight, TokenWall enables full-coverage pre-execution mediation while reducing remote arbitration and latency. Experiments on CIK-Bench show that TokenWall reduces attack success rate to 12.5% while maintaining a 97.4% benign executable pass rate without human confirmation. TokenWall further introduces only 0.69 seconds of additional latency on benign cases, demonstrating that semantic runtime containment can achieve a practical security-utility trade-off for persistent AI agents.

</details>


### [68] [TRACE: A Two-Channel Robust Attribution Watermark via Complementary Embeddings for LLM-Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08400)
*Zheng Gao,Xiaoyu Li,Xiaoyan Feng,Jiaojiao Jiang,Yang Song,Yulei Sui,Zhenchang Xing,Liming Zhu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: TRACE is a dual-channel watermark for LLM agent trajectories that survives adversarial deletion (via content-keyed selection) and rewriting (via position-keyed tally), is provably distortion-free in action distribution, and forces resellers who erase both channels to corrupt the trajectories they resell.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are distributed through resellers who may rebrand or substitute models. When provenance is disputed, attribution relies on the trajectory log (the record of tool calls, observations, and actions — not internal reasoning). Existing agent watermarks fail because an adversary has full read/write access to this very evidence. A robust watermark must survive both deletion (which desynchronizes position-derived keys) and rewriting (which alters content), but no single key can serve both purposes simultaneously.

Method: TRACE superposes two watermarks: (1) a **selection channel** that determines which action is chosen, keyed on local content with a distortion-free sampler, making the agent's action distribution provably unchanged and enabling detection to resynchronize after deletions; and (2) a **tally channel** that determines how many records each decision group contains, keyed solely on the log's skeleton (positional structure), which no rewriting can alter. The signal is purchased with decision entropy — each decision pays at least half its entropy, and deterministic decisions contribute nothing. Erasing both channels forces the reseller to corrupt trajectories.

Result: On ToolBench and ALFWorld benchmarks, TRACE matches the unwatermarked agent's success rate. The selection channel achieves detection scores approaching z ≈ 100 on long-horizon trajectories, remains detectable under 70% step deletion, and the tally channel remains exactly unchanged under LLM rewriting of any strength.

Conclusion: TRACE is the first agent watermark that is simultaneously distortion-free in action choices, self-synchronizing under deletion, and unconditionally invariant under rewriting — solving a fundamental tension where no single key suffices by using two complementary channels that together force adversarial resellers to corrupt trajectories if they wish to erase attribution.

Abstract: LLM agents reach users through resellers, who may rebrand a developer's agent or substitute a cheaper model. When provenance is disputed, attribution rests on the trajectory log (the record of tool calls, observations, and executed actions, not the model's reasoning), which the reseller stores and processes to meter usage. A watermark must therefore survive an adversary with full read/write access to the very evidence it is detected from; existing agent watermarks do not, as their attribution is read straight off that log. We present TRACE, to our knowledge the first agent watermark that is distortion-free in its action choices, self-synchronizing under deletion, and unconditionally invariant under rewriting. Deletion desynchronizes a position-derived key and rewriting alters content, so a deletion-robust key must come from content and a rewrite-robust key from position, and no single key serves both. A trajectory, however, has room for two watermarks. TRACE superposes a selection channel that sets which action is chosen, keyed on local content with a distortion-free sampler, so the agent's distribution is provably unchanged and detection resynchronizes after deletions, and a tally channel that sets how many records each decision group holds, keyed on the log's skeleton alone, which no rewriting can touch. We prove this behavioral watermark's signal is bought with decision entropy, each decision paying at least half its entropy and deterministic decisions nothing, and that erasing both channels forces the reseller to corrupt the trajectories it resells. On ToolBench and ALFWorld, TRACE matches the unwatermarked agent's success rate while its selection channel reaches detection scores near z = 100 on long-horizon trajectories, stays detectable under 70% step deletion, and keeps a tally channel exactly unchanged under LLM rewriting of any strength.

</details>


### [69] [TRM-Raft: A Byzantine-Resistant Raft Consensus via Integrated Trust and Reputation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08666)
*Jie Zhang,Xubo Fan,Xiaohong Li,Zhiyong Feng*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: TRM-Raft enhances the standard Raft consensus protocol with Byzantine fault tolerance by integrating a Blockchain-based Trust and Reputation Model (B-TRM). It uses multi-dimensional behavioral scoring, adaptive penalties, and reputation-aware mechanisms for leader election and log replication, achieving resilience against up to 40% Byzantine nodes with minimal performance overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Raft is widely used in Internetware systems (e.g., service registries, blockchains) but only tolerates crash faults, leaving it exposed to Byzantine attacks such as election forgery and log tampering. Existing BFT protocols are too heavyweight, and ad-hoc hardening approaches lack a unified, principled defense.

Method: TRM-Raft non-intrusively embeds a Blockchain-based Trust and Reputation Model (B-TRM) into Raft's consensus core. It quantifies multi-dimensional node behaviors, applies adaptive penalties to distinguish accidental faults from malicious acts, and uses reputation scores in two key mechanisms: (1) reputation-aware leader election that penalizes term/index forgery and excludes low-reputation nodes from leadership; (2) a Schnorr-signature-based log verification mechanism where followers detect tampering, triggering reputation decay and leader replacement. Evaluation is conducted on Hyperledger Fabric in a realistic Internetware setting.

Result: With up to 40% Byzantine nodes, TRM-Raft keeps the malicious leader ratio below 5%. Performance overhead is minimal: less than 10% throughput loss and under 5% latency increase compared to vanilla Raft.

Conclusion: TRM-Raft provides a lightweight, practical path toward trustworthiness for Internetware systems that rely on Raft, offering robust Byzantine resilience without the heavy cost of traditional BFT protocols.

Abstract: Internetware envisions autonomous software entities collaborating over the open Internet. Raft consensus is widely adopted for its simplicity and performance in distributed coordination, e.g., service registries and blockchains. However, Raft assumes crash faults only, making it vulnerable to Byzantine behaviors like election forgery and log tampering. Existing BFT protocols incur high overhead, while ad-hoc hardening lacks unified defense.
  We propose \textbf{TRM-Raft}, a Byzantine-resistant enhancement that non-intrusively integrates a Blockchain-based Trust and Reputation Model (B-TRM) into the consensus core. It quantifies multi-dimensional node behaviors, applies adaptive penalties distinguishing accidental faults from malice, and embeds reputation into leader election and log replication. A reputation-aware election penalizes term/index forgery, excluding low-reputation nodes from leadership. A Schnorr-signature-based mechanism lets followers verify log integrity; tampering triggers reputation decay and leader replacement. Evaluated on Hyperledger Fabric in a realistic Internetware setting, TRM-Raft keeps malicious leader ratio below 5\% even with 40\% Byzantine nodes, with <10\% throughput loss and <5\% latency increase over vanilla Raft. TRM-Raft offers a lightweight, practical trustworthiness path for Internetware systems relying on Raft.

</details>


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [70] [fog: Expressing Motion and Emotion through Function Composition of AI-Generated Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07952)
*Vivian Liu,Lydia Chilton*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Fog is a function composition framework for creating motion and emotion in animations. It enables users to build an open-ended motion vocabulary from verbs, adverbs, gestures, and emotions, complemented by an animation editor. Perceptual evaluation (452 animations) shows 68% recognition accuracy (2.68x over chance), and a user study demonstrates fog supports rapid iteration, exploration, and control for both professionals and novices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motion and emotion are fundamental to intelligent, expressive behavior, yet authoring expressive motion remains challenging. There is a need for a compositional framework that allows users to flexibly define, combine, and refine motion functions — particularly for conveying semantics like verbs, adverbs, gestures, and emotions — without requiring deep technical expertise.

Method: The authors propose fog, a function composition framework for implementing and composing motion functions, applied to Heider-Simmel style animations. It includes a code generation component for producing functions corresponding to verbs, adverbs, gestures, and emotions, forming an extensible motion vocabulary. An accompanying animation editor enables direct manipulation and dynamically generated UI for refinement. Evaluation includes (1) a perceptual study with 452 generated animations testing semantic recognition, and (2) a mixed-methods user study with both professionals and novices assessing the interface's usability.

Result: In the perceptual evaluation, fog-generated motion functions achieved 68% recognition accuracy, a 2.68x improvement over the chance baseline. The mixed-methods user study demonstrated that the fog interface supports more rapid iteration, broader exploration, and greater control for both professional and novice users.

Conclusion: Fog provides an effective function composition approach for authoring semantically meaningful motion and emotion in animations. The combination of code generation and direct manipulation enables an open-ended, recognizable motion vocabulary and facilitates expressive, iterative animation design across skill levels.

Abstract: Motion and emotion are core parts of intelligent, expressive behavior. In this paper, we introduce fog, a function composition framework for implementing and compose motion functions. We demonstrate how fog can be used to express motion and emotion in Heider-Simmel style animations. This code generation framework can help users generate functions for verbs, adverbs, gestures, and emotions to create an open-ended motion vocabulary. It is complemented by an animation editor that helps users refine motion through direct manipulation and dynamically generated UI. We evaluate our approach with a perceptual evaluation, where we test 452 fog-generated animations to see if people can recognize the semantic meaning of the motion. We find that fog's motion functions can be recognized at 68% accuracy, a 2.68x improvement over a chance baseline. In a mixed-methods user study with professionals and novices, we show that fog in interface form can support users with more rapid iteration, exploration, and control.

</details>


### [71] [The Behavioural Reflection Test: A time-efficient measure of reflective reasoning in morally and epistemically charged decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07961)
*Sion Weatherhead,Flora Salim,Aaron Belbasis,Ben R. Newell*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The Behavioural Reflection Test (BRT) is introduced as an open-ended measure of reflective reasoning in morally/epistemically charged scenarios, paired with a bespoke 4-item CRT (bCRT). Among 473 adults, bCRT predicted evidence-sensitive, ethical decisions and linguistically distinct expression — associations a familiarity-adjusted CRT failed to capture — while remaining time-efficient (~12 min).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classic Cognitive Reflection Test (CRT) suffers from widespread item exposure, reducing its validity. The authors sought a measure that: (1) is less susceptible to prior exposure, (2) captures reflective tendencies in ecologically richer, morally and epistemically charged contexts, and (3) reveals how such tendencies manifest in decision style and linguistic expression.

Method: The Behavioural Reflection Test (BRT) was developed as an open-ended measure presenting morally and epistemically charged scenarios. A 4-item bespoke CRT (bCRT) served as a low-exposure anchor for convergent validity. 473 online adults completed both measures along with decision-making tasks and linguistic analysis. The bCRT was compared against a familiarity-adjusted standard CRT to test whether exposure-driven attenuation was recovered.

Result: Higher bCRT scores predicted: (1) more evidence-sensitive, ethically driven decisions; (2) greater reliance on high-quality information sources; and (3) linguistic patterns marked by emotional engagement, risk-attentiveness, and economical expression. The familiarity-adjusted CRT did not recover these associations. The bCRT demonstrated convergent validity and contributed item information beyond mean ability. Despite being open-ended, the BRT remained time-efficient (median 11.8 minutes).

Conclusion: The BRT offers a valid, exposure-resistant, time-efficient behavioural assay of reflective reasoning that extends beyond abstract problem-solving into morally and epistemically rich domains, capturing decision-style and linguistic correlates that the standard CRT — even familiarity-adjusted — misses.

Abstract: How readily people override intuitive conclusions through reflection shapes how they navigate dense information environments with reliable and misleading sources; yet the effectiveness of a prominent measure, the Cognitive Reflection Test (CRT), is eroded by widespread exposure to classic items and leaves open how such tendencies manifest more generally in decision style and linguistic expression. The Behavioural Reflection Test (BRT) addresses these issues with a brief open-ended measure of reasoning in morally and epistemically charged scenarios, alongside a four-item bespoke CRT (bCRT) as a low-exposure anchor. Among 473 online adults, higher bCRT predicted more evidence-sensitive, ethically driven decisions and reliance on high-quality sources, marked by more emotionally engaged, risk-attentive, economical language; associations the familiarity-adjusted CRT did not recover. The bCRT showed convergent validity, added item information above mean ability. Though open-ended, the BRT remained a time-efficient (median 11.8 minutes) behavioural assay of reflection with scope to extend across domains.

</details>


### [72] [HeadRoom: Lightweight, Edge-deployable Pipeline for Adaptive Notification Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08083)
*Dinithi Dissanayake,Prasanth Sasikumar,Suranga Nanayakkara*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: HeadRoom is an edge-deployable pipeline that uses egocentric video and audio to estimate visual/auditory channel availability in real time, enabling adaptive notification routing that reduces response time under high perceptual load.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emerging wearables (e.g., smart glasses) can deliver notifications through multiple sensory channels, but there is limited understanding of how to select the appropriate channel dynamically based on the user's current perceptual state.

Method: The authors propose HeadRoom, a lightweight, edge-deployable pipeline that estimates real-time availability of visual and auditory channels from egocentric video and audio. A controlled user study with 25 participants was conducted to evaluate channel-aware notification routing under varying perceptual loads.

Result: Under high perceptual load, routing notifications to the more available sensory channel (as estimated by HeadRoom) significantly reduced response time compared to routing them to the less available channel.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates the feasibility and benefit of real-time, sensor-driven adaptive notification routing in wearable and immersive systems, opening new directions for context-aware multimodal interaction design.

Abstract: Emerging wearables, such as smart glasses, can deliver notifications through multiple sensory channels, but there is still a limited understanding of how to choose the right channel at the right moment. We propose HeadRoom, a lightweight, edge-deployable pipeline that estimates the availability of visual and auditory channels in real time from egocentric video and audio. Our controlled user study (N=25) shows that, under high perceptual load, routing notifications to the more available channel reduces response time relative to routing them to the less available channel. This work opens up a new possibility for adaptive routing of notifications in wearable and immersive systems.

</details>


### [73] [How Analysts Use AI in High-Stakes Crime Linkage: An Industrial Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08274)
*Jessica Woodhams,Amy Burrell,Wanyin Li,Fahim Ahmed,Matthew Tonkin,Jan Lemeire,Arkady Konovalov,Steven Frisson,Mark Webb,Sarah Galambos,Vesna Nowack,Dalal Alrajeh*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: An industrial evaluation of an AI-enabled crime linkage tool co-developed with UK law enforcement reveals that analysts use AI predictions selectively, cross-validate them against traditional behavioural evidence, and value model explanations but desire better integration into their workflow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crime linkage analysis — identifying offence series committed by the same offender — is time-consuming, cognitively demanding, and exposes analysts to disturbing material. AI tools could alleviate this burden, but their practical usability and trustworthiness in real investigative contexts remain underexplored.

Method: A mixed-methods usability study was conducted within a UK law enforcement agency, combining direct observation, eye-tracking, mouse-tracking, and surveys to assess how analysts interact with AI predictions and model feature explanations in an operational setting.

Result: Analysts used AI predictions selectively, routinely cross-validating them against non-AI behavioural evidence, indicating partial trust. They attended to and valued the presented model features, but identified opportunities for improving explanation presentation and workflow integration.

Conclusion: AI-enabled decision-support tools in high-stakes domains must better integrate explanations with traditional analytical methods. In-situ evaluation is critical for engineering usable, trustworthy AI systems that align with practitioners' established workflows and professional judgment.

Abstract: Crime linkage analysis is used in many countries to identify series of offences that may have been committed by the same individual. In practice, specialist analysts manually search for behavioural and situational connections across large crime databases, an effort that is time-consuming, cognitively demanding, and can involve repeated exposure to disturbing material. To support this work, an Artificial Intelligence (AI)-enabled decision-support tool was co-developed with a UK law enforcement agency to assist analysts in identifying likely crime linkages.
  This paper reports an industrial evaluation of the crime-linkage tool. We conducted a mixed-methods usability study combining direct observation, eye-tracking, mouse-tracking, and surveys to examine how analysts engage with AI predictions and with the model features presented as explanations. Our findings show that analysts used the AI predictions selectively and frequently validated them against behavioural (non-AI) evidence, reflecting partial trust and an ongoing reliance on established analytical practices. We also found that analysts attended to the presented model features and valued their availability, while identifying opportunities to improve how explanations are presented and integrated into the workflow. Overall, our results highlight the need for AI-enabled decision-support tools to better integrate explanations and traditional analytical methods, and demonstrate the importance of in-situ evaluation for engineering usable and trustworthy AI in high-stakes settings.

</details>


### [74] [ImputeViz: A Visual Analytics Dashboard for Diagnosing Missing Data and Comparing Imputation Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08579)
*Aitik Dandapat,Lalith Punepalle Raveendrareddy,Mithilesh Kumar Singh,Klaus Mueller*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: ImputeViz is a visual analytics dashboard that integrates multiple imputation methods (MICE, Random Forest, XGBoost, kNN, and a novel geographically-informed gKNN) into a coordinated, interactive environment for diagnosing missingness patterns, configuring models, and comparing imputation results across methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Missing data biases analyses across scientific and public health research, yet analysts lack integrated tools that make missingness patterns explicit, support cross-method comparison as a first-class task, and provide provenance-based accountability—particularly for geospatial contexts where regional donor contributions matter.

Method: ImputeViz provides coordinated views including missingness heatmaps, co-missingness summaries, and distributional diagnostics for assessing MCAR/MAR/MNAR patterns. It integrates MICE, Random Forest, XGBoost, and kNN alongside a novel gKNN variant that blends socioeconomic and spatial distances with exposed donor contributions. The system features a Method Comparison Summary table (MAE, RMSE, Delta RMSE, runtime), distributional overlays, variable-level discrepancy views, cached per-method results, and locked axis scales for consistent method switching.

Result: Case studies demonstrate that ImputeViz enables analysts to surface sensitive variables, identify where methods disagree, select effective imputation strategies, and assess model robustness. The gKNN variant provides provenance transparency by showing which regions drive each estimate.

Conclusion: ImputeViz advances imputation practice by making cross-method comparison a visual, interactive, and accountable process—bridging standard imputation techniques with geospatial awareness and provenance tracking in a single, method-agnostic environment.

Abstract: Missing data is a persistent obstacle in scientific, social science, and public health research, often biasing analyses and placing accountability on analysts for how they handle missing values. We introduce ImputeViz, an integrated visual analytics dashboard that supports diagnosing missingness, configuring imputation models, and evaluating results. The system brings together widely used methods, including MICE, Random Forest, XGBoost, and kNN, within an interactive environment that makes missingness patterns explicit. To support geospatial reasoning, we introduce gKNN, a geographically informed kNN variant that blends socioeconomic and spatial distances and exposes donor contributions, enabling provenance-based visual accountability by showing which regions drive each estimate. Our primary contribution is a method-agnostic visual analytics environment that makes cross-method comparison a first-class visual task and integrates gKNN alongside standard methods. Coordinated views reveal missingness structure through heatmaps, co-missingness summaries, and distributional diagnostics that help analysts reason about missingness patterns (MCAR/MAR) and cases where missingness may be non-random (MNAR). Users can compare and tune models and interrogate results via distributional overlays, a Method Comparison Summary reporting MAE, RMSE, Delta RMSE, and runtime for each algorithm on the current target and mask, along with variable-level discrepancy views. Cached per-method results and locked axis scales reduce cognitive overhead from shifting ranges during method switching. These comparisons highlight where methods disagree, which variables are sensitive, and how imputation choices affect downstream summaries. Case studies demonstrate how ImputeViz helps analysts select effective strategies, surface sensitive variables, and assess model robustness.

</details>


### [75] [How YouTube Frames ChatGPT Use in Education: An Epistemic Network Analysis with Supporting Multimodal Metadata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08698)
*Shayla Sharmin,Mohammad Al-Ratrout,Mohammad Fahim Abrar,Roghayeh Leila Barmaki*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Analysis of 52 educational YouTube videos reveals three distinct discourse groups framing ChatGPT: as a thinking scaffold (G1), a retrieval/skill-building tool (G2), and an output generator (G3). Epistemic Network Analysis confirmed significant differences across multimodal metadata. Output-oriented content achieved reach comparable to learning-oriented content despite lower pedagogical depth, revealing a structural tension between visibility and educational quality in informal AI learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Little is known about how large language models like ChatGPT are framed in informal, creator-driven educational discourse on platforms like YouTube. Understanding these framings is critical because they shape public AI literacy, learner expectations, and engagement patterns at scale.

Method: Following PRISMA guidelines, 52 educational YouTube videos were systematically selected. The authors conducted a multimodal analysis encompassing transcripts, titles, thumbnails, and viewer comments. Epistemic Network Analysis (ENA) was used to quantitatively model discourse structures and test for group differences, with effect sizes reported. Three distinct creator groups (G1, G2, G3) were identified based on how they framed ChatGPT.

Result: ENA revealed statistically significant group differences with large effect sizes across the three groups. Multimodal metadata (transcripts, titles, thumbnails) consistently reflected these discourse distinctions. Viewers of learning-oriented videos (G1, G2) described ChatGPT as a thinking partner/tutor; viewers of output-oriented videos (G3) expressed concerns about over-reliance, surface learning, and cognitive offloading. Notably, G3 achieved platform reach comparable to G2 despite substantially weaker learning-oriented framing.

Conclusion: A structural tension exists in self-directed AI learning on social media: content prioritizing quick outputs competes effectively for visibility against pedagogically deeper content. This raises critical concerns about whose vision of AI literacy scales and what substantive learning outcomes learners are ultimately left with.

Abstract: We examine educational YouTube videos through multimodal metadata, such as transcripts, titles, thumbnails, and viewer comments, to investigate how ChatGPT is framed across creator groups and how those framings relate to audience response and platform reach. Little is known about how large language models are presented to learners in informal, creator-driven public discourse. Following PRISMA, we selected 52 videos for analysis. We identified three structurally distinct discourse groups: (G1) videos that positioned ChatGPT as a conceptual scaffold for thinking, (G2) videos oriented toward retrieval practice and skill-building, and (G3) videos that framed ChatGPT as a tool for output generation. Epistemic Network Analysis revealed statistically significant group differences with large effect sizes. Multimodal metadata consistently reflected these distinctions across transcript discourse, titles, and thumbnails. Viewers of learning-oriented content described ChatGPT as a thinking partner or tutor, whereas viewers of output-oriented content raised concerns about over-reliance, surface-level learning, and cognitive offloading. G3 achieved comparable platform reach to G2, yet with substantially weaker learning-oriented framing. This may suggest that output-oriented content competes for visibility despite lower pedagogical depth. These findings reveal a structural tension in self-directed AI learning: content that prioritizes quick outputs reaches far more learners than content that promotes deep engagement. This gap raises critical questions about whose vision of AI literacy scales and what learners are actually left with.

</details>


### [76] [Sculptable Mesh Structures for Room-Scale Form-Finding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08736)
*Jesse T. Gonzalez,Yanzhen Zhang,Dian Zhu,Alice Yu,Sapna Tayal,Nazm Furniturewala,Ziying Qi,Somin Ella Moon,Leyi Han,Alexandra Ion,Scott E. Hudson*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A room-scale, shape-aware mesh structure with resistive length sensors enables physical, hands-on low-fidelity prototyping that digitally captures geometry for seamless transition into CAD workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical prototyping with materials like paper, clay, and foam is essential for capturing real-world form interaction, but it is time-consuming (especially at large scales) and difficult to translate back into CAD environments for further refinement. The gap between physical model-making and digital design pipelines needs bridging.

Method: A user-adjustable, room-scale mesh structure where edges are equipped with resistive length sensors. Users physically lengthen or shorten edges to sculpt coarse forms by altering curvature. The sensors transmit edge-length configurations to a central computer, enabling digital reconstruction of the physical form in software.

Result: The system successfully captures physically sculpted mesh geometry in real time and reproduces it digitally, connecting low-fidelity physical prototyping directly to the computational design pipeline.

Conclusion: The proposed shape-aware mesh structure effectively bridges the gap between tangible, iterative physical prototyping and digital CAD refinement, reducing the time and translation effort typically required in the model-making process.

Abstract: It can be hard to design a physical structure entirely within the confines of a computer monitor. To better capture the interplay between real-world objects and a designer's work-in-progress, practitioners will often go through a sequence of low-fidelity prototypes (paper, clay, foam) before arriving at a form that satisfies both functional and aesthetic concerns. While necessary, this model-making process can be quite time-consuming, particularly at larger scales, and the resulting geometry can be difficult to translate into a CAD environment, where it will be further refined.
  This paper introduces a user-adjustable, room-scale, "shape-aware" mesh structure for low-fidelity prototyping. A user physically manipulates the mesh by lengthening and shortening the edges, altering the overall curvature and sculpting coarse forms. The edges are equipped with resistive length sensors, and transmit their configuration to a central computer. The structure can later be reproduced in software, connecting this prototyping stage to the larger computational design pipeline.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [77] [BACH: A Bayesian Admixture of Contrastive Heads for Multi-Interest Two-Tower Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08107)
*Quoc Phong Nguyen,Paul Albert,Long Vuong,Vuong Le,Julien Monteil*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: BACH introduces a Bayesian multi-interest retrieval model that uses soft mixture weights over contrastive heads, trained via variational inference to mitigate routing collapse and provide per-user interest importance estimates, outperforming hard-routing baselines on three large-scale benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-tower retrievers collapse diverse user interests into a single embedding, while existing multi-interest models suffer from routing collapse (under-utilization of heads) during hard-routing training and lack per-user estimates of how much each interest matters at serving time.

Method: BACH frames multi-interest two-tower retrieval as a per-user mixture distribution over contrastive heads. It applies variational inference to learn soft assignments, producing a posterior over heads for each user. This soft mixture trains every head (mitigating collapse) and yields reusable per-user interest weights. A shared global-codebook variant with precomputable retrieval is also introduced. Training uses a "best-head" scoring objective consistent with serving-time retrieval.

Result: On MovieLens-20M, Taobao, and Netflix benchmarks, BACH outperforms hard-routing multi-interest and single-vector baselines at every head count. Scoring candidates by their best head (serving-consistent) surpasses target-routed training, and BACH yields further gains.

Conclusion: Soft mixture modeling via Bayesian inference is an effective solution to routing collapse in multi-interest retrieval, with the added benefit of interpretable per-user interest weightings and a precomputable codebook variant for efficient serving.

Abstract: Two-tower retrievers compress each user into a single embedding, limiting their ability to serve diverse interests. Multi-interest models give each user several heads scored by a maximum inner product, but their hard-routing training under-utilizes heads (routing collapse) and gives no per-user estimate of how much each interest matters for serving. We present \textbf{BACH} (\emph{Bayesian Admixture of Contrastive Heads}), which casts multi-interest two-tower retrieval as a per-user mixture over the heads, fit by variational inference. The soft mixture trains every head (mitigating collapse), produces a per-user weighting of the interests that is reused at serving, and admits a shared global-codebook variant with precomputable retrieval. On three large-scale benchmarks, MovieLens-20M, Taobao, and Netflix, BACH improves top-of-ranking retrieval over hard-routing multi-interest and single-vector baselines at every head count; we further find that scoring every candidate by its best head, consistent with serving, outperforms the usual target-routed training, and that BACH improves further still.

</details>


### [78] [DaV-Gen: End-to-End Generative Retrieval via Draft-and-Verify](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08365)
*Meng Zhao,Chunmei Liu,Qinyong Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: DaV-Gen proposes a unified "Draft-and-Verify" framework for search and recommendation that combines efficient vector-based candidate drafting with generative verification, replacing traditional multi-stage cascade architectures to reduce error propagation while maintaining serving efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-Stage Cascade Architectures (MCAs) suffer from inconsistent optimization objectives across stages, causing early-stage errors to propagate and amplify. End-to-end generative models unify the pipeline but are too slow for online serving due to auto-regressive decoding. A solution is needed that achieves both unified optimization and efficient inference.

Method: DaV-Gen introduces a "Draft-and-Verify" mechanism inspired by speculative decoding. A single model is jointly trained with a composite loss: (1) contrastive loss for structuring the embedding space to enable efficient vector-based drafting, and (2) fusion loss combining generative likelihood with vector similarity for high-quality verification scoring. At inference, it first drafts candidates via efficient vector search, then verifies them using the fused scoring function.

Result: (The abstract does not report specific quantitative results.)

Conclusion: DaV-Gen achieves both the speed of sparse drafting and the precision of generative models within a unified end-to-end architecture, fundamentally refactoring the search/recommendation paradigm.

Abstract: Mainstream industrial information retrieval systems (e.g., search and recommendation) are usually built upon Multi-Stage Cascade Architectures (MCAs), which balance effectiveness and efficiency through a coarse-to-fine ``retrieval-ranking'' pipeline. However, the optimization objectives across different stages are substantially inconsistent, propagating or even amplifying the early-stage errors that ultimately degrade the quality of final results. While emerging end-to-end generative models offer a potential solution by unifying the pipeline, their online serving performance is severely hindered by the auto-regressive process inherited from the standard decoder-only structure. To bridge this gap, we introduce \textbf{DaV-Gen}, a novel unified solution designed to fundamentally refactor the paradigm for both search and recommendation via a ``Draft-and-Verify'' mechanism. Inspired by the process used by speculative decoding, our framework redesigns the generation task into two synergistic operations within a single model. During training, the model is concurrently optimized for both candidate drafting and fine-grained verification. This is achieved by a composite loss function that jointly trains the model on two distinct but related objectives: 1) a contrastive loss that structures the embedding space for efficient drafting, and 2) a fusion loss that combines generative likelihood with vector similarity to produce a superior verification score. This integrated training strategy equips the model with dual capabilities. At inference time, it first performs highly efficient vector-based drafting to generate a candidate set, and then verifies these candidates using the more powerful fused scoring function, thereby achieving both the speed of sparse drafting and the precision of advanced generative models within a unified, end-to-end architecture.

</details>


### [79] [H3D: Benchmarking Unsupervised Text Hashing for Fine-Grained Document Deduplication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08382)
*Qianren Mao,Jiaxun Lyu,Junnan Liu,Zhijun Chen,Jingzheng Li,Hanwen Hao,Bo Li*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: H3D is a benchmark for unsupervised text hashing on fine-grained scientific document deduplication, comparing non-learning methods (MinHash, SimHash, etc.) with frozen-embedding-based semantic hashing. It evaluates ranking quality, efficiency, and robustness across two complementary datasets, revealing a trade-off between lexical methods (strong on near-duplicates) and semantic methods (better under content rewriting).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hashing pipeline comparisons lack a unified protocol for fine-grained scientific documents, making it difficult to assess trade-offs between different hashing approaches for document deduplication in scientific and biomedical domains.

Method: H3D benchmarks unsupervised non-learning hashing methods (MinHash, SimHash, Winnowing, FuzzyHash, FlyHash) alongside two semantic-sensitive methods (BGE-BIHash, BGE-LSHash) built from frozen BGE embeddings with different quantization strategies. Evaluation uses CSFCube (facet-level scientific similarity) and RELISH (biomedical similarity search) under metrics including MAP, NDCG@20, efficiency, and robustness to text compression.

Result: Lexical/structural fingerprints are competitive for near-duplicate matching, while semantic-sensitive representations better preserve similarity under content rewriting but incur higher computational cost. The benchmark also identifies conditions where different similarity measures become rank-equivalent for specific hash representations.

Conclusion: There exists a consistent trade-off between lexical-structural and semantic hashing approaches. The choice of method should depend on the deduplication scenario: near-duplicate detection favors efficient lexical methods, while paraphrase-tolerant matching benefits from semantic methods. H3D improves reproducibility and interpretability of hashing comparisons.

Abstract: Document hashing provides compact representations for efficient similarity search and document deduplication, but existing studies rarely compare hashing pipelines under a unified protocol for fine-grained scientific documents. H3D is an unsupervised text hashing benchmark for fine-grained document deduplication. It evaluates representative unsupervised non-learning hashing approaches (MinHash, SimHash, Winnowing, FuzzyHash, FlyHash) together with semantic-sensitive methods built from frozen BGE embeddings and two quantization strategies (BGE-BIHash and BGE-LSHash). The non-learning methods generate hash fingerprints through manually designed mathematical rules without training or labeled similarity pairs, which distinguishes them from neural semantic hashing models. We benchmark all methods on CSFCube and RELISH, two datasets that provide complementary evaluation settings: facet-level analysis for scientific-document similarity and larger-scale split-level evaluation for biomedical similarity search. H3D jointly reports ranking quality (MAP, NDCG@20), efficiency, and robustness under controlled text compression. The results show a consistent trade-off: lexical and structural fingerprints are competitive for near-duplicate matching, while semantic-sensitive representations better preserve similarity under content rewriting, at higher computational cost. We further analyze when different similarity measures become rank-equivalent for specific hash representations, improving the interpretability and reproducibility of method comparisons.

</details>


### [80] [Conversational Retrieval and On-the-Fly Knowledge Modeling of Historical Penitentiary Repression Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08459)
*Paula Font Solà,Adrià Molina Rodríguez,Josep Lladós*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper presents a document analysis system for historical digital libraries that combines RAG with an evolving graph-based knowledge model, enabling complex queries across documents by incorporating both retrieved content and dynamically stored expert knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG-based conversational access to digital libraries excels at extractive tasks on individual records but fails to interpret document collections holistically or incorporate expert knowledge dynamically. There is a need for systems that can handle long-term dependencies across documents, link discovery, and integration of curator expertise not explicitly present in source materials.

Method: The authors propose a document analysis system featuring on-the-fly knowledge modeling with a graph-based storage structure. Facts produced by expert archivists or derived from document retrieval are stored in this graph, which serves as an external memory for a language model. The system retrieves from both primary documents and previously modeled knowledge, enabling iterative enrichment through continuous professional interaction.

Result: The graph-based index acting as memory allows the system to handle increasingly complex queries involving long-term dependencies, link discovery, and expert knowledge integration. This yields richer and more comprehensive information generation compared to standard RAG approaches limited to individual record extraction.

Conclusion: Augmenting RAG with a dynamic, graph-based knowledge model that accumulates both expert and retrieval-derived facts significantly enhances document analysis capabilities in historical digital libraries, enabling holistic interpretation and complex cross-document reasoning.

Abstract: Recent developments in digital libraries increasingly favor conversational and natural language access to information through Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). Although these approaches are effective for extractive tasks grounded in individual records, they remain limited in their ability to interpret document collections holistically and to incorporate expert knowledge dynamically. In this article, we present a document analysis system designed for the management of historical digital libraries that supports on-the-fly knowledge modeling. The system is equipped with the capability to store facts produced either by expert archivists or derived from document retrieval processes within a graph-based structure. Through continuous professional interaction, the system can retrieve information not only from primary sources such as documents, but also from previously modeled knowledge, with the graph-based index acting as a memory for the language model to access. This enables increasingly complex queries involving long-term dependencies across documents, link discovery, and the integration of expert knowledge that may not be explicitly present in the original sources. As a result, the proposed approach facilitates the generation of richer and more comprehensive information.

</details>


### [81] [Log-Insight: Automating Microservice Incident Diagnosis via Neuro-Symbolic Log Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08529)
*Carlos Garcia-Hernandez,Aymane Abdali,Guangyu Wu,Mingxue Wang,Fei Shen,Zhaoyu Pang,Yanbin Zhang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: Log-Insight is a production-deployed incident diagnosis system at Huawei that compresses millions of log lines into a compact evidence dossier for LLM-based root cause analysis, achieving 0.790 MRR with top-3 accuracy over 90% within one minute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production microservice incidents generate massive log volumes (2M+ lines, 1.2B characters in 30 minutes) that far exceed LLM context windows. Template parsers lack semantic reasoning, deep-learning detectors produce opaque binary outputs, and naive LLM pipelines suffer context overflow and domain hallucination on raw telemetry.

Method: A six-stage symbolic pipeline that automates SRE triage workflow: sampling, schema understanding, pattern clustering, and statistical anomaly ranking. This reduces raw events by 1,000–7,000× into a compact, pre-ranked evidence dossier, which an LLM then synthesizes into a diagnostic hypothesis report.

Result: Evaluated on 11 historical production incidents across 110 runs with SRE-validated ground truth: MRR = 0.790, correct root cause returned in top-3 hypotheses in over 90% of runs, with sub-minute latency. Operators highlighted the Forensic Evidence section (exact log templates and skew statistics) as critical for adoption.

Conclusion: Log-Insight demonstrates that combining symbolic pre-processing with LLM synthesis effectively bridges the gap between massive telemetry volumes and context-limited LLMs. Its forensic transparency transforms the system from an opaque oracle into an investigative assistant, with documented failure modes and open research directions.

Abstract: Diagnosing production incidents in large-scale microservice systems is time-critical for Site Reliability Engineers (SREs). A single 30-minute incident window in our deployment can generate over two million log lines--approximately 1.2 billion characters, far exceeding standard LLM context windows--making direct LLM-based Root Cause Analysis (RCA) infeasible. Existing approaches leave gaps: template-based parsers lack semantic anomaly reasoning, deep-learning detectors emit black-box binary signals, and LLM pipelines suffer context overflow and domain hallucination on raw telemetry.
  We present Log-Insight, an automated incident-diagnosis system deployed in production at Huawei. The core design principle automates the SRE's manual triage workflow: symbolic stages replicate the structured investigation a skilled SRE would perform--sampling, schema understanding, pattern clustering, and statistical anomaly ranking. This hands the LLM a compact, pre-ranked evidence dossier to synthesise into a hypothesis report. Our six-stage pipeline reduces millions of raw events by 1,000-7,000x while preserving statistically significant failure signals.
  Evaluated on 11 historical production incidents (110 runs, SRE-validated ground truth), Log-Insight achieves MRR = 0.790, returning the correct root cause within the top-3 hypotheses in over 90% of runs in under a minute of latency. We report systematic failure modes, active mitigations, and open research directions. The Forensic Evidence section--listing exact log templates and skew statistics--was consistently identified by operators as a key adoption factor, shifting the system's perceived role from opaque oracle to investigative assistant.

</details>


### [82] [Improving Ad-hoc Search Effectiveness for Conversational Information Retrieval via Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08540)
*Ahmed Rayane Kebir,Jose G. Moreno,Lynda Tamine*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper proposes model merging as a training-free approach to create a single retrieval model that performs well on both ad-hoc search and conversational retrieval, avoiding the costly fine-tuning and catastrophic forgetting issues of traditional methods. Experiments with Model Soup and Slerp show up to 15% NDCG@3 improvement under zero-shot settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational IR is challenging due to topic shifts and coreference resolution across conversation turns. Existing approaches rely on fine-tuning or multi-tasking, which are costly (require retraining) and suffer from catastrophic forgetting—where the model loses its foundational ad-hoc retrieval performance. The authors aim to fill the gap with a training-free solution.

Method: The authors introduce model merging as a training-free strategy, combining parameters of separately trained ad-hoc and conversational retrieval models. They experiment with two merging strategies: Model Soup (linear interpolation) and Slerp (spherical linear interpolation), applied in a parameter-wise manner. No additional fine-tuning is required. Evaluation is conducted on standard ad-hoc search and conversational retrieval datasets under zero-shot conditions.

Result: Model merging significantly enhances the ad-hoc search capabilities of conversational retrievers while improving generalizability across task-specific datasets. The approach achieves up to 15% higher NDCG@3 under zero-shot conditions.

Conclusion: Model merging is an effective, training-free alternative to fine-tuning or multi-tasking for building unified retrieval models that handle both ad-hoc and conversational search, mitigating catastrophic forgetting and reducing computational cost while boosting zero-shot performance.

Abstract: Conversational information retrieval is challenging since it requires the consideration of the conversation history which potentially gives rise to topic shifts and coreference resolution across previous turns. To address these challenges, previous work mainly rely on traditional fine-tuning of ad-hoc retrievers on conversational datasets or extrapolates their generalizability through multi-tasking. However, this mainstream approach is costly - since it requires model re-training - and exhibits catastrophic forgetting, where the model loses its foundational ad-hoc retrieval performance. In this paper, we fill this gap by introducing model merging as a training-free strategy enabling the design of a single retrieval model that operates across both ad-hoc and conversational settings with no additional fine-tuning. We conduct experiments using linear and non-linear parameter-wise merging strategies - namely Model Soup and Slerp - on standard ad-hoc search and conversational retrieval datasets. Our results demonstrate that model merging significantly enhances the ad-hoc search capabilities of conversational retrievers while improving generalizability across task-specific datasets, achieving up to 15% higher NDCG@3 under zero-shot conditions.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [83] [REFORGE: A Method for Benchmarking LLMs' Reverse Engineering Capabilities in Decompiled Binary Function Naming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07738)
*Nicolas Koller,Andreas u. Schmidt*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces Reforge, a provenance-tracked pipeline for constructing function-level ground truth in binary analysis benchmarks. It reveals that binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization is the key obstacle to fair LLM evaluation, not model capability, and proposes an uncertainty-aware benchmarking framework using an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-assisted binary analysis benchmarks treat ground-truth construction as a solved problem and report accuracy without disclosing how many functions were reliably evaluable. Claims about LLM capability outpace measurement ability, and the principal undiagnosed obstacle is alignment reliability under compiler optimization, which introduces survivorship bias into performance comparisons.

Method: The authors propose Reforge, a provenance-tracked pipeline that constructs function-level ground truth from C source through compilation, DWARF and syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation. It operationalizes alignment uncertainty as an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification (high/medium/low confidence). A controlled micro-benchmark and a proof-of-concept evaluation with seven LLMs on function naming are conducted.

Result: High-confidence yield drops from 87.2% to 65.9% as optimization levels increase. Unpaired (confidence-unaware) comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay due to survivorship bias. The proof-of-concept with seven LLMs validates the substrate and demonstrates the need for uncertainty-aware benchmarking.

Conclusion: Fair evaluation of LLMs for binary analysis requires provenance-tracked, uncertainty-aware ground-truth construction. Reforge provides this substrate, and the community should adopt confidence-stratified reporting to avoid survivorship bias and enable meaningful cross-model comparisons.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to reverse-engineering tasks, and recent threat-intelligence reporting shows them operating inside live offensive-security workflows. Claims about their capability, however, outpace our ability to measure it. Existing benchmarks for LLM-assisted binary analysis treat the construction of function-level ground truth as a solved pre-processing step and report accuracy without disclosing how many functions were reliably evaluable. We argue that the principal obstacle to fair evaluation is not model capability but the reliability of binary-to-source alignment under compiler optimization. This paper presents Reforge, a provenance-tracked pipeline that constructs function-level ground truth from C source through compilation, DWARF and syntactic extraction, alignment, and decompilation, and that operationalizes alignment uncertainty as an eight-gate confidence funnel with three-tier stratification. On a controlled micro-benchmark, high-confidence yield falls from 87.2% to 65.9% across optimization levels, and unpaired comparisons overstate optimization-induced performance decay through survivorship bias. A proof-of-concept evaluation of seven contemporary LLMs on function naming demonstrates the substrate and motivates uncertainty-aware benchmarking practice

</details>


### [84] [PERFOPT-Bench: Evaluating Coding Agents on Software Performance Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07744)
*Yingyun Cui,Yi Xie,Piaohong Wang,Jiawei Ma,Bo Liu,Liangliang Cao*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PERFOPT-Bench introduces a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on performance optimization tasks — going beyond functional correctness to measure reproducible speedups. Testing 7 agent stacks on 7 long-horizon tasks reveals that optimization success is workload-dependent, no single stack dominates, and raw speedup scores can be misleading due to shortcut exploitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding-agent benchmarks focus narrowly on functional correctness (producing patches that pass tests). However, production software requires measurable performance improvements. Performance optimization is a distinct, multi-step agentic task involving profiling, cross-layer bottleneck diagnosis, safe code editing, and verification that speedups are genuine rather than measurement artifacts. No benchmark adequately evaluates this full loop.

Method: PERFOPT-Bench comprises 7 long-horizon optimization tasks, each providing a correct but deliberately suboptimal codebase with a target performance metric. Evaluation uses three components: (1) hidden correctness tests to ensure optimization does not break functionality, (2) verified-speedup measurement to confirm gains are reproducible, and (3) trajectory-level audit to inspect how agents achieved their results. Seven agent stacks — combining different LLMs and agent frameworks — are benchmarked across all tasks.

Result: Key findings: (1) optimization performance is workload-dependent — no single LLM or agent framework dominates across all tasks. (2) Changing the agent framework can materially alter the same LLM's per-task speedup profile. (3) Raw speedup is an unsafe metric, as some large gains stem from benchmark-specific shortcut exploitation rather than genuine optimization. (4) An exploratory "relay pilot" shows that restarting from an externalized optimization summary can recover additional performance headroom after an initial session stalls.

Conclusion: Performance optimization is a distinct and challenging agentic task that cannot be reduced to model identity alone. Workload-dependence, framework sensitivity, and shortcut exploitation all complicate evaluation, suggesting that benchmarks like PERFOPT-Bench, with multi-faceted scoring and trajectory auditing, are essential for measuring genuine optimization capability in coding agents.

Abstract: Coding-agent benchmarks have largely measured whether agents can produce functionally correct patches, but production software also demands measurable speedups on real execution targets. Performance optimization is a distinct agentic task: agents must profile executions, diagnose cross-layer bottlenecks, edit code without breaking correctness, and verify that gains are reproducible rather than measurement artifacts. We introduce PERFOPT-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this full performance-engineering loop. Each task provides a correct but deliberately suboptimal codebase and asks the agent to improve a target performance metric; scoring requires hidden correctness tests, verified-speedup measurement, and trajectory-level audit. We evaluate 7 agent stacks with different LLMs and agent frameworks on 7 long-horizon optimization tasks. The results show that optimization performance is workload-dependent rather than determined by model identity alone: no single stack dominates, and changing the agent framework can materially change the same LLM's per-task speedup profile. We further find that raw speedup is unsafe as a benchmark score, since some large gains arise from benchmark-specific shortcut exploitation; an exploratory relay pilot suggests that restarting from an externalized optimization summary can recover additional headroom after an initial session stops. The benchmark and our evaluation are available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Dataset-D3CC.

</details>


### [85] [Functional and Secure Code Generation with Task Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07881)
*Felix Wang,Anudeep Das,Mei Nagappan,N. Asokan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SecVecCoder uses task-vector arithmetic on LLM weights to jointly improve code functionality and security without post-generation fixes, achieving up to 36pp gains in trustworthy code generation across six models with negligible decoding overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coding LLMs struggle to generate code that is simultaneously functional and secure. Prior work separates functionality and security evaluation, or addresses vulnerabilities only after generation. Meanwhile, alignment techniques like task-vector arithmetic enable efficient behavior tuning, but have not been applied to the joint functional-correctness and security problem in code generation.

Method: SecVecCoder applies task-vector arithmetic — linear operations on model weights — to create a combined model that integrates both code functionality and security capabilities. This is done by computing task vectors from fine-tuned variants and merging them into the base model, requiring no changes to decoding strategy.

Result: On the CodeGuard+ benchmark, SecVecCoder improves trustworthy code completions by 2.1–36.0 percentage points across six LLMs from three families. Improvements on unseen CWE types reach up to 39.1 percentage points. Decoding latency remains within 0.6% of the base model on average.

Conclusion: Task-vector arithmetic is a lightweight, decoding-agnostic method to jointly enhance code functionality and security in LLMs, substantially outperforming base models while adding negligible latency — making it practical for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for code generation, but they struggle to generate functional code free of security vulnerabilities. Prior work to improve the secure code generation abilities of such coding LLMs has largely focused on evaluating code functionality and security separately using different datasets, or focused on finding vulnerabilities post-generation. At the same time, the text-generation domain has seen significant work on alignment techniques, where models are tuned such that their outputs exhibit certain qualities (e.g., helpfulness, harmlessness). Of particular interest is task-vector arithmetic, where linear operations on LLM weights can be used to arbitrarily enhance alignment while incurring only minimal computational overhead. We develop a novel method, SecVecCoder, leveraging task vectors to produce trustworthy code that is simultaneously functional and secure without the need for post-generation adjustment. Across six coding LLMs from three families on the CodeGuard+ benchmark, SecVecCoder improves the rate of trustworthy code completions by 2.1-36.0 percentage points over the base model, with improvements on unseen CWE types reaching up to 39.1 percentage points. Since the effectiveness of the coding LLM relies only on changing the model weights, SecVecCoder requires no method-specific decoding and hence achieves a decoding latency within 0.6% of the base model's, on average.

</details>


### [86] [Bug Report Specification Refinement with Trajectory Guidance for Automated Program Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07882)
*S M Farah Al Fahim,Md Nakhla Rafi,Md Ahasanuzzaman,Zeyang Ma,Dong Jae Kim,Shaowei Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TrajSpec is a trajectory-guided approach that refines bug reports for repository-level automated program repair by collecting repository evidence, organizing it into a three-level representation, and applying repository-based review. Refined reports improve Pass@1 across multiple repair agents (e.g., from 41% to 59.67% with GPT-5-mini on SWE-Bench Lite), and ablations confirm the importance of both the hierarchical representation and the review step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bug reports for repository-level APR agents typically describe only the observed failure and lack repair-relevant details such as failure-inducing behavior, behavioral requirements, and implementation scope. This causes agents to inspect irrelevant code, infer incorrect requirements, or produce superficial patches that address symptoms without restoring intended behavior.

Method: TrajSpec operates in several stages: (1) A trajectory-collection agent runs on the original report and pre-fix repository to produce an unverified trajectory. (2) TrajSpec extracts trajectory-derived specification evidence and organizes it into a three-level representation: a high-level issue interpretation, diagnostic findings supporting that interpretation, and concrete repository observations. (3) It generates a draft refined report. (4) A repository-based review step removes unsupported claims, revises uncertain claims, and adds repository-supported details to produce the final refined specification.

Result: On all 300 SWE-Bench Lite instances, TrajSpec-refined reports improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 59.67% with GPT-5-mini and from 54.67% to 64.33% with MiniMax M2.5 (using Mini-SWE-Agent V2). On a stratified 100-instance sample, Pass@1 improves from 41.00% to 71.00% with Agentless and from 47.00% to 72.00% with AutoCodeRover. Ablation: removing repository-based review drops Pass@1 from 59.67% to 48.00%; removing the hierarchical evidence representation drops it to 47.67%.

Conclusion: TrajSpec demonstrates that trajectory-guided, repository-supported bug report refinement provides actionable context that consistently and substantially improves repair performance across multiple APR agents. The three-level evidence representation and repository-based review are both critical to this improvement.

Abstract: Bug reports serve as task specifications for repository-level automated program repair (APR) agents, but they often describe only the observed failure and omit repair-relevant information such as the failure-inducing behavior, behavioral requirement, and implementation scope. As a result, a repair agent may inspect irrelevant code, infer an incorrect requirement, or generate a patch that addresses the reported symptom without restoring the intended repository behavior. We present TrajSpec, a trajectory-guided approach for repository-supported bug report specification refinement. Given an original report and a pre-fix repository, TrajSpec runs a trajectory-collection agent and uses the resulting unverified trajectory as a source of trajectory-derived specification evidence. It organizes this evidence into a three-level representation consisting of a high-level interpretation of the issue, diagnostic findings supporting that interpretation, and concrete repository observations. TrajSpec then generates a draft refined report and applies repository-based review to remove unsupported claims, revise uncertain claims, and add repository-supported details. We evaluate TrajSpec on all 300 SWE-Bench Lite instances using Mini-SWE-Agent V2. TrajSpec's refined reports improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 59.67% with GPT-5-mini and from 54.67% to 64.33% with MiniMax M2.5. On a stratified sample of 100 instances, TrajSpec's refined reports also improve Pass@1 from 41.00% to 71.00% with Agentless and from 47.00% to 72.00% with AutoCodeRover. Ablation results show that removing repository-based review or the hierarchical evidence representation reduces Pass@1 from 59.67% to 48.00% and 47.67%, respectively. Overall, TrajSpec provides actionable repository-supported context that consistently improves repair performance.

</details>


### [87] [The Memory Wall of Green Software: Empirical Energy Evaluation of Memento Design Pattern](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07944)
*Imane Jriri,Tarik Houichime,Younes El Amrani*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper empirically measures the energy cost of the Memento design pattern, comparing a no-pattern baseline against Classic (full-snapshot) and Differential (delta-encoding) strategies. Using RAPL-based power telemetry on state volumes from 10–200 MB, results show the Differential strategy saves up to 65.8% energy at mid-scale but collapses at 200 MB due to GC thrashing and power spikes, yielding evidence-based heuristics for energy-aware software architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As Green Software Engineering elevates energy efficiency to a first-class non-functional requirement, design patterns — despite their structural benefits — carry a hidden "metabolic cost" from abstraction overhead. The Memento pattern's energy implications, particularly across different implementation strategies and state scales, remain empirically unquantified, leaving architects without data-driven guidance for reconciling design quality with sustainability.

Method: The authors empirically compare three approaches: (1) a direct, unabstracted baseline, (2) the Classic Memento with full-snapshot storage, and (3) a Differential Memento using delta-encoding. Energy dissipation is measured via the RAPL (Running Average Power Limit) hardware interface across state volumes scaling from 10 MB to 200 MB, capturing CPU and DRAM energy with high fidelity.

Result: The Differential strategy reduces memory traffic and achieves up to 65.8% energy savings for mid-scale states. However, at 200 MB, a "memory wall" emerges where GC thrashing and non-linear power spikes completely negate algorithmic optimizations, revealing a sharp discontinuity in the energy–scale relationship.

Conclusion: Architects face a non-trivial trade-off: the Differential Memento excels at moderate scales but fails catastrophically beyond a saturation threshold. The authors distill findings into evidence-based heuristics, providing a framework for selecting Memento implementations that balance structural design integrity with Green IT sustainability mandates.

Abstract: As Green Software Engineering matures, energy efficiency has transitioned into a mission-critical non-functional requirement. While software design patterns ensure structural integrity, their inherent abstraction layers impose an implicit "metabolic cost" that often remains obscured during the design phase. This paper empirically investigates the energy dynamics of the Memento design pattern, contrasting a direct, unabstracted baseline against Classic full-snapshot and Differential delta-encoding strategies. Leveraging the RAPL interface for high-fidelity hardware telemetry, we quantify energy dissipation across state volumes scaling from 10 MB to 200 MB. Our empirical results expose a critical architectural trade-off: the Differential strategy minimizes memory traffic, yielding a maximum energy reduction of 65.8% for mid-scale states, but collides with a catastrophic "memory wall" at 200 MB. At this saturation point, algorithmic optimizations are completely neutralized by severe GC thrashing and non-linear power spikes. We synthesize these findings into evidence-based heuristics, providing architects with a robust framework to reconcile structural design quality with sustainable Green IT imperatives.

</details>


### [88] [DeepSWE: Measuring Frontier Coding Agents on Original, Long-Horizon Engineering Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07946)
*Wenqi Huang,Charley Lee,Leonard Tng,Serena Ge*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: DeepSWE is a new benchmark of 113 original software engineering tasks designed to evaluate coding agents more reliably than existing benchmarks like SWE-bench by using hand-crafted tasks with custom verifiers instead of mined GitHub fixes, avoiding data contamination and brittle test-based grading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks like SWE-bench mine merged fixes from public GitHub, causing two key problems: (1) data contamination — models may have seen fixes during pretraining, making high scores reflect recall rather than genuine problem-solving; and (2) brittle evaluation — tests shipped with merged fixes were designed to validate one specific solution, so they can reject valid alternative implementations or pass incomplete ones.

Method: DeepSWE constructs 113 tasks from scratch across 91 active open-source repositories spanning five programming languages. Tasks are never contributed back upstream, keeping reference solutions out of public training data. Each task is graded by a hand-written verifier that checks whether the requested functionality is provided, accepting any correct implementation rather than enforcing a single approach. An independent LLM judge was used to audit grading reliability by comparing DeepSWE's verifier against SWE-bench Pro's inherited tests.

Result: The LLM judge disagreed with DeepSWE's verifier only 1.4% of the time, versus 32.4% for SWE-bench Pro's tests — an order-of-magnitude improvement. Despite prompts roughly half the length of SWE-bench Pro, reference solutions touch 5.5× more code. The benchmark separates frontier agents across a wider score band than leaderboards where they otherwise cluster.

Conclusion: DeepSWE provides a more reliable, contamination-free, and discriminating evaluation framework for coding agents. The benchmark, verifiers, and full evaluation trajectories are publicly released.

Abstract: DeepSWE is a benchmark of 113 original, long-horizon software engineering tasks for evaluating coding agents. Most public agentic coding benchmarks follow SWE-bench in mining merged fixes from public GitHub repositories, which creates two problems: the fixes and their discussion were likely seen during pretraining, so a high score can reflect recall rather than problem-solving; and each task is graded by the tests that shipped with its merged fix, which were written to confirm one specific fix rather than grade an arbitrary solution, so they can fail a correct alternative or pass an incomplete one. DeepSWE avoids both. Its tasks are written from scratch across 91 active open-source repositories and five languages and are never contributed back upstream, so their reference solutions stay out of the public record that model training scrapes; and each task is graded by a hand-written verifier that checks the requested functionality and accepts any implementation that provides it. When an independent LLM judge re-reviews graded runs, it disagrees with DeepSWE's verifier about an order of magnitude less often than with SWE-Bench Pro's inherited tests (1.4% versus 32.4%). Despite being about half the length of SWE-Bench Pro's prompts, DeepSWE's prompts describe tasks whose reference solutions touch 5.5x more code, and the benchmark separates frontier agents across a wider score band than the leaderboards on which they otherwise cluster. We release the benchmark, its verifiers, and the full record of evaluation trajectories.

</details>


### [89] [3100 Opinions on Code Review in an AI World: Building Causal Theory from Practitioner Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07980)
*Shyam Agarwal,Courtney Miller,Christian Kästner,Bogdan Vasilescu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Coding agents are transforming code review, but surface-level repository mining yields contradictory trends. This paper synthesizes 38,709 grey-literature documents into a causal theory with 26 constructs and 67 relationships, arguing that code review is the control point where a team's expertise and process structure determine whether AI's effect is positive or negative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing repository-mining studies capture surface trends (e.g., merge speed, review frequency) but fail to explain the underlying mechanisms, and the trends themselves flip under different analysis choices. Practitioners hold sharply conflicting views on whether human review remains necessary, whether review becomes a bottleneck, and whether understanding is eroded. The authors seek to move beyond unstable observational data to an explanatory causal theory grounded in practitioner discourse.
