Method: The authors collect 38,709 grey-literature documents (engineering blogs and Reddit threads), filter to those substantively about code review, and code a stratified random sample of 3,100 documents using an LLM-assisted qualitative coding pipeline. From this, they build a causal model comprising 26 constructs and 67 relationships (64 directed, 3 contested). A motivating observational analysis of public GitHub data is also conducted to illustrate the instability of surface-level metrics.

Result: The observational GitHub analysis shows that agent-authored PRs are reviewed less often, merged faster, and discussed less — but these trends reverse under alternative defensible analysis choices. The causal theory's central claim is that code review is the control point mediating AI's effect on software quality, and that the sign of this effect (positive or negative) is determined by team expertise and review process structure, not by AI itself. Twenty-six constructs and 67 relationships are formally specified.

Conclusion: The theory reframes "AI is changing code review" into falsifiable propositions with named constructs and moderators, making competing practitioner positions explicit. As a secondary contribution, the LLM-assisted grey-literature theory-building method is offered as a scalable template for software engineering research, with a public implementation.

Abstract: Coding agents now author entire pull requests, and practitioners sharply disagree about what this does to code review: whether it becomes the bottleneck, whether human review is still necessary, and whether it quietly erodes the understanding that it once built. Repository-mining studies measure surface trends but seldom explain the mechanisms beneath them, and the trends themselves prove unstable. A motivating observational analysis of public GITHUB activity finds that agent-authored pull requests are reviewed less often, merged several times faster, and discussed less than human-authored ones, yet the direction of these trends flips under different but equally defensible analysis choices, so the traces establish what is changing without explaining why. To recover the mechanisms, we synthesize practitioner discourse at scale into an explanatory theory: we collect 38,709 grey-literature documents (engineering blogs and Reddit threads), filter to those substantively about code review, and code a stratified random sample of 3,100 with an LLM-assisted pipeline, from which we build a causal model of 26 constructs and 67 relationships (64 directed, 3 contested). Its organizing claim is that review is the control point through which a coding agent's effect on software is decided, and that AI does not fix the sign of that effect: the team sets it, through the expertise its humans bring and how it structures the review process. The theory makes the competing positions explicit and turns "AI is changing code review" into falsifiable propositions with named constructs and moderators. As a secondary contribution, we offer the underlying LLM-assisted, grey-literature theory-building method as a scalable template for software-engineering research, with a public implementation.

</details>


### [90] [On the Correctness of Software Merge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07987)
*Akira Mori,Masatomo Hashimoto*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new structural merge tool grounded in formal correctness criteria: the merge result must be parsable (syntactically valid) and universal (incorporating all and only the edit operations from each branch, with common edits applied once, formalized via category-theoretic pushouts). In experiments across 43,774 merge scenarios from 76 Java projects, the proposed tool produced no incorrect results, unlike existing tools such as Git's merge tool.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Three-way merge tools are critical to modern collaborative software development (e.g., pull requests), yet their correctness criteria have historically been intuitive rather than formal. Merge results are rarely scrutinized, and existing tools can produce incorrect merges. There is a need for rigorous, syntactic correctness criteria and a merge tool that adheres to them.

Method: The authors propose two syntactic criteria for merge correctness: (1) parsability — the result must be syntactically valid per the language grammar; and (2) universality — the result must include all and only the edit operations from each branch, with edits common to both branches applied only once, precisely defined using the notion of pushouts in category theory. They implement a new structural merge tool based on these criteria and evaluate it in a large-scale experiment on 43,774 file merge scenarios from 76 open-source Java projects, comparing against existing tools like Git's merge tool, d3j, and 2,582 developer-resolved merges, plus 2,459 merge scenarios across 21 refactoring types.

Result: In 43,774 file merge scenarios from 76 Java projects, existing tools (including Git's companion merge tool) produced a number of incorrect results, whereas the authors' structural merge tool reported none. Comparisons with 2,582 developer-resolved merges and 2,459 refactoring-involved merge scenarios (covering 21 refactoring types) highlighted both strengths and current limitations of the structural merge approach.

Conclusion: Clear, formal correctness criteria — parsability and universality (via pushouts) — are essential for reliable merge tools. The proposed structural merge tool demonstrates that adhering to such criteria eliminates incorrect merges observed in existing tools. The criteria provide a foundation for developing more principled and dependable merge tools.

Abstract: Three-way merge tools play crucial roles in modern software development, where a developer forks a branch to make local modifications and requests it to be merged into the main branch via a "pull request." Despite its importance, the task has traditionally been defined in an intuitive manner, and the results of merge tools are often accepted without scrutiny. In this paper, we present a new structural merge tool in comparison with existing tools based on the syntactic criteria we propose for evaluating the merge results. We require the merge result to be both parsable and universal. Being parsable means that the result is syntactically valid according to the grammar of the programming language. Being universal means that the result incorporates all and only the edit operations occurring in each branch while ensuring that edits common to both branches are applied only once. This requirement can be precisely defined using the notion of pushouts in category theory. In a large-scale experiment involving 43,774 file merge scenarios from 76 open-source Java projects, we found a number of incorrect results reported by existing tools such as the Git companion merge tool, whereas our tool reports none. We further compared d3j's results with 2,582 developer-resolved merges and with 2,459 merge scenarios involving 21 refactoring types. These experiments revealed both the strengths and current limitations of structural merge, and underscore the importance of clear correctness criteria. We expect that the proposed criterion will provide a foundation for developing more reliable and principled merge tools.

</details>


### [91] [Aleena: Alignment Agent for Research Software Engineering Collaborations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08043)
*Kshitij Dani,Cordero Core,Landung Setiawan,Carlos Garcia Jurado Suarez,Anshul Tambay,Vani Mandava,Anant Mittal*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Aleena is an open-source agentic AI system that addresses the problem of misalignment in research software engineering by using GitHub as a shared surface to transform multi-modal stakeholder interactions into structured project records, tracking decisions, risks, and open questions across the software lifecycle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research software collaborations generate decisions across disparate artifacts (Slack threads, meetings, pull requests, GitHub issues), causing the original rationale to be lost. This leads domain researchers and research software engineers to develop divergent mental models of project intent, ownership, and scientific assumptions — creating a continuous alignment problem throughout the project lifecycle.

Method: The authors designed Aleena, an agentic AI system grounded in university-based research software engineering center experiences. It uses GitHub as a shared collaboration surface to capture and transform multi-modal stakeholder interactions into structured project records. The system surfaces risks, tracks open questions, and preserves decision continuity without replacing human decision-making.

Result: The paper presents the motivating problem, system design, a working prototype, and illustrative lifecycle scenarios demonstrating how Aleena supports stakeholder alignment and project-state tracking across the research software engineering lifecycle.

Conclusion: Alignment in research software engineering is a continuous lifecycle problem that agentic AI can help address. Aleena demonstrates that AI agents can support — rather than replace — human decision-making by providing structured continuity across fragmented collaboration channels.

Abstract: Research software collaborations span meetings, informal chats, pull requests, and GitHub issues. A decision surfaced in a Slack thread, refined in a meeting, and implemented in a pull request can lose its original rationale across these artifacts, leaving domain researchers and research software engineers with divergent mental models of project intent, ownership, and scientific assumptions. We argue that alignment in research software engineering is a continuous lifecycle problem, and that agentic AI can support stakeholder alignment and project-state tracking without replacing human decision-making. We present Aleena, an open-source lifecycle alignment agent that uses GitHub as a shared collaboration surface, transforming multi-modal stakeholder interactions into structured project records that surface risks, track open questions, and preserve decision continuity. Grounded in university-based research software engineering center experiences, this paper presents the motivating problem, system design, prototype, and illustrative lifecycle scenarios for Aleena.

</details>


### [92] [ProjAgent: Procedural Similarity Retrieval for Repository-Level Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08691)
*QiHong Chen,Aaron Imani,Iftekhar Ahmed*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ProjAgent introduces procedural similarity as a new retrieval signal for repository-level code generation, combining agentic workflow-based retrieval with static-analysis feedback to achieve 41.14% Pass@1 on REPOCOD, outperforming existing baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing retrieval methods for repository-level code generation rely on lexical, structural, or semantic similarity but miss functions that share similar procedural logic despite differing identifiers or domains. This gap motivates a retrieval dimension based on how code operates procedurally.

Method: ProjAgent decomposes the target function into intermediate reasoning steps, then uses an agentic workflow to retrieve repository functions with similar procedural behavior at each step. This procedural context is combined with conventional semantic retrieval. A conservative static-analysis feedback loop iteratively repairs generated code using compiler and static-analysis feedback.

Result: ProjAgent achieves 41.14% Pass@1 on the REPOCOD benchmark, outperforming all existing retrieval-based baselines.

Conclusion: Procedural similarity is an effective and previously unexplored retrieval dimension for repository-level code generation, demonstrating that reasoning about how code operates — rather than just what it looks like or what it semantically represents — significantly improves generation quality.

Abstract: Repository-level code generation requires implementing target functions while accounting for complex cross-file dependencies and project-specific conventions. Existing retrieval methods predominantly rely on lexical, structural, or semantic similarity, often overlooking repository functions that implement similar procedural logic despite differing in identifiers or application domains. We propose ProjAgent, a repository-level code generation system that introduces procedural similarity as an explicit retrieval signal. ProjAgent decomposes the target function into intermediate reasoning steps and employs an agentic workflow to retrieve repository functions that exhibit similar procedural behavior at each step. The retrieved procedural context is integrated with conventional semantic retrieval to construct a richer repository context for code generation. ProjAgent further incorporates a conservative static-analysis feedback loop that iteratively repairs generated code using compiler and static-analysis feedback. Evaluated on REPOCOD, ProjAgent achieves 41.14% Pass@1, outperforming existing retrieval-based baselines. These results demonstrate that procedural similarity is an effective and previously unexplored retrieval dimension for repository-level code generation.

</details>


### [93] [TTHE: Test-Time Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08124)
*Jun Nie,Yonggang Zhang,Jun Song,Qianshu Cai,Dahai Yu,Yike Guo,Xinmei Tian,Bo Han*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: TTHE proposes evolving the executable harness (control program) of an LLM agent during test-time evaluation rather than pre-deploying a fixed workflow. Using only unlabeled execution traces, a population of candidate harnesses is refined via an agentic proposer and judge—both roles played by the same frozen LLM—without updating model weights or requiring gold labels. The approach yields persistent, inspectable improvements across diverse coding and agentic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent systems optimize the harness (the control program managing context, tool calls, verification, and recovery) on training/development data before deployment, freezing it at test time. This fails when test distributions, failure modes, or tool interactions diverge from development conditions. The authors aim to enable harness adaptation at test time using only the agent's own unlabeled execution traces, eliminating dependence on gold labels or task-specific supervision.

Method: TTHE maintains a population of candidate harness programs during evaluation. An agentic proposer reasons over execution traces to suggest improved harness variants; a judge selects the best candidate using proxy reliability signals derived from execution (not gold labels). The winning harness persists for subsequent test inputs. All roles—solver, proposer, judge—are implemented as different harnesses around the same frozen LLM, so all adaptation occurs through program-level changes without weight updates or external adaptation models.

Result: TTHE improves over fixed ReAct-style baseline harnesses across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks. The improvements are persistent (carrying forward to future inputs) and inspectable (expressed as executable programs), distinguishing the approach from per-query retries or pre-searched static workflows.

Conclusion: Test-time adaptation for LLM agents can be recast as evolution over executable control programs. Execution-derived proxy reliability emerges as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement, pointing to future work on better proxy signals for harness selection without ground-truth labels.

Abstract: The behavior of an LLM agent is determined not only by the underlying model, but also by its harness: the executable program that constructs context, invokes tools, verifies intermediate results, and recovers from failures. Existing approaches optimize such harnesses before deployment, searching training or development data for a fixed agent workflow that is then frozen at test time. This limits adaptation when the test distribution, failure modes, or tool interactions differ from those seen during development. We ask whether the harness can instead be optimized during evaluation itself, using only the unlabeled execution traces the agent produces on the test inputs. We introduce Test-Time Harness Evolution (TTHE), which treats the executable harness as the state of test-time adaptation. During evaluation, TTHE maintains a population of candidate harnesses and refines them through an agentic proposer that reasons over their execution traces, without gold labels or task-specific supervision; a judge then commits an improved harness from execution-derived proxy signals, and the selected program persists to govern subsequent inputs. Crucially, TTHE does not update model weights, require gold labels, or train a separate adaptation model: solver, proposers, and judge are different roles and harnesses around the same frozen LLM, so all adaptation occurs through changes to the surrounding program. Across text-to-SQL, competitive programming, software engineering, data-science coding, and agentic tool-use tasks, TTHE improves fixed ReAct-style baseline harnesses, yielding persistent, inspectable improvements rather than a pre-searched workflow or per-query retries. These results recast test-time adaptation for LLM agents as evolution over executable control programs and identify execution-derived proxy reliability as a central challenge for robust unsupervised agent improvement.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [94] [Context Graphs for Proactive Enterprise Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07721)
*Avinash Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a proactive AI agent framework built on a "Context Graph" — a live relational model of enterprise entities — that surfaces actionable insights to workers before they query, using a Delta Detection Engine, Proactivity Scorer, and LLM-powered Surfacing Layer. It demonstrates significant improvements in precision and response time over reactive baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG and agentic frameworks are fundamentally reactive, waiting for human queries before acting. This limits enterprise productivity because workers must know what to ask for. The authors argue that genuine productivity gains require proactive agents that anticipate information needs and surface relevant, actionable insights before users explicitly query.

Method: The authors propose three core components: (1) a Context Graph — a live relational data structure modeling enterprise entities, their relationships, and state transitions over time using NetworkX; (2) a Delta Detection Engine that continuously monitors state changes in the graph; (3) a Proactivity Scorer that ranks candidate insights by urgency, relevance, and persona-fit; and (4) a Surfacing Layer powered by the Claude API (LLM) that delivers ranked notifications with grounded explanations. A unified Proactivity Score function mathematically formalizes the ranking. The system is implemented end-to-end in Python.

Result: Evaluation across three enterprise case studies — contract lifecycle management, engineering incident response, and sales pipeline hygiene — showed Precision@5 of 0.83, a false positive rate of 0.11, and mean time to surface reduced from 47 minutes (reactive baseline) to under 30 seconds.

Conclusion: Context-graph-driven proactive agents significantly outperform reactive baselines in surfacing timely, relevant enterprise insights, enabling a shift from query-driven to notification-driven enterprise AI workflows.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and agentic frameworks have advanced enterprise AI considerably, yet agents remain fundamentally reactive: they wait for a human query before acting. This paper argues that genuine enterprise productivity gains require proactive agents: systems that surface relevant, actionable information to workers before they ask. We propose the Context Graph, a live relational data structure that models enterprise entities, their relationships, and state transitions over time. Built on this graph, we define a Delta Detection Engine that continuously monitors state changes, a Proactivity Scorer that ranks candidate insights by urgency, relevance, and persona-fit, and a Surfacing Layer powered by an LLM that delivers ranked notifications with grounded explanations. We formalize each component, derive a unified Proactivity Score function, and provide a complete end-to-end Python implementation using NetworkX and the Anthropic Claude API. Evaluation across three generic enterprise case studies (contract lifecycle management, engineering incident response, and sales pipeline hygiene) demonstrates that context-graph-driven proactivity achieves Precision@5 of 0.83, a false positive rate of 0.11, and reduces mean time to surface from 47 minutes (reactive baseline) to under 30 second.

</details>


### [95] [AI-integrated models for assessing agricultural resilience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07759)
*Joshua R. Waite,Dana Golden,Brett Indelicato,Kevin Camp,Mojdeh Saadati,Shannon Regan,Patrick Schnable,Baskar Ganapathysubramanian,Carlos Messina,Suzanne Thornsbury,Soumik Sarkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI-powered tool combining economic (GTAP) and crop simulation (APSIM) models to analyze agricultural supply chain disruptions via natural language queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agricultural supply chains face coupled biophysical and economic shocks, creating a need for integrated cross-disciplinary analysis tools that are accessible to both policymakers and market participants.

Method: Development of an AI-powered platform that integrates the Global Trade Analysis Project (GTAP) economic model with the Agricultural Production Systems Simulator (APSIM) biophysical model, enabling natural language querying and response generation for supply chain disruption analysis.

Result: The tool successfully bridges economic and biophysical modeling frameworks, allowing users to assess cross-disciplinary impacts of supply chain shocks through natural language interaction.

Conclusion: Integrating economic and biophysical models via AI-driven natural language interfaces can democratize access to complex supply chain disruption analysis for diverse stakeholders.

Abstract: Agricultural supply chains are vulnerable to disruptions through linked biophysical and economic systems. We develop an AI-powered tool that integrates economic models (GTAP) with biophysical models (APSIM) to analyze supply chain shocks, enabling policymakers and market participants to assess cross-disciplinary impacts through queries and responses written in natural language.

</details>


### [96] [Adversarial Social Epistemology for Assemblies of Humans and Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07760)
*Mihnea C. Moldoveanu,Joel A. C. Baum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an Adversarial Social Epistemology (ASE) framework to analyze how bad actors exploit the trust-dependent scaffolding of public communication (chains of testimony, inference, certification) for manipulation, going beyond standard concepts like echo chambers and misinformation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing concepts like epistemic bubbles, echo chambers, and misinformation diffusion inadequately capture a deeper phenomenon: how communicative agents strategically exploit the very commitments, entitlements, and inferential scaffolding that normally render public assertions trustworthy. This exploitation for private, reputational, rhetorical, or material gain requires a new analytical framework.

Method: Develops an Adversarial Social Epistemology (ASE) framework using: (1) enriched language to analyze scaffolded public communications, (2) identification of mechanisms that subvert trust in these scaffoldings, (3) machinery for auditing and redressing trust breaches — specifically targeting subversion of inferential chain auditability — drawing on epistemic networks and an inferentialist semantics for interpreting assertions.

Result: The paper delivers a conceptual apparatus that reveals how communicative agents exploit the commitments and entitlements underpinning scaffolded assertions, and provides machinery for auditing and redressing breaches arising from subverted inferential audit trails within epistemic networks.

Conclusion: An inferentialist, network-based Adversarial Social Epistemology is necessary to diagnose and redress sophisticated forms of epistemic manipulation that operate by exploiting — rather than merely bypassing — the trust scaffolding of public communicative landscapes.

Abstract: We outline an adversarial social epistemology (ASE) for densely interactive communicative landscapes in which public assertions are scaffolded by chains of testimony, inference, institutional certification, and tacit trust. In such landscapes, agents have incentives and affordances to distort, color, omit, fabricate, or strategically under-specify information for private, reputational, rhetorical, or material gains. We argue that these phenomena are not adequately captured by familiar descriptions of epistemic bubbles, echo chambers, or misinformation diffusion. What requires explanation is how communicative agents exploit the commitments and entitlements that normally make scaffolded assertions trustworthy. We provide language that delivers the requisite analysis, outline mechanisms that subvert trust in scaffolded public communications, and outline machinery for auditing and redressing trust breaches arising from subverting the auditability of inferential chains, drawing on epistemic networks, enriched with an inferentialist semantics for interpreting assertions.

</details>


### [97] [Aligning Clinical Needs and AI Capabilities: A Survey on LLMs for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07761)
*Qi Peng,Jiatong Li,Sirui Huang,Yiyang Jiang,Kaisong Gong,Ronger Ding,Shijie Ye,Changmeng Zheng,Yi Cai,Xiaobo Yang,Jin Huang,Xiao-Yong Wei,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey analyzes medical LLMs through a dual-view framework: a clinical five-level competency scheme (Miller's Pyramid) and computational reasoning patterns (deductive, inductive, abductive). A benchmark across five reasoning levels tested on 18 models reveals specialist models excel at diagnosis while general models lead in decision support and dialogue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in healthcare for clinical reasoning and patient care, yet there lacks a systematic framework connecting clinical competencies with computational reasoning methods. The authors aim to bridge this gap and provide structured evaluation of medical LLM capabilities.

Method: A dual-view framework: (1) Clinical view — a five-level competency hierarchy based on Miller's Pyramid (knowledge recall → dynamic case management); (2) Computational view — mapping deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning to medical goals and tasks. A benchmark dataset spanning five medical reasoning levels is constructed and evaluated against 18 state-of-the-art models.

Result: Medical specialist models outperform on diagnosis-centric tasks, while general-purpose models lead on decision support and dialogue tasks. The benchmark reveals capability gaps across reasoning levels, and persistent challenges in hallucination, data limitations, and grounding.

Conclusion: Medical LLMs show promise but face significant hurdles. Future work must address data quality, hallucination mitigation, and clinical grounding to produce safer, workflow-ready systems. The dual-view framework offers a structured path for evaluating and advancing medical reasoning in LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as important tools in healthcare, showing growing potential for clinical reasoning and patient care. This survey examines recent progress in medical LLMs, focusing on reasoning applications and requirements. We present a dual-view approach that connects clinical practice with computational methods. On the clinical side, we establish a five-level competency scheme following Miller's Pyramid, progressing from knowledge recall to dynamic case management. On the computational side, we link deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning patterns to common medical goals and tasks. We also introduce a benchmark dataset spanning five levels of medical reasoning capability and report results on 18 state-of-the-art models, revealing that medical specialist models excel in diagnosis-centric tasks while general models lead in decision support and dialogue. We conclude by discussing current progress and open challenges, including data limitations, hallucination, and grounding issues, and outline directions toward safer, more reliable, and workflow-ready systems.

</details>


### [98] [ASMR: Agentic Schema Generation for Ship Maintenance Report Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08177)
*Sohrab Namazi Nia,Amogh Dalal,Ning Sa,Peter Ly,Marti Zentmaier,Tomek Strzalkowski,Jay Miller,Rishi Singh,Senjuti Basu Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASMR proposes a two-agent framework for automatically generating schemas from historical ship maintenance reports: a Field Generation Agent uses multi-granularity clustering to extract candidate schema fields, while a Structural Optimizer Agent applies reinforcement learning to produce compact, non-redundant schemas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual schema design for diverse ship maintenance and operational reports is labor-intensive and inconsistent. Automating schema discovery from historical narratives can ensure reports are more complete, consistent, and actionable, but the heterogeneity across form categories makes this a challenging information extraction problem.

Method: ASMR is a modular agentic framework with two specialized agents: (1) a Field Generation Agent that extracts semantic concepts from historical report narratives and generates candidate schema fields via adaptive multi-granularity clustering; (2) a Structural Optimizer Agent that uses reinforcement learning to select compact, informative, and non-redundant schema representations from the candidate fields.

Result: Preliminary results demonstrate the promise of the ASMR approach, though the paper notes several open research challenges remain at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

Conclusion: The ASMR framework shows potential for automating schema generation in domain-specific, multi-category report settings. Future work is needed to address the identified open challenges spanning data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the automatic schema generation problem: given a collection of historical ship maintenance and operational reports across multiple form categories, automatically discover compact and informative schemas that capture the essential information requirements of each report type. To address this challenge, we propose ASMR, a modular agentic framework consisting of two specialized agents. A Field Generation Agent extracts semantic concepts from historical narratives and generates candidate schema fields through adaptive multi-granularity clustering, while a Structural Optimizer Agent employs reinforcement learning to identify compact, informative, and non-redundant schema representations. The resulting schemas can guide report authors toward producing more complete, consistent, and actionable reports. Preliminary results demonstrate the promise of the proposed approach and highlight several open research challenges at the intersection of data management, agentic AI, and human-centered AI.

</details>


### [99] [Alignment Plausibility: A New Standard for Assuring AI in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07766)
*Gwydion Williams,Sara Zannone,Bilal A Mateen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs in mental health risk prioritizing engagement over therapeutic friction. The authors propose a three-level alignment framework (value specification, embedded training, deployment oversight) mirroring clinical supervision, yielding "alignment plausibility" — a regulatory construct analogous to biological plausibility for evaluating whether AI systems are genuinely safe and beneficial for patients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for mental health support, but their design is rooted in an attention economy that incentivizes sustained engagement. Current safety measures are reactive and focused on acute harms, neglecting subtler long-term risks such as dependency, boundary erosion, and amplification of distorted beliefs. A structural, proactive safety framework is needed.

Method: The authors propose organizing LLM alignment into three levels analogous to clinical practice safeguards: (1) explicit value specification grounded in codified clinical normative commitments; (2) training that embeds those values in the model; and (3) deployment oversight that detects drift and long-term harm, akin to clinical supervision. These three levels collectively produce "alignment plausibility."

Result: The paper introduces "alignment plausibility" as a regulatory construct for AI in health, drawing an analogy to biological plausibility. It serves as a structured demonstration that a system's values, training, and oversight mechanisms are consistent with safe, positive health outcomes — enabling principled arguments for or against trust in deployed systems.

Conclusion: Making LLMs structurally safe for mental health requires moving beyond reactive safety patches to a comprehensive alignment framework. Alignment plausibility offers a regulatory standard for evaluating whether AI systems will cause no harm, align to positive health outcomes, and ultimately benefit patients.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have become significant providers of mental health support, yet they remain products of an attention economy whose operational and commercial targets favour sustained engagement over the friction that effective psychological support often requires. Developers' safety responses have been largely reactive, addressing the most visible and acute harms while subtler, longer-term patterns of risk (e.g., dependency, boundary erosion, the amplification of distorted beliefs) receive less attention. We contend that making LLMs structurally safe requires alignment organised at three levels that mirror how society assures the safety of human clinical practice: 1) explicit value specification grounded in the codified normative commitments of clinical practice; 2) training that embeds those values in the model; and 3) oversight that detects drift and longer-term harm during deployment, much as clinical supervision does for human practice. Organising alignment in this way yields a construct we call alignment plausibility - a structured demonstration that a system's values, training regime, and oversight mechanisms are together consistent with safe and positive outcomes. We propose alignment plausibility as a regulatory construct (by drawing analogy to the established construct of biological plausibility) for AI in health: a principled way to argue for, or against, trust that systems are aligned to positive health outcomes, will cause no harm even where capable of doing so, and will ultimately lead to patient benefit.

</details>


### [100] [Idiobionics: The Unification of Privacy and Intelligent Robotic Prostheses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07775)
*Kwesi Afari Darfoor,Patrick M. Pilarski,Bailey Kacsmar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "idiobionics," a new research area examining privacy risks in intelligent bionic limbs. As robotic prostheses become more capable through advanced sensors and AI, they also introduce new threat vectors for privacy violations. The authors define idiobionics, demonstrate potential adversarial attacks on bionic limbs, and present open research questions to guide future work at the intersection of privacy and wearable robotics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic prostheses are increasingly integrating advanced sensors and AI-based control, making them semiautonomous wearable robots that co-adapt with users. However, these same technological advances create privacy vulnerabilities that malicious actors could exploit. To ensure user adoption and fully realize the benefits of next-generation bionic limbs, these privacy risks must be systematically understood and addressed — a gap the authors aim to fill.

Method: The paper defines the novel concept of "idiobionics" by grounding it in related literature spanning wearable robotics, privacy, and human-facing autonomous systems. It then provides preliminary evidence through discussion of potential adversarial attack vectors that could exploit intelligent bionic limb designs. Finally, it curates a set of open research questions to scope this new field of inquiry.

Result: The paper formally establishes idiobionics as a holistic framework for investigating privacy issues in intelligent bionic limbs. It identifies and discusses specific adversarial attack scenarios that threaten user privacy through the sensing and control pipelines of modern prostheses. A curated list of open research questions is contributed to guide future interdisciplinary research.

Conclusion: Idiobionics research is essential for unlocking the full potential of robotic prostheses and related bionic devices. By directly confronting privacy risks, the field can help remove barriers to user adoption and ensure that the benefits of tightly coupling biological and digital systems are realized safely and responsibly.

Abstract: The human body is at the center of a growing family of technologies designed to tightly and persistently couple biological and digital systems. Robotic prostheses are a representative example of this tight coupling. Also referred to as bionic limbs, robotic prostheses are devices that support people who have lost limbs in pursuing daily life activities such as walking and grasping objects. Bionic limbs are now perceptive and responsive owing to their integration with advanced sensors and artificial intelligence-based control approaches. Consequently, such robotic prostheses can now be viewed as semiautonomous wearable robotic systems that can co-adapt with their users. However, the same sensing and control advancements that increase the capability of robotic prostheses also introduce threat vectors that could be exploited by malicious entities to violate the privacy of users. To fully realize the benefits of next-generation bionic limbs, we maintain it is important to directly understand and address these privacy risks and the barriers they might present to user adoption. This paper therefore introduces a new line of inquiry we term idiobionics to holistically investigate issues at the intersection of privacy and intelligent bionic limbs. As the main contribution of this paper, we define idiobionics, ground it in related literature, and provide preliminary evidence showing and discussing potential adversarial attacks that could exploit intelligent bionic limb designs. We then contribute a curated list of open research questions within idiobionics that are relevant to researchers in wearable robotics and other human-facing autonomous systems. We expect that idiobionics research will help unlock the full potential of robotic prostheses and related bionic devices.

</details>


### [101] [Infinity-Parser2 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07836)
*Zuming Huang,Jun Huang,Kexuan Ren,Baode Wang,Weizhen Li,Jianming Feng,Yu Wang,Yichen Yao,Shijun Lin,Yige Tang,Cheng Peng,Weidi Xu,Wei Chu,Yinghui Xu,Yuan Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Infinity-Parser2 is a large multimodal model for end-to-end document parsing that combines a controllable data synthesis pipeline (producing the 5M-sample Infinity-Doc2-5M corpus) with multi-task joint reinforcement learning across eight objectives. Two variants are released: Flash (low-latency) and Pro (high-precision, achieving SOTA on olmOCR-Bench and ParseBench).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the persistent scarcity of faithfully annotated document parsing corpora, and unify perception, structure extraction, and reasoning tasks under a single optimization framework using verifiable multi-task reinforcement learning.

Method: A three-part approach: (1) a scalable synthesis engine with a controllable rendering framework and iterative refinement loop to generate Infinity-Doc2-5M, a 5M-sample bilingual corpus annotated with bounding boxes, canonical content (Markdown, HTML, LaTeX, SMILES, charts), and reading order; (2) a verifiable multi-task reward system enabling Joint Reinforcement Learning across eight co-trained objectives (document parsing, layout analysis, table/math/chart/chemical formula parsing, document VQA, and general multimodal understanding); (3) two model variants (Flash and Pro) under a shared architecture.

Result: Infinity-Parser2-Pro achieves state-of-the-art scores: 87.6% on olmOCR-Bench and 74.3% on ParseBench, surpassing DeepSeek-OCR-2, PaddleOCR-VL-1.5, and MinerU2.5. Infinity-Parser2-Flash achieves a 3.68× throughput gain over Infinity-Parser-7B. Strong generalization is demonstrated on charts, chemical formulas, and document VQA.

Conclusion: The combination of a controllable large-scale synthetic data pipeline and multi-task joint RL proves highly effective, yielding a unified document parsing model that sets new SOTA across multiple benchmarks while offering both speed-optimized and precision-optimized variants.

Abstract: We present Infinity-Parser2, a large multimodal model that couples a controllable data-synthesis pipeline with multi-task reinforcement learning for end-to-end document parsing, addressing the persistent scarcity of faithfully annotated parsing corpora. Our contributions are threefold. First, we build a scalable synthesis engine, pairing a controllable rendering framework with an iterative refinement loop, and use it to construct and open-source Infinity-Doc2-5M: a 5-million-sample bilingual (Chinese/English) corpus spanning diverse document types, annotated with element bounding boxes, canonical content forms (Markdown, HTML, LaTeX, SMILES, structured charts), and full-page reading order. Second, we introduce a verifiable, multi-task reward system that enables Joint Reinforcement Learning across eight co-trained objectives (document parsing, layout analysis, table parsing, math formula parsing, chart parsing, chemical formula parsing, document VQA, and general multimodal understanding), unifying perception, structure, and reasoning in a single optimization signal. Third, we release two variants under a shared architecture: Infinity-Parser2-Flash, optimized for low-latency inference with a $3.68\times$ throughput gain over Infinity-Parser-7B, and Infinity-Parser2-Pro, engineered for precision-critical settings. Infinity-Parser2-Pro reaches state-of-the-art 87.6% on olmOCR-Bench and 74.3% on ParseBench, surpassing DeepSeek-OCR-2, PaddleOCR-VL-1.5, and MinerU2.5, with strong generalization to charts, chemical formulas, and document VQA.

</details>


### [102] [VectorizationLLM: Smart Vectorization Based AI Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07846)
*Ryan Duke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VectorizationLLM is a Google-based LLM with RAG architecture designed to assist students in learning MATLAB vectorization, Fourier analysis, and differential equations without providing direct answers, deployed in an engineering technology course at NYIT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to provide an instructive AI assistant for students in CTEC 247: Applied Computational Analysis II that explains concepts using in-class examples (code, text, images) while deliberately avoiding giving direct answers, thereby promoting active learning in MATLAB-based computational topics.

Method: The approach uses a specialized Large Language Model built on Google open-weight LLMs, augmented with a RAG (Retrieval Augmented Generation) knowledge base and system prompt architecture. The model delivers multi-modal responses (code, text, and images) sourced from in-class notes to explain smart vectorization, time/wave vector analysis, piecewise functions, Fourier analysis, and differential equations.

Result: The paper describes the design and architecture of VectorizationLLM, including its RAG knowledge base and prompt engineering, along with examples of its instructional responses across multiple modalities. Specific quantitative or qualitative evaluation results are not detailed in the abstract.

Conclusion: VectorizationLLM demonstrates a pedagogical application of LLMs in engineering education, using constrained response design (no direct answers) and course-specific RAG to serve as a supplementary instructional tool for computational analysis coursework.

Abstract: VectorizationLLM is a specialized Large Language Model based on Google open-weight LLMs. The model is designed to assist students to learn smart vectorization, time/wave vector analysis, piecewise functions, Fourier analysis, and differential equations in MATLAB. The course application is CTEC 247: Applied Computational Analysis II by the Department of Electrical & Computer Engineering Technology at New York Institute of Technology Old Westbury. The LLM model is designed to be an instructive assistant, providing detailed explanations of concepts with examples from in-class notes without providing direct answers to questions. The model is designed with a RAG (Retrieval Augmented Generation) knowledge base and system prompt architecture. Examples in both code, text, and images are provided in the LLM responses.

</details>


### [103] [Feedback Manipulation Regularization: Enabling Offline Agent Alignment for Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07859)
*Benjamin Poole,Minwoo Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FMR is an algorithm-agnostic method that uses evaluative feedback to regularize imitation learning policies, achieving up to 98% reduction in misalignment on adapted Safety Gymnasium benchmarks, even with scarce or noisy data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing alignment methods combine human demonstrations and feedback via multi-stage pipelines designed for contextual bandit settings (e.g., language generation), leaving a gap for single-stage offline training in fully sequential decision-making environments where these complementary signals could be leveraged jointly.

Method: Feedback Manipulation Regularization (FMR) — an algorithm-agnostic technique that incorporates evaluative feedback as a corrective signal to regularize imitation learning policies during single-stage offline training, rather than relying on separate stages or contextual bandit formulations.

Result: FMR demonstrated improved alignment aptitude and up to a 98% reduction in misalignment across multiple imitation learning algorithms on adapted Safety Gymnasium environments; remains robust under limited data and noisy, uninformative demonstrations.

Conclusion: Evaluative feedback can serve as an effective corrective signal for regularizing imitation learning in fully sequential settings, enabling robust alignment even with limited or low-quality data, and highlighting the value of interconnected human signals in single-stage offline RL training.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) research has increasingly shifted focus towards alignment, ensuring agents learn behaviors adhering to human values. While human demonstrations and feedback have proven crucial for alignment, existing approaches predominantly combine these signals using multi-stage pipelines designed for the contextual bandit framing of language generation. Yet little work explores how these complementary inputs can serve as a richer, interconnected signal for single-stage offline training in fully sequential decision-making environments. We propose Feedback Manipulation Regularization (FMR), an algorithm-agnostic method that harnesses evaluative feedback as a corrective signal to improve the alignment of imitation learning policies. We adapt Safety Gymnasium environments to be a principled testbed for alignment evaluation, demonstrating improved aptitude and up to a 98\% reduction in misalignment across a range of imitation learning algorithms. FMR remains robust in limited data regimes, even when learning from scarce aligned and uninformative noisy demonstrations.

</details>


### [104] [A Graph Neural Network Model for Real-Time Gesture Recognition Based on sEMG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07850)
*Pragatheeswaran Vipulanandan,Kamal Premaratne,Manohar Murthi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A graph neural network approach for sEMG-based hand gesture recognition achieves 99% accuracy with 48ms inference time on an M1 Pro CPU, enabling real-time prosthetic and AR control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate and immediate hand gesture recognition is essential for seamless control of advanced hand prostheses and augmented reality applications. Existing sEMG-based methods can be improved upon in terms of both accuracy and speed.

Method: The authors propose representing sEMG signals as graph networks that capture forearm muscle activation patterns. A graph neural network (GNN) is then trained for real-time hand gesture classification. Data was collected using a MyoBand (8 electrodes around the forearm) from 8 healthy subjects.

Result: The method achieved 99% average classification accuracy, outperforming state-of-the-art techniques. Total latency for graph construction and prediction was 48ms on an M1 Pro CPU.

Conclusion: The graph-network-based sEMG representation combined with GNN classification is both highly accurate and sufficiently fast for real-time hand gesture recognition, making it suitable for practical deployment in prosthetics and AR systems.

Abstract: For seemless control of advanced hand prostheses and augmented reality, accurate and immediate hand gestures recognition is essential. Surface electromyography (sEMG) signals obtained from the forearm are commonly employed for this purpose. In this paper, we present a novel approach for sEMG representation that utilizes graph networks which contain information about muscle activation patterns in the forearm. Based on these graph networks, we have developed a machine learning algorithm capable of real-time hand gesture recognition using a graph neural network. The algorithm's performance was evaluated using sEMG signals acquired from myoband, which has 8 electrodes placed around the forearm, involving 8 healthy subjects. The proposed method demonstrated an average classification accuracy of 99\%, surpassing the performance of state-of-the-art techniques. The average time for both graph construction and prediction stood at 48ms utilizing a M1 pro CPU, rendering the approach well-suited for real-time applications.

</details>


### [105] [Agentic AI and Retrieval-Augmented Models in Straight-Through Underwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07858)
*Robert Richardson,Josh Meyers,Brian Hartman,David Sandberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper explores how agentic AI architectures — combining LLMs, RAG, and multi-agent coordination — can enhance actuarial workflows. Using a synthetic BOP underwriting experiment, the authors demonstrate that a multi-agent pipeline outperforms single-LLM and naive RAG baselines, especially in complex, multi-step scenarios requiring reflection and structured retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Actuarial practice increasingly demands reasoning over unstructured documents and heterogeneous data under strict regulatory constraints. Existing automation ranges from rigid rules to LLMs, but there is no clear guidance on which AI architecture best balances performance with actuarial requirements like transparency, auditability, and human-in-the-loop governance — particularly for straight-through decision processes.

Method: The authors construct a synthetic but realistic experimental environment for straight-through underwriting of small commercial Business Owner Policies (BOPs). They compare three pipelines: (i) a single-LLM baseline, (ii) a naive RAG system, and (iii) a multi-agent "Agentic RAG" pipeline featuring targeted retrieval, third-party data checks, and explicit multi-step rule evaluation.

Result: The agentic system achieves the best overall performance. The largest improvements occur in multi-step and missing-information scenarios, where structured retrieval and reflection mechanisms prevent the model from making unsupported straight-through decisions.

Conclusion: Agentic AI architectures that integrate structured retrieval, tool use, and reflective multi-step reasoning offer a promising path for actuarial automation. They outperform simpler LLM and RAG setups while better supporting the transparency, auditability, and governance demands of regulated insurance workflows.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is beginning to reshape actuarial practice, particularly in domains that require reasoning over unstructured documents, heterogeneous data sources, and regulated decision workflows. Actuaries now face a design space that ranges from traditional rule-based automation to large language models (LLMs), retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and multi-agent ``agentic'' systems that plan, retrieve, call tools, and reflect. This paper examines how these emerging architectures can support actuarial priorities such as transparency, auditability, and human-in-the-loop governance, with a focus on straight-through decision processes. To make these ideas concrete, we develop and analyze an agentic AI framework for straight-through underwriting of small commercial Business Owner Policies (BOPs). We construct a synthetic but realistic experimental environment and compare three underwriting pipelines: (i) a single-LLM baseline, (ii) a naive RAG system, and (iii) a multi-agent ``Agentic RAG'' pipeline that combines targeted retrieval, third-party data checks, and explicit multi-step rule evaluation. The agentic system performs best overall, with the largest gains in multi-step and missing-information scenarios, where structured retrieval and reflection help the model avoid unsupported straight-through decisions.

</details>


### [106] [AutoPersonas: A Multi-Timescale Loop Engine for Open-Ended Persona Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08252)
*Mengchen Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Long-term persona agents suffer from "self-locking" — a failure mode where generated lives collapse into repetitive, stagnant patterns despite plausible local events. The authors introduce AutoPersonas, an engine that separates divergent future-facing material from evidence-governed absorption, and demonstrate through simulations that this separation reduces thematic repetition while preserving identity continuity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persona agents in long-running simulations must balance identity continuity with adaptation to new events. The authors identify a critical failure mode called "self-locking," where agent lives drift toward familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages due to model-level convergence toward high-probability behavioral channels and system-level context gravity. Existing approaches lack mechanisms to maintain both stability and meaningful evolution over extended time horizons.

Method: The authors propose AutoPersonas, a multi-timescale life-environment engine built around an OSO loop (Occurrences, accumulated Observations, persona State). The architecture enforces a separation: divergent future-facing material can be generated freely, but only evidence-governed material may be absorbed into State or trigger reachability changes. The system was evaluated through a three-year compressed simulation and an eight-model 40-day stress test generating 1,600 events, plus an A/B test comparing direct-loop runs against context-slice masking with per-sample divergence targeting. A juvenile-goblin fictional-world run tested generalizability.

Result: The stress test revealed mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition rates of 95.2%-97.6%, with all models exceeding 90% by day 11. Semantic re-keeping showed 79.0%-88.0% macro-theme repetition in direct-loop runs. The A/B test with context-slice masking plus divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. The fictional-world run reproduced the anti-fixation regime, confirming the approach generalizes beyond real-world settings.

Conclusion: Separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption reduces persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity. However, the authors frame this as a bounded claim, acknowledging unresolved challenges including environment watermark shells, occurrence-hardening gaps, and weak relationship persistence.

Abstract: Long-term persona agents must remain identifiable while adapting to new events, relationships, evidence, and social conditions. We identify self-locking as a runtime failure mode in continuing persona-life loops: locally plausible events keep appearing while the generated life collapses toward familiar environments, weak relationships, suspended decisions, and stale life stages. We trace this failure to model-level convergence toward high-probability behavioral channels and system-level context gravity from State, memory, history, and environment summaries. We introduce AutoPersonas, a multi-timescale life-environment engine for bounded persona-level recursive self-evolution. It separates environment-side Occurrences, accumulated Observations, and persona State. Its OSO loop admits divergent future-facing material while requiring evidence-governed absorption before State or reachability changes. A three-year compressed simulation exposed environment watermark shells, occurrence-hardening gaps, slow-change accumulation failures, recursive indecision, and weak relationship persistence. An eight-model 40-day stress test generated 1,600 events and found mean rolling 5-day action-category repetition of 95.2%-97.6%, with all models crossing 90% by day 11. Semantic re-keeping found 79.0%-88.0% macro-theme repetition across all direct-loop runs. In a same-runtime 40-day A/B, context-slice masking plus per-sample divergence targeting reduced macro-theme repetition from 61.8% to 36.3% and roughly doubled cumulative theme count. A juvenile-goblin fictional-world run reproduced the anti-fixation regime without hard real-world intrusions. These results support a bounded claim: separating controlled divergence from evidence-governed absorption can reduce persona-environment self-locking while preserving identity continuity.

</details>


### [107] [Nigeria Machinery: A Low-Resource Industrial Dataset with a Domain-Grounded Reasoning Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07883)
*Gospel Bassey,Vincent Fakiyesi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper releases the Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset (89 records, 28 indicators, 2006–2025) and a chain-of-thought reasoning layer (94 prompt-completion-reasoning rows). It also identifies and fixes a problem where language model–generated prompts matched numeric values but lacked domain grounding, improving domain-grounded prompts from 1/78 to 94/94.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a scarcity of public, model-ready industrial machinery data for African economies, hindering quantitative analysis and the training of language models on numeric tasks grounded in that context.

Method: Two contributions: (1) curation and release of the Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset — 89 machine-level records across 28 indicators covering Nigeria's manufacturing and oil/gas sectors (2006–2025), each with a public source and codebook; (2) a method for constructing chain-of-thought reasoning examples from sparse numeric values, producing 94 rows with prompts naming the real indicator, subsector, year, and source. The authors also diagnose and correct a data-generation flaw where LM-generated prompts matched numeric targets but lacked real domain content.

Result: The fix raised domain-grounded prompts from 1 out of 78 (in an earlier release) to 94 out of 94. Every retrieval answer now matches its source value (84 out of 84). The dataset, reasoning layer, and per-row provenance file are released under CC-BY-4.0.

Conclusion: The dataset is explicitly a reference/seed dataset (not a large training corpus), limited by 89 records and 17 single-observation indicators, with most reasoning rows being retrieval rather than multi-step computation. It partially addresses the data gap for African industrial machinery analysis.

Abstract: There is relatively little, public, and model-ready data on industrial machinery for African economies. This makes it hard to do quantitative analysis or to train language models on numeric tasks grounded in that setting. We release two things to help with part of this problem. The first is the Nigeria Machinery Usage and Failures Dataset: 89 machine-level records across 28 indicators, covering Nigeria's manufacturing and oil and gas sectors from 2006 to 2025. Every record names a public source and is decoded by a codebook. The second is a method for building chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning examples from these sparse numeric values. The result is 94 prompt, completion, and reasoning-trace rows. In every row, the prompt names the real indicator, subsector, year, and source of the record it comes from. The data adaptation work was carried out by Adaption Labs. Along the way we describe a problem that is common when language models are used to build datasets. The prompts can match the real numbers while saying nothing about the real domain. We show that fixing this raises the share of domain-grounded prompts from 1 out of 78 in an earlier release to 94 out of 94, and that every retrieval answer now matches its source value (84 out of 84). We release the data, the reasoning layer, and a per-row provenance file under CC-BY-4.0. We are clear about the limits. With 89 records and 17 indicators that have only one observation, this is a reference and seed dataset, not a large training set. Most reasoning rows are retrieval rather than multi-step computation.

</details>


### [108] [From Prompts to Contracts: Harness Engineering for Auditable Enterprise LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08028)
*Joongho Ahn,Moonsoo Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Presents a harness-engineering approach that transforms exploratory LLM prototypes into traceable, auditable applications by shifting deterministic behaviors into code, manifests, schemas, and validation artifacts. Validated on Korean corporate data, the harness preserves grounding, routing, traceability, and safety contracts under model substitution, while code-owned enforcement achieves both safety and utility — unlike prompting alone or external guardrails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise LLM applications start as prototypes driven by prompts and retrieval context, but productization introduces requirements for source provenance, entity routing, answer contracts, and reproducible traces that prompting alone cannot reliably satisfy.

Method: A harness-engineering architecture that separates deterministic behaviors (routing, contract enforcement, tracing, validation) into code, manifests, and schemas around a replaceable model-composition boundary. Source-backed claims remain authoritative for runtime answers. Evaluated on a public-data slice of five Korean corporate groups (25 listed companies) across three research questions with fault-injection, model-substitution, and ablation studies comparing code-owned enforcement against prompt-only instructions and bolt-on external guardrails.

Result: 1) Harness preserves all contracts across fixed validation scenarios; fault injection correctly flags broken contracts. 2) Under model substitution across three hosted models, all 270 composition-boundary runs passed; model-side failures were caught and recorded. 3) Prompt-only instructions allowed recommendation-language and trace-leakage violations to reach the reader; external guardrails blocked violations but over-refused (88/120 utility); only code-owned enforcement preserved both safety and full utility (120/120).

Conclusion: The harness-engineering pattern is reusable for converting exploratory prototypes into auditable, production-grade LLM applications with versioned source, control, and validation artifacts, where code-owned enforcement uniquely preserves both safety and utility.

Abstract: Enterprise large language model (LLM) applications often begin as prototypes whose behavior is carried by prompts and retrieval context. Productization adds requirements for source boundaries, entity routing, answer contracts, and reproducible traces. We present a harness-engineering approach that reconstructs this pattern into a traceable, auditable LLM-agent architecture: deterministic behavior moves into code, manifests, schemas, and validation artifacts around a replaceable composition boundary, while source-backed claims remain the authority for runtime answers. We instantiate it on a public-data slice of five Korean corporate groups (25 listed companies) and evaluate three research questions. (1) The harness preserves its source-grounding, entity-routing, trace, output-hygiene, and recommendation-language contracts across the fixed validation scenarios; a fault-injection control confirms the validators flag deliberately broken contracts. (2) The checks the harness enforces held under model substitution: across three hosted models, they passed on all 270 composition-boundary runs; failures were confined to the model-composed side and were caught and recorded. (3) The code-owned guarantees are load-bearing, not reproducible by prompting alone: holding the model fixed and varying only the enforcement layer, prompt instructions alone let recommendation-language and internal-trace-leakage violations reach the reader, which the harness blocks entirely. A bolt-on external guardrail prevents such violations too but over-refuses, dropping utility to 88/120 where the harness preserves full utility (120/120); in this ablation, only code-owned enforcement preserves both safety and utility. The result is a reusable engineering pattern for turning exploratory prototypes into auditable applications with versioned source, control, and validation artifacts.

</details>


### [109] [Persona Cartography: Charting Language Model Personality Traits in Weight Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07916)
*Luke Baines,Anton Gonzalvez Hawthorne,Mariia Koroliuk,Irakli Shalibashvili,Clément Dumas,Konstantinos Voudouris,David Demitri Africa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a method to decompose and control LLM personas using the OCEAN personality framework. By training low-rank adapters to modulate specific traits, the authors demonstrate monotonic trait scaling, additive composition of mixed personas, and downstream safety implications—bridging personality psychology with model editing for interpretable behaviour control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs exhibit recurring behavioural patterns (personas) that affect generalization and safety, yet there are no reliable tools for systematically decomposing, measuring, or controlling these personas. A principled framework is needed to map model behaviour onto interpretable trait dimensions and to manipulate those traits in weight space.

Method: The authors adopt the OCEAN (Big Five) personality framework to represent model personas along five trait axes: Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, and Neuroticism. They train low-rank adapters (LoRA) to amplify or suppress individual traits. Evaluation uses three approaches: an LLM-judge calibrated against human-validated panels, trait-specific multiple-choice benchmarks, and standard capability benchmarks. They also introduce an unsupervised psychometric pipeline that recovers four behavioural factors (tone, initiative, didacticism, epistemic caution) from model rollouts without predefined trait labels.

Result: Across six models from three families (4B–32B parameters): (1) each adapter moves its target trait largely monotonically with adapter scale; (2) adapters combine approximately additively to construct mixed personas; (3) moderate-scale adapters preserve performance on capability benchmarks; (4) trait axes affect safety-relevant behaviour—neuroticism influences frustration and agreeableness influences sycophancy in downstream evaluations; (5) the unsupervised pipeline recovers four interpretable behavioural factors from rollouts.

Conclusion: Persona control can be understood as learning, scaling, and composing behavioural traits in weight space. The OCEAN-based adapter framework provides a reliable, interpretable bridge between personality measurement, model editing, and AI safety, enabling both precise trait manipulation and unsupervised discovery of latent behavioural dimensions.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit recurring behavioural patterns -- personas -- that shape generalisation and safety, but we lack reliable tools for decomposing, measuring, and controlling them. Our central insight is to treat personas as positions in a space of behavioural traits, using the OCEAN framework to describe model personas in terms of Openness, Conscientiousness, Extraversion, Agreeableness, and Neuroticism. We train low-rank adapters to amplify or suppress individual traits, and evaluate their effects using an LLM-judge calibrated against a human-validated panel, trait-specific multiple-choice benchmarks, and standard capability evaluations. Across six models from three families (4B-32B), we find that each adapter moves its target trait largely monotonically with scale, combines approximately additively with other adapters to construct mixed personas, and preserves performance on capability benchmarks at moderate scales. We further show that the induced trait axes affect safety-relevant behaviour in downstream evaluations: for example, moving along neuroticism and agreeableness axes affects frustration and sycophancy respectively. We also introduce an unsupervised psychometric pipeline that recovers four interpretable behavioural factors (tone, initiative, didacticism, epistemic caution) from model rollouts. Persona control can then be considered in terms of learning, scaling, and composing traits in weight space, providing a bridge between personality measurement, model editing, and safety.

</details>


### [110] [Evaluating the Effect of Frame Rate in Sequence-Based Classification of Autism-Related Self-Stimulatory Hand Idiosyncrasies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07957)
*Raunak Mondal,Peter Washington*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper optimizes video-based autism detection by comparing LSTM vs. GRU architectures at varying frame sampling rates (peak accuracy ~98% at 15-frame intervals) and evaluating ten data augmentation strategies, finding horizontal flip most effective individually and upsampling most critical overall for small behavioral datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Over 75 million people worldwide have ASD, yet scalable computational methods for remote behavioral screening are lacking. The study addresses two gaps: no prior systematic comparison of sequence-based architectures (LSTM/GRU) with optimized temporal sampling for stereotypic behavior detection, and no rigorous ablation study quantifying which data augmentation techniques are essential when training on small clinical video datasets.

Method: Using the SSBD dataset with pose-derived features, LSTM and GRU models were trained at six frame sampling intervals (1, 5, 15, 30, 45, 90 frames). Performance was benchmarked against prior CNN baselines. Separately, an I3D transfer learning pipeline was augmented with ten strategies (e.g., horizontal flip, upsampling), and an ablation study measured each technique's marginal contribution. A personalized per-subject modeling approach with temporal train/test splits was also evaluated.

Result: Both LSTM (97.5%) and GRU (98.75%) substantially outperformed prior CNN baselines (62-76%), with optimal sampling every 15 frames. Horizontal flip yielded the highest standalone augmentation accuracy (48.78%), while removing upsampling caused the largest degradation, confirming it as essential. Personalized per-subject models produced consistent predictions (mean loss 1.84, SD 0.79).

Conclusion: GRU/LSTM architectures with moderate temporal sampling (~15-frame intervals) are strongly recommended over CNNs for video-based ASD behavior classification. Upsampling is critical for augmentation in small-sample behavioral video domains. The findings offer actionable guidance on architecture, sampling rate, and augmentation for practitioners in data-scarce clinical settings.

Abstract: Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) affects over 75 million individuals worldwide, yet scalable computational methods for remote behavioral screening remain limited. This study addresses two complementary challenges in automated detection of autism-related self-stimulatory behaviors from video: (1) identifying the optimal sequence-based neural network architecture and temporal sampling rate, and (2) characterizing data augmentation strategies for training on small behavioral datasets. For the first objective, long short-term memory (LSTM) and gated recurrent unit (GRU) models were trained on pose-derived features from the Self-Stimulatory Behavior Diagnosis (SSBD) dataset at frame sampling intervals of 1, 5, 15, 30, 45, and 90 frames. Both architectures exceeded prior convolutional neural network (CNN) baselines (62-76% accuracy), with peak accuracies of 97.5% (LSTM) and 98.75% (GRU) at a sampling interval of every 15 frames. For the second objective, ten data augmentation strategies were applied to an I3D transfer learning pipeline, with an ablation study quantifying the marginal contribution of each technique. Horizontal flip achieved the highest standalone accuracy (48.78%), while exclusion of upsampling from the augmentation pipeline produced the largest performance degradation, indicating its necessity for complex behavioral video augmentation. A personalized machine learning approach, in which per-subject models were trained and tested on temporally split segments of each video, produced consistent predictions (mean loss 1.84, SD 0.79). These results provide practitioners with concrete guidance on architecture selection, sampling rate, and augmentation strategy for video-based behavioral classification in data-scarce clinical domains.

</details>


### [111] [Workflow as Knowledge: Semantic Persistence for LLM-Mediated Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08740)
*Emanuele Quinto,Carlo Andrea Rozzi,Francesco Zanitti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a conceptual model for LLM application workflows where workflows, inference records, context snapshots, and dependencies are represented as persistent, inspectable knowledge objects. Its key distinction is between deterministic "derive" and LLM-mediated "infer," enabling semantic persistence so workflows themselves become resumable and reviewable knowledge artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM applications increasingly rely on explicit workflows (tool use, retrieval, branching, checkpointing, human approval), yet existing systems focus on execution mechanics rather than semantic representation. The authors aim to provide a principled conceptual foundation where workflows are not merely execution traces but first-class, persistent knowledge objects that can be inspected, resumed, and reviewed.

Method: The paper takes a Lisp-inspired but language-independent approach, using symbolic forms, object identity, and live-image thinking as explanatory lenses (not implementation commitments). The model represents workflow definitions, instances, inference records, context snapshots, and dependency relations as persistent knowledge objects in a shared knowledge substrate. Semantically, it distinguishes "derive" (deterministic computation over available state) from "infer" (mediated LLM judgment under declared context with executor-controlled capability policy).

Result: The result is a preliminary conceptual account of semantic persistence: workflows can be represented as inspectable, resumable, and reviewable knowledge objects, rather than merely producing knowledge and leaving execution traces behind. Formal transition semantics remain as future work.

Conclusion: The paper delivers a conceptual framework for treating LLM workflows as persistent knowledge objects with clear semantic distinctions between derivation and inference. While the account is preliminary and lacks formal transition semantics, it establishes a foundation for building workflow systems where execution history and workflow structure are unified in a shared, introspectable knowledge substrate.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) applications increasingly use explicit workflows for tool use, retrieval, branching, checkpointing, and human approval. Existing workflow systems already address many execution concerns. This paper proposes a Lisp-inspired but language-independent conceptual model: symbolic forms, object identity, and live-image thinking are used as explanatory lenses, not implementation commitments. In this model, workflow definitions, workflow instances, inference records, context snapshots, and dependency relations are represented as persistent knowledge objects in a shared knowledge substrate. Its central semantic distinction is between derive and infer: derive is deterministic computation over available state; infer is mediated LLM judgment under declared context and executor-controlled capability policy.
  The result is a preliminary conceptual account of semantic persistence: workflows do not merely produce knowledge and leave traces, but can themselves be represented as inspectable, resumable, and reviewable knowledge objects, while formal transition semantics remain future work.

</details>


### [112] [Agentic Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07984)
*Seokhoon Jeong,Mijung Kim,Taehwan Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentNAS introduces a three-phase pipeline where an LLM generates a seed architecture and decomposes it into a "slotted architecture" with interchangeable modules, defining a task-specific NAS search space. This hybrid approach achieves SOTA on 11 of 17 diverse tasks, with LLM seeds already surpassing published baselines and NAS providing complementary combinatorial gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NAS methods are efficient but constrained by manually engineered, task-specific search spaces requiring domain expertise.
