LLMs can generate architectures in open-ended spaces, but it is unclear how to optimally combine LLM-driven design with NAS-driven search. The paper aims to bridge these two paradigms.

Method: AgentNAS is a modular three-phase pipeline: (1) An LLM generates a high-quality seed architecture; (2) The seed is decomposed into a "slotted architecture" — a scaffold with named, interchangeable module slots that automatically defines a bounded, task-specific search space; (3) Conventional NAS explores this space via combinatorial recombination across slots. The LLM uses three different capability-level models to test robustness.

Result: On 17 tasks across NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS (spanning classification, dense regression, segmentation, multi-label tagging, and diverse modalities), AgentNAS achieves SOTA on 11 tasks, outperforming task-specific expert designs. Ablations show the LLM seed alone surpasses published baselines on most tasks; NAS adds further gains through slot-wise combinatorial recombination that independent LLM sampling cannot replicate. Results hold across three LLMs of varying capability.

Conclusion: The division of labor between LLM-driven architectural seeding and NAS-driven combinatorial search is broadly complementary and robust across LLM capability levels. The slotted architecture mechanism eliminates manual search space engineering while achieving strong empirical performance.

Abstract: Neural architecture search (NAS) methods have grown increasingly efficient, yet they remain bounded by manually engineered search spaces that require substantial domain expertise and must be rebuilt for every new task. Large language models (LLMs) can generate architectures in an open-ended space, but how to optimally divide the labor between LLM-driven design and NAS-driven search remains unexplored. We propose a mechanism that bridges these two paradigms: an LLM produces a high-quality seed architecture, then decomposes it into a "slotted architecture", a scaffold with named, interchangeable module slots that automatically defines a bounded, task-specific search space for conventional NAS to explore, without manual engineering. We instantiate this mechanism in AgentNAS, a modular three-phase pipeline in which each component's contribution can be measured independently. On 17 tasks spanning classification, dense regression, segmentation, and multi-label tagging across diverse modalities (NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS), AgentNAS establishes a new state of the art on 11 tasks, outperforming published baselines including task-specific expert designs. Ablation studies show that the two search mechanisms are broadly complementary: the LLM-generated seed already surpasses published baselines on the majority of tasks, and NAS delivers additional gains in most cases through combinatorial recombination across slots, a mode of search that independent LLM samples cannot replicate. These patterns hold across three LLMs of different capability levels, confirming that the division of labor is robust. Our code is available at https://github.com/alroimfebruary/AgentNAS.

</details>


### [113] [Using AI-based Learning Assistants in Higher Education: A Large-Scale Descriptive Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08748)
*Kristina Schaaff,Quintus Stierstorfer,Valerie Heckel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large-scale analysis of 77,543 distance-learning students' usage patterns of an AI-based learning assistant (Syntea) reveals that while the tool is embedded in many learners' routines, usage varies significantly across gender, age, study cluster, degree, and study mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on educational chatbots has been limited to small sample sizes and self-reported survey data, leaving a gap in large-scale, objective evidence on actual usage behavior. This study addresses that gap.

Method: Descriptive analysis of objective log data from 77,543 students enrolled in distance studies, examining usage patterns of the Syntea AI-based learning assistant across gender, age group, study cluster, degree, and study mode.

Result: Syntea is already integrated into the study routines of many learners. However, usage patterns differ notably across demographic (gender, age) and structural (study cluster, degree, study mode) contexts.

Conclusion: The study provides a novel empirical foundation for developing AI-based learning support systems and contributes the first large-scale, log-data-driven analysis of educational chatbot usage in higher education.

Abstract: In this study, we present a large-scale descriptive analysis of the use of an AI-based learning assistant (Syntea) in higher education. Based on objective log data from 77,543 students enrolled in distance studies, we examine usage patterns across gender, age group, study cluster, degree, and study mode. To date, existing research on educational chatbots has largely relied on comparatively small samples and self-reported survey data, while large-scale evidence on actual usage behavior remains limited. Our findings show that Syntea is already embedded in the study routines of many learners, but that usage differs across demographic and structural contexts. By identifying these patterns, our study provides an empirical basis for the further development of AI-based learning support and contributes a large-scale analysis of educational chatbot usage in higher education.

</details>


### [114] [Concretized Proposition Prompting Resolves Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08018)
*Changhun Lee,Minguk Jeon,Jongkyung Shin,Chiehyeon Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy — the tension between compositional reasoning and factual knowledge in LLMs — and proposes Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP), which grounds reasoning by making relevant propositions explicit. CPP excels in knowledge-intensive domains like medicine while remaining competitive in math reasoning, offering a scalable solution across model sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs face a fundamental trade-off between compositionality (logical, multi-step reasoning) and knowledgeability (factual grounding). The authors formalize this as the Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy and seek a prompting framework that reconciles both, particularly for domains like medicine where factual precision and structured reasoning are both critical.

Method: The authors propose Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP), a framework that explicitly extracts and concretizes propositions relevant to a given question. These propositions serve as grounded atomic facts that scaffold the LLM's reasoning process, ensuring that compositional chains are anchored in verified knowledge rather than ungrounded inference.

Result: CPP significantly improves reasoning performance, with the strongest gains on medical benchmarks where precise factual knowledge is essential. It remains competitive on math benchmarks where deductive reasoning dominates. The framework also demonstrates scalability across diverse foundation models and parameter sizes.

Conclusion: CPP resolves the composition-knowledge dichotomy by providing a unified paradigm that supports both logically organized reasoning and factually grounded inference. It establishes a fundamental approach that bridges composition-based and knowledge-based methods.

Abstract: LLMs often struggle to balance compositionality with knowledgeability, a challenge we define as Composition-Knowledge Dichotomy. To address this, we propose Concretized Proposition Prompting (CPP), a framework that explicitly concretizes propositions relevant to questions. The results demonstrate that CPP significantly enhances reasoning performance, particularly in medical benchmarks where precise knowledge is paramount, while being competitive on math benchmarks where deductive reasoning is prioritized. Additional experiments reveal that CPP is scalable to various foundation models and parameter sizes, being a fundamental paradigm that bridges the gap between composition- and knowledge-based approaches. Consequently, CPP resolves the composition-knowledge dichotomy by providing a solid foundation for logically organized and factually grounded reasoning.

</details>


### [115] [A safety-oriented hypothetico-deductive framework for AI-assisted differential diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08038)
*Fan Ma,Mauro Giuffrè,Donald Wright,Kent McCann,Mark Iscoe,Lingfei Qian,Mingyang Jiang,Chi Wing Ng,Na Hong,Huan He,Cathy Shyr,Qingyu Chen,Lee Schwamm,Lucila Ohno-Machado,Hua Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AegisDx is a safety-oriented LLM framework for clinical diagnosis that uses hypothetico-deductive reasoning with verification gates, outperforming standalone LLMs in diagnostic accuracy and safety metrics across multiple clinical case datasets and a real-world ED evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based diagnostic systems treat diagnosis as a one-shot prediction task, lacking mechanisms to flag high-risk missed alternatives or rigorously verify reasoning. This poses patient safety risks from diagnostic error.

Method: AegisDx coordinates specialized LLM components via role-specific contracts, structured intermediate outputs, evidence-retrieval interfaces, and verification gates. It generates broad differentials, screens for "must-not-miss" conditions, verifies reasoning against grounded evidence, and structures actionable next steps. Evaluated on NEJM/JAMA case reports, Annals of Emergency Medicine cases, and a blinded physician review of 43 Yale New Haven Health ED notes.

Result: Top-3 accuracy: 59.9% vs 52.1% (JAMA), 62.7% vs 51.4% (NEJM), 85.7% vs 68.6% (Annals of EM). Must-not-miss capture: 78.0% vs 52.0%. Blinded ED evaluation: composite safety score improved from 4.31 to 4.55 (p=2.1×10⁻⁴).

Conclusion: Engineering diagnostic AI as a safety-oriented reasoning framework rather than optimizing for raw accuracy alone yields safer, more transparent, and clinically meaningful decision support for acute care.

Abstract: Diagnostic error is a major threat to patient safety, yet current large language model (LLM) systems often treat diagnosis as a one-shot prediction task, lacking safeguards against missed high-risk alternatives or rigorous verification of their reasoning. Here, we present AegisDx, a safety-oriented framework for hypothetico-deductive clinical reasoning. AegisDx coordinates specialized LLM components through role-specific contracts, structured intermediate outputs, evidence-retrieval interfaces, and verification gates to generate broad differential diagnoses, enforce explicit screening for dangerous "must-not-miss" conditions, verify reasoning against grounded medical evidence, and structure actionable next steps. We evaluated AegisDx across three layers. On literature-derived case reports from NEJM and JAMA, with GPT-oss-120B as the shared backbone, Top-3 diagnostic accuracy was 59.9% versus 52.1% for the standalone LLM on JAMA cases and 62.7% versus 51.4% on NEJM cases. On cases from Annals of Emergency Medicine, Top-3 accuracy was 85.7% versus 68.6%; against physician-consensus must-not-miss diagnosis sets, AegisDx captured at least one such condition among its top three diagnoses in 78.0% of cases versus 52.0%. In a blinded physician evaluation of 43 real-world emergency department notes from the Yale New Haven Health System compared against GPT-5, AegisDx improved the physician-rated composite safety score from 4.31 to 4.55 on a 5-point scale (adjusted p = 2.1x10^-4), with qualitative gains in must-not-miss identification and reasoning safety. Our findings suggest that engineering diagnostic AI as a safety-oriented reasoning framework, rather than optimizing raw predictive accuracy alone, can provide a safer, more transparent, and clinically meaningful layer of bedside decision support for acute care workflows.

</details>


### [116] [When LLMs Agree, Are They Right? Auditing Self-Consistency and Cross-Model Agreement as Confidence Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08065)
*Kaihua Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper challenges the assumption that consistency (agreement among LLM judges or self-consistency) reliably indicates correctness. Through a large-scale study (265,000 samples across 53 runners), it demonstrates that agreement is only a weak, regime-dependent predictor of accuracy, with frontier models exhibiting particularly dangerous over-confidence — agreeing at high rates even when wrong. Self-consistency should be treated as a conditional proxy, not a standalone confidence score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-judge and ensemble evaluation methods are widely deployed in enterprise pipelines under the assumption that inter-model or intra-model agreement signals correctness. The authors seek to empirically test whether this assumption holds, and under what conditions agreement can serve as a usable proxy for accuracy.

Method: A large-scale cross-runner study was conducted: 53 runners each drew K=50 samples for overlapping cases on GPQA Diamond and AIME benchmarks, totaling 265,000 samples. Comparisons spanned model tiers, prompting strategies, and compute scales. Majority-correctness served as the deployment label, with analysis using hierarchical runner-clustered bootstrap and an exploratory cross-family check across three Claude tiers.

Result: Agreement is a positive but weak predictor of correctness (rho 0.20–0.59). Its utility is regime-dependent: best for unsaturated mid-tier models and compute allocation, worst for frontier models. The most consistent frontier model achieved agreement ≥0.8 on 77% of GPQA cases, yet 48% of those were wrong. Cross-family checks confirmed frontier over-confidence, with confident errors recurring across providers above a marginal-preserving null.

Conclusion: Agreement is not accuracy. Self-consistency and inter-model agreement reflect shared biases, memorized heuristics, and option-position priors rather than truth. These metrics should be used only as conditional proxies for correctness, not as standalone confidence scores. The authors publicly release de-identified per-run data and answer distributions.

Abstract: LLM-as-judge (Zheng et al., 2023) is increasingly the default for evaluating AI systems in enterprise pipelines, often scaled to ensembles (Verga et al., 2024) or "mixture-of-experts" (Shazeer et al., 2017) panels of judges. These systems share a key assumption: that consistency -- agreement among judges, or among a model's own samples -- indicates correctness. We show this assumption is unreliable. Agreement is not accuracy: a model can agree with itself, and different models can agree with each other, out of shared bias, a memorized heuristic, or an option-position prior rather than truth. We ask when agreement is nonetheless a usable proxy, in a large-scale cross-runner study: 53 runners drew K=50 samples for assigned overlapping cases across comparisons of model tier, prompting, and scale on GPQA Diamond and AIME -- 265,000 samples. Using majority-correctness as the deployment label and a hierarchical runner-clustered bootstrap, agreement is a positive but weak predictor (rho 0.20-0.59, all positive under item-clustered resampling) whose usefulness is regime-dependent: best for unsaturated mid-tier models and for allocating compute, and worst -- over-confident yet no more accurate -- for the most consistent frontier model (agreement >=0.8 on 77% of GPQA case-result entries, 48% of those wrong). An exploratory cross-family check on three Claude tiers shows the same frontier over-confidence, with confident errors recurring across providers above a marginal-preserving null. Self-consistency is thus a conditional proxy for correctness, not a standalone confidence score. We publicly release the de-identified per-run rows and answer distributions.

</details>


### [117] [Persuasion Attacks Can Decrease Effectiveness of CoT Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08066)
*Jennifer Za,Julija Bainiaksina,Nikita Ostrovsky,Tanush Chopra,Victoria Krakovna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoT monitoring can backfire under adversarial persuasion, increasing harmful action approval by 9.5%. A model-diverse fact-checking framework mitigates this, reducing policy-violating approvals by up to 45%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is considered a key safety mechanism for detecting misaligned AI agent behavior, but LLMs are known to be vulnerable to persuasion-based jailbreaks. The authors investigate whether this vulnerability extends to the monitoring setting itself — i.e., whether an adversarial agent can persuade its CoT monitor to approve harmful actions, paradoxically making monitoring counterproductive.

Method: The authors design an evaluation framework comprising 40 distinct tasks and analyze thousands of agent-monitor interactions. In the adversarial setting, agents are explicitly instructed to argue for policy-violating proposals. They then introduce a fact-checking monitoring framework where a separate fact-checker model verifies claims made in the agent's CoT before the monitor makes its decision. Crucially, they test model-diverse pairings (e.g., Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor with GPT-4.1 fact-checker) versus same-model pairings.

Result: Under adversarial conditions, providing the monitor access to the agent's CoT reasoning increases (rather than decreases) approval of harmful actions by an average of 9.5%, as the CoT serves as an additional persuasion channel. The proposed fact-checking framework with model-diverse pairings reduces approval of policy-violating actions by up to 45%, compared to only a 6% reduction when using the same model family for both fact-checking and monitoring roles.

Conclusion: CoT monitoring alone is insufficient as a safety mechanism against adversarial persuasion and can be counterproductive. Model-diverse fact-checking — where the monitor and fact-checker come from different model families — provides a substantially more robust mitigation against adversarial agents attempting to subvert oversight.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising safety mechanism for AI agents, based on the premise that visible reasoning traces can surface misaligned or deceptive behavior. While effective in standard scenarios, recent work highlights that LLMs remain vulnerable to persuasion-based jailbreaks, where natural-language arguments override model constraints. We stress-test whether this vulnerability extends to monitoring LLMs: can an adversarial agent persuade its CoT monitor to approve proposed actions that violate the monitor's policy? We design an evaluation framework with 40 tasks and analyze thousands of agent-monitor interactions, where agents are instructed to argue for policy-violating proposals. We find that in such adversarial settings, monitor access to the agent's CoT reasoning increases rather than decreases approval of harmful actions on average by 9.5%, as the scratchpad provides an additional persuasion channel. To address this, we introduce a fact-checking monitoring framework. We find that a fact-checker and monitor pairing from different model families, for example a Claude 3.7 Sonnet monitor paired with a GPT-4.1 fact-checker, reduces approval of policy-violating actions by up to 45%, compared to only 6%, when using the same model for both fact-checking and monitoring roles. Our results demonstrate that CoT monitoring alone may be insufficient against adversarial persuasion, and that model-diverse fact-checking provides a robust mitigation.

</details>


### [118] [PARA-PV: Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented PV Prediction Based on Frozen Foundation Model and Distribution Shift Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08079)
*Hang Fan,Weican Liu,Ying Lu,Dunnan Liu,Long Cheng,Wei Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PARA-PV is a physics-aware retrieval-augmented framework for PV power forecasting that retrieves historically analogous patterns, calibrates predictions against a frozen time-series foundation model, applies physics-driven distribution shift correction, and uses a regime-adaptive physics-constrained loss to improve accuracy across peak, ramping, night-time, and regular operating states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PV power forecasting is difficult due to weather variability, day-night transitions, regime-dependent dynamics, and physical constraints. Existing methods often fail to capture these diverse operating regimes, and dominant regular regimes can suppress learning of operationally critical states like peaks and ramps.

Method: The framework consists of four components: (1) Patch-level encoding of multivariate PV observations; (2) a physics-aware retrieval-augmented learner that retrieves historically similar patches and analog trajectories based on temporal shape, power level, operating state, and intra-day period to produce a base forecast; (3) a lightweight residual adapter that calibrates the base forecast against a frozen Chronos time-series foundation model to inject general temporal knowledge without overriding physical grounding; (4) a physics-aware distribution shift correction module that applies gated mean-shift and scale corrections using power, weather, timestamp, and day/night conditions; (5) a physics-constrained loss function that partitions samples into peak, ramping, night-time, and regular regimes with adaptive reweighting.

Result: The paper demonstrates improved forecasting accuracy across multiple PV operating regimes, with the physics-constrained adaptive loss preventing dominant regular regimes from suppressing learning in critical states. Code is publicly available.

Conclusion: Embedding physical knowledge throughout retrieval, calibration, correction, and loss design yields a PV forecasting framework that is both physically grounded and data-adaptive, addressing regime-dependent challenges more effectively than purely data-driven approaches.

Abstract: Accurate photovoltaic (PV) power forecasting is essential for reliable grid dispatch and renewable energy integration, yet it remains challenging because PV generation is jointly shaped by weather variability, day-night transitions, regime-dependent dynamics, and strict physical constraints. We propose PARA-PV, a Physics-Aware Retrieval-Augmented framework that embeds physical knowledge throughout the forecasting process. The framework first encodes multivariate PV observations into patch-level representations and, through a physics-aware retrieval-augmented learner, retrieves historical patches and analog trajectories that are consistent with the current window in temporal shape, power level, PV operating state, and intra-day period; this yields a physically grounded base forecast. To supplement local memory with broader temporal knowledge, the base forecast is then calibrated against a frozen Chronos time-series foundation-model prior through a lightweight residual adapter, so that general temporal regularities are adapted to PV-specific dynamics without overriding the physically grounded prediction. Because residual conditional distribution shifts persist when weather and diurnal regimes change, a physics-aware distribution shift correction module subsequently adjusts the preliminary forecast using power, weather, timestamp, and day/night conditions, applying gated mean-shift and scale corrections selectively. Finally, a physics-constrained loss function partitions the samples into peak, ramping, night-time, and regular regimes and adaptively reweights their error contributions, preventing the dominant regular regime from suppressing learning of operationally critical states. Our code is available at https://github.com/weican1103/PARA-PV.

</details>


### [119] [CausalDS: Benchmarking Causal Reasoning in Data-Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08093)
*Andrej Leban,Yuekai Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CausalDS is a benchmark that evaluates LLMs as agentic data-science agents by combining symbolic causal reasoning with realistic data analysis. It generates synthetic scenes from structural causal models (SCMs) paired with natural-language stories, deriving tasks across all three rungs of Pearl's ladder, including coding components, imperfect observations, and abstention as a first-class outcome.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks suffer from a critical schism: causal reasoning benchmarks lack realistic data analysis, while data-science benchmarks lack principled causal structure. Furthermore, current causal datasets rely on templatized variations of curated examples rather than systematic generation of novel synthetic SCMs, creating a "causal parrot" risk where models exploit memorized patterns rather than demonstrating genuine causal reasoning.

Method: Each CausalDS instance is a "scene" built from a sampled SCM with generated observational data and a synthetic natural-language story. Scenes are optionally grounded in empirical distributions from real-world datasets to retain realistic structure while using completely synthetic generation to mitigate memorization. Tasks are derived across all three of Pearl's rungs (association, intervention, counterfactual), with prediction tasks on Rung 1. An observation model introduces imperfect observations, requiring multi-step tool use and coding. Abstention is scored as a first-class outcome when questions admit no warranted answer.

Result: The benchmark jointly evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data-science proficiency, uncertainty quantification, abstention behavior, and tool-use/coding capability in an integrated, principled framework that bridges the gap between purely symbolic and purely empirical evaluation paradigms.

Conclusion: CausalDS provides a unified evaluation framework that addresses the fragmentation in current benchmarks by systematically generating novel causal structures grounded in empirical distributions, thereby enabling robust assessment of LLMs as integrated causal data-science agents across the full spectrum of Pearl's causal hierarchy.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as integrated data-science agents, combining abstract reasoning with advanced tool use. Yet the relevant benchmark landscape largely divides into symbolic causal reasoning benchmarks without realistic data analysis or data analysis benchmarks without a principled causal data-generating structure. Furthermore, existing causal evaluation datasets are often restricted to curated examples from existing sources, with diversity coming from limited templatized variations rather than from systematic generation of novel synthetic causal structures. We introduce CausalDS, a benchmark for evaluating causal reasoning in agentic data-science workflows. Each benchmark instance is a scene consisting of a sampled structural causal model (SCM) with generated observational data and an accompanying synthetic natural-language story grounded in a realistic domain. We optionally ground the composition of the benchmark components in empirical distributions obtained from real-world datasets, thus retaining empirical structure while reducing the "causal parrot" risk through completely synthetic generation. From each scene, we then derive tasks spanning all three of Pearl's rungs, with typical data-science prediction tasks appearing as Rung 1. Most tasks include a data science coding component, where the model typically needs to use several tools to arrive at the final answer due to the frequent presence of imperfect observations, which are generated by an observation model. Additionally, recognizing when a question admits no warranted answer and abstaining is treated as a first-class scored outcome. The benchmark thus jointly evaluates symbolic causal reasoning, data science, uncertainty quantification, abstention, and tool use/coding.

</details>


### [120] [Answer Set Programming Energised! End-to-End Neurosymbolic Reasoning and Learning with ASP and Energy Based Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08136)
*Jakob Suchan,Julius Monsen,Salim Baloch,Mehul Bhatt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neurosymbolic framework integrating Answer Set Programming (ASP) with energy-based models, enabling joint optimisation in continuous latent space with declarative semantics, non-monotonic reasoning, and end-to-end training for dynamic perception and reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neurosymbolic approaches lack a unified framework that fully incorporates background knowledge, constraints, and non-monotonic inference into continuous latent-space learning. There is a need for ASP-centric, robust, end-to-end trainable systems applicable to dynamic domains involving perception and interaction.

Method: Modular integration of Answer Set Programming with an energy-based model (EBM) substrate. ASP provides declarative semantics for background knowledge, constraints, and non-monotonic reasoning, while the EBM enables optimisation and learning in continuous latent space. The approach generalises recent work at the intersection of answer sets, probabilistic logic, and answer set modulo theories.

Result: The methodology is implemented in a practical platform. It is demonstrated on MNIST and evaluated on two benchmarks: the Clevr visual question-answering dataset and the MOT (multi-object tracking) benchmark.

Conclusion: The proposed neurosymbolic methodology offers a generalised, practical platform for ASP-centric end-to-end training, effectively bridging symbolic reasoning with continuous perception for dynamic real-world applications.

Abstract: We present a general neurosymbolic reasoning and learning methodology based on a modular integration of answer set programming with an energy based model substrate. Key contributions are: (1) supporting joint optimisation in the continuous latent space through explicit ASP-based declarative semantics fully incorporating background knowledge, constraints, non-monotonic inference; and (2) advancing recent works at the interface of answer sets, probabilistic logic, and answer set modulo theories by providing a generalised model and practical platform for ASP-centric robust, end-to-end training for applications in dynamic domains (e.g., involving perception and interaction). We provide a practical implementation, and demonstrate basic use and application (with MNIST), and evaluate with the visual question-answering benchmark Clevr and the multi-object tracking benchmark MOT.

</details>


### [121] [Overthinking: Amplifying Reasoning Weights to Extract Learned Secrets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08173)
*Jack Hopkins,Dipika Khullar,Fabien Roger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "overthinking" — amplifying reasoning task vectors beyond the original reasoning model to better elicit hidden information during black-box auditing. By linearly interpolating parameters between a non-reasoning and a reasoning-distilled model with α>1, and using layer-wise attenuation, overthinking surfaces secrets up to 10× more frequently than the base reasoning model across 2B–32B models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Black-box auditing of language models may miss subtle misalignment and hidden information. The authors aim to develop a method that more effectively elicits concealed behaviors or secrets acquired during training, improving the thoroughness of pre-deployment safety audits.

Method: The authors define the overthinking model as θ_Oα = θ_M + α(θ_R − θ_M) where M is a non-reasoning instruct model, R is a reasoning-distilled model, and α > 1 amplifies reasoning beyond R. They also propose layer-wise attenuation strategies to selectively boost reasoning while preserving output quality and coherence. The approach is tested across four experimental settings and model sizes from 2B to 32B.

Result: Overthinking models reveal hidden information up to 10× more frequently than the original reasoning model. The mechanism by which secrets surface depends on the secret type: some require perturbation specifically along the reasoning direction, while others emerge from any sufficiently large weight perturbation.

Conclusion: Reasoning amplification via overthinking is a promising technique for surfacing hidden information during model auditing. It exposes training-acquired secrets significantly more often, and the differential emergence patterns based on secret type offer insights into how hidden information is encoded in model weights.

Abstract: Black box auditing of language models is an essential pre-deployment tool, but it may miss subtle forms of misalignment and hidden information. To better elicit hidden information during an auditing process, we introduce \emph{overthinking}: the process of using reasoning task vectors to amplify the propensity to think out loud of reasoning models. Given the parameters of a non-reasoning instruct model $M$ and reasoning-distilled model $R$, we define the \emph{overthinking model} as $\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{O}_α} = \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}} + α(\boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{R}} - \boldsymbolθ_{\mathcal{M}})$, where $α> 1$ amplifies reasoning beyond the pure reasoning model $R$. Additionally, we introduce new layer-wise attenuation strategies that selectively amplify reasoning without losing quality and coherence of model outputs. We demonstrate that overthinking models are more likely to reveal hidden information across four experimental settings, across 2B-32B models. Our findings suggest that reasoning amplification may surface secrets or unintended behaviors acquired during training up to $10\times$ more frequently than the original reasoning model. How secrets surface depends on the secret type: some require perturbation along the reasoning direction, while others yield to any sufficiently large weight perturbation.

</details>


### [122] [A First-Principles Theory of Slow Thinking and Active Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08196)
*Hongkang Yang,Zhi-Qin John Xu,Feiyu Xiong,Weinan E*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes "active lifting," a mathematical framework for perception and thinking based on lifting probability distributions between observable and latent spaces. It derives slow thinking (active perception) from first principles, yielding a design space for large language models, a training objective akin to minimum-length coding, and a three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a rigorous mathematical formulation of thinking and perception from first principles, unifying the design, training, and inference of slow thinking models under one theoretical framework. The authors aim to explain how complex data distributions can be represented by simple function families (neural networks) and how agency in perception — including the emergence of slow thinking formats — arises naturally.

Method: The core mechanism is lifting and projection of probability distributions between observable and latent spaces. The proposed "active lifting" theory samples latent sequences and incorporates an intrinsic drive to reduce uncertainty at maximum rate. This yields: (1) a static theory subspace containing slow thinking models, (2) a representation hierarchy and sampler hierarchy, (3) an inference process with an internal time axis, and (4) a training objective resembling minimum-length coding and language invention.

Result: The theory produces: a three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking models; a unified approach to constructing encoders and generative models across all data modalities; a priori formation of human-like visual representations; and a possible solution to policy collapse. It also characterizes the agency of perception and the emergence of slow thinking formats.

Conclusion: Active lifting provides a principled, first-principles account of perception as active inference with intrinsic uncertainty reduction. It formally derives slow thinking within a hierarchical design space and offers practical by-products for advancing LLM architectures, representation learning, and generative modeling.

Abstract: As part of a series on first-principles modeling of cognitive functions, this paper attempts to provide a mathematical formulation of thinking and perception. It formally derives slow thinking or more generally, active perception, and encompasses the design, training and inference of slow thinking large language models. Our starting point is the lifting and projection of probability distributions on the observable and latent spaces, with the objective of representing complex data distributions by simple function families such as neural networks. A theory called "active lifting" is proposed, based on the sampling of latent sequences and an intrinsic drive to reduce uncertainty with maximum rate. It derives a large design space, containing the slow thinking models in a subspace that we call the static theory. These models are positioned on the representation hierarchy and sampler hierarchy induced by the static theory, and can be upgraded by climbing the two hierarchies. Active lifting further derives an inference process with an internal time axis, and a training objective that resembles minimum-length coding as well as the invention of languages. Thus, it characterizes the agency of perception, including the emergence of the slow thinking formats. Technical by-products of this theory include a three-stage pathway for improving slow thinking models, a unified approach to constructing encoders and generative models for all data modalities, a priori formation of human-like visual representations, and a possible solution to policy collapse.

</details>


### [123] [Playing ZendoWorld: Challenging AI Agents on Active Visual Concept Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08233)
*Sophia Koehler,Antonia Wüst,Inga Ibs,Wasu Top Piriyakulkij,Wolfgang Stammer,Constantin Rothkopf,Kevin Ellis,Kristian Kersting*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZendoWorld is a controlled interactive environment for studying how agents jointly perceive, hypothesize, and experiment to infer logical rules from visual game observations. Evaluating VLM-based, Bayesian, concept-discovery, and neuro-symbolic agents reveals that label prediction accuracy doesn't imply rule recovery, perception and induction are distinct bottlenecks, and VLM agents fail to design informative experiments. Human benchmarks highlight gaps in inductive reasoning, especially for complex rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building intelligent systems requires agents that can jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and design informative experiments to test those hypotheses — a capability central to scientific discovery. Existing benchmarks often fail to disentangle perception from induction or to require active experimentation. The authors introduce ZendoWorld to fill this gap with a controlled, interactive setting that isolates these sub-capabilities.

Method: The authors propose ZendoWorld, an interactive environment where agents observe visual game scenes, infer an underlying logical rule, and actively propose new scenes to gain informative feedback. They evaluate four agent classes: (1) pure VLM reasoning, (2) Bayesian particle filtering, (3) dynamic concept discovery, and (4) neuro-symbolic methods. Human subjects are also tested on the same task to provide a comparative benchmark for inductive reasoning performance across rule complexity levels.

Result: Three main findings: (1) high label-prediction accuracy on observed examples does not imply successful recovery of the underlying logical rule; (2) perception and induction constitute distinct bottlenecks that affect different agent classes differently; (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments, failing to reduce hypothesis uncertainty through active experimentation. Human data reveals a gap in inductive reasoning, particularly pronounced for more complex rules.

Conclusion: ZendoWorld represents a meaningful step toward evaluating intelligent agents on integrated perception-hypothesis-experimentation loops. The results identify concrete shortcomings — particularly in active experimentation and complex rule induction — and point toward future improvements relevant to scientific discovery and broader AI capabilities.

Abstract: A central challenge in building intelligent systems is enabling agents to jointly perceive complex inputs, form hypotheses about hidden patterns, and design informative experiments to test them. To study this problem, we propose ZendoWorld, a controlled interactive environment in which agents must infer a logical rule about visual game observations, acquire information by proposing new scenes, and refine their hypotheses based on feedback from the game environment. We evaluate several agents spanning pure VLM reasoning, Bayesian particle filtering, dynamic concept discovery, and neuro-symbolic methods. Our main findings are: (1) high accuracy in predicting labels for observed examples does not imply recovery of the underlying rule; (2) perception and induction are distinct bottlenecks for different agent classes; and (3) VLM-based agents propose near-uninformative experiments, failing to actively reduce hypothesis uncertainty. To compare these results, we collect human data on the task, which reveals a gap in inductive reasoning, particularly for more complex rules. Overall, ZENDOWORLD takes an important step toward evaluating intelligent agents and identifies concrete avenues for improvement, particularly in domains like scientific discovery.

</details>


### [124] [Compete Then Collaborate: Frontier AI Teachers Build a Verifiable Curriculum to Improve a Coding Student Beyond Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08255)
*Miseong Shawn Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a compete-then-collaborate framework where four frontier LLMs are ranked via execution-based verification, then jointly construct a curriculum. Key findings: teacher rankings don't predict student outcomes; imitation (SFT) on verified solutions degrades performance; but using the same curriculum for RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) yields significant gains, demonstrating that AI-teacher collaboration is valuable for building learning environments, not answer pools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional multi-teacher knowledge distillation merges outputs without identifying which teacher is best, often relying on biased LLM judges. The authors aim to replace subjective evaluation with execution-based verification and determine whether teacher ranking matters for student distillation, and whether imitation or reinforcement learning better leverages multi-teacher collaboration.

Method: A compete-then-collaborate pipeline: four frontier models (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) compete head-to-head on coding problems judged by unit tests and stdin-stdout checks with fairness controls. After ranking, teachers collaboratively build a verifiable curriculum. The student model (Qwen2.5-Coder at 7B and 32B) is trained under two paradigms: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on verified solutions, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) using GRPO.

Result: (1) Saturation effect: all teachers solve standard problems near-perfectly (99-100%) after self-correction; competition problems separate them (Gemini 77% > Claude/Codex 69% > Grok 50%). Student outcomes are independent of teacher ranking. (2) SFT degrades the student: MBPP-test drops from 76.7% to 72.7% (7B) and competition problems from 5.9% to 2.9% (32B). (3) RLVR improves the student from 5.9% to 8.8% on competition problems (+49% relative), reversing SFT's negative direction.

Conclusion: The value of AI-teacher collaboration lies not in pooling answers for imitation but in jointly constructing verifiable environments where students learn by doing. RLVR is the effective paradigm for leveraging multi-teacher curricula. The authors release a reproducible on-prem pipeline (NVIDIA GB10) with GRPO framework patches.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly serve as teachers generating training data for smaller students. Prior multi-teacher knowledge distillation methods merge outputs without determining which frontier model teaches best, often relying on an LLM judge biased toward its own outputs. We introduce a compete-then-collaborate framework where four frontier AI teachers (Claude, Codex-GPT, Grok, Gemini) are ranked head-to-head by an execution-based judge (unit tests and stdin-stdout checks) with fairness controls, and then collaborate to build a verifiable curriculum for a student (Qwen2.5-Coder). We report three findings. (1) Under execution verification, all teachers solve standard problems near-perfectly after self-correction (99-100%) due to a saturation effect, but harder competition problems separate them (Gemini 77% > Claude 69% = Codex 69% > Grok 50%); however, the robust student-side results do not depend on teacher ranking. (2) Imitation (SFT) on verified solutions does not improve, and can degrade, an already-competent student at 7B and 32B (e.g., from 76.7% to 72.7% on MBPP-test, and 5.9% to 2.9% on competition problems). (3) Using the same collaborative curriculum as a reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) environment improves the student (from 5.9% to 8.8% peak on competition problems, a +49% relative gain), reversing SFT's direction. The value of AI-teacher collaboration lies not in pooling answers to imitate, but in jointly constructing a verifiable environment where the student learns by doing. We release a reproducible on-prem pipeline (NVIDIA GB10) with framework patches for running GRPO on a bleeding-edge stack.

</details>


### [125] [MentalHospital: A Virtual Environment for Evaluating Psychiatric Clinical Encounters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08257)
*Yuming Yang,Xiao Sun,Yuanwei Zou,Zhengxiao Wu,Yun Chen,Jiang Zhong,Haoyang Zeng,Jingwang Huang,Kaiwen Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MentalHospital is a virtual evaluation environment that simulates complete psychiatric clinical encounters using the S.O.A.P. workflow with skill-augmented standardized patients from 1,193 de-identified EHR cases. It employs MentalEval, five domain-specific evaluators trained with SFT and DPO, to assess LLM performance dual-track. Benchmarking reveals even the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points, with mental status assessment as a key bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLMs in psychiatry evaluate isolated tasks (dialogue, diagnosis, treatment planning) rather than complete clinical encounters. There is a gap in holistic evaluation frameworks that simulate real-world psychiatric workflows and assess both clinical accuracy and process quality at scale.

Method: The authors introduce MentalHospital, a virtual environment implementing the S.O.A.P. (Subjective Interviewing, Objective Examination, Diagnostic Assessment, Treatment Planning) workflow. Standardized patients are constructed from 1,193 de-identified psychiatric EHR cases across 76 ICD-11 disorders. Evaluation uses a dual-track protocol: (1) objective comparison against EHR-derived references, and (2) subjective assessment of clinical process quality via MentalEval — five evaluators (communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, treatment appropriateness) trained using rubric-grounded supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and expert-guided Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). Clinical fidelity is validated via 22-clinician surveys, and evaluator alignment is measured with Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK).

Result: MentalHospital achieves a clinical fidelity rating of 3.88/5 from 22 clinicians. MentalEval evaluators achieve strong expert alignment with an average QWK of 0.944. In benchmarking, the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment identified as the primary bottleneck.

Conclusion: MentalHospital and MentalEval provide a validated, scalable framework for holistic evaluation of LLMs in psychiatric clinical encounters. Current LLMs, despite strong isolated-task performance, significantly underperform clinicians in complete psychiatric workflows — particularly in mental status assessment — highlighting critical gaps for future model development.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on isolated psychiatric tasks, including dialogue, diagnosis, and treatment planning, yet existing benchmarks rarely simulate complete psychiatric clinical encounters. We introduce $\textbf{MentalHospital}$, a virtual evaluation environment for LLM-based psychiatric clinical encounters. MentalHospital instantiates the Subjective Interviewing, Objective Examination, Diagnostic Assessment, and Treatment Planning (S.O.A.P.) workflow, using skill-augmented standardized patients constructed from 1,193 de-identified psychiatric electronic health record (EHR) cases spanning all major ICD-11 categories and 76 disorders. Each encounter is assessed through a dual-track protocol that combines objective comparison against EHR-derived references with subjective assessment of clinical process quality. To scale specialist judgment, we develop $\textbf{MentalEval}$, five domain-specific evaluators covering communication empathy, interviewing professionalism, clinical-note quality, diagnostic rigor, and treatment appropriateness, trained with rubric-grounded SFT and expert-guided DPO. Survey responses from 22 clinicians support MentalHospital's clinical fidelity (3.88/5), while MentalEval achieves strong expert alignment with an average QWK of 0.944. Benchmarking shows that even the strongest LLM trails clinicians by 37.28 percentage points in objective psychiatric competence, with mental status assessment as a key bottleneck.

</details>


### [126] [Different Teachers, Different Capabilities: Sub-1B On-Device Distillation for Structured Text Enrichment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08268)
*Vinay Kumar Chaganti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates distilling a large reasoning model (DeepSeek-R1, 8B) into a small on-device model (Qwen3-0.6B) for structured JSON extraction from news articles, measuring per-sub-task quality. The student achieves ~50× speedup while recovering 58% of the quality gap, with gains attributed to the teacher's reasoning capability rather than scale. A per-field routing map is proposed since no single model excels across all sub-tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-volume structured extraction from text incurs prohibitive latency and cost when using large models per item. Distilling into a small on-device model promises comparable output at a fraction of the time and cost, but the actual per-sub-task gains from such distillation have not been systematically measured.

Method: The authors distill an 8B reasoning teacher (DeepSeek-R1:8B) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B) using QLoRA with three seeds. Each news article is mapped to one JSON object containing a short summary and five categorical labels. Two teacher controls are included: a same-size non-reasoning teacher and a larger managed pipeline. Evaluation uses a blinded, reference-free, three-judge panel scoring each arm against the full article, with two non-distillation baselines: few-shot prompting and constrained decoding.

Result: The student runs at ~0.8s/article vs. the teacher's 39s, recovering 58% of the base-to-teacher gap on summary quality. It beats constrained decoding by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by +4.9. A same-size non-reasoning teacher produces students no better than the untuned base, indicating reasoning capability drives the gain. The reasoning teacher transfers writing quality; the managed pipeline transfers label diversity. Reasoning-lineage students show lower faithfulness on thin-source articles (55 vs. 74 faithful out of 93), though this subgroup is small and the effect is directional rather than statistically significant.

Conclusion: Distillation from a reasoning teacher yields meaningful on-device gains for structured extraction, particularly in writing quality, but trade-offs exist across sub-tasks. No single model excels in every dimension, so the practical deliverable is a per-field routing map that selects the best engine per sub-task for on-device enrichment.

Abstract: High-volume structured extraction pays a large model's latency on every item, so distilling the task into a small on-device model is attractive: comparable output at a fraction of the time and cost. We measure what that distillation actually delivers, per sub-task. Each news article is mapped to one JSON object with a short summary and five categorical labels. We distill an 8B reasoning teacher (deepseek-r1:8b) into a 0.6B student (Qwen3-0.6B; QLoRA, three seeds), and add two teacher controls: a same-size non-reasoning teacher and a larger managed pipeline. A blinded, reference-free, three-judge panel scores every arm against the full article, alongside two non-distillation baselines, few-shot prompting and constrained decoding. The student runs at about 0.8 s per article against the teacher's 39 s, and recovers 58% of the base-to-teacher gap on summary quality, beating its primary baseline (constrained decoding) by +16.8 points and few-shot prompting by a secondary +4.9. A same-size non-reasoning teacher trains a student no better than the untuned base, so the summary gain follows from the teacher's reasoning nature rather than its scale. Capabilities then split by teacher: the reasoning teacher transfers writing quality and the managed pipeline transfers label diversity, while a same-size instruction teacher's students stay more grounded on the 22 short, thin-source articles in the 93-item test set (74 versus 55 faithful), where the reasoning-lineage student fabricates. That grounding difference is a consistent ordering rather than a significant aggregate effect, and the subgroup is small, so we report it as a direction. Because no single engine wins every field, the deliverable is a per-field routing map for on-device enrichment.

</details>


### [127] [PolyUQuest: Verifiable Structure-Aware Web RAG over Heterogeneous Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08269)
*Ying Liu,Yi Ye,Quanyu Feng,Mingxi Ye,Mingtao Zhang,Haoyang Li,Chen Jason Zhang,Qing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PolyUQuest is a structure-aware web RAG system that models web content as a heterogeneous graph (hyperlinks, DOM hierarchy, entity-relation knowledge) and uses a two-tier router to select among three retrieval modes, achieving superior answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness with lower token consumption compared to flat-text RAG baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems flatten web pages into unstructured text, discarding the rich structural and semantic signals inherent in HTML — such as DOM hierarchy, hyperlink topology, and cross-page entity relationships. This leads to suboptimal retrieval, reduced verifiability, and inefficient token usage.

Method: PolyUQuest constructs a heterogeneous graph integrating three signal types: (1) hyperlink topology between pages, (2) DOM hierarchy within pages, and (3) entity-relation knowledge across pages. A two-tier router classifies each query by structural need and dispatches it to one of three retrieval modes: direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, or multi-hop entity reasoning. Every cited answer block is annotated with source page, heading path, and entity links for full traceability.

Result: Evaluated on the PolyU official website corpus (4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, 37,680 relations) with a multi-type benchmark, PolyUQuest outperformed existing RAG systems on answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness while using significantly fewer LLM tokens per query.

Conclusion: Structure-aware graph-based retrieval that preserves HTML/DOM signals yields more accurate, verifiable, and token-efficient QA than flat-text RAG. The system is being prepared for deployment as a student-facing service at PolyU, with an interactive demo supporting answer inspection, retrieval trace comparison, and evidence graph exploration.

Abstract: Existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems treat web pages as flat text, losing the structural and semantic signals encoded in HTML. We present PolyUQuest, a verifiable, structure-aware web RAG framework built on a heterogeneous graph that unifies hyperlink topology between pages, DOM hierarchy within pages, and entity-relation knowledge across pages. A two-tier router dispatches each query to one of three retrieval modes matched to its structural need, including direct block retrieval, cross-page graph traversal, and multi-hop entity reasoning. Every answer is fully verifiable, as each cited block carries its source page, heading path, and entity links so that users can trace any claim back to its structural evidence. We evaluate on the official websites of the Hong Kong Polytechnic University (PolyU), comprising 4,240 pages, 31,086 DOM blocks, 29,119 entities, and 37,680 relations, together with a multi-type evaluation benchmark. PolyUQuest outperforms existing RAG systems in answer correctness, coverage, and faithfulness, while consuming significantly fewer LLM tokens per query. The demonstration provides an interactive interface for inspecting cited answers, comparing retrieval traces across routing modes, and exploring evidence graph paths. PolyUQuest is being prepared for deployment as a student-facing QA service at PolyU.

</details>


### [128] [Understanding Axes of Difficulty For Long Context Tasks Via PredicateLongBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08284)
*Siddhartha Jain,Ameya Velingker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PredicateLongBench is a new benchmark that systematically stress-tests LLM long-context reasoning by having models identify the longest contiguous subsequence satisfying given predicates, scaling difficulty along multiple axes using both synthetic and real-world data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context benchmarks (NIAH, multi-hop reasoning, summarization) focus on average-case performance, are often saturated, lack robustness, and fail to systematically probe how models degrade as task difficulty scales across different axes.

Method: The benchmark asks models to find the longest contiguous subsequence of words satisfying specified predicates/constraints (e.g., lexicographic ordering) within a long input. Difficulty is systematically varied along multiple axes. Two complementary generation pipelines are used: a fully synthetic setup with random word-like strings, and a real-world setup sampling words from natural documents while preserving distributional properties. No LLM-based generation or judging is required.

Result: Frontier models struggle to perform well as task difficulty scales up along the benchmark's axes, revealing limitations in current long-context capabilities.

Conclusion: PredicateLongBench provides a robust, conceptually simple framework for stress-testing long-context reasoning that avoids judge-model dependencies and saturation issues, offering clearer insight into the failure modes of LLMs under increasing difficulty.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated rapidly improving long-context capabilities, prompting a wave of benchmarks designed to evaluate them. However, existing long-context evaluations - from Needle-in-a-Haystack (NIAH) tests to more recent multi-hop reasoning and summarization tasks - predominantly measure average-case performance, and many are either saturated or lack robustness. Notably absent is a systematic way to probe how models perform as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along various axes. We address this gap by proposing PredicateLongBench, a benchmark that stress-tests long-context reasoning by asking models to identify the longest contiguous subsequence of words in a long input that satisfies given predicates/constraints (e.g., lexicographic ordering), drawn from a broader predicate class. The central innovation of our benchmark is the identification and systematic exploration of multiple different axes of difficulty which test multiple aspects of long context understanding. We provide two complementary generation pipelines - a fully synthetic setup using random word-like strings, and a real-world setup that samples words from natural documents while preserving their distributional properties. We find that frontier models struggle to perform well as we scale up the difficulty of tasks along our axes, demonstrating the utility of our benchmark in understanding the limitations of current long-context capabilities. Furthermore, the tasks in PredicateLongBench, though challenging, are conceptually simple and do not require LLM-based generations or judges.

</details>


### [129] [Psychological Competence as a Missing Dimension in AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08285)
*Marcos Economides,Paul M. Sacher,Samuel Salzer,Alexis Michelle Abellar,Fendi Tsim,Antoine Ferrère*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper argues that current AI evaluation frameworks overlook a critical dimension: psychological competence — the capacity of human-facing AI systems to appropriately support user cognition, emotion, and decision-making during interactions. The authors define this construct, outline its core domains, and propose assessment methods without introducing a specific benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI benchmarks focus on technical metrics (accuracy, robustness, reasoning), but as AI systems increasingly serve as advisors, coaches, tutors, and companions, their effects on user psychology — how people reason, feel, trust, and decide — become a critical yet unmeasured dimension. The relevant unit of evaluation must shift from the model alone to the human-AI interaction.

Method: The paper develops a conceptual framework by drawing on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research. It defines the construct of psychological competence, delineates its boundaries, identifies core domains (framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, conversational guidance), and describes assessment approaches including scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods. No specific benchmark is proposed.

Result: The paper delivers a theoretically grounded definition and framework for psychological competence, clarifying what it encompasses and how it differs from existing evaluation paradigms. It identifies interaction-level properties that influence user psychology and maps out feasible evaluation pathways for operationalizing the construct.

Conclusion: Psychological competence should become a core consideration across the AI ecosystem — for model providers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators. Integrating this dimension into evaluation practice is essential for understanding and governing the real-world psychological effects of human-facing AI systems.

Abstract: Current AI evaluation frameworks focus primarily on technical performance, including accuracy, robustness, reasoning ability, and policy compliance. These measures remain essential, but they are not sufficient for systems that interact directly with users through natural language. Human-facing AI systems are increasingly used as advisors, coaches, tutors, and companions. In these roles, their responses can shape how users reason, interpret emotions, form beliefs, calibrate trust, and make decisions. The relevant unit of evaluation is therefore not only the model, but the human-AI interaction. This paper introduces psychological competence as a missing dimension in AI evaluation. We define psychological competence as the capacity of a human-facing AI system to support user cognition, emotional interpretation, and behavioral decision-making in ways that are appropriate to the user, context, and purpose of the interaction. This includes interaction properties such as framing, tone, perceived authority, responsiveness, uncertainty handling, and conversational guidance. Existing evaluation approaches capture parts of this problem but rarely assess these psychological effects directly. Drawing on behavioral science and human-AI interaction research, we outline a conceptual framework for psychological competence and its core domains. Rather than proposing a specific benchmark, we define the construct, clarify its boundaries, and describe how it may be assessed through scenario-based probes, structured human evaluation, and model-assisted evaluation methods. We argue that psychological competence should become a core consideration for model providers, deploying organizations, researchers, and regulators concerned with the real-world effects of human-facing AI systems.

</details>


### [130] [INTENT: An LSTM Framework for Vehicle Intention Prediction in Intersection Scenarios with Comprehensive Ablation Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08316)
*Logine M. Zaki,Catherine M. Elias*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes the INTENT framework, an LSTM-based model for predicting vehicle intentions (going straight, turning left, turning right) at intersections 2 seconds ahead of events, achieving 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous vehicles need human-like interpretation of driver intentions to handle complex interactive scenarios (intersections, roundabouts, emergency stops). Accurate intention prediction enables timely evasive actions, enhances safety, and can improve trajectory prediction (intention-conditioned trajectory prediction).

Method: The INTENT framework employs an LSTM (Long Short-Term Memory) model to predict vehicle intention at intersections with a 2-second prediction horizon. The model classifies three intention categories: going straight, turning left, or turning right. Model experiments and an ablation study are conducted on the InD dataset.

Result: The proposed INTENT framework achieves 99.71% accuracy on the InD dataset for predicting vehicle intentions at intersections 2 seconds ahead of event occurrence.

Conclusion: The INTENT framework demonstrates highly accurate vehicle intention prediction at intersections, which can contribute to safer and more agile autonomous driving, particularly in complex interactive scenarios, and support downstream tasks like intention-conditioned trajectory prediction.

Abstract: Vehicle intention prediction is a pivotal aspect in the agility and safety of autonomous vehicles in all driving scenarios; if genuine enhancement of autonomous vehicles are required, we need to make them adopt human interpretation of driver's intention especially in cases that require a lot of human interaction as well as complex driving behaviors like the ones at intersections, roundabouts and emergency cases such as sudden stops where vehicle intention prediction helps in taking the correct evasive action within a real time period where every second of action makes an impact and can prevent a catastrophe from taking place. In the worst case, it helps minimize the damage and make safety a priority. Intention prediction can also be used to enhance trajectory prediction (intention conditioned trajectory prediction). In this study, The INTENT framework is proposed using LSTM model to predict the vehicle's intention at intersections 2 seconds ahead of the event occurrence to predict whether the cars in intersections are going straight, turning left, or turning right. Various model experiments and ablation study are thoroughly tested on InD dataset achieving 99.71% accuracy.

</details>


### [131] [Blind-Spots-Bench: Evaluating Blind Spots in Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08317)
*Matteo Santelmo,Xiuying Wei,Israa Fakih,Felix Bauer,Juan Garcia Giraldo,Chengkun Li,Etienne Bamas,Emmanuel Abbé*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces blind-spots-bench, a 235-sample benchmark sourced from student questions to expose AI blind spots on tasks simple for humans. Finds closed-source models outperform open-weight by ~10%, no single model dominates all tasks, and some tasks challenge all models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks under-measure persistent blind spots in modern AI models—systems that perform well on standard benchmarks yet fail on trivially simple tasks for humans (e.g., string manipulation, drawing a dog with five legs). A benchmark purpose-built to expose these specific weaknesses is needed.

Method: Collect raw questions from students in an AI course; clean and annotate them with structured reference solutions; develop a task taxonomy tailored to the resulting 235-sample dataset; build an automated grading pipeline to evaluate language, vision-language, and image-generation models (both open-weight and closed-source).

Result: Closed-source frontier models outperform open-weight models by ≈10% on blind-spots-bench, even when achieving comparable scores on existing benchmarks. No single model dominates across all task types; certain tasks remain unsolved by every evaluated model.

Conclusion: blind-spots-bench serves as an effective diagnostic stress test that reveals concrete, persistent weaknesses in current models—weaknesses that standard benchmarks fail to capture.

Abstract: Modern AI models achieve strong performance on many established benchmarks, yet they still fail on tasks that humans find almost trivial, such as manipulating a string or drawing a dog with five legs. These examples suggest that existing benchmarks may under-measure persistent blind spots in current systems. We introduce $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$, a benchmark designed to expose such blind spots through tasks that appear simple for humans but remain challenging for modern AI. We collect raw questions from students in an AI course, clean and annotate them with structured reference solutions, and propose a task taxonomy tailored to the resulting dataset of 235 samples. We further develop an automated grading pipeline to evaluate a wide range of models, including open-weight and closed-source language, vision-language, and image-generation models. Our analysis on $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ reveals that closed-source frontier models can substantially outperform open-weight models with even $\approx10\%$ gap, even when they attain comparable performance on existing benchmarks. A more fine-grained analysis shows that no single model dominates across all task types, and that some tasks remain challenging for all evaluated models. These results highlight the value of $\texttt{blind-spots-bench}$ as a diagnostic stress test for identifying concrete weaknesses in current modern models.

</details>


### [132] [MobiDiff: Semantic-Aware Multi-Channel Discrete Diffusion for Human Mobility Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08357)
*Rongchao Xu,Lin Jiang,Dahai Yu,Ximiao Li,Taichi Liu,Desheng Zhang,Yuan Tian,Guang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MobiDiff is a discrete diffusion framework that generates synthetic human mobility data by directly denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons (spatial, activity, temporal), avoiding costly continuous/latent trace pipelines. It achieves high fidelity, privacy preservation, and is 5.3× faster than state-of-the-art methods like GeoGen.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world human mobility data is expensive to collect and difficult to share due to privacy concerns. While diffusion-based generative models have shown promise, existing methods rely on continuous or latent spatio-temporal representations that cannot natively model discrete semantic events (region, activity type, time, interval). They also require costly interpolation, latent trace construction, and coarse-to-fine generation pipelines.

Method: MobiDiff is an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework. Each check-in event is decomposed into three discrete semantic channels: spatial (region), activity (type), and temporal (time/interval). The model applies structured masking at three levels — event-level, group-level, and channel-level — to jointly capture both trajectory-level mobility patterns and within-event dependencies across channels. Generation proceeds by directly denoising these multi-channel semantic skeletons, eliminating interpolation and latent trace steps.

Result: Evaluated on large-scale real-world datasets from Atlanta, Boston, and Seattle, MobiDiff effectively preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions and remains competitive on broader mobility statistics. In terms of efficiency, it is on average 5.3× faster than GeoGen during inference. The framework also demonstrates strong privacy-preserving properties.

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion provides an interpretable and efficient alternative for synthetic mobility data generation. By operating directly on discrete semantic event structures, MobiDiff avoids the complexity of continuous/latent approaches while achieving competitive fidelity, privacy protection, and significantly faster inference.

Abstract: Human mobility data are essential for transportation optimization, urban planning, and resource allocation, yet real-world mobility data are costly to collect and difficult to share due to privacy concerns. Recent diffusion-based methods have shown promise in synthesizing realistic mobility patterns, but they typically rely on continuous or latent spatio-temporal traces, limiting their ability to natively model discrete semantic events with explicit region, activity, time, and interval structures. To address this issue, we introduce MobiDiff, an end-to-end discrete diffusion framework that efficiently generates mobility data by directly denoising multi-channel semantic skeletons, avoiding the costly interpolation, latent trace construction, and coarse-to-fine realization pipelines widely used in existing diffusion-based methods. Specifically, MobiDiff decomposes each human check-in event into spatial, activity, and temporal channels, and employs structured event-, group-, and channel-level masking to jointly capture trajectory-level mobility patterns and within-event dependencies. We evaluate generation fidelity, privacy-preserving, and efficiency on three large-scale real-world datasets from Atlanta, Boston, and Seattle. Results show that MobiDiff effectively preserves trajectory length and temporal interval distributions while remaining competitive across broader mobility statistics; it is also much faster than state-of-the-art methods, e.g., 5.3$\times$ faster than GeoGen on average during inference. These findings suggest that discrete diffusion offers an interpretable and efficient framework for synthetic mobility data generation.

</details>


### [133] [FedOPAL: One-Shot Federated Learning via Analytic Visual Prompt Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08368)
*Lingyu Qiu,Daniela Annunziata,Stefano Izzo,Fabio Giampaolo,Francesco Piccialli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedOPAL proposes a one-shot federated learning framework that uses visual prompts as feature rectifiers with local proximal constraints to align heterogeneous data distributions, enabling efficient analytical (gradient-free) aggregation that matches iterative methods' accuracy at zero server-side training cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Communication bandwidth is the core bottleneck in edge-intelligence federated learning. One-shot FL reduces rounds but existing methods have high server costs or hyperparameter sensitivity. Analytical FL offers efficient closed-form aggregation but fails under non-IID data due to static feature assumptions causing manifold misalignment.

Method: FedOPAL employs visual prompts as feature rectifiers, applying local proximal constraints to actively correct heterogeneous data feature distributions into a linearly separable space. This satisfies the theoretical assumptions required by analytical federated learning, enabling least-squares closed-form gradient-free aggregation.

Result: FedOPAL significantly outperforms original analytical methods on multiple benchmarks and achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art iterative methods while maintaining zero server-side training costs.

Conclusion: FedOPAL provides a new engineering paradigm for efficient collaboration of large models on the edge by combining the communication efficiency of one-shot FL with the computational simplicity of analytical aggregation, effectively resolving the non-IID data challenge.

Abstract: With the widespread deployment of basic models in edge intelligence, communication bandwidth has become a core bottleneck restricting the scalability of federated learning. Although one-shot federated learning alleviates this problem by minimizing communication rounds, existing iterative fine-tuning or knowledge distillation methods still face challenges such as high server-side computational costs and hyperparameter sensitivity. Analytical federated learning achieves efficient gradientfree aggregation using least-squares closed-form solutions, but in environments with non-independent and identically distributed data, its static feature assumptions fail, leading to feature manifold misalignment and severely impairing model performance. To address this contradiction, this paper proposes the FedOPAL framework. This framework adapts the visual prompts as feature rectifiers, actively correcting the feature distribution of heterogeneous data to a linearly separable space by applying local proximal constraints, thereby satisfying the theoretical assumptions of analytical federated learning. Experimental results show that FedOPAL not only significantly outperforms the original analytical methods on several benchmarks, but also achieves accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art iterative methods while maintaining zero server-side training costs, providing a new engineering paradigm for efficient collaboration of large models on the edge.

</details>


### [134] [Towards Mechanistically Understanding Why Memorized Knowledge Fails to Generalize in Large Language Model Finetuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08393)
*Lu Dai,Ziyang Rao,Yili Wang,Hanqing Wang,Hao Liu,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-tuning LLMs with new facts reveals a "Knowing--Using Gap": models memorize facts quickly but lag in applying them for reasoning. The authors use "self-patching" to diagnose this as a knowledge-circuit misalignment — memorized knowledge exists but isn't routed to the right layers. A heuristic intervention recovers 58–75% of generalization failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can rapidly memorize new facts during fine-tuning, yet struggle to leverage them for downstream reasoning tasks. The authors formalize this discrepancy as the Knowing--Using Gap and aim to understand its mechanistic underpinnings.

Method: The authors introduce "self-patching," a novel causal intervention technique that monitors internal activation dynamics during fine-tuning.
