It identifies activation locations where relocating representations (patching from later checkpoints or successful cases) substantially improves previously failed generalization. This tracks the spatial permeation of knowledge through the network over time.

Result: Results support a knowledge-circuit misalignment hypothesis: memorized representations exist within the model but are not properly routed to computation-effective layers. A simple heuristic based on this diagnostic recovers 58–75% of the oracle headroom in generalization failures. Cross-domain experiments confirm robustness.

Conclusion: The Knowing--Using Gap is a real and diagnosable phenomenon rooted in circuit-level misrouting of memorized knowledge. Self-patching provides a practical tool for both understanding and partially remedying this failure mode, with significant recovery of lost generalization performance.

Abstract: Fine-tuning LLMs to inject new knowledge faces a critical challenge: LLMs can quickly memorize new facts, yet fail to use them for downstream reasoning tasks. We formalize this failure as the \textit{\textbf{Knowing--Using Gap}}, characterized by an accuracy gap and a temporal lag between memorization and generalization. To understand this phenomenon, we fine-tune LLMs with unseen knowledge and monitor the spatial permeation dynamics of the knowledge internally using a novel intervention technique called self-patching. Self-patching identifies activation locations where relocating representations substantially improves failed generalization cases. These results are consistent with a knowledge-circuit misalignment hypothesis: memorized representations can exist internally but may not be routed to computation-effective layers. To demonstrate the practicality of this diagnostic finding, we design a simple heuristic strategy which recovers 58--75\% of the oracle headroom in generalization failure. Experiments are done cross-domain for the robustness of this finding.

</details>


### [135] [Game Theory Driven Multi-Agent Framework Mitigates Language Model Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08403)
*Runzhe Liu,Biquan Bie,Zihao Wang,Yuchao Ma,Yexin Liu,Xinghai Li,Harry Yang,Wenbo Yang,Jinzhe Cao,Shengyang Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework (G-Frame) combining Bayesian and game-theoretic principles generates high-quality synthetic data to train a 7B model (OmniChem) that matches GPT-4o mini on chemistry benchmarks while reducing hallucinations by ~79%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lightweight LLMs struggle in rule-based scientific domains because they mimic surface-level linguistic patterns rather than performing genuine axiomatic reasoning, leading to frequent hallucinations. There is a need for a scalable method to overcome this deficiency without resorting to larger, costlier models.

Method: G-Frame, an adaptive multi-agent framework that integrates Bayesian inference and team game principles, establishes an automated closed-loop for data synthesis and model training. It enforces internalization of domain constraints through structured reasoning to synthesize a specialized corpus (363,045 chains-of-thought, 199,589 QA pairs), which is then used to fine-tune a 7B model into OmniChem.

Result: OmniChem achieves performance parity with GPT-4o mini on custom benchmarks and ChemBench. It exhibits a 79.46% reduction in hallucinations relative to its base architecture. Advanced capabilities are demonstrated in molecular design and synthesis planning.

Conclusion: The adaptive multi-agent paradigm effectively overcomes inherent reasoning deficiencies in lightweight LLMs for specialized scientific domains, offering a feasible and scalable pathway for accelerating knowledge discovery in fields like chemistry.

Abstract: The application of lightweight Large Language Models in rule-based scientific domains remains severely limited by their tendency to mimic linguistic patterns rather than reproduce axiomatic reasoning, causing frequent hallucinations. Here, we show that G-Frame, an adaptive multi-agent framework integrating Bayesian and team game principles, establishes an automated closed-loop for high-quality data synthesis and model training. By forcing the internalization of domain constraints through structured reasoning, we synthesized a specialized corpus of 363,045 chains-of-thought and 199,589 question-answer pairs. The resulting 7B model OmniChem achieves performance parity with GPT 4o mini on custom benchmarks and ChemBench while exhibiting a 79.46% reduction in hallucinations relative to its base architecture. We further demonstrate the advanced capabilities of OmniChem in molecular design and synthesis planning. This work establishes a scalable paradigm utilizing adaptive multi-agents to overcome inherent reasoning deficiencies, offering a feasible pathway for accelerating knowledge discovery in specialized scientific fields.

</details>


### [136] [OmniFood-Bench: Evaluating VLMs for Nutrient Reasoning and Personalized Health Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08423)
*Qian Jiang,Zhecheng Shi,Jingpu Yang,Zirui Song,Miao Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniFood-Bench is a new benchmark evaluating Vision-Language Models on three progressive food understanding tasks — from ingredient identification to nutritional reasoning and disease-specific dietary advice — exposing a critical "Semantic-Physical Gap" where models excel at dish naming but fail catastrophically at mass estimation and safe medical recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLM benchmarks for food systems only assess coarse-grained classification (e.g., dish naming), ignoring the full reasoning chain needed for real-world dietary management: identifying hidden ingredients, estimating physical mass, and generating safety-critical medical advice. This "Systemic Information Asymmetry" between visual appearance and nutritional content remains unaddressed.

Method: The authors introduce OmniFood-Bench, built on the MM-Food-100K dataset, structured around three progressive evaluation tiers: (1) Basic Perception — identifying ingredients and cooking methods; (2) Quantitative Reasoning — estimating portion size and nutritional profiling; (3) Safety-Critical Advisory — generating disease-specific dietary recommendations. Six state-of-the-art VLMs (including GPT-5.1, Gemini-3-Flash, and Qwen3-VL-8B) are benchmarked.

Result: Models achieve near-human accuracy in dish naming (Basic Perception) but exhibit catastrophic failure in mass estimation (Quantitative Reasoning) and frequently hallucinate benign yet inappropriate advice for high-risk diabetic profiles (Safety-Critical Advisory), revealing a stark "Semantic-Physical Gap."

Conclusion: The paper establishes a rigorous trustworthiness standard for autonomous agents in public health, demonstrating that strong visual-semantic capabilities do not translate to reliable physical reasoning or safety-critical decision-making, and calls for closing the Semantic-Physical Gap before deploying VLMs in real dietary management systems.

Abstract: The rapid integration of Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) into critical infrastructure promises to revolutionize
  personalized healthcare and dietary management. However, in the domain of food systems, autonomous agents face a
  unique and persistent challenge: the "Systemic Information Asymmetry" between visual appearance and intrinsic
  nutritional composition. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on coarse-grained classification tasks, such as food
  category recognition, which fail to evaluate the intricate reasoning chain required for real-world dietary management
  -- specifically, the ability to traverse from identifying hidden ingredients to estimating physical mass, and finally
  synthesizing safety-critical medical advice. In this paper, we introduce OmniFood-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark
  constructed from the MM-Food-100K dataset. Unlike previous works, OmniFood-Bench evaluates VLMs across three
  progressive capabilities: Basic Perception (Ingredients & Cooking Methods), Quantitative Reasoning (Portion Size &
  Nutritional Profiling), and Safety-Critical Advisory (Disease-Specific Recommendations). We evaluate six
  state-of-the-art VLMs, including gpt-5.1, gemini-3-flash, and qwen3-vl-8B. Our extensive experiments reveal a
  startling "Semantic-Physical Gap": while models achieve near-human accuracy in naming dishes, they exhibit
  catastrophic failure in mass estimation and frequently hallucinate benign advice for high-risk diabetic profiles. This
  work establishes a rigorous standard for trustworthiness in autonomous agents deployed for public health. The code
  and datasets are available in: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/OmniFood-Bench-7D0B

</details>


### [137] [Applying JEPA-Style Predictive Learning to JA4-Derived Network Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08465)
*Javier Izquierdo,Aygul Zagidullina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The authors apply JEPA-style predictive learning to JA4 network fingerprints, training a Transformer on ~397K samples from JA4DB and CIC-IDS-2017. A frozen kNN probe on TLS/DNS/SSH protocol classification achieves 0.9899 cosine similarity and 0.9220 accuracy, demonstrating that JEPA objectives yield useful embeddings even with incomplete multi-view overlap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: I-JEPA and V-JEPA have shown strong results in image and video domains by predicting latent representations rather than reconstructing raw inputs. This paper investigates whether the same self-supervised predictive objective generalizes to a fundamentally different modality: compact, structured network fingerprint data (JA4 and its subfields).

Method: A Transformer-based model, JA4-JEPA, is trained using a JEPA-style objective — matching latent predictions to target encoder outputs — on JA4, JA4H, JA4S, and JA4X subfields drawn from JA4DB and CIC-IDS-2017 (~397K combined samples, though no single sample contains all four view families). The learned representations are evaluated via a frozen kNN probe on protocol-family classification across TLS, DNS, and SSH using 39,416 held-out samples.

Result: On the held-out set, JA4-JEPA achieves a cosine similarity of 0.9899 and kNN accuracy of 0.9220 for protocol-family classification (TLS, DNS, SSH).

Conclusion: JEPA-style predictive learning is effective for producing useful embeddings from JA4-derived network fingerprints, even when training data suffers from incomplete view overlap across sources. This extends the JEPA paradigm beyond vision to the network security domain.

Abstract: I-JEPA and V-JEPA learn by matching latent predictions to target encoder outputs rather than regenerating the original input, and this has worked well for images and video. We explore whether the same objective works for compact network fingerprints. We built JA4-JEPA, a Transformer-based model trained on JA4, JA4H, JA4S, and JA4X subfields drawn from JA4DB and CIC-IDS- 2017. The training data combines roughly 397K samples from both sources, though no single sample contains all four view families. We evaluated the learned representations with a frozen kNN probe on protocol-family classification across TLS, DNS, and SSH. On 39,416 heldout samples the model achieved a cosine similarity of 0.9899 and a kNN accuracy of 0.9220. These results indicate that JEPA-style predictive learning can produce useful embeddings from JA4-derived fingerprints, even with incomplete view overlap across sources.
  Keywords: JA4, network fingerprinting, JEPA, predictive representation learning, self-supervised learning

</details>


### [138] [Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) for Adaptive Semantic Modeling in Biomedical Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08490)
*Bharathwaj Vijayakumar,Sahana K. Varadaraju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DATGR is a lightweight framework that models biomedical concept evolution by dynamically rewiring co-occurrence graph edges based on estimated semantic drift, improving link-prediction AUROC by ~0.066 over static baselines without retraining embeddings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical language evolves rapidly, causing static embeddings and co-occurrence graphs to lose semantic fidelity over time, degrading retrieval and knowledge discovery performance. Existing methods fail to capture temporal semantic change efficiently.

Method: The Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) framework dynamically updates co-occurrence edges using a logistic update rule driven by estimated semantic drift. It performs lightweight, feedback-driven edge rewiring without requiring full retraining of embeddings for each time slice.

Result: On BIOMRC, DATGR achieved a mean AUROC of 0.699 vs. 0.633 for the static baseline (Δ ≈ +0.066). AUPRC remained comparable (0.738 vs. 0.744), indicating improved recall without precision loss.

Conclusion: Edge-level, drift-aware adaptation effectively captures temporal semantic change in evolving biomedical text, offering both computational efficiency and interpretability over full retraining approaches.

Abstract: Biomedical language evolves rapidly as new discoveries emerge, causing traditional text models to lose semantic fidelity over time. Static embeddings and co-occurrence graphs cannot capture such evolution, leading to performance degradation in retrieval and knowledge discovery tasks. This paper introduces a Drift-Aware Temporal Graph Rewiring (DATGR) framework that models concept evolution by dynamically updating co-occurrence edges based on estimated semantic drift. Instead of retraining embeddings for each time slice, DATGR performs lightweight, feedback-driven rewiring using a logistic update rule applied to edge weights. Evaluated on the Biomedical Multi-Relation Corpus (BIOMRC), the method achieved a mean Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic (AUROC) improvement of approximately 0.066 absolute difference (0.699 vs. 0.633) over a static baseline. Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC) remained comparable (0.738 vs. 0.744), showing that drift-aware adaptation enhances link-prediction recall without a loss in precision. These results demonstrate that edge-level adaptation effectively captures temporal semantic change in evolving biomedical text while remaining computationally efficient and interpretable.

</details>


### [139] [AI-guided stimuli discovery and generation to optimize facial emotion perception studies in autism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08533)
*Kushin Mukherjee,Na Yeon Kim,Maren Wehrheim,Ralph Adolphs,Kohitij Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autistic-neurotypical differences in facial emotion perception are concentrated in a sparse subset of diagnostic images rather than uniformly distributed. Using population-specific neural network models, the authors identified these images and, with a GAN, synthesized transformed versions that reduced group differences, establishing a model-guided framework for optimized behavioral phenotyping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies on facial emotion perception differences between autistic and neurotypical adults show inconsistent findings. The authors hypothesized this variability arises because group differences are sparse and image-specific, not uniform across stimuli, necessitating a method to discover and manipulate the diagnostic features driving divergence.

Method: Population-specific artificial neural networks were trained to predict emotion judgments separately for autistic and neurotypical participants. These models identified diagnostic images that maximized group separation. In an independent cohort, model-selected faces were tested against matched random images. Additionally, a generative adversarial network (GAN) was used to transform diagnostic images toward predicted group agreement, with phenotype-matched validation comparing original and synthesized images.

Result: Model-selected images produced significantly larger behavioral differences between groups than matched random images. GAN-synthesized transformations of diagnostic images reduced behavioral separation relative to their matched originals, confirming that the models captured meaningful, manipulable features driving group divergence.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates a model-guided framework for discovering and transforming stimuli that reveal population-specific perceptual differences, advancing behavioral phenotyping beyond averaging across fixed stimulus sets toward optimized, condition-specific assays of neurodivergent perception.

Abstract: Understanding perceptual differences between autistic and neurotypical adults requires behavioral assays that are sensitive, reliable, and mechanistically informative. Facial emotion perception is a useful test case because group differences have been reported, but findings vary across studies. Here we show that this variability may reflect image-level sparsity: autistic-neurotypical differences in emotion judgments were concentrated in a small subset of diagnostic facial expressions rather than spread uniformly across stimuli. We trained population-specific artificial neural network models to predict image-level judgments for autistic and neurotypical participants, then used these models to select novel faces predicted to maximize group separation. In an independent cohort, model-selected images produced larger behavioral differences than matched random images. We then used the same models with a generative adversarial network to transform diagnostic images toward greater predicted group agreement. In phenotype-matched validation, synthesized images reduced behavioral separation relative to their matched originals. These results establish a model-guided framework for discovering and transforming stimuli that reveal population-specific perceptual differences. More broadly, they show how behavioral phenotyping can move beyond averaging across fixed stimulus sets toward optimized assays that identify the conditions under which neurodivergent perception diverges or converges.

</details>


### [140] [CommuniWave:A Machine Learning Model for Quantifying the Degree of Temporary Informal Behavior in Urban Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08554)
*Hongye Yang,Shien Liu,Zhihao Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CommuniWave is a machine learning model that quantifies residents' informal behaviors in urban communities using street video analysis, generating Dynamic Informal Behavior (DIB) fluctuation charts to aid urban managers in resilience-oriented decision-making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current community planning follows a top-down paradigm and lacks effective metrics to quantify residents' informal behaviors, causing frequent conflicts between actual usage patterns and original plans. There is a need for dynamic monitoring tools to enhance territorial resilience under complexity and uncertainty.

Method: CommuniWave integrates three components: (1) a Behavior Capture Net (BCN) based on mmaction2 for action recognition, (2) a self-developed YOLOv10 variant (YLX) for object detection, and (3) a Behavior Eval Model (BEM) using random forest for quantifying the Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB). DIB fluctuation charts are generated from street video inputs.

Result: The model produces DIB fluctuation charts derived from street videos, enabling dynamic monitoring of informal behaviors in urban communities.

Conclusion: CommuniWave provides a data-driven tool for urban managers and designers to monitor informal behaviors dynamically, supporting refined, resilience-enhancing decision-making in community planning.

Abstract: For urban managers and designers, improving the functional attributes of urban communities to enhance territorial resilience in the face of complexity and uncertainty is crucial. Currently, community planning often follows a top-down approach and lacks effective metrics to quantify informal behaviors of residents, leading to frequent conflicts with original plans. This study introduces CommuniWave, a machine learning model designed to efficiently detect and quantify the Degree of Informal Behavior (DIB) in urban communities. The model integrates a Behavior Capture Net (BCN) based on mmaction2, a self-developed YOLOv10 model (YLX), and a Behavior Eval Model (BEM) using random forest. Ultimately, by generating DIB fluctuation charts from street videos, the model facilitates dynamic monitoring, supporting urban managers in making refined decisions to enhance the overall resilience of communities.

</details>


### [141] [SHAP-Weighted Cross-Modal Expert Fusion for Emotion and Sentiment Recognition: Evidence and Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08573)
*Adis Alihodzic,Selma Skopljakovic Hubljar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how SHAP attribution reduction methods affect XAI-guided adaptive fusion (XGAF) for multimodal emotion/sentiment recognition, finding that sum-abs reduction preserves cross-modal expert contributions better than mean-abs or median-abs, achieving performance comparable to early fusion while retaining modularity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early fusion is accurate but monolithic; late fusion is modular but loses cross-modal interactions. Prior XGAF work used TreeSHAP-guided routing but suffered from dimension-dependent bias in SHAP reduction, where mean-abs and median-abs suppress high-dimensional cross-modal experts. This paper systematically investigates how the choice of SHAP reduction method affects expert weighting and overall fusion performance.

Method: XGAF framework: tree-based mixture of unimodal and cross-modal experts with sample-level weights derived from TreeSHAP attribution magnitudes. Three SHAP reduction methods are compared — mean-abs, median-abs, and sum-abs — which differ in how they aggregate feature attributions across experts with unequal dimensionalities. Evaluated on MELD (7-class emotion, three face-sequence aggregators including Transformer) and CMU-MOSEI (3-class sentiment) using weighted F1, with McNemar significance testing and ablation studies isolating the contribution of cross-modal experts vs. per-sample routing.

Result: Sum-abs XGAF with Transformer aggregator reaches 0.5983 wF1 on MELD vs. 0.6018 (early fusion) and 0.4598 (late fusion), with no significant difference from early fusion (McNemar p=1.000). On CMU-MOSEI, sum-abs XGAF (0.6519) slightly exceeds early fusion (0.6485). Ablation reveals the main gain stems from adding cross-modal experts (especially trimodal) rather than complex routing. Mean-abs and median-abs produce near-uniform weights; sum-abs concentrates weights on the trimodal expert.

Conclusion: The choice of SHAP attribution reduction is critical when experts have unequal dimensions: sum-abs should be preferred to avoid suppressing high-dimensional cross-modal experts. A simple trimodal cross-modal expert provides most of the performance gain, suggesting complex per-sample routing may be unnecessary. This yields a practical modular fusion approach that matches early fusion accuracy.

Abstract: Multimodal emotion and sentiment recognition is commonly addressed by early fusion, which concatenates modalities before classification, or late fusion, which combines independently trained unimodal predictors. Early fusion can be accurate but monolithic, while late fusion is modular but may lose cross-modal interactions. This paper revisits XAI-guided adaptive fusion (\xgaf), a tree-based mixture of unimodal and cross-modal experts whose sample-level weights are derived from TreeSHAP attribution magnitudes. We focus on the effect of SHAP attribution reduction when experts have unequal feature dimensionalities. In this setting, mean-abs and median-abs reductions can suppress high-dimensional cross-modal experts, whereas sum-abs reduction preserves total attribution mass. On MELD 7-class emotion recognition, sum-abs \xgaf{} nearly matches early fusion across three face-sequence aggregators; the Transformer variant reaches 0.5983 \wf{}, compared with 0.6018 for early fusion and 0.4598 for probability-average late fusion. McNemar testing shows no significant difference between sum-abs \xgaf{} and early fusion on MELD ($p=1.000$), while \xgaf{} remains significantly better than late fusion ($p<0.0001$). On CMU-MOSEI 3-class sentiment recognition, sum-abs \xgaf{} reaches 0.6519 \wf{}, slightly exceeding early fusion (0.6485) and late fusion (0.5696). Ablation studies show that the main gain comes from adding cross-modal experts, especially the trimodal expert, rather than from complex per-sample routing. Diagnostics further show that mean-abs and median-abs weights are nearly uniform, while sum-abs weights concentrate on the trimodal expert. Thus, the main contribution is a transparent empirical analysis of how SHAP reduction, expert dimensionality, and cross-modal expert design affect modular multimodal fusion.

</details>


### [142] [Towards Precision Therapy in Hepatocellular Carcinoma: A Clinical-Reasoning LLM for Risk Stratification and Treatment Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08602)
*Peng Cui,Jitao Wang,Siyan Xue,Yao Huang,Haoming Xia,Dong Li,Dengxiang Liu,Weilin Wang,Liping Liu,Leida Zhang,Yunfu Cui,Tao Peng,Daolin Ji,Haitao Zhao,Wei Zhang,Xiaojuan Wang,Weijie Ma,Zongren Ding,Jinlong Li,Yuan Ding,Jiajing Zhao,Zhiyu Chen,Chengkun Yang,Ziyue Huang,Jiaqi Liu,Fusheng Liu,Yang Zhou,Xiaojuan Wang,Zhongquan Sun,Shiyun Bao,Xiaojun Wang,Ming Yang,Guangxin Li,Bin Shu,Yong Liao,Hongxuan Li,Yao Tang,Shizhong Yang,Yongyi Zeng,Yufeng Yuan,Yinpeng Dong,Jihui Hao,Jun Zhu,Jiahong Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HCC-STAR is a large language model fine-tuned on ~30,000 EMR-style narratives derived from SEER data that jointly performs risk staging, treatment recommendation with rationales, and survival prediction for hepatocellular carcinoma. In a 6,668-patient multi-center Chinese cohort, it outperformed clinical guidelines (BCLC, CNLC) and frontier models (GPT-5, Gemini-2.5 Pro), with hypothetical overall survival of 51 months under its recommendations vs. 29-32 months for guidelines. Blinded specialists rated its reasoning as trustworthy, and it surpassed resident/attending physicians in treatment accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current HCC staging systems (e.g., BCLC, CNLC) provide only coarse risk categories, missing within-stage heterogeneity and failing to leverage the rich clinical context embedded in electronic medical record narratives. There is an unmet need for a system that reads unstructured EMR text and delivers fine-grained, individualized staging, treatment, and prognosis in a clinically aligned manner.

Method: The authors curated ~30,000 HCC cases from SEER and expanded them into EMR-style narratives via a clinician-validated, prompt-based augmentation workflow. On this corpus, they trained HCC-STAR using a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework optimized with a step-verifiable composite reward, designed to move beyond surface-level guideline memorization toward genuine clinical reasoning. The model outputs risk scores, ranked treatments with evidence-based rationales, and individualized survival estimates jointly.

Result: In a multi-center evaluation across 12 Chinese hospitals (6,668 patients), HCC-STAR achieved SOTA on treatment recommendation and risk stratification vs. clinical guidelines, GPT-5, and Gemini-2.5 Pro. Hypothetical OS analysis: median survival 51 months (HCC-STAR) vs. 29 months (BCLC) vs. 32 months (CNLC). Blinded hepatobiliary specialists rated its reasoning trustworthy. The model surpassed resident and attending physicians in treatment accuracy and improved physician decision-making speed and accuracy when used as an assistant.

Conclusion: HCC-STAR represents a reliable, verifiable AI decision-support system for HCC risk stratification and precision therapy, capable of integrating unstructured EMR narratives to deliver guideline-consistent, evidence-based, and individualized clinical recommendations that may improve survival outcomes over current staging systems.

Abstract: Hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) is a common malignancy and a leading cause of cancer-related mortality. Current guidelines and staging systems provide coarse categories, but often miss within-stage heterogeneity and the clinical context in electronic medical records (EMRs). We present HCC-STAR (Hepatocellular Carcinoma Staging, Treatment And pRognosis), a clinically aligned large language model that reads routine EMR narratives and jointly outputs risk score-based staging, ranked guideline-consistent treatments with evidence-based rationales, and individualized survival estimates. We curated about 30,000 HCC cases from SEER and expanded them into EMR-style narrative training data using a clinician-validated, prompt-based augmentation workflow. On this corpus, we developed a knowledge-aligned reasoning framework optimized with a step-verifiable composite reward, moving beyond text-level memorization of clinical guidelines. In a multi-center cohort of 6,668 patients from 12 hospitals in China, HCC-STAR achieved state-of-the-art performance in treatment recommendation and risk stratification compared with clinical guidelines and competitive models, including GPT-5 and Gemini-2.5 Pro. Hypothetical overall-survival analysis showed a median survival of 51 months under adherence to HCC-STAR recommendations, compared with 29 and 32 months under BCLC and CNLC. In clinician-centric evaluations, blinded hepatobiliary specialists rated HCC-STAR's reasoning and evidence-based justifications as trustworthy. The model surpassed resident and attending physicians in treatment accuracy and helped physicians make more accurate decisions faster when used as an assistant. These findings support HCC-STAR as a reliable and verifiable decision-support system for risk stratification and precision therapy in HCC.

</details>


### [143] [The complexities of patient-centred conversational artificial intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08625)
*João Matos,Olivia Buege,Donny Cheung,Gary S. Collins,Paula Dhiman,Nan Li,Bingyu Mao,Benjamin W. Nelson,Michail Ouroutzoglou,Paul Varghese,Jonathan Amar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations reveals wide variability in communication and emotional expression. A multi-dimensional patient simulator was developed and validated via Turing-inspired evaluation (55% human grader accuracy). Testing 1,164 clinician-graded cases across 5 patient personae and 4 LLMs demonstrated that communication style significantly alters triage outcomes, warning that idealized interactions risk real-world underperformance and health disparities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health chatbot development and evaluation predominantly rely on cooperative, articulate simulated patients, which fails to capture the real-world diversity of patient communication patterns, emotional expression, and conversational strategies. This gap risks deploying systems that underperform for diverse real users and amplify health disparities.

Method: The authors analyzed 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations to characterize variability in communication. They built a multi-dimensional patient simulator that separately models: (1) clinical content, (2) emotional state, (3) conversational strategy, and (4) communication style. A Turing-inspired evaluation with 15 human graders assessed whether simulated conversations could be distinguished from real ones. Five distinct patient personae were then used across 1,164 clinician-graded cases to evaluate urgency assessment performance of four LLMs.

Result: Simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones, with human graders achieving only 55% accuracy (near chance). Communication style was shown to significantly alter triage outcomes across the four LLMs tested, demonstrating that non-clinical conversational factors can substantively affect clinical decision support.

Conclusion: Patient-centered conversational AI must accommodate communication diversity. Systems designed for idealized rather than realistic interactions risk underperforming and amplifying health disparities when deployed in real-world settings. The multi-dimensional patient simulator offers a robust tool for more ecologically valid evaluation of health LLMs.

Abstract: Consumer-facing health chatbots powered by large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for symptom assessment. However, chatbot development and evaluation often rely on cooperative, articulate, simulated patients. We analysed 2,053 real patient-chatbot conversations and found that communication patterns and expression of emotions vary widely across users. We developed a patient simulator that separately models clinical content, emotional state, conversational strategy, and communication style. In a Turing-inspired evaluation of realism with 15 human graders, simulated conversations were nearly indistinguishable from real ones, with human graders achieving an accuracy of 55%. We used five distinct patient personae, across 1,164 clinician-graded cases, to evaluate the performance of four LLMs in urgency assessment. We found that communication style can significantly alter triage outcomes. Patient-centred conversational artificial intelligence must accommodate communication diversity: systems designed for idealised, rather than realistic, interactions risk underperforming and amplifying health disparities when deployed in the real world.

</details>


### [144] [Formal Mechanisms for Market Stability in Self-Interested Agent Societies: A Marketplace Simulation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08652)
*Eugene Ng Yi Sheng,Bingquan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers simulate a multi-agent marketplace where 18 LLM agents trade under different governance mechanisms. Mediation outperforms all other mechanisms in maintaining cooperation and honest-agent utility, even under sustained, iteratively optimised adversarial attacks — bending but never breaking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-interested agents in repeated social dilemmas tend toward defection, causing market collapse. The authors investigate which formal communication-layer mechanisms can sustain cooperative trade and market stability, and how resilient those mechanisms are against adversarial exploitation.

Method: A multi-agent marketplace simulation with 18 DeepSeek-V3 LLM agents possessing complementary production specialties trades within a constrained social network over 200 rounds. Phase 1 compares eight governance mechanisms under progressive troll injection. Phase 2 red-teams the best mechanism (Mediation) using iteratively prompt-optimised LLM-driven adversarial trolls across six attack generations (v1–v6).

Result: Mediation was identified as the top-performing mechanism across all conditions. The strongest adversarial attack (v6) reduced honest-agent utility by only 13.3% and failed to collapse the market. Mediation enabled recovery even under sustained pressure, demonstrating that it can be bent but not broken.

Conclusion: Mediation is a robust governance mechanism: it sustains positive honest-agent utility under optimised adversarial attacks where other mechanisms fail. The authors define adversarial robustness operationally as the ability to maintain positive utility despite optimised attack, establishing Mediation as a resilient institutional design for LLM-agent societies.

Abstract: Self-interested agents, left unconstrained, tend toward defection in repeated social dilemmas, causing cooperative gains from trade to collapse. This paper investigates what formal mechanisms, layered on top of unrestricted communication, are sufficient for a society of such agents to maintain market stability, and how resilient those mechanisms are to adversarial attack. We instantiate the research question as a multi-agent marketplace simulation where 18 LLM agents (DeepSeek-V3) with complementary production specialties must trade within a constrained social network to obtain utility. We conduct two experimental phases: (1) a mechanism comparison across eight conditions under progressive troll injection over 200 rounds, identifying Mediation as the top-performing mechanism; and (2) adversarial red-teaming of Mediation using iteratively prompt-optimised LLM-driven trolls, finding that the best attack (v6) reduces honest-agent utility by 13.3% but cannot collapse the market. Mediation enables recovery even under sustained adversarial pressure. We define adversarial robustness as a mechanism's ability to sustain positive honest-agent utility under optimised attack, and find that Mediation is robust: it can be bent but not broken.

</details>


### [145] [SolarChain-Eval: A Physics-Constrained Benchmark for Trustworthy Economic Agents in Decentralized Energy Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08681)
*Shilin Ou,Yifan Xu,Luyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes SolarChain-Eval, a physics-constrained benchmark for evaluating trustworthy AI agents in decentralized energy markets, revealing a utility-safety trade-off where RL agents improve market utility but can exploit invalid data, while an LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer improves auditability but cannot fully compensate for misspecified reward functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems in cyber-physical environments (e.g., decentralized energy markets) need evaluation beyond task performance to include trustworthiness — agents may exploit invalid physical data, create artificial liquidity, and produce unstable governance, yet existing benchmarks lack physics-constrained, multi-dimensional trustworthiness evaluation.

Method: SolarChain-Eval formulates market governance as a Gymnasium-compatible Markov Decision Process with hourly decisions. It evaluates policies across six dimensions: market utility, physical safety, slippage, action smoothness, spatial fairness, and auditability. An LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer is integrated: the Planner sets episode-level action bounds and audit rules, while the Auditor reviews and revises high-risk actions, with all interventions logged (trigger signals, proposed/revised actions, audit rationales).

Result: Experiments with static, random, myopic, RL, and RL+LLM policies reveal a clear utility-safety trade-off. RL agents improve market utility but still produce unsafe behaviors. When the physics penalty is removed, reward-maximizing agents exploit invalid generation and increase artificial liquidity. The LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability and mitigates selected risks but cannot fully compensate for a misspecified reward function.

Conclusion: Trustworthy agentic AI evaluation requires both physical constraints and transparent intervention traces. Reward function design remains critical, as LLM-based auditing layers cannot remedy fundamentally misspecified objectives. The benchmark and data are released open-access on GitHub.

Abstract: As agentic AI systems are increasingly applied to cyber-physical environments, their evaluation requires assessment of both task performance and trustworthiness. In decentralized energy markets, autonomous agents may improve market utility, but may also exploit invalid physical data, create artificial liquidity, and produce unstable governance decisions. Therefore, we propose SolarChain-Eval, a physics-constrained benchmark for evaluating trustworthy economic agents. It formulates market governance as a Gymnasium-compatible Markov Decision Process, where agents make hourly decisions. SolarChain-Eval evaluates each policy across multiple dimensions, including market utility, physical safety, slippage, action smoothness, spatial fairness, and auditability. To support agentic evaluation, SolarChain-Eval incorporates an LLM-based Planner/Auditor layer. The Planner defines episode-level action bounds and audit rules, while the Auditor reviews and revises high-risk actions. All interventions are recorded through structured logs, including trigger signals, proposed actions, revised actions, and audit rationales. Experiments with static, random, myopic, RL, and RL+LLM policies reveal a clear utility-safety trade-off. RL agents improve market utility but can still produce unsafe behavior. When the physics penalty is removed, reward-maximizing agents exploit invalid generation and increase artificial liquidity. The LLM Planner/Auditor improves auditability and mitigates selected risks, but it cannot fully compensate for a misspecified reward function. These results indicate that trustworthy agentic AI evaluation requires both physical constraints and transparent intervention traces. We release data and code as open access on GitHub for replicability.

</details>


### [146] [Remember When It Matters: Proactive Memory Agent for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08716)
*Yifan Wu,Lizhu Zhang,Yuhang Zhou,Mingyi Wang,Bo Peng,Serena Li,Xiangjun Fan,Zhuokai Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "behavioral state decay" — the failure mode where decision-relevant information gets buried or lost in long trajectories. To counter this, the authors propose an active memory agent that runs alongside an unmodified action agent, selectively injecting memory-grounded reminders from a structured memory bank into the context window. The approach is plug-and-play and shows significant pass@1 improvements (+8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench 2.0, +6.8 pp on τ²-Bench) over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In long-horizon tasks, relevant state (task requirements, facts, prior attempts, diagnoses, open subgoals) becomes scattered across an expanding trajectory and can fall outside the context window, causing the action agent to make decisions without access to critical information. This phenomenon, termed "behavioral state decay," motivates an active memory mechanism rather than passive retrieval.

Method: A separate memory agent operates alongside a frozen, unmodified action agent. The memory agent: (1) updates a structured memory bank from the recent trajectory, and (2) decides whether to inject a memory-grounded reminder or remain silent. The module is designed to be plug-and-play with frontier action agents and existing agent harnesses. An open-weight version is trained (Qwen3.5-27B on SETA) using SFT and GRPO.

Result: On Terminal-Bench 2.0: +8.3 pp pass@1 improvement. On τ²-Bench: +6.8 pp pass@1 improvement. Gains hold for both weaker and stronger action agents. Ablations show selective intervention outperforms passive bank exposure, always-on injection, advisor-only guidance, and general retrieval. The trained Qwen3.5-27B shows improved validation reward and partial transfer to Terminal-Bench.

Conclusion: Active, selective memory intervention via a dedicated memory agent effectively mitigates behavioral state decay in long-horizon agent tasks. The plug-and-play nature makes it broadly applicable, and early results on training open-weight memory policies are promising.

Abstract: In long-horizon tasks, decision-relevant state is often scattered across an expanding trajectory, while the action agent must surface it and act. As trajectories grow, task requirements, environment facts, prior attempts, diagnoses, and open subgoals can be buried in the context window or pushed beyond it, failing to influence decisions when needed. We call this failure mode "behavioral state decay". We study memory as an active intervention mechanism rather than passive retrieval. A separate memory agent runs alongside an unmodified action agent, updating a structured memory bank from the recent trajectory and deciding whether to inject a memory-grounded reminder or remain silent. The module is plug-and-play with frontier action agents and existing agent harnesses. Across Terminal-Bench 2.0 and $τ^2$-Bench, it improves pass@1 for both weaker and stronger action agents, with gains of +8.3 pp on Terminal-Bench and +6.8 pp on $τ^2$-Bench. Ablations show that selective intervention outperforms passive bank exposure, always-on injection, advisor-only guidance, and general retrieval. As an early step toward open-weight memory policies, we train Qwen3.5-27B on SETA using SFT and GRPO, improving validation reward and achieving partial transfer to Terminal-Bench.

</details>


### [147] [The Illusion of Equivalency: Statistical Characterization of Quantization Effects in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08734)
*Baha Rababah,Cuneyt Gurcan Akcora,Carson K. Leung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Quantization preserves task accuracy but alters model behavior. The authors introduce "correctness agreement" — overlap in correct predictions between base and quantized models — and show behavioral divergence emerges at moderate quantization levels. Attention analysis reveals non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths, with query/key projections being more sensitive than value/output projections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of post-training quantization relies on accuracy and perplexity, which the authors argue fail to capture behavioral changes. There is a need for metrics that reveal when a quantized model makes different decisions than its base counterpart, even at similar accuracy levels.

Method: The authors propose "correctness agreement," a decision-level metric measuring overlap in correct predictions between base and quantized models. They analyze quantization as a structural operator on attention weights, using statistical and distributional measures to quantify layer-wise distortions across multiple models and quantization schemes (8-bit to 2-bit).

Result: Behavioral divergence emerges under moderate quantization even when task performance appears preserved. Non-linear breakpoints appear at low bit-widths. Query and key projections are consistently more sensitive to quantization than value and output projections.

Conclusion: There exists an "illusion of equivalence" between base and quantized models. Behavioral evaluation via metrics like correctness agreement should complement conventional performance metrics when deploying quantized LLMs.

Abstract: Post-training quantization is widely used to deploy large language models in resource-constrained settings, yet its evaluation relies almost exclusively on accuracy and perplexity. We show that these metrics fail to capture behavioral changes induced by quantization. We introduce correctness agreement, a decision-level metric that measures overlap in correct predictions between a base model and its quantized variants, independent of absolute accuracy. Across multiple models and quantization schemes from 8-bit to 2-bit, we find that behavioral divergence emerges under moderate quantization even when task performance appears preserved. To explain this effect, we analyze quantization as a structural operator on attention weights and quantify layer-wise distortions using statistical and distributional measures. Our results reveal non-linear breakpoints at low bit-widths and show that query and key projections are consistently more sensitive than value and output projections. These findings expose an illusion of equivalence between base and quantized models and motivate behavioral evaluation beyond conventional performance metrics.

</details>


### [148] [AUTOPILOT VQA: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Incident-Centric Dashcam Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08745)
*Siddharth Damodharan,Radhika Gupta,Ali Alshami,Ryan Rabinowitz,Jugal Kalita*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AUTOPILOT-VQA is a benchmark for evaluating vision-language models on safety-critical reasoning in autonomous driving, using structured VQA questions grounded in real-world dashcam incidents and near-incidents across multiple safety-relevant categories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autonomous driving models (VLMs, LLMs, MLLMs) excel at scene understanding and decision-making, but there is no reliable way to evaluate whether they can reason about safety-critical incidents. Existing benchmarks focus on object recognition rather than temporally grounded, safety-aware reasoning about real-world driving incidents and near-incidents.

Method: The authors construct AUTOPILOT-VQA, an incident-centric VQA benchmark from dashcam footage capturing real-world driving incidents and near-incidents. The dataset features structured questions spanning categories such as weather/lighting, traffic environment, road layout, road surface, signage, involved entities, accident occurrence, impact location, and avoidability reasoning. Questions require models to reason about both contextual scene properties and event-level incident details.

Result: The benchmark serves as a standardized evaluation framework released as part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition, enabling systematic assessment of VLM/LLM/MLLM reliability in safety-critical driving scenarios.

Conclusion: AUTOPILOT-VQA advances evaluation beyond object recognition toward temporally grounded, safety-aware reasoning, supporting the development of more interpretable, robust, and safety-conscious vision-language systems for real-world autonomous driving.

Abstract: Recent advances in Vision-Language Models, Large Language Models, and Multimodal Large Language Models have improved autonomous driving tasks such as scene understanding, decision making, trajectory prediction, and visual question answering. However, evaluating whether these models can reliably reason about safety-critical incidents remains challenging. To address this gap, we present AUTOPILOT-VQA, an incident-centric visual question answering benchmark for dashcam video understanding. The dataset evaluates different systems through structured questions designed around real-world driving incidents and near-incidents. The benchmark covers diverse safety-relevant categories, including weather and lighting conditions, traffic environment, road layout, road surface state, signage, involved entities, accident occurrence, impact location, and avoidability-related reasoning. By requiring models to answer grounded questions about both contextual scene properties and event-level incident details, AUTOPILOT-VQA moves beyond object recognition toward temporally grounded, safety-aware reasoning. The dataset is released as part of the AUTOPILOT CVPR 2026 competition and provides a standardized benchmark for assessing the reliability of autonomous driving systems in different scenarios. Our benchmark support developments for more interpretable, robust, and safety-conscious vision-language systems for real-world autonomous driving.

</details>


### [149] [Ideas Have Genomes: Benchmarking Scientific Lineage Reasoning and Lineage-Grounded Idea Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08758)
*Yifan Zhou,Qihao Yang,Yan Li,Donggang Li,Xiru Hu,Hokin Deng,Ziyang Gong,Xuanyi Zhou,Huacan Wang,Xiangchao Yan,Wanghan Xu,Wenlong Zhang,Shaofeng Zhang,Yue Zhou,Yifan Yang,Zhihang Zhong,Xue Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IG-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating AI scientific reasoning through the lens of evolutionary lineage — representing papers as "Idea Genomes" and tracking how ideas are inherited, mutated, or imported across research. It exposes that even the best LLM-based scientists achieve only 27.3% accuracy on lineage reasoning, revealing a compositional bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific progress follows an evolutionary, inheritance-based structure — ideas recombine, mutate, and descend from prior work. Existing AI benchmarks ignore whether systems can reason about or generate ideas following this lineage structure, creating a gap in evaluating scientific AI capabilities.

Method: The authors introduce the IdeaGene framework: each paper is decomposed into minimal, typed, evidence-grounded "Idea Genome" objects, and pairwise "GenomeDiff" records capture six evolutionary dynamics (inheritance, mutation, loss, external import, novel insertion). IG-Bench includes 1,961 golden lineage traces, 1,085 Idea Genome objects, and 920 GenomeDiff records across 10 domains. Two evaluation modes are provided: (1) IG-Exam — 42 task types and 1,029 instances testing closed-form lineage reasoning (abstraction, inheritance tracing, evolutionary reasoning, verification); (2) IG-Arena — a generation task scored by the Population-Evolution Score (PES), measuring whether a generated proposal coherently descends from a lineage population by inheriting correct Idea Genome objects, varying meaningfully, and offering selection value. Fourteen LLM-based scientist systems are benchmarked.

Result: The strongest system achieves only 27.3% exact accuracy on lineage reasoning tasks. Structured lineage context does not uniformly improve all systems; instead, it reshuffles system rankings, indicating that current models lack robust compositional reasoning over scientific inheritance structures.

Conclusion: Current LLM-based scientist systems exhibit a fundamental compositional bottleneck in scientific lineage reasoning. The IG-Bench framework demonstrates that structured lineage awareness is not a universal enhancer but a discriminative signal that reorders system capabilities, highlighting the need for architectures that can better model the evolutionary, recombinatory nature of scientific knowledge.

Abstract: Scientific ideas rarely start from a blank page. They inherit mechanisms, repair known limitations, and recombine pieces of earlier work, much like biological genomes. Current benchmarks still say little about whether AI systems can follow this inheritance structure. We present IdeaGene-Bench (IG-Bench), a benchmark for scientific lineage reasoning and lineage-grounded idea generation. IG-Bench is organized around the IdeaGene framework: each paper or proposal is represented as a set of minimal, typed, evidence-grounded Idea Genome objects, and a GenomeDiff aligns these objects to record inheritance, mutation, loss, external import, and novel insertion under six operational evolutionary dynamics. The benchmark contains 1,961 golden lineage traces, 1,085 curated Idea Genome objects, and 920 pairwise GenomeDiff records across 10 scientific domains. It supports two evaluations. IG-Exam (42 task types, 1,029 instances) tests closed-form lineage reasoning across Idea Genome abstraction, inheritance tracing, evolutionary reasoning, and lineage verification. IG-Arena evaluates generation with a lineage-conditioned Population-Evolution Score(PES), asking whether a proposal can be inserted as a coherent descendant of a given lineage population: it should inherit the right Idea Genome objects, vary meaningfully from nearby work, and offer selection value for future research. Experiments on 14 LLM-based scientists expose a compositional bottleneck. The strongest system reaches only 27.3% exact accuracy on lineage reasoning, and structured lineage context reshuffles system rankings rather than helping every participant uniformly.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [150] [Towards the Explainability of Temporal Graph Networks via Memory Backtracking and Topological Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07716)
*Yazheng Liu,Xi Zhang,Sihong Xie,Hui Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes MemExplainer, a novel explanation method for Temporal Graph Networks (TGNs) that attributes predictions by tracing influence through both the topology attribution tree and a memory backtracking tree, addressing the gap that existing explainers overlook the memory module's role in capturing historical event influence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explanation methods for TGNs overlook the memory module — the core component that records and updates node histories — leaving the influence of past events on model predictions unexplored. This limits trustworthiness.

Method: The method constructs two structures: (1) a topology attribution tree that captures influence from neighbors and their memory vectors, and (2) a memory backtracking tree that quantifies how historical events shape node memory vectors. Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP) is applied within TGNs to ensure that total contribution of events equals the model logits. To address unfaithfulness from naive top-k selection caused by nonlinear logit-to-probability mapping, optimization objectives are designed to identify truly important events.

Result: Experiments across nine temporal graph datasets spanning node property prediction, link prediction, and graph classification tasks demonstrate that MemExplainer provides faithful explanations and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: By explicitly modeling the memory module's influence via attribution trees and LRP, coupled with optimization-based event selection, the proposed approach achieves superior explanation faithfulness for TGNs across diverse tasks and datasets.

Abstract: Temporal graphs are ubiquitous in real-world applications and Temporal Graph Networks (TGNs) have achieved superior predictive accuracy. Understanding which historical events drive model predictions can enhance trustworthiness of TGNs. Existing explanation methods overlook the memory module, the core component that records and updates node histories, leaving the influence of past events unexplored. To address this, we attribute TGNs predictions through the topology attribution tree and memory backtracking tree. The topology attribution tree captures the influence of neighbors and their memory vectors, then the memory backtracking tree quantifies how historical events shape node memory vectors. We apply the LRP in TGNs, ensuring that the total contribution of events equals the logits of model. Finally, top-k selection may be unfaithful due to the nonlinear mapping from logits to probabilities, we design optimization objectives to identify the important events. Experiments on nine temporal graph datasets, spanning node property prediction, link prediction tasks and graph classification tasks, show that our method provides faithful explanations and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/yazhengliu/MemExplainer

</details>


### [151] [Who Gets Missed in the Tail? Thresholded Subgroup Underdiagnosis in Long-Tailed Chest X-ray Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07717)
*Ha-Hieu Pham,Hai-Dang Nguyen,Dang P. M. Cao,Thanh-Huy Nguyen,Min Xu,Trung-Nghia Le,Ulas Bagci,Huy-Hieu Pham*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Audit study showing that fairness in CXR classification for rare findings depends jointly on finding, subgroup, and operating threshold—not label frequency or ranking metrics alone. Tail-aware weighting plus thresholding substantially reduces missed rare-positive patients across sex, age, race, and insurance subgroups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Even with acceptable aggregate ranking performance (e.g., mAP), long-tailed CXR classifiers can miss rare-positive patients, particularly within demographic subgroups. The authors frame this as a pre-deployment fairness audit: after scores become binary decisions, which patients are systematically missed?

Method: A diagnostic ladder is used to isolate four components: (1) class-level long-tail losses, (2) subgroup-aware weighting, (3) group robustness (GroupDRO reference), and (4) threshold selection. Paired bootstrap contrasts evaluate FNR reductions. Experiments span VinDr-CXR and MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT datasets across sex, age, race, and insurance subgroups.

Result: On VinDr-CXR, group-tail weighting + tail-aware thresholding reduces tail FNR from 0.665→0.269, sex worst-group FNR from 0.705→0.157, and age worst-group FNR from 0.822→0.133, with macro-mAP improving from 0.611→0.635. On MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, tail FNR drops from 0.866→0.741 with worst-group FNR reductions across all demographics; residual missed-positive rates remain high. GroupDRO alone fails to eliminate rare subgroup misses.

Conclusion: Rare-label fairness in CXR is a joint function of finding, subgroup, and operating threshold—not reducible to label frequency or ranking metrics. The study supports a narrow audit claim and demonstrates that threshold-aware mitigation strategies are essential for equitable deployment.

Abstract: In chest X-ray (CXR) classification, acceptable ranking performance can still leave rare-positive patients below threshold, especially within subgroups. We study this pre-deployment fairness problem as an audit question: after a long-tailed multi-label CXR model is converted from scores into decisions, who is missed? Across VinDr-CXR and MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, we use a diagnostic ladder to separate class-level long-tail losses, subgroup-aware weighting, group robustness, and threshold selection. On VinDr-CXR, group-tail weighting followed by tail-aware thresholding reduces tail FNR from 0.665 to 0.269, sex worst-group FNR from 0.705 to 0.157, and age worst-group FNR from 0.822 to 0.133, while macro-mAP increases from 0.611 to 0.635. On MIMIC-CXR/CXR-LT, the same score-to-threshold comparison reduces tail FNR from 0.866 to 0.741 and lowers worst-group FNR across sex, age, race, and insurance; residual missed-positive rates nevertheless remain high. Paired bootstrap contrasts on VinDr support the thresholded FNR reductions, and GroupDRO reference runs indicate that aggregate group robustness alone does not remove rare subgroup misses in this setting. The study supports a narrow audit claim: rare-label fairness in CXR depends jointly on the finding, subgroup, and operating threshold, not on label frequency or ranking metrics alone.

</details>


### [152] [LLT: Local Linear Transformer for PDE Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07718)
*Oded Ovadia,Eli Turkel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLT (Local Linear Transformer) combines linear global attention with local spatial mixing to overcome standard attention's quadratic scaling and lack of local inductive bias for PDE operator learning, achieving competitive accuracy with 1.8–2.5× faster training than Transolver across diverse PDE problems and discretizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention in transformer-based neural operators for PDEs suffers from two key limitations: (1) quadratic computational complexity in the number of nodes, making it expensive for large meshes; and (2) no explicit bias toward local interactions, which are fundamental to most physical systems governed by PDEs.

Method: The proposed Local Linear Transformer (LLT) architecture combines two complementary mechanisms: linear global attention (reducing complexity from quadratic to linear) and local spatial mixing (injecting locality bias). It also incorporates coordinate and geometry information to handle unstructured and structured meshes across different discretization schemes.

Result: LLT achieves competitive or lower relative L2 error compared to existing neural operator and transformer baselines across elasticity, plasticity, airfoil flow, pipe flow, and Darcy flow problems. On matched structured discretizations, training wall-clock time is reduced by 1.8–2.5× relative to Transolver. The method also scales to a 3D car aerodynamics problem with 32,186 unstructured mesh points per sample.

Conclusion: LLT provides an accurate and computationally efficient operator learning framework that generalizes across diverse PDE settings, discretization types (FEM, FVM, FDM), and mesh structures (structured and unstructured), making it a practical alternative to both standard transformer operators and other neural operator baselines.

Abstract: Neural operators have become a common approach for learning PDE solution maps and accelerating numerical simulations. Transformer-based neural operators are of particular interest, since attention can learn long-range dependencies in the computational domain. However, standard attention has two major limitations when applied to PDEs: it scales quadratically with the number of computational nodes, and it lacks an explicit bias toward local interactions. To address these issues, we introduce Local Linear Transformer (LLT) for PDE operator learning. The architecture combines linear global attention with local spatial mixing, and incorporates coordinate and geometry information. We evaluate LLT on several PDE problems, including elasticity, plasticity, airfoil flow, pipe flow, and Darcy flow. The reference data for these problems span finite-element, finite-volume, and finite-difference discretizations on structured and unstructured meshes. Compared with other neural-operator and transformer baselines from prior studies, LLT achieves competitive or lower relative $L_2$ error across these problems. On matched structured discretizations, wall-clock time per training iteration is reduced by factors of 1.8 to 2.5 relative to Transolver. We also scale the approach and apply it to a three-dimensional car aerodynamics dataset with 32,186 unstructured mesh points per sample. Together, these results indicate that LLT provides an accurate and computationally efficient operator for PDE problems across discretizations, mesh types, and problem settings.

</details>


### [153] [ReCoLoRA: Spectrum-Aware Recursive Consolidation for Continual LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07719)
*Wentao Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReCoLoRA is a continual fine-tuning framework that recursively consolidates low-rank adapters using SVD-based initialization and spectrum-aware rank selection, preventing catastrophic forgetting across sequential tasks while training fewer parameters than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LoRA-style fine-tuning stacks independent low-rank updates on frozen weights across a task sequence, causing each new task to overwrite previous ones (catastrophic forgetting). Existing continual learning approaches for LLMs lack principled mechanisms to consolidate knowledge from prior tasks while maintaining parameter efficiency.

Method: ReCoLoRA operates in three stages per task: (1) adapters are initialized from a randomized SVD of the pretrained weight; (2) per-layer effective ranks are selected automatically via an elbow criterion on singular value decay curves; (3) the principal subspace is adapted before residual capacity is opened. Crucially, before each new task, ReCoLoRA re-decomposes the current effective weight (not the original pretrained weight) into a frozen residual, a slowly updated principal component, and a fresh adapter — a recursive consolidation strategy that ensures each task starts from a model that has absorbed all predecessors.

Result: On a six-task continual GLUE benchmark across four 7-8B parameter backbones, ReCoLoRA achieves the best final average score on three of four backbones compared to rank-swept LoRA, PiSSA, AdaLoRA, and DoRA baselines, while training fewer parameters. An oracle-routed task-bank variant provides an upper bound under full task isolation.

Conclusion: ReCoLoRA demonstrates that recursive SVD-based consolidation of low-rank adapters effectively mitigates forgetting in continual LLM fine-tuning, outperforming both standard and adaptive LoRA variants with greater parameter efficiency. The spectrum-aware rank selection and progressive subspace adaptation are key to its effectiveness.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning adapts a large language model to one task cheaply, but across a task sequence LoRA-style methods keep stacking low-rank updates on the same frozen weight, so each new task tends to overwrite the previous ones. We present ReCoLoRA (Recursive Consolidation of Low-Rank Adapters), a spectrum-aware framework for continual fine-tuning: adapters are initialized from a randomized SVD of the pretrained weight, per-layer effective ranks are selected by an elbow criterion, and the principal subspace is adapted before residual capacity is opened. Before each new task, ReCoLoRA re-decomposes the current effective weight, rather than the original one, into a frozen residual, a slowly updated principal component, and a fresh adapter (recursive consolidation), so every task starts from the model that has already absorbed its predecessors. On a six-task continual GLUE sequence over four 7-8B backbones, ReCoLoRA attains the best final average score on three of the four backbones against rank-swept LoRA, PiSSA, AdaLoRA, and DoRA baselines while training fewer parameters; an oracle-routed task-bank variant serves as an upper bound under full task isolation. Code: https://github.com/bhqy666/ReCoLoRA.

</details>


### [154] [Omni-Sleep: A Sleep Foundation Model via Hierarchical Contrastive Learning of CNS--ANS Dynamic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07720)
*Zhoujie Hou,Song Wang,Kexin Lou,Mo Wang,Chen Wei,Quanying Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Omni-Sleep is a sleep foundation model that incorporates CNS/ANS physiological hierarchy as a topological prior, pre-trained on 100,000+ hours of multimodal PSG data using three objectives (intra-system consistency, inter-system synchronization, and masked temporal modeling), achieving state-of-the-art performance on sleep staging and disease classification with improved generalization, label efficiency, and robustness to missing modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sleep foundation models fuse heterogeneous biosignals (EEG, EOG, EMG, ECG, respiration) in a topology-agnostic manner, ignoring the physiological organization of the CNS and ANS. This overlooks the structured coordination between neural and cardio-respiratory subsystems that underpins sleep physiology.

Method: Omni-Sleep leverages the CNS/ANS partition as a physiological prior for topology-constrained representation learning via three objectives: (1) intra-system consistency — captures shared factors within CNS (EEG, EOG, EMG) and ANS (ECG, respiration) signals separately; (2) inter-system synchronization — aligns CNS and ANS subsystem trajectories to model brain–body dynamics; (3) latent-space masked temporal modeling — captures long-horizon sleep dynamics. The model is pre-trained on over 100,000 hours of multi-center multimodal PSG data.

Result: Omni-Sleep outperforms strong foundation-model baselines on sleep staging and multi-disease classification across multiple datasets and modality-ablation settings. It demonstrates improved label efficiency, cross-dataset generalization, and robustness to missing modalities.

Conclusion: Incorporating physiological hierarchy (CNS/ANS organization) as a structural prior significantly improves the generalizability and robustness of sleep representation learning, establishing a new paradigm for multimodal biosignal foundation models.

Abstract: Sleep physiology arises from the coordinated dynamics of the central nervous system (CNS) and autonomic nervous system (ANS), as reflected by multimodal polysomnography signals including EEG, EOG, EMG, ECG, and respiration. However, existing sleep foundation models often fuse heterogeneous biosignals in a topology-agnostic manner, overlooking their physiological organization. We introduce Omni-Sleep, a sleep foundation model that uses the CNS/ANS partition as a physiological prior for topology-constrained representation learning. Omni-Sleep learns structured representations through three objectives: intra-system consistency, which captures shared subsystem-level factors within neural and cardio-respiratory signals; inter-system synchronization, which aligns subsystem trajectories to model brain--body dynamics; and latent-space masked temporal modeling, which captures long-horizon sleep dynamics. Pre-trained on over 100,000 hours of multi-center multimodal PSG data, Omni-Sleep is evaluated on sleep staging and multi-disease classification. Across datasets and modality-ablation settings, Omni-Sleep outperforms strong foundation-model baselines, showing improved label efficiency, cross-dataset generalization, and robustness to missing modalities. These results highlight the value of physiological hierarchy for generalizable sleep representation learning. Code is available at https://github.com/AutoBrain-sleep/OmniSleep.

</details>


### [155] [Uncertainty-gated selection for block-sparse attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07724)
*Thomas Rossi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a value-of-information router that identifies queries where the top-k block selection is ambiguous (near-tied scores at the cutoff) and doubles the kept set for those queries, recovering critical evidence dropped by standard block-sparse attention. The method yields substantial recall gains (+28pp on LongBench-v2) with moderate runtime overhead (0.62–0.80x dense wall time at 128K).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Block-sparse attention methods like Quest use a myopic top-k selection: when the k-th and (k+1)-th key blocks have nearly identical scores, the cutoff discards the (k+1)-th block without any recourse, potentially losing answer-critical evidence that cannot be recovered downstream. The authors aim to address this fragility by adaptively allocating additional attention budget only where the cutoff decision is most uncertain.

Method: A value-of-information router is proposed that quantifies, per query, the decisiveness of the top-k cutoff by measuring the score gap between the k-th and (k+1)-th blocks. For queries where this gap is smallest (i.e., the cutoff is least decisive), the kept set is doubled. The router is backbone-agnostic and stacks on existing block-scoring methods (demonstrated with Quest). A fused selection-plus-kernel pipeline is implemented for efficient execution.

Result: On LongBench-v2 medium (n=215), router-on-Quest achieves paired recall of 0.75 versus top-k baseline at 0.47 (+28pp, McNemar p<0.01), landing within 2pp of dense attention on RULER NIAH multikey. Gains reproduce across four models from three architectures (Qwen2.5, Mistral-Nemo, Qwen3.6). At 128K context, the router preserves 0.81 and 0.89 of dense accuracy on Qwen2.5-7B-1M and Qwen3.6 respectively (vs. SSA-style top-k at 0.09 on the former), with wall time at 0.62x and 0.80x of dense.

Conclusion: Adaptive budget allocation via a value-of-information router effectively mitigates the myopic cutoff problem in block-sparse attention, recovering near-dense accuracy at a fraction of the cost. The approach is simple, model-agnostic, and composable with existing block-scoring techniques.

Abstract: Block-sparse attention scales long-context language models by replacing the O(N^2) softmax with a per-query top-k selection over key blocks. This cutoff is myopic: when the k-th and (k+1)-th blocks are nearly tied in score, the selector commits without spending extra budget, and a dropped block carrying answer evidence is unrecoverable downstream. We propose a value-of-information router that measures, for each query, how decisively the top-k cut was made, and doubles the kept set for the queries where that gap is smallest; the rule is backbone-agnostic and stacks with existing block-scoring methods such as Quest. On LongBench-v2 medium at n=215 (the entire dataset subset), router-on-Quest reaches paired recall 0.75 vs. top-k 0.47 -- +28 pp over the SSA-style baseline (McNemar p<0.01) -- and lands within 2 pp of dense on RULER NIAH multikey at the same context. The lift reproduces on four models from three architectures (Qwen2.5, Mistral-Nemo, Qwen3.6). At 128K, the router preserves 0.81 and 0.89 of dense accuracy on Qwen2.5-7B-1M and Qwen3.6 (vs. SSA-style top-k at 0.09 on the former) while the fused selection-plus-kernel pipeline runs at 0.62x and 0.80x dense wall time.

</details>


### [156] [SHIFT: Survival Prediction from Incomplete and Heterogeneous Genomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07725)
*Muhammet Sami Yavuz,Ayhan Can Erdur,Sabri Mustafa Kahya,Benedikt Wiestler,Jana Lipkova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SHIFT is a transformer-based survival model that handles incomplete genomic features across different sequencing panels without imputation, using masked self-attention with a feature-availability mask and variable-rate feature masking during training. It demonstrates strong cross-cohort generalization in glioblastoma and lung cancer, outperforming standard survival baselines and imputation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Genomic prediction models fail to transfer across institutions because different sites use different sequencing panels, causing structural feature missingness. Existing solutions (shared-gene restriction, patient exclusion, test-time imputation) reduce robustness and waste multi-center data. A model that natively handles incomplete features without imputation is needed.

Method: SHIFT represents each genomic feature separately and employs masked self-attention with an explicit feature-availability mask, ensuring predictions depend only on observed inputs. During training, variable-rate feature masking is applied to simulate heterogeneous missingness patterns, improving robustness. A single model is used across all feature sets without test-time imputation.

Result: SHIFT shows strong generalization on glioblastoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma with external validation across multiple cohorts, including severe cross-cohort panel mismatch. It compares favorably against standard survival baselines and imputation-based approaches. Incorporating patients from incomplete cohorts during training further improves external performance.

Conclusion: Missingness-aware modeling via SHIFT is a practical and effective strategy for multi-center survival prediction in precision oncology, enabling the use of all available data without discarding patients or restricting to shared genes.

Abstract: Genomic prediction models often fail to transfer across institutions because sequencing panels differ across sites, creating structural feature missingness at deployment. Existing approaches to this challenge typically restrict analysis to genes shared across cohorts, exclude patients with incomplete profiles, or rely on test-time imputation, all of which can reduce robustness and limit the use of multi-center data. We propose Survival prediction Handling Incomplete Features using Transformer (SHIFT), a missingness-aware survival model that directly predicts from incomplete genomic inputs without test-time imputation. SHIFT represents each genomic feature separately and uses masked self-attention, along with a feature-availability mask, so that predictions are based only on observed inputs. Further, we introduce variable-rate feature masking during training to improve robustness to heterogeneous missingness patterns. We evaluate the approach on glioblastoma and lung squamous cell carcinoma with external validation across multiple cohorts, including a challenging setting with severe cross-cohort panel mismatch. Across these settings, SHIFT shows strong generalization and compares favorably with standard survival baselines and imputation-based approaches, while using a single model across differing feature sets. We also find that incorporating patients from incomplete cohorts during development can improve performance on external data, suggesting that partially observed cohorts need not be excluded from model building.
