These results support missingness-aware modeling as a practical strategy for multi-center survival prediction in precision oncology.

</details>


### [157] [Jet-Long: Efficient Long-Context Extension with Dynamic Bifocal RoPE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07740)
*Haozhan Tang,Zerui Wang,Yuxian Gu,Song Han,Han Cai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Jet-Long is a tuning-free, zero-shot context extension method that uses a bifocal attention mechanism — combining a local RoPE-faithful window with a dynamically-rescaled long-range window — to maintain short-context fidelity while enabling clean long-context extrapolation, achieving state-of-the-art results on benchmarks like RULER and HELMET-RAG with minimal inference overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern LLMs are deployed in long-context scenarios (RAG, repository-level coding, agentic workflows) where inputs far exceed the pretraining window. Existing zero-shot context extension methods fix a single rescaling factor upfront, creating an inherent trade-off: aggressive rescaling degrades short-context performance, while conservative rescaling fails at long contexts. A method is needed that handles both regimes without retraining.

Method: Jet-Long introduces a bifocal attention construction: (1) a local window that remains RoPE-faithful (no rescaling), preserving exact base model behavior at short inputs; (2) a long-range window whose RoPE rescaling factor adapts dynamically to the current sequence length; (3) an inclusion-exclusion attention merge that combines both windows; (4) an on-the-fly RoPE correction rotation. The entire mechanism is fused into a single CuTe kernel, achieving up to 1.39× FA2 throughput on H100 GPUs with ≤4% single-batch generation overhead.

Result: On Qwen3 models (1.7B/4B/8B) at up to 128K context: RULER improvements of +4.79/+2.18/+2.03 pp over the strongest baseline; best overall accuracy on HELMET-RAG; lowest PG-19 perplexity. The method also generalizes to hybrid architectures (Jet-Nemotron) and remains robust across hyperparameter choices.

Conclusion: Jet-Long demonstrates that dynamic, length-adaptive rescaling with a bifocal window design effectively resolves the short-vs-long context trade-off in zero-shot context extension, achieving superior performance across diverse benchmarks while maintaining practical inference efficiency through kernel fusion.

Abstract: Modern LLMs are increasingly deployed in long-context applications such as retrieval-augmented generation, repository-level coding, and agentic workflows whose accumulated reasoning and tool traces routinely push the input an order of magnitude past the pretraining window, making zero-shot context extension the dominant deployment path for open-weight checkpoints. Most existing zero-shot methods fix a single rescaling factor up front, so an aggressive factor sacrifices short-context fidelity while a conservative one breaks down at long contexts. We propose Jet-Long, a tuning-free zero-shot method that pairs a local RoPE-faithful window with a long-range window whose rescaling factor adapts dynamically to the current sequence length, recovering the base model exactly at short inputs while extrapolating cleanly at long ones. An inclusion-exclusion attention merge and an on-the-fly RoPE correction rotation make the bifocal construction essentially free at inference; fused into a single CuTe kernel, long-context prefill reaches up to $1.39\times$ FA2 throughput on H100 (approaching the Hopper-only FA4), and single-batch generation incurs $\le 4\%$ overhead at every length. On Qwen3-1.7B/4B/8B up to 128K context, Jet-Long leads RULER by $+4.79$/$+2.18$/$+2.03$~pp over the strongest baseline at 1.7B/4B/8B, achieves the best overall accuracy on HELMET-RAG (a benchmark identified by HELMET as the most efficient predictor of downstream long-context performance) and attains the lowest PG-19 perplexity. Jet-Long also generalizes to hybrid attention architectures such as Jet-Nemotron for further long-context improvement without retraining, and remains hyperparameter-resilient for ease of deployment.

</details>


### [158] [Architecture Generalization with MetaNCA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07743)
*Meet Barot,Daniel Berenberg,Sina Khajehabdollahi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetaNCA introduces a framework that uses learned local update rules, implemented via a Weight Transformer with linear attention, to self-organize the weights of neural networks without backpropagation, scaling to 2M parameters and generalizing to unseen architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biological neurons learn efficiently through local synaptic interactions and adapt over time. Neural Cellular Automata (NCA) have successfully modeled morphogenesis with local rules, demonstrating stability and robustness. This motivates extending the NCA paradigm to the self-organization of artificial neural network weights, enabling adaptability and local interaction-driven learning without backpropagation.

Method: The authors propose Meta Neural Cellular Automata (MetaNCA), where a learned rule network iteratively updates a task network's weights using only local information on the computation graph. The rule network employs a novel Weight Transformer architecture with linear attention to aggregate signals from neighboring weights and hidden states. After meta-training, the rule network can generate weights for diverse architectures (MLPs, CNNs, ResNets) without backpropagation.

Result: MetaNCA successfully generates weights for feedforward MLPs, CNNs, and ResNets on MNIST and CIFAR-100, scaling to networks with up to 2 million parameters. It generalizes to architectures unseen during meta-training, and architectural diversity during training improves this generalization capability.

Conclusion: MetaNCA demonstrates that local update rules, implemented via a Weight Transformer, can effectively self-organize neural network weights at scale. The framework's ability to generalize to unseen architectures and benefit from architectural diversity highlights its potential as a biologically-inspired alternative to gradient-based optimization.

Abstract: Self-organization is an emergent property of life, driven by the collective behavior of individual components acting on local information. Biological neurons, through local interactions transmitted through synapses, are able to learn efficiently and can adapt their connections over an organism's lifespan. Motivated by these desirable properties of adaptability and local interaction, neural cellular automata (NCA) models have been successful at learning morphogenesis solely through local update rules, demonstrating stability over many updates and robustness to perturbations. In this work, we introduce Meta Neural Cellular Automata (MetaNCA), a framework that learns local rules which self-organize the weights of artificial neural networks. A learned rule network iteratively updates the weights of a task network using only local interactions on the computation graph. We propose a novel Weight Transformer architecture for the local rule network, which uses linear attention to aggregate signals from neighboring weights and hidden states. Once trained, the rule network generates task networks of diverse architectures without backpropagation. We show that MetaNCA generates weights for feedforward MLPs, CNNs, and ResNets on MNIST and CIFAR-100, scaling to networks of 2 million parameters. We further show that MetaNCA generalizes to architectures not seen during meta-training, and that architectural diversity in the training phase strengthens this generalization.

</details>


### [159] [LiST: Lipschitz Scaling Training for Robust and Calibrated Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07745)
*Arthur Chiron,Franck Mamalet,Thomas Massena,Thomas Deltort,Mathieu Serrurier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LiST proposes a training paradigm that links Lipschitz constraints with temperature scaling for calibration, enabling neural networks to simultaneously achieve accuracy, robustness, and calibration by iteratively adjusting the Lipschitz constant to a principled operating point.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks struggle to simultaneously satisfy accuracy, adversarial robustness, and calibration. Lipschitz-constrained models provide guaranteed robustness, but the Lipschitz constant L is manually chosen and its relationship to calibration is unexplored. The authors seek a principled method to select L that jointly optimizes all three objectives.

Method: The authors establish a theoretical link between the enforced Lipschitz constraint and Temperature Scaling for calibration. They identify an optimal Lipschitz value L* that yields naturally calibrated networks. Based on this, they introduce Lipschitz Scaling Training (LiST), which iteratively adjusts the global Lipschitz constant during training to reach L*. A margin parameter in the loss enables construction of a fully calibrated accuracy-robustness Pareto front. LiST also allows reintegration of calibration data into training for improved sample efficiency.

Result: LiST achieves competitive accuracy and certified robustness against both constrained and unconstrained baselines on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet, while delivering calibrated networks out of the box without post-hoc calibration methods.

Conclusion: Calibration serves as a principled criterion for selecting the Lipschitz constant in robust neural networks. LiST unifies accuracy, robustness, and calibration into a single training framework, eliminating the need for manual Lipschitz tuning or separate post-hoc calibration.

Abstract: While accuracy, robustness, and calibration are all essential for reliable neural networks, they are often studied separately; developing models that satisfy all three simultaneously remains a central challenge. Lipschitz-constrained models guarantee robustness by design, yet the manual selection of the Lipschitz constraint L governs the resulting accuracy-robustness trade-off, and their calibration properties remain largely underexplored. In this work, we highlight a theoretical and empirical link between the enforced Lipschitz constraint and Temperature Scaling, a state-of-the-art calibration method. Specifically, we find that for a given training scheme, there exists a non-trivial value L* that yields an out-of-the-box calibrated network, and that calibration acts as a principled criterion to select a well-defined operating point on the accuracy-robustness Pareto front. Leveraging these insights, we introduce Lipschitz Scaling Training (LiST), a novel training paradigm that iteratively adjusts the global Lipschitz constant to reach this operating point. Through a margin parameter in the training loss, LiST further enables the construction of a fully calibrated Pareto front, allowing users to navigate the accuracy-robustness trade-off while remaining calibrated throughout. At convergence, LiST also enables the reintegration of calibration data into training, improving sample efficiency without sacrificing calibration. We validate LiST on CIFAR-10/100 and Tiny-ImageNet, demonstrating competitive accuracy and robustness against constrained and unconstrained baselines, while remaining calibrated out of the box. Code is available at GitHub.

</details>


### [160] [Selective Left-Shift: Turning Test-Time Compute and Difficulty-based Curation into Training Data for Low-Resource Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07748)
*Didula Samaraweera,Anjana Supun,Srinath Perera*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A three-phase pipeline for low-resource programming languages (LRPLs) that decouples syntax acquisition from algorithmic reasoning: offline compiler-feedback data synthesis, SFT on verified data, then RLVR with I/O test rewards. Achieves significant gains on Julia (+7.6 MultiPL-E, +14.2 LiveCodeBench) and Ballerina (49.7% Pass@1) using Qwen3-8B, at 1/3 the data and 1/6 the cost of prior SOTA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs perform poorly on low-resource programming languages (LRPLs) like Julia. Improving SLMs for LRPLs faces a trilemma: SFT is limited by data scarcity, inference-time scaling is prohibitively expensive, and RL from scratch yields negligible gains due to sparse syntax rewards. A method is needed that efficiently imparts both syntactic and reasoning capabilities to SLMs for LRPLs.

Method: A three-phase pipeline: (1) "Left-shifted" offline data synthesis using iterative compiler and test feedback to generate verified training examples. (2) Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on the synthetic verified data to embed strong syntactic priors into the SLM. (3) Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward (RLVR) grounded by language-agnostic I/O tests, where the SFT prior constrains exploration away from syntax errors. Applied to Qwen3-8B.

Result: On Julia: +7.6 points Pass@1 on MultiPL-E, +14.2 points on Agnostics LiveCodeBench over SOTA, using 1/3 the data and 1/6 the cost. On Ballerina (near-zero pretraining representation): 49.7% MultiPL-E Pass@1. Ablations confirm both SFT phase and execution-grounded rewards are necessary for stable training.

Conclusion: Decoupling syntax acquisition (via offline compiler-feedback synthesis + SFT) from algorithmic reasoning (via RLVR) resolves the LRPL trilemma, enabling strong SLM code generation for low-resource languages at substantially reduced data and computational cost. The approach generalizes even to languages with minimal pretraining exposure.

Abstract: Large Language Models achieve strong code generation for high resource languages like Python and Java but suffer sharp performance drops on Low-Resource Programming Languages~(LRPLs) such as Julia. Improving Small Language Models~(SLMs) for these languages faces a trilemma: Supervised Fine-Tuning~(SFT) is bottlenecked by data scarcity, inference-time scaling is too expensive for deployment, and Reinforcement Learning from scratch yields near zero advantages. We propose a three-phase pipeline that resolves this trilemma by decoupling syntax acquisition from algorithmic reasoning. First, we \emph{left-shift} inference-time compute to an offline data synthesis engine that uses iterative compiler and test feedback to generate verified training examples. Second, we fine-tune an SLM on this synthetic, verified data to embed strong syntactic priors. Third, we apply Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward~(RLVR) grounded by language-agnostic Input/Output tests, where the SFT prior constrains exploration away from syntax errors. Applied to Qwen3-8B, our pipeline improves pass@1 by up to +7.6 points on MultiPL-E and +14.2 points on the Agnostics LiveCodeBench for Julia compared to SOTA results. Furthermore, the pipeline only used $\frac{1}{3}$ data and $\frac{1}{6}$ cost over the previous state-of-the-art. We further demonstrate that the pipeline generalizes to Ballerina achieving 49.7\% MultiPL-E Pass@1, a language with near-zero pretraining representation. Ablations confirm that both the SFT phase and execution-grounded rewards are necessary for stable training.

</details>


### [161] [A Transdiagnostic Space of Disorder Like Phenotypes in Reinforcement Learning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07753)
*Hari Prasad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reframes modeling psychological disorders in RL agents as dose-controllable manipulation of cognitive appraisal signals, expressing seven disorders as single adjustable knobs grounded in computational psychiatry. Across 1000+ runs, each disorder shows graded monotone dose-response curves. Novel findings include emergent self-organization into a 2D affective space, differential treatment responses (reward distortion vs. avoidance disorders), nonadditive comorbidity interactions, and successful transfer to a 3D pixel environment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on modeling psychological disorders in artificial agents suffers from several limitations: hand-tuned reward shaping, post hoc behavioral labeling, induction of only 1-2 disorders at a time, and reporting of single runs without statistical rigor. The authors aim to establish a principled, reproducible, and parameterized framework for inducing multiple disorders through a unified mechanism—cognitive appraisal—while providing preregistered quantitative assays and robust statistics.

Method: The authors use an appraisal-guided PPO agent where cognitive appraisal signals are systematically manipulated via dose-controllable "knobs," each grounded in computational psychiatry accounts. Seven disorders (anxiety, mania, OCD-checking, depression, impulsivity, addiction, PTSD) are each expressed as a single parameter manipulation. Symptoms are measured by preregistered behavioral assays mapped to recognized clinical paradigms. Experiments span 10 seeds × 4 controls with 95% confidence intervals. Transfer is tested in MiniWorld with a standard convolutional agent lacking an appraisal critic.

Result: Every disorder exhibits a graded, monotone dose-response curve that no control reproduces. Three emergent findings: (1) disorders self-organize into a 2D affective space where mania mirrors anxiety; (2) removing a knob remits reward-distortion disorders (mania, checking, addiction) but not avoidance disorders (anxiety, PTSD), which instead recover under graded exposure; (3) two simultaneous knobs interact nonadditively, generating testable comorbidity predictions. Three disorder knobs (depression, addiction, anxiety) transfer successfully to MiniWorld with cross-assay dissociation confirmed across both domains.

Conclusion: Appraisal weights parameterize a controllable space of affective phenotypes where the same knobs inducing a disorder can model its treatment. The framework is principled, statistically rigorous, and not specific to grid worlds or PPO's appraisal critic, suggesting a general mechanism for computational psychiatry modeling in RL agents.

Abstract: Modelling psychological disorders in artificial agents offers both a testbed for computational psychiatry and a lens on the failure modes of affective control. Prior work induces one or two disorders in a reinforcement learning (RL) agent by hand-tuned reward shaping, labels the behaviour post hoc, and reports single runs. We recast disorder modelling as dose-controllable manipulation of cognitive appraisal signals in an appraisal-guided PPO agent, expressing seven disorders (anxiety, mania, obsessive-compulsive checking, depression, impulsivity, addiction, and post-traumatic stress) each as a single knob grounded in a computational psychiatry account, with each symptom measured by a preregistered assay mapped to a recognised paradigm. Across more than a thousand runs (10 seeds, four controls, 95% confidence intervals) every disorder shows a graded, monotone dose-response that no control reproduces. Beyond these induced effects, three findings emerge that were not written into the reward: the disorders self-organise into a two-dimensional affective space in which mania mirrors anxiety; removing a knob remits reward distortion disorders (mania, checking, addiction) but not avoidance disorders (anxiety, PTSD), which instead recover under a graded exposure curriculum; and two simultaneous knobs interact nonadditively, yielding testable comorbidity predictions. Appraisal weights thus parameterise a controllable space of affective phenotypes in which the same knobs that induce a disorder can model its treatment. We also show that three disorder knobs (depression, addiction, anxiety) transfer to a three-dimensional pixel environment (MiniWorld) with a standard convolutional agent and no appraisal critic, with cross-assay dissociation confirmed across both domains, indicating the framework is not specific to grid worlds or to PPO's appraisal critic.

</details>


### [162] [Image classification via a quantum-inspired strategy involving a mixture of experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07754)
*Kumari Jyoti,Rohith Babu,Apoorva D. Patel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hybrid classical-quantum framework using a mixture-of-experts strategy with quantum-inspired amplitude encoding, local unitary convolutions, and stabilizer-code feature extraction, demonstrating ~2x failure rate reduction on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST with moderate GPU overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical CNNs rely on diffusion-based downsampling and pooling, which may not optimally capture structural features. A more efficient, quantum-inspired approach could improve feature extraction and classification performance.

Method: The proposed hybrid framework has a quantum part (amplitude encoding of images, convolution via local unitary operations, multiple parallel experts with different parameters, and feature extraction using quantum stabilizer codes) and a classical part (a fully connected neural network that jointly processes features from all experts for final classification).

Result: On MNIST and Fashion-MNIST, joint expert analysis outperforms individual experts and reduces the failure rate by approximately a factor of two. The overhead on GPU workstations is moderate.

Conclusion: The quantum-inspired mixture-of-experts framework is a practical, competitive alternative to classical CNN schemes, with demonstrated performance gains and feasible execution on both classical GPU hardware and future quantum processors.

Abstract: Pattern recognition problems arise in a variety of physical image processing situations, and convolutional neural networks are a popular scheme for the required feature extraction and classification tasks. The classical networks use diffusion-based smearing and block-wise pooling to downsample the image data and capture important structural features. In this work, we propose and demonstrate a more efficient quantum-inspired strategy involving a mixture of experts. It is a hybrid classical-quantum framework. The quantum part consists of amplitude encoding of the images, convolution using local unitary operations, multiple experts processing the same image with different parameters, and feature extraction using quantum stabiliser codes. The classical part then jointly processes the features extracted by different experts using a standard fully connected neural network for image class prediction. Using MNIST and Fashion-MNIST datasets as benchmarks, we demonstrate that the joint expert analysis outperforms the individual expert one, as well as reduces the failure rate of image class prediction by around a factor of two. The overhead of our quantum-inspired strategy is only moderate on GPU workstations, which makes our proposal a practical alternative to existing classical schemes. We also point out how the quantum part of our framework can be executed on a quantum processor.

</details>


### [163] [Federated Deep Learning for Privacy-Preserving Cardiovascular Disease Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08595)
*Hyunho Mo,Djura Smits,Mahlet A. Birhanu,Maarten J. G. Leening,Daniel Bos,Pim van der Harst,Esther E. Bron*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated deep learning across two heterogeneous population cohorts (Lifelines: ~148K participants, self-reported outcomes; Rotterdam Study: ~10K participants, clinical outcomes) improved cardiovascular disease risk prediction over locally trained models, with C-statistic gains from 0.728→0.739 (Rotterdam) and 0.783→0.787 (Lifelines), while preserving patient data privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CVD risk prediction models are typically developed on single-institution or centrally pooled datasets, limiting generalizability. Privacy regulations (e.g., GDPR) restrict patient-level data sharing, and federated learning in healthcare is challenging due to heterogeneity in dataset size, population characteristics, and outcome definitions across institutions.

Method: Federated deep survival models trained across two Dutch population-based cohorts: the large Lifelines cohort (148,230 participants, self-reported outcomes) and the smaller Rotterdam Study (10,155 participants, clinically linked outcomes). Primary evaluation was conducted on the Rotterdam Study due to its complete follow-up data. Performance compared federated vs. locally trained models using C-statistic with 95% confidence intervals.

Result: Federated learning consistently outperformed local training. Rotterdam Study C-statistic: 0.728 (local) → 0.739 (federated). Lifelines C-statistic: 0.783 (local) → 0.787 (federated). All improvements were statistically significant based on non-overlapping confidence intervals for Rotterdam.

Conclusion: Federated deep learning can effectively leverage heterogeneous multi-cohort data to improve CVD risk prediction performance without compromising individual-level data privacy, demonstrating a viable path for privacy-preserving collaborative model development in healthcare.

Abstract: Cardiovascular disease risk prediction models often rely on data from a single institution or centrally pooled datasets. Extending these models across institutions could be limited by privacy regulations and constraints on sharing patient-level data. Federated learning enables collaborative model development without transferring sensitive patient data, but its application in healthcare remains challenging because datasets often differ in size, population characteristics, and outcome definitions. In this study, we present a federated deep learning approach for privacy-preserving cardiovascular disease risk prediction that integrates two population-based cohorts with different characteristics: Lifelines, including 148,230 participants meeting the study inclusion criteria with self-reported outcomes, and the Rotterdam Study, including a smaller cohort of 10,155 participants with digitally linked clinical outcomes. Model performance was primarily evaluated on the Rotterdam Study because of its complete follow-up. Deep survival models trained using federated learning achieved higher predictive performance than models trained locally without federation. For the Rotterdam Study, the C-statistic increased from 0.728 (95% CI: 0.717-0.739) to 0.739 (95% CI: 0.728-0.749). For Lifelines, the C-statistic increased from 0.783 (95% CI: 0.775-0.791) to 0.787 (95% CI: 0.780-0.792). These findings suggest that federated deep learning across heterogeneous cohorts can improve cardiovascular disease risk prediction while preserving the privacy of individual-level patient data.

</details>


### [164] [The Importance of Encoder Choice:A Tabular-Image Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07756)
*Ilia Koloiarov,Diego Coello de Portugal Mecke,Vijaya Krishna Yalavarthi,Tom Hanika,Lars Schmidt-Thieme*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper evaluates state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in multimodal (image-tabular) learning, addressing a key obstacle where In-Context Learning models require labels during inference. The authors propose a solution for unified embedding of train/test instances and emphasize the importance of encoder choice in multimodal settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite advances in deep learning, tabular data remains challenging ("the last unconquered castle"). Prior multimodal image-tabular work predominantly uses plain MLPs as tabular encoders, which may be suboptimal. The authors argue that stronger tabular encoders could significantly improve multimodal performance, motivating the first systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art tabular models in this context.

Method: The study evaluates state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in an image-tabular multimodal learning framework. A key methodological contribution is addressing the obstacle posed by In-Context Learning (ICL) tabular models, which require labels to process instances — making consistent embedding of training and test instances non-trivial. The authors develop a solution to harmonize embedding across train/test splits for multiple ICL-family models.

Result: The study demonstrates that stronger tabular encoders (beyond plain MLPs) can be effectively integrated into multimodal image-tabular pipelines. The proposed method successfully resolves the label-dependency issue of ICL-based tabular models, enabling their use as encoders in multimodal settings where both training and testing instances must be embedded identically.

Conclusion: The encoder factor is critically important in multimodal learning. The tabular modality deserves stronger encoders than plain MLPs, and even complex models like ICL-based ones can be adapted for multimodal use with proper methodological adjustments. This opens the door for leveraging the best tabular models in multimodal pipelines.

Abstract: Multimodal learning usually requires a dedicated encoder per modality. When a tabular modality is involved, prior work has been mostly using a \emph{plain MLP} as the encoder. Yet if it were a strong encoder, the tabular domain would not be ``the last unconquered castle for deep learning''. This study evaluates state-of-the-art tabular models as encoders in the image-tabular setting for the first time. An obstacle stands out. In-Context Learning models, among the best performing methods in the tabular domain, require labels to process instances, making it non-trivial to embed training and test instances the same way. We addressed this problem across multiple models of this family. With this study, we would like to highlight the importance of encoder factor in the multimodal learning.

</details>


### [165] [Dimensionality Reduction Meets Network Science: Sensemaking on UMAP's kNN Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08746)
*Duen Horng Chau,Donghao Ren,Fred Hohman,Dominik Moritz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UMAP's internal kNN graph—often discarded after dimensionality reduction—encodes valuable manifold structure in the original high-dimensional space. The authors show that applying standard graph algorithms (PageRank, k-core decomposition, clustering coefficient) to this graph yields effective data sensemaking: representative point selection, density-based region detection, and tight-knit neighborhood identification, competitive with purpose-built methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typical UMAP workflows discard the kNN graph after projection, focusing only on the 2D embedding. However, this internal graph preserves the true high-dimensional manifold structure without the distortion UMAP's 2D projection inevitably introduces. The authors aim to demonstrate that this overlooked representation is a rich, readily-available resource for data sensemaking tasks.

Method: The authors extract UMAP's internally constructed kNN graph (in the original high-dimensional space) and apply three standard graph algorithms: (1) PageRank to identify representative/exemplar data points, (2) k-core decomposition to reveal core–periphery density structure, and (3) clustering coefficient to detect tightly interconnected neighborhoods of highly similar points. Evaluation is performed quantitatively and qualitatively on MNIST and Fashion MNIST.

Result: The graph-based analyses proved practical and effective. PageRank-selected exemplars were competitive with k-medoids for representative selection. k-core decomposition meaningfully stratified dense versus sparse regions, complementing HDBSCAN for density-based clustering. Clustering coefficient successfully identified tight-knit, semantically coherent neighborhoods. The approaches were both competitive with and complementary to purpose-built alternatives.

Conclusion: UMAP's internal kNN graph is an underutilized asset that encodes genuine high-dimensional manifold structure. Applying off-the-shelf graph algorithms to this graph provides a simple yet powerful toolkit for data sensemaking, rivaling specialized methods without additional computational overhead.

Abstract: While UMAP is widely used for exploring high-dimensional data, typical workflows focus on its lower-dimensional embedding, largely overlooking the rich k-nearest-neighbor (kNN) graph that UMAP constructs internally. This graph encodes the data manifold in its original high-dimensional space, before the distortion that UMAP's 2D projection introduces. We demonstrate the untapped potential of this internal representation, showing how standard graph algorithms applied to this graph enhance data sensemaking: (1) PageRank identifies representative data points, (2) k-core decomposition reveals dense core regions versus sparse periphery, and (3) clustering coefficient detects tight-knit neighborhoods with highly-similar data points. Through quantitative and qualitative evaluation on MNIST and Fashion MNIST, we show that these graph-based analyses are not only practical but also competitive with or complementary to purpose-built methods (e.g., k-medoids for exemplar selection, HDBSCAN for density-based clustering).

</details>


### [166] [Scalable and Trustworthy Earth Observation Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07758)
*Syed Usama Imtiaz,Mitra Nasr Azadani,Nasrin Alamdari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Remote sensing foundation models (RSFMs) require domain-specific adaptation due to the unique physical and operational constraints of Earth observation data. This chapter reviews RSFM design principles, synthesizes the current landscape, highlights evaluation inconsistencies, and presents two case studies demonstrating physics-informed and reinforcement-learning-driven domain adaptation. The core argument is that next-generation RSFMs must be evaluated on modality-aware transfer and physically plausible representations, not just benchmark accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models have revolutionized ML by enabling general-purpose pretraining, but their application to Earth observation is non-trivial. EO data are governed by measurement physics (e.g., sensor radiometry, atmospheric effects) and operational constraints (e.g., revisit cycles, multimodal acquisitions), while labeled data remain sparse. Existing RSFMs cannot be transferred reliably without domain-specific adaptation, and current evaluation practices are inconsistent, preventing fair comparison and trustworthy deployment.

Method: The chapter takes a review and synthesis approach: (1) defines the foundation model paradigm in remote sensing, (2) surveys pretraining objectives, architecture designs, and downstream adaptation strategies, (3) analyzes recent benchmark evidence on model comparability, (4) presents two illustrative case studies — physics-informed spectral targeted masking for harmful algal bloom prediction and reinforcement learning for adaptive environmental monitoring station selection — to demonstrate domain-guided principles in practice.

Result: Key findings include: no single geospatial foundation model is universally best across tasks; inconsistent evaluation protocols remain a major barrier to fair comparison; and domain-guided approaches (physics-informed pretraining, adaptive operational strategies) show practical promise in environmental monitoring applications.

Conclusion: Next-generation RSFMs should be assessed beyond standard benchmark accuracy — specifically on modality-aware transfer capability and the production of physically plausible representations — to enable trustworthy EO decision-making. Domain-specific constraints must be first-class design considerations, not afterthoughts.

Abstract: Foundation models (FMs) have transformed machine learning from isolated task-specific model development toward general-purpose models pretrained on broad data and adapted to multiple downstream tasks. Earth observation (EO) is an important domain for this paradigm because satellite and airborne archives are large, high-revisit, and increasingly multimodal, while reliable field labels are often sparse. Remote sensing foundation models (RSFMs) cannot be transferred reliably/optimally without domain-specific adaptation. This is because EO data are governed by measurement physics and operational decision constraints. This chapter reviews the design principles arising from these domain-specific constraints. It first defines the FMs paradigm in remote sensing (RS), then synthesizes the current model landscape, pretraining objectives, architecture designs, downstream adaptation and trustworthiness requirements. The chapter also incorporates recent benchmark evidence showing that no single geospatial foundation model is universally best and that inconsistent evaluation remains a major issue to fair comparison and reliable deployment. In addition, two brief environmental monitoring case studies; physics-informed spectral targeted masking for harmful algal bloom prediction and reinforcement learning for adaptive environmental monitoring station selection to illustrate the FMs domain-guided principles in practice. This chapter posits that next-generation RSFMs should be evaluated not only by benchmark accuracy, but also by modality-aware transfer and physically plausible representations for trustworthy EO decisions.

</details>


### [167] [Trustworthy Machine Learning through the Lens of Combinatorial Optimization: Survey and Research Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07762)
*Thibaut Vidal,Julien Ferry*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey examines how combinatorial optimization (CO) techniques can enhance trustworthy machine learning, providing formal guarantees, certificates, and explicit trade-off analysis across tasks like interpretability, robustness, fairness, privacy, and model compression — capabilities that purely heuristic or gradient-based methods often lack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern ML models with similar empirical performance can differ significantly in trustworthiness dimensions (transparency, robustness, fairness, privacy). There is a need for principled frameworks beyond empirical metrics to characterize, certify, and audit these properties, motivating the integration of combinatorial optimization methods into trustworthy ML pipelines.

Method: The paper surveys and synthesizes recent work at the intersection of combinatorial optimization and trustworthy ML, covering CO formulations applied to both training-time and post-training tasks: interpretable model learning, explanation generation, robustness analysis, fairness auditing, model compression, and privacy attacks/protections. CO approaches provide global guarantees, formal certificates, and explicit trade-off handling.

Result: CO-based methods offer distinct advantages over heuristic/gradient-based alternatives, including global optimality guarantees, formal certification of properties, and explicit multi-objective trade-off analysis. However, scalability remains a key challenge, though ongoing advances in solvers and hybrid algorithms are steadily expanding the practical reach of CO in ML contexts.

Conclusion: Combinatorial optimization provides a principled framework for addressing trustworthiness concerns in ML, complementing empirical approaches with formal guarantees. As solver technology and hybrid methods continue to improve, CO is poised to play an increasingly important role in the design, auditing, and deployment of trustworthy ML systems.

Abstract: Modern machine learning (ML) increasingly relies on complex models whose behavior is difficult to characterize beyond empirical performance metrics. Across a wide range of tasks, including prediction, generation, and decision-making, models with similar empirical performance can exhibit markedly different properties in terms of their transparency, interpretability, robustness, fairness, privacy, and certifiability. This survey highlights how optimization- and certification-oriented reasoning can provide a useful framework for reasoning about such differences, supporting tasks ranging from model training and selection to auditing and certification. We review and synthesize recent advances at the intersection of combinatorial optimization (CO) and trustworthy ML, covering both training and post-training tasks, including interpretable model learning, explanation generation, robustness analysis, fairness auditing, model compression, and privacy attacks and protections. Across these domains, CO formulations offer additional capabilities over purely heuristic approaches, e.g., gradient-based ones, notably global guarantees, formal certificates, and explicit treatment of trade-offs. While scalability remains an important challenge, continued progress in solvers and hybrid algorithms suggests a growing role for CO in the design and deployment of trustworthy ML systems.

</details>


### [168] [Unlocking Temporal Generalization in Hamiltonian Video Dynamics Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07763)
*Eli Laird,Corey Clark*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HGNs fail to generalize across timescales in non-conservative settings due to latent magnitude growth and integrator truncation errors. The authors identify and fix these failure modes, enabling stable continuous-time video prediction at resolutions far beyond training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models with baked-in discrete time steps cannot serve applications requiring multi-scale temporal queries (hierarchical planning, sim-to-real, game engines). While HGNs theoretically offer frame-rate-independent continuous-time dynamics via an energy function, their practical temporal generalization collapses in dissipative/forced environments — a critical, unresolved barrier to real-world deployment.

Method: The authors analyze HGN rollouts under external forcing and dissipation, diagnosing two distinct failure modes: (1) unbounded latent magnitude growth from an unregularized action-force mapping, and (2) global truncation error accumulation due to under-resolved numerical integration. They propose targeted remedies — constraining the action-force map and improving integrator resolution — and validate them on continuous-time video prediction tasks, testing generalization to step sizes well outside the training distribution.

Result: The identified fixes eliminate the two failure modes, yielding stable HGN rollouts at temporal resolutions far beyond the training regime. The paper provides a detailed empirical analysis and a set of actionable strategies for enabling temporal generalization in continuous-time generative video models.

Conclusion: Temporal generalization in continuous-time world models is achievable once the specific failure mechanisms in non-conservative settings are addressed. The work offers both diagnostic insight and practical recommendations, advancing HGNs toward robust multi-timescale simulation for hierarchical planning, sim-to-real transfer, and related applications.

Abstract: World models are typically trained to predict discrete-time physical dynamics with a fixed step size baked into the model weights, preventing prediction at variable temporal resolutions. This matters for hierarchical planning, sim-to-real transfer, and scientific or game-engine applications that must query the same dynamics at multiple timescales. Hamiltonian Generative Networks (HGN) offer a principled path forward, grounding predictions in a continuous-time energy function that is, in principle, independent of the observation frame rate. In practice, however, their temporal generalization breaks down in non-conservative settings. We show that in externally forced, dissipative environments, HGN rollouts at step sizes beyond the training regime fail due to distinct failure modes, including latent magnitude growth driven by an unconstrained action-force map, and global truncation error accumulation from an under-resolved integrator. We identify a targeted fix for each mechanism and demonstrate stable dynamics prediction at temporal resolutions well outside the training distribution. In a detailed analysis, we recommend several strategies for enabling temporal generalization in continuous-time video generation.

</details>


### [169] [Principled Analysis of Deep Reinforcement Learning Evaluation and Design Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07769)
*Ezgi Korkmaz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper examines canonical evaluation and design paradigms in deep reinforcement learning (RL), introduces theoretical foundations for scaling laws, and demonstrates through large-scale experiments that traditional performance rankings are non-monotonic across data regimes — meaning conclusions drawn under standard paradigms may be incorrect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The past decade has seen remarkable progress in RL, from deep Q-networks mastering complex games to algorithms solving problems without explicit rules. However, the authors identify a critical concern: the canonical evaluation and design paradigms in RL research may produce misleading conclusions. The paper seeks to rigorously investigate whether asymptotic performance rankings hold consistently across different data regimes, and whether the standard research methodology in RL is fundamentally sound.

Method: The authors (1) introduce theoretical foundations for scaling laws in reinforcement learning, (2) analyze the relationship between performance rankings and data regimes, and (3) conduct large-scale experiments to empirically test whether algorithmic rankings are preserved across varying amounts of training data/compute. The methodology compares multiple RL algorithms under different data budgets to detect non-monotonic relationships in performance rankings.

Result: The paper demonstrates that the asymptotic performance of RL algorithms does not exhibit a monotonic relationship between performance rankings and data regimes. Large-scale experiments reveal that conclusions drawn under canonical design and evaluation paradigms in RL can be incorrect, as an algorithm that appears superior in one data regime may underperform in another.

Conclusion: A significant line of RL research conducted under prevailing evaluation paradigms has produced flawed conclusions. The work provides foundational analysis on scaling, capacity, and complexity in deep RL, calling for a re-examination of how RL algorithms are designed, evaluated, and compared — particularly with respect to how performance scales with data.

Abstract: Starting from the utilization of deep neural networks to approximate the state-action value function that led to winning one of the most challenging games, to algorithmic advancements that allowed solving problems without even explicitly stating the rules of the challenge at hand, reinforcement learning research has been the center of remarkable scientific progress for the past decade. In this paper, we focus on the key ingredients of this research progress and we analyze the canonical evaluation and design paradigms in reinforcement learning. We introduce the theoretical foundations of scaling laws in reinforcement learning and show that the asymptotic performance of reinforcement learning algorithms does not have a monotone relationship between performance rankings and data-regimes. We conduct large-scale experiments and our results demonstrate that a line of reinforcement learning research under the canonical design and evaluation paradigms resulted in incorrect conclusions. Our analysis and results provide a core analysis on scaling, capacity and complexity of deep reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [170] [Graph-Regularized Deep Learning for EEG-Based Emotion Recognition with Psychologically-Grounded Label Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07773)
*Dongyang Kuang,Zizheng Ma,Yushan Zhang,Xiaocong Zeng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a graph-regularized learning framework for EEG-based emotion recognition that exploits psychological interdependencies among emotion classes via three regularization strategies (Graph Label Smoothing, Graph Laplacian Commuting Distance, and Sliced Wasserstein Distance), achieving up to +5.42% accuracy gains and 39% reduction in psychologically implausible errors across multiple architectures on SEED-IV and SEED-V datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning methods for EEG emotion recognition treat emotion classes as independent, one-hot encoded labels, neglecting the psychological relationships among emotions (e.g., "sad" is closer to "fear" than to "happy"). This ignores well-established dimensional emotion theories (e.g., valence-arousal models) where emotions exist along continuous axes rather than as discrete isolated categories.

Method: The framework models emotions as nodes in a graph with edges encoding proximity based on dimensional emotion theories. Three graph-based regularization strategies are adapted, ordered by increasing complexity: (1) Graph Label Smoothing — softens hard labels to reflect neighbor proximity; (2) Commuting Distance via Graph Laplacian — leverages spectral graph theory to penalize predictions violating manifold structure; (3) Sliced Wasserstein Distance — uses optimal transport on the graph to align predicted and target distributions. These are evaluated with AudioTransformer, Conformer, and DCGNN backbones on SEED-IV (4-class) and SEED-V (5-class) datasets.

Result: The framework yields architecture-agnostic, consistent improvements across all backbone types: best-case accuracy gains of up to +5.42%, a 39% reduction in psychologically implausible misclassifications, and an elevated performance upper bound compared to standard training approaches.

Conclusion: Incorporating psychological structure of emotions via graph-regularized learning significantly improves EEG emotion recognition performance and plausibility. The framework is modular, backbone-agnostic, and sets a new performance ceiling for standard approaches. Code will be publicly released.

Abstract: EEG-based emotion recognition is critical for mental health monitoring and affective brain-computer interfaces, yet existing deep learning approaches often treat emotion classes as isolated labels, ignoring their psychological interdependencies. We propose a graph-regularized learning framework that conceptualizes emotions as nodes in a graph where edges encode proximity based on dimensional emotion theories. We adapt three complementary regularization strategies--Graph Label Smoothing (intuitive soft labeling), Commuting distance on graph via Graph Laplacian (spectral graph theory), and Sliced Wasserstein Distance (optimal transport on graph)--ordered by increasing computational complexity. These strategies penalize model predictions that deviate from the established emotion topology. Our framework is evaluated across three representative backbone architectures: AudioTransformer (pure transformer), Conformer (CNN-transformer hybrid), and DCGNN (causal graph neural network), demonstrating architecture-agnostic benefits. Experiments on SEED-IV (4 classes) and SEED-V (5 classes) datasets show consistent improvements: best case up to +5.42% accuracy and 39% reduction in psychologically implausible misclassifications. Ultimately, our framework help raise the upper bound of performance achievable with standard approaches. Code will be released.

</details>


### [171] [A law of robustness for two-layer neural networks with arbitrary weights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07778)
*Yitzchak Shmalo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proves a conjectured law: any two-layer neural network fitting noisy labels must have Lipschitz constant scaling as ~√(n/m), up to a log factor. Holds for continuous piecewise-linear activations (e.g., ReLU) with unbounded weights, via a function-space covering argument and a rigidity lemma controlling kink coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bubeck, Li, and Nagaraj conjectured that two-layer networks fitting noisy labels must exhibit a Lipschitz constant of order √(n/m). Bubeck-Sellke proved this under a polynomial parameter bound, but the unbounded-weight, two-layer case remained open and requires a different approach since parameter-space covering is impossible.

Method: Replaces parameter-space covering (infeasible for unbounded weights) with function-space covering. Central ingredient: a rigidity lemma showing that on the ball B₂ or sphere S^{d-1} (d≥3), canonical kink coefficients are controlled by the Lipschitz constant of the realized function, because kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel generically. The bound also extends to a realized-kink-count version (k(f)+1).

Result: Any width-m two-layer network (continuous piecewise-linear activation, arbitrary weights/biases/skip connection) fitting data ε below the noise floor satisfies Lip(f) ≥ c·ε·√(n/(m̄·log(C·m̄·n·d/ε))) with high probability, where m̄=(K-1)m+1. For d=2, rigidity fails; an explicit two-layer ReLU interpolant with O(1) Lipschitz constant at width 2n matches the bound at the overparameterized limit.

Conclusion: The conjectured law is established for two-layer unbounded-weight networks, with a logarithmic gap. Rigidity holds for d≥3 but genuinely fails at d=2, providing a sharp phase transition in dimension.

Abstract: Bubeck, Li and Nagaraj conjectured that, for generic data, any two-layer neural network with $m$ neurons that fits $n$ noisy labels must have Lipschitz constant at least of order $\sqrt{n/m}$, with no restriction on the size of the weights. Bubeck and Sellke proved a universal version of this law for Lipschitz-parameterized classes, but under a polynomial bound on the parameters; at depth three that boundedness hypothesis is genuinely necessary. The two-layer unbounded-weight case requires a different argument. We prove the conjectured law, up to one logarithmic factor, for every continuous piecewise-linear activation, in particular for ReLU networks. For data drawn uniformly from $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$, $d\ge3$, or from $N(0,I_d/d)$, labels in $[-1,1]$ with noise level $σ^2>0$, and any width-$m$ two-layer network with arbitrary real weights, biases and affine skip connection, fitting the data $\varepsilon$ below the noise floor forces $\mathrm{Lip}(f)\ge c\,\varepsilon\sqrt{n/(\bar m\log(C\bar m nd/\varepsilon))}$, $\bar m=(K-1)m+1$, with high probability. A realized-kink-count version holds on the same event: every realized two-layer piecewise-linear function with $k(f)\le n$ distinct kink hyperplanes obeys the bound with $\bar m$ replaced by $k(f)+1$, irrespective of how many redundant hidden units parameterize it. The proof replaces parameter-space covering, impossible for unbounded weights, by a function-space covering. The central deterministic ingredient is a rigidity lemma: on $B_2$, and on $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ for $d\ge3$, the coefficient of each canonical kink is controlled by the Lipschitz constant of the realized function, because kinks on distinct hyperplanes cannot cancel at generic points. Rigidity genuinely fails at $d=2$, and an explicit two-layer ReLU interpolant with $O(1)$ Lipschitz constant at width $2n$ matches the law at the overparameterized endpoint.

</details>


### [172] [Predicting Pseudo-nitzschia harmful algal blooms along the Portuguese Coast using satellite-derived predictors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07834)
*Ayman Bnoussaad,El Khalil Cherif,Ligia Pinto,Ramiro Neves,Alexandra D. Silva,Alexandre Bernardino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A spatio-temporal machine-learning framework using satellite-derived predictors was developed to forecast harmful algal blooms of Pseudo-nitzschia along the Portuguese Atlantic coast. Random Forest and Extra Trees models achieved AUC scores of 0.74–0.77, with seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions as dominant predictors. The framework demonstrates operational viability for satellite-supported HAB early-warning systems in eastern boundary upwelling regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pseudo-nitzschia diatoms pose recurrent risks to coastal ecosystems and shellfish harvesting along the Portuguese Atlantic coast. There is a need for predictive tools that can forecast harmful algal bloom (HAB) occurrence using readily available, remotely sensed data under realistic operational constraints, to support early-warning and management decisions.

Method: A decade-long dataset (2013–2023; 1,440 observations, >1,000 satellite-based predictors including SST, upwelling index, chlorophyll-a, and plankton functional types) was used. Sampling locations were partitioned into ecologically meaningful sub-regions via a river-aware spatial clustering scheme. A stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation strategy simultaneously withholding entire years and spatial clusters was employed to prevent leakage and mimic real-world forecasting. Ensemble tree-based methods (Random Forest, Extra Trees) were trained and evaluated, with feature importance analyses conducted to identify dominant predictors.

Result: HAB occurrence was moderately predictable. Random Forest achieved an AUC of 0.74 ± 0.05 using environmental predictors alone; Extra Trees reached 0.77 ± 0.06 with biological variables added. Feature importance analyses showed seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions dominated model decisions, while biological indicators refined bloom likelihood within physically favourable periods.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates operationally relevant skill for satellite-supported HAB early-warning systems along eastern boundary upwelling coasts, providing a transferable approach that integrates spatial, temporal, and biological information under realistic forecasting constraints.

Abstract: Pseudo-nitzschia diatoms pose recurrent risks to coastal ecosystems and shellfish harvesting along the Portuguese Atlantic coast. Here we develop and evaluate a spatio-temporal machine-learning framework to predict harmful algal bloom (HAB) occurrence using exclusively satellite-derived predictors under realistic forecasting constraints. We characterised environmental and biological variability across shellfish production zones (L1-L9) using 5,882 observations, providing system-wide context. Predictive models were developed for zones L1-L2, a hotspot for Pseudo-nitzschia and domoic acid events, using a decade-long dataset (2013-2023; 1,440 observations; more than 1,000 satellite-based predictors including sea surface temperature, an upwelling index, chlorophyll-a, and plankton functional types). Sampling locations were partitioned into ecologically meaningful sub-regions using a river-aware spatial clustering scheme. A stringent spatio-temporal cross-validation strategy that simultaneously withholds entire years and spatial clusters prevents leakage and closely mimics real-world forecasting conditions. HAB occurrence proved moderately predictable across model classes and feature configurations. Ensemble tree-based methods achieved the strongest discrimination: Random Forest reached 0.74 +/- 0.05 with environmental predictors; Extra Trees reached 0.77 +/- 0.06 with biological variables added. Feature-importance analyses revealed that seasonal structure, spatial context, and lagged environmental conditions dominate model decisions, while biological indicators refine bloom likelihood within physically favourable periods. The framework demonstrates operationally relevant skill for satellite-supported HAB early-warning systems along eastern boundary upwelling coasts.

</details>


### [173] [Explaining Near-Zero Hessian Eigenvalues Through Approximate Symmetries in Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07845)
*Marcel Kühn,Bernd Rosenow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The vast number of near-zero Hessian eigenvalues in neural networks arise from weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes of continuous symmetries in the parametrization. ReLU nonlinearities weakly break exact flat directions present in deep linear networks, and the bulk spectrum lies almost entirely within the symmetry subspace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While existing work explains the largest Hessian eigenvalues, the origin of the vast multitude of vanishingly small eigenvalues — the "bulk" of the spectrum — remains poorly understood. The authors aim to provide a principled explanation for these near-zero modes.

Method: The authors first analyze deep linear networks, where continuous parametrization symmetries generate exact flat directions; they construct the corresponding zero-mode eigenvectors explicitly. They then treat ReLU nonlinearity as a weak explicit symmetry-breaking perturbation. Eigenvector-level spectral analysis is performed to show high-curvature directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace while the bulk lies within it. The mechanism is demonstrated on a two-layer ReLU student-teacher model, a CIFAR-10 trained network, and a convolutional example.

Result: In deep linear networks, exact zero modes correspond to symmetry generators and their eigenvectors are constructible. Under ReLU perturbation, these become weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes comprising the bulk. High-curvature directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace; the bulk resides almost entirely within it. The diagnostic generalizes beyond fully connected layers to convolutional architectures.

Conclusion: The Hessian bulk is linked to weakly broken continuous symmetries of the network parametrization. Near-zero eigenvalues are pseudo-Goldstone modes, providing a unified framework for understanding the flat directions that dominate the loss landscape of neural networks.

Abstract: The Hessian of the training loss governs the local geometry of the loss landscape, yet despite existing explanations for its largest eigenvalues, the origin of the vast multitude of vanishingly small eigenvalues remains elusive. We argue that the bulk consists of the weakly lifted pseudo-Goldstone modes of the continuous symmetries of the network parametrization. In deep linear networks these symmetries are exact: they generate flat directions and hence exact zero modes, whose eigenvectors we construct explicitly. Introducing a ReLU nonlinearity as a perturbation, we show that it breaks these symmetries weakly and explicitly. Resolving the spectrum at the level of eigenvectors, we find that the high-curvature directions are orthogonal to the symmetry subspace, while the bulk lies almost entirely within it. We demonstrate the mechanism in a two-layer ReLU student--teacher model and in a network trained on CIFAR-10. A convolutional example demonstrates that the same diagnostic extends beyond fully connected layers. Together, these results link the Hessian bulk to weakly broken symmetries and clarify the origin of near-zero modes.

</details>


### [174] [When Does Continual Learning Require Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07847)
*Anne Harrington,Nayan Saxena,Michael Murphy,Anastasia Borovykh,Zeyu Yun,Sridhar Kamath,Ara Eindra Kyi,Trevor Darrell,Jitendra Malik,Yutong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper reframes continual learning for LLMs as adapting to change along two axes — space (new domains) and time (data drift) — and evaluates prompt-based, supervised, RL, and context compression methods under a unified sequential benchmark protocol, finding that no single method excels universally; rather, different environmental change patterns demand fundamentally different update strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current continual learning research for LLMs focuses narrowly on context management and forgetting mitigation. The authors argue this framing is incomplete: continual learning should be about increasing model competence as the world changes. They aim to study continual learning under realistic, multi-faceted conditions — sequential domain arrival, factual drift, and agentic state accumulation — to understand which methods suit which types of environmental change.

Method: The authors recast standard LLM benchmarks as sequential problems and introduce a mechanism-agnostic evaluation protocol. They systematically compare four families of methods: (1) prompt-based methods (GEPA, ACE), (2) supervised learning (SFT, SDFT), (3) reinforcement learning (GRPO, SDPO), and (4) context compression (Cartridges, In-place TTT). Evaluation spans two change axes: spatial (new domains over time) and temporal (data drift under fixed tasks).

Result: Prompt-based methods adapt quickly to each new stage but degrade on future tasks. Distillation-based approaches accumulate knowledge stably but fail to update outdated facts. Context compression improves efficiency without substantially boosting new-task learning. Online reinforcement learning adapts best to knowledge updates but is sensitive to noisy reward signals. No single method handles all change patterns effectively.

Conclusion: Continual learning is not a monolithic capability. Different patterns of environmental change require fundamentally different update behaviors, determining when adaptation must occur inside model weights versus through external scaffolding. Understanding where each method succeeds and fails should guide the design of stronger continual learning systems.

Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly capable, the next question is how can we enable models to continually learn? Today, the field largely frames this as a problem of context management and mitigating forgetting. We argue this framing is incomplete: continual learning is fundamentally about increasing model competence as the world changes. We disentangle this change along two axes -- space, where the model encounters new domains, and time, where the underlying data drifts under a fixed task. This framing lets us study continual learning under realistic conditions: new domains arrive over time, facts drift past their training cutoff, and agentic interactions accumulate state across episodes. To evaluate methods under this setting, we recast widely used LLM benchmarks as sequential problems and introduce a single mechanism-agnostic protocol that compares prompt-based methods (GEPA, ACE), supervised learning (SFT, SDFT), reinforcement learning (GRPO, SDPO), and context compression (Cartridges, In-place TTT). Prompt-based methods fit each new stage quickly but degrade on future tasks. Distillation-based methods accumulate knowledge stably but struggle to update outdated facts. Context compression improves efficiency without substantially improving the ability to learn new tasks. Online reinforcement learning adapts most effectively to knowledge updates but remains sensitive to noisy reward signals. Overall, our results suggest that continual learning is not a single capability: different patterns of environmental change require fundamentally different update behaviors, determining when adaptation must be learned inside model weights and when it can be achieved through external scaffolding. We hope that understanding where each method succeeds and fails will guide the design of stronger continual learning systems.

</details>


### [175] [NFTR: From Provable Mode-Averaging to Geodesic Subgoal Selection in Offline Goal-Conditioned RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07855)
*Erdemt Bao,Xing Lei,Jun Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NFTR improves offline goal-conditioned RL subgoal selection by replacing Gaussian policies with Normalizing Flows and introducing a triangle-slack reweighting mechanism to prevent optimistic bias and mode collapse, achieving provably stable subgoal generation under stochastic dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HIQL's advantage-based subgoal selection suffers from two coupled failures: (1) optimistic bias treats lucky stochastic outcomes as skillful, and (2) mode collapse reduces multi-modal subgoal distributions to a single Gaussian mean that often lands in unreachable areas. Both undermine reliable long-horizon planning.

Method: Two components: (1) Replace the Gaussian policy with a conditional Normalizing Flow, shown via closed-form mode-averaging to be the minimal generative class for AWR-based subgoal selection. (2) Introduce a triangle-slack score — based on the architectural triangle inequality, not distance accuracy — that multiplicatively reweights AWR weights to downweight subgoals whose detour cost exceeds average reachability. The slack vanishes on geodesics in deterministic MDPs and upper-bounds composability violation under stochastic dynamics. The resulting RWDR objective preserves AWR's population-level monotonic improvement and yields a three-term suboptimality decomposition.

Result: NFTR provably avoids Gaussian mode collapse and remains stable under stochastic dynamics. The triangle-slack provides a conservative upper bound on composability violation without requiring accurate distance estimates.

Conclusion: Combining Normalizing Flows with triangle-slack reweighting yields a theoretically grounded, stable offline subgoal selection method that overcomes the core failure modes of advantage-only selection.

Abstract: Hierarchical Implicit Q-Learning (HIQL), an offline goal-conditioned RL method, selects subgoals by value-function advantages alone. This rule has two coupled failure modes. Optimistic bias treats lucky stochastic outcomes as skillful choices, and mode collapse reduces a multi-modal subgoal distribution to a single Gaussian mean that often falls in unreachable regions. We propose NFTR (Normalizing Flows subgoal policies with Triangle-slack Reweighting). A conditional Normalizing Flow replaces the Gaussian policy, and a closed-form mode-averaging result identifies NFs as the minimal generative class for AWR-based subgoal selection. A triangle slack score, built on the architectural triangle inequality without relying on distance accuracy, multiplicatively corrects the AWR weight to downweight subgoals whose detour cost exceeds average reachability. Triangle-slack vanishes on geodesics in deterministic MDPs and remains a conservative upper bound on composability violation under stochastic dynamics. The RWDR objective preserves AWR's population-level monotonic improvement and admits a three-term suboptimality decomposition. Together, these two ingredients yield subgoal selection that provably avoids the Gaussian collapse described above and remains stable under stochastic dynamics. GitHub page: https://github.com/erdemtbao/NFTR

</details>


### [176] [Physics-Informed Machine Learning Under Small-Data Constraints: Lessons from Abrasive Waterjet Milling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07863)
*Sarah Grewe,Jörg Frochte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For small, expensive machining datasets, the authors show that how data is curated, how models are evaluated, and how physics is integrated matter more than the choice of learning algorithm. Using abrasive waterjet milling of Inconel 718 (n=155), they advocate treating data curation as competing hypotheses, demonstrate instability in single-split evaluation, and find GP residual-learning on a physics baseline performs best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In physically dominated machining processes, experimental datasets are inherently small, costly, and material-specific. In this low-data regime, standard ML practices around data preprocessing, model evaluation, and physics integration are underexamined — yet these methodological choices can dominate predictive performance, potentially outweighing algorithm selection.

Method: Using an abrasive waterjet milling dataset (n=155, Inconel 718), the authors make three contributions: (1) They decouple physics-based data cleaning from statistical curation, framing curation choices as explicit competing modelling hypotheses. (2) They compare evaluation strategies, contrasting a 15-point hold-out set with 10-fold cross-validation to assess ranking stability. (3) They systematically vary the level of physics integration (from pure data-driven to residual learning on a compact physics baseline) across GP, tree-based, and SVR models, with Bayesian hyperparameter tuning.

Result: Single-split hold-out rankings are unstable: the hold-out winner drops from rank 1 to rank 7 under 10-fold CV, while GP variants dominate the top ranks under CV. Residual learning on a physics baseline benefits GP (lower variance, interpretable decomposition) but degrades tree-based models. Bayesian tuning helps parameter-sensitive models (gradient boosting, SVR) but harms multi-stage hybrid pipelines at this sample size. GP uncertainty intervals achieve ~86% empirical coverage at a nominal 90% level.

Conclusion: For small, expensive process datasets, reliable model comparison requires explicit curation hypotheses, robust cross-validation, and judicious choices about how physics enters the model. No single approach dominates universally; the interaction between model class and physics integration strategy is critical.

Abstract: In physically dominated machining processes, experimental datasets are small, expensive, and material-specific; in this regime, data curation, evaluation design, and the form of physics integration can matter as much as the learning algorithm. Using an abrasive waterjet milling dataset ($n{=}155$, Inconel\,718), we make three methodological contributions. First, we separate physics-based data \emph{cleaning} from statistical \emph{curation} and treat the latter as competing modelling hypotheses rather than silent preprocessing. Second, we find that model rankings from a 15-point hold-out set can be unstable: the single-split winner drops from rank~1 to rank~7 under 10-fold cross-validation, while Gaussian Process (GP) variants occupy the top ranks. Third, we study a spectrum of physics integration levels and find that residual learning on a compact physics baseline is competitive for GP, yielding lower variance and an interpretable decomposition, but degrades tree-based models. Bayesian hyper parameter tuning improves parameter-sensitive baselines such as gradient boosting and SVR, yet harms multi-stage hybrid pipelines at this sample size. GP uncertainty intervals are approximately calibrated ($86\%$ empirical coverage at nominal $90\%$). The resulting picture is methodological: for small, expensive process datasets, our results suggest that, in this setting, reliable model comparison benefits from explicit curation hypotheses, robust evaluation, and careful choices about how physics enters the model.

</details>


### [177] [Multimodal Unlearning Across Vision, Language, Video, and Audio: Survey of Methods, Datasets, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07907)
*Nobin Sarwar,Shubhashis Roy Dipta,Zheyuan Liu,Vaidehi Patil*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A survey paper that systematizes multimodal unlearning — the selective removal of sensitive, copyrighted, biased, or unsafe cross-modal knowledge from vision-language-audio-video foundation models — providing a taxonomy, comparing trade-offs (deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, robustness), and identifying open problems for future research and deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal foundation models (VLMs, DMs, LLMs, AFMs) inadvertently encode undesirable cross-modal associations from training data. Retraining is impractical, and targeted forgetting is challenging due to distributed representations. Multimodal unlearning addresses the need for selective knowledge removal across modalities without destroying overall model utility, motivated by deletion requests, policy compliance, and safety requirements.

Method: The authors present a unified, system-oriented survey with a structured taxonomy that enables systematic comparison of multimodal unlearning approaches across model architectures and modalities (vision, language, audio, video). The taxonomy is grounded in recent advances, emerging applications, and characterizes trade-offs along key dimensions: deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness.

Result: The survey provides a comprehensive mapping of the multimodal unlearning landscape, clarifies the design space and inherent trade-offs among competing objectives, catalogs open problems, and offers practical deployment considerations. A curated repository of resources accompanies the paper.

Conclusion: Multimodal unlearning is an emerging, critical area for responsible deployment of foundation models. The survey identifies open challenges and practical considerations, laying groundwork for future research and real-world adoption, supported by a publicly curated resource repository.

Abstract: With the growing adoption of VLMs, DMs, LLMs, and AFMs, these multimodal foundation models can inadvertently encode sensitive, copyrighted, biased, or unsafe cross-modal associations that originate from their training data. Retraining after deletion requests or policy updates is often impractical, and targeted forgetting remains difficult because knowledge is distributed across shared representations. Multimodal unlearning addresses this challenge by enabling selective removal across modalities while retaining overall utility. This survey offers a unified, system-oriented view of multimodal unlearning across vision, language, audio, and video, grounded in recent advances, emerging applications, and open problems. Our taxonomy enables systematic comparison across model architectures and modalities, clarifying trade-offs among deletion strength, retention, efficiency, reversibility, and robustness. This survey highlights open problems and practical considerations to support future research and deployment of multimodal unlearning. We release a curated repository: https://smsnobin77.github.io/Awesome-Multimodal-Unlearning/

</details>


### [178] [Optimal Learning Rate Scaling Depends on Data in Deep Scalar Linear Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07884)
*Yedi Zhang,Peter E. Latham,Leena Chennuru Vankadara,Andrew Saxe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes gradient descent dynamics in deep scalar linear networks, showing that optimal layer-wise learning rate scaling is data-dependent, not data-agnostic. Under optimal scaling, convergence rates become constant across all depths, including infinite depth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing works on deep linear networks often propose data-agnostic scaling rules for learning rates across depths. The authors challenge this by showing that even in the simplest possible deep network — a scalar linear product — the optimal depth-wise learning rate scaling inherently depends on the data distribution, and data-agnostic rules fail to generalize across depths.

Method: The authors study the continuous-time gradient descent dynamics of deep scalar linear networks of the form f(x) = (∏_{l=1}^L w_l) · x, for which exact time-course solutions exist for any integer depth L. They derive the optimal depth-dependent learning rate scaling that depends on the second moment of the input data and analyze the resulting convergence behavior, including an extension to networks with residual connections.

Result: The optimal learning rate scaling is data-dependent, governed by the input data's second moment. With this optimal scaling, learning dynamics become independent of data and only weakly dependent on depth, yielding a constant linear convergence rate across all depths, including the infinite-depth limit. Similar data-dependent effects are demonstrated in scalar linear networks with residual connections.
