Conclusion: Even in the simplest deep linear scalar model, data-agnostic learning rate scaling rules are suboptimal. Data-dependent scaling yields depth-invariant, constant-rate convergence, highlighting that data statistics fundamentally govern optimal training dynamics in deep networks.

Abstract: In this short note we consider the gradient descent dynamics of deep scalar linear networks, $f(x) = \prod_{l=1}^L w_l x$, which enjoy exact time-course solutions for any integer depth. We show that even in this minimal model, the optimal depth-wise learning rate scaling depends on data, whereas data-agnostic scaling rules fail to transfer across depths. Under the data-dependent optimal scaling, the learning dynamics is independent of data and weakly dependent on depth, resulting in a constant linear convergence rate across all depths including infinity. We further show similar data-dependent effects in deep scalar linear networks with residual connections.

</details>


### [179] [Efficient Safety Alignment of Language Models via Latent Personality Traits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07918)
*Mohamed Amine Merzouk,Nolan Smyth,Damiano Fornasiere,Linh Le,David Williams-King,Adam Oberman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LPA adversarially trains LLMs on just 66 personality statements instead of harmful prompts, achieving near-zero jailbreak attack success rates while preserving utility and using 75x fewer examples than standard Latent Adversarial Training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety methods (including LAT) are vulnerable to adversarial jailbreak attacks. LAT, while effective, degrades model utility and requires large datasets of harmful prompts. The authors hypothesize that personality-anchored representations share latent structure with harm avoidance, so stabilizing them can implicitly constrain the subspace exploited by jailbreak attacks.

Method: Latent Personality Alignment (LPA) adversarially trains LLMs on only 66 harm-agnostic statements drawn from psychometric personality literature, replacing explicit harm refusal training. The method performs adversarial training in the latent space using these personality-anchored representations, and the entire procedure completes in minutes on a single GPU.

Result: LPA achieves near-zero attack success rates on HarmBench across direct requests and five jailbreak methods, despite never seeing harmful content during training. It maintains standard benchmark performance (no utility degradation) and uses 75x fewer examples than standard LAT. Extensive ablations confirm robustness, efficiency, and generalization.

Conclusion: Personality-aligned adversarial training in latent space is a lightweight, efficient, and effective safety alignment method that decouples harm mitigation from harmful training data, offering a promising alternative to traditional safety fine-tuning approaches.

Abstract: Current safety methods for large language models are known to be vulnerable to adversarial attacks, motivating research into robust alternatives. Latent Adversarial Training (LAT) is among the most effective defenses, but can degrade utility and requires training on large datasets of harmful prompts. We introduce Latent Personality Alignment (LPA), which replaces explicit harm refusal with adversarial training on just 66 harm-agnostic statements drawn from psychometric personality literature. We hypothesize that personality-anchored representations share latent structure with harm avoidance, so adversarially stabilizing them implicitly constrains the subspace exploited by jailbreak attacks. LPA achieves near-zero attack success rates on HarmBench across direct requests and five jailbreak methods, despite never seeing harmful content during training and no loss of performance on standard benchmarks. Moreover, the training process is lightweight; the entire procedure completes in minutes on a single GPU and uses 75x fewer examples than standard LAT. Extensive ablations demonstrate the robustness, efficiency, and generalization of our method.

</details>


### [180] [Distributed Sketching on Data Partitions for OLS Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07888)
*Luyuan Yang,Brayden Garner,Shayan Shafaei,Chao Lan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes distributed sketching for OLS regression, where small sketches are built on partitioned data subsets rather than the full dataset. Under a fixed design setting, it characterizes the exact excess loss of the averaged OLS estimator and shows this loss is comparable to full-dataset sketching when subset covariance divergence is small.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on distributed sketching for OLS regression applies sketching to the entire dataset, which still incurs substantial computational cost. The authors aim to further reduce computation by sketching on partitioned subsets independently across machines, and then theoretically characterize when this approach performs comparably.

Method: The paper adopts a fixed design setting and analyzes the averaged OLS estimator constructed from independent sketches on partitioned data subsets. It derives the exact excess loss (relative to the full-data OLS estimator) in closed form, decomposing it into components that depend on sketching error and the divergence among subset covariance matrices.

Result: The exact excess loss is characterized and shown to consist of a term from sketching on the whole dataset plus an additional term proportional to the divergence among subset covariances. When this divergence is small, the distributed sketching approach achieves comparable performance to full-dataset sketching while offering lower per-machine computational cost.

Conclusion: Distributed sketching on partitioned subsets is a viable strategy for large-scale OLS regression, offering reduced computational burden with negligible loss in estimation accuracy provided that the covariate distributions across subsets are reasonably homogeneous.

Abstract: This paper studies distributed sketching for ordinary least squares (OLS) regression, an approach that distributes small sketches of a large data set over multiple machines to separately construct OLS estimators and average them. Unlike prior studies that consider sketching on the whole data set, we consider sketching on partitioned subsets to further reduce computational cost. Under the fixed design setting, we characterize the exact excess loss of the averaged OLS estimator. Results show that this loss is comparable to the established loss for sketching on the whole data set when the divergence among subset covariances is small.

</details>


### [181] [path_boost: A Python Package for Interpretable Graph-Level Prediction using Path-Based Gradient Boosting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07935)
*Claudio Meggio,Johan Pensar,Riccardo De Bin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PathBoost is a Python package implementing a gradient boosting algorithm for interpretable supervised learning on graph data. It automatically discovers predictive labeled paths, producing additive models that reveal which substructures drive predictions, avoiding exhaustive path enumeration. Compatible with scikit-learn, it supports regression, classification, parallel training, and variable importance, and is benchmarked against GNNs and graph kernel methods on molecular datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph neural networks (GNNs) achieve strong predictive performance on graph-structured data but suffer from poor interpretability — it is difficult to understand which substructures drive their predictions. There is a need for methods that combine competitive predictive power with explicit, human-interpretable models that reveal the underlying graph patterns influencing outcomes.

Method: The paper introduces PathBoost, a gradient boosting algorithm that iteratively selects and extends labeled paths within graphs based on their predictive power. Instead of exhaustively enumerating all possible paths, it uses boosting to combine weak learners (path-based features) into a strong ensemble. The resulting model is an additive prediction function over discovered paths. The Python package (path_boost) supports regression and binary classification, custom base learners and selectors, automatic starting node selection, parallel training across anchor nodes, and variable importance computation, with scikit-learn compatibility.

Result: PathBoost is demonstrated on molecular property prediction of transition metal compounds and benchmarked against an established GNN and a graph kernel method across six molecular datasets, showing competitive performance while maintaining interpretability through explicit path-based explanations of predictions.

Conclusion: PathBoost provides an effective, interpretable alternative to GNNs for supervised learning on graph-structured data, combining the predictive strength of gradient boosting with transparent, path-based feature discovery. Its open-source availability on PyPI and GitHub facilitates adoption in cheminformatics and other graph-based domains.

Abstract: We present path_boost, a Python package for interpretable supervised learning on graph-structured input data. The package implements PathBoost, a gradient boosting algorithm that automatically discovers predictive labeled paths within graphs during the learning process. Unlike graph neural networks, which are generally difficult to interpret, PathBoost produces an additive prediction model over path-based features that explicitly reveals which substructures drive predictions. To avoid an exhaustive enumeration of all possible paths, the algorithm iteratively selects and extends paths during learning based on their predictive power, using boosting to combine weak learners into a strong ensemble. The package supports both regression and binary classification. Key features include compatibility with scikit-learn workflows, support for custom base learners and selectors, automatic starting node selection, parallel training across anchor nodes, and built-in variable importance computation. We demonstrate PathBoost on molecular property prediction of transition metal compounds, where atoms serve as nodes and bonds as edges, and further benchmark PathBoost against an established graph neural network and a graph kernel method across six molecular datasets. The package is available on PyPI and GitHub under an open-source license.

</details>


### [182] [Evaluating the Generalizability of Foundation Models for Extreme Environmental Events: Case Study of California Wildfire PM2.5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07951)
*Yongcan Huang,Li Jiang,Ze Yu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A benchmark of six time series foundation models against traditional baselines for forecasting extreme PM2.5 during wildfire events reveals that a fine-tuned BiLSTM consistently outperforms all foundation models, including LoRA-adapted variants, on both standard error metrics and rare hazardous-level exceedance detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) show strong performance on general benchmarks, but their reliability under extreme out-of-distribution conditions—specifically rare, hazardous wildfire smoke spikes—is unknown. There is a need to systematically evaluate whether pretrained models generalize to these high-stakes forecasting scenarios.

Method: The authors benchmark six TSFM configurations (zero-shot TimesFM, Chronos-2, Moirai-2, Time-MoE; LoRA fine-tuned Chronos-2 and Time-MoE) against fully trained LSTM, BiLSTM, Transformer, and persistence baselines. Evaluation uses a 12-year hourly PM2.5 dataset (1,375 wildfire incidents, 79 California sites) under a leave-one-incident-out (LOIO) protocol. Metrics include MAE, RMSE, and exceedance F1 at EPA AQI thresholds across 6-, 12-, and 24-hour horizons.

Result: The BiLSTM achieves the lowest MAE (5.16 µg/m³) and highest exceedance F1 at every AQI threshold, including 0.63 for the Hazardous band (>225.5 µg/m³) vs. at most 0.54 for any foundation model. Zero-shot TSFMs improve only modestly over persistence. Zero-shot Chronos-2 exhibits severe RMSE instability (23.4 µg/m³, negative R²). LoRA fine-tuning substantially improves adapted models and repairs instability, but no foundation model surpasses the trained recurrent baselines on any metric.

Conclusion: Larger pretrained models do not universally dominate environmental forecasting. For wildfire air quality prediction, well-tuned recurrent architectures (particularly BiLSTM) remain superior, providing actionable deployment guidance that challenges the assumption of foundation model supremacy in extreme out-of-distribution settings.

Abstract: Wildfire smoke events produce extreme PM$_{2.5}$ concentrations that pose severe public health risks, yet forecasting rare, hazardous-level spikes remains a fundamental challenge. Time series foundation models (TSFMs), pretrained models offering zero-shot inference and efficient adaptation, perform strongly on general benchmarks, but their behavior under extreme out-of-distribution conditions is poorly understood. We present the first systematic benchmark comparing six TSFM configurations (zero-shot TimesFM, Chronos-2, Moirai-2, and Time-MoE, plus LoRA fine-tuned Chronos-2 and Time-MoE) against fully-trained baselines (LSTM, BiLSTM, Transformer) and naive persistence on a 12-year (2013--2025) hourly PM$_{2.5}$ dataset covering 1,375 wildfire incidents across 79 California monitoring sites. A leave-one-incident-out (LOIO) protocol evaluates generalization to unseen fires, using MAE, RMSE, and exceedance F1 at EPA AQI thresholds across 6-, 12-, and 24-hour horizons. Results reveal a consistent hierarchy. The BiLSTM achieves the lowest MAE ($5.16\,μg/m^3$) and the highest exceedance F1 at every threshold, including the Hazardous band ($>225.5\,μg/m^3$), reaching 0.63 versus at most 0.54 for any foundation model. Zero-shot TSFMs improve on persistence only modestly, and zero-shot Chronos-2 exhibits severe RMSE tail instability ($23.4\,μg/m^3$, negative $R^2$) from sporadic large errors. LoRA fine-tuning substantially improves both adapted families and largely repairs this instability, yet no foundation model surpasses the trained recurrent baselines on any metric. These findings challenge the assumption that larger pretrained models universally dominate environmental forecasting and provide actionable deployment guidance for wildfire air quality prediction.

</details>


### [183] [Linear Attention Architectures: Mechanisms, Trade-offs, and Cross-Layer Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07953)
*Tommaso Cerruti,Tim Rieder,George Rowlands,Lingfeng Jin,Imanol Schlag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Compares softmax attention with four recurrent linear-attention architectures (DeltaNet variants). 350M-parameter models trained on 15B tokens show Kimi Delta Attention + Muon achieves lowest validation loss, Gated DeltaNet + AdamW yields highest throughput. Introduces Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR), which modestly improves DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quadratic cost of softmax attention limits long-context training and inference. Recurrent linear-attention architectures promise lower complexity, but lack systematic comparison across expressivity, throughput, and optimization.

Method: Unified recurrent-memory notation comparing DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, and Gated DeltaNet-2. Experiments at 350M params / 15B tokens with optimizer (AdamW vs. Muon), hybrid vs. pure stacks, runtime profiling, scaling to 1.3B/3B, and downstream evaluation. Introduces cross-layer routing: delta-rule write error forwarding and value forwarding (CLVR).

Result: Kimi Delta Attention + Muon: lowest final validation loss. Pure Gated DeltaNet + AdamW: highest normalized training throughput. Hybrid stacks: better loss at throughput cost. Muon consistently outperforms AdamW in matched settings. Delta-rule write error routing fails; CLVR (value routing into hidden stream) yields modest loss reduction for both DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet.

Conclusion: Linear recurrent attention architectures present practical accuracy–throughput trade-offs. Muon optimizer and CLVR both improve outcomes. Key open questions remain regarding inference-speed benchmarking and scaling behavior beyond 350M parameters.

Abstract: Self-attention lets each token retrieve information from the full context, but its quadratic cost in sequence length limits training and inference at long context. This paper presents a comparative study of softmax attention and four recent recurrent linear-attention architectures: DeltaNet, Gated DeltaNet, Kimi Delta Attention, and Gated DeltaNet-2. We express these mechanisms in a common recurrent-memory notation, making explicit how they differ in expressivity, memory decay, erase and write control, training throughput, and implementation complexity. Our experiments center on 350M-parameter models trained for 15B tokens, and include optimizer and learning-rate comparisons, hybrid-versus-pure stack comparisons, sequence-length runtime measurements, larger DeltaNet runs at 1.3B and 3B parameters, and a small set of downstream evaluations. The reported speed results measure training throughput and iteration time; we do not provide an empirical inference-speed benchmark. Within the reported 350M-parameter, 15B-token sweep, Kimi Delta Attention with Muon reaches the lowest final validation loss, a pure Gated DeltaNet stack trained with AdamW has the highest normalized training throughput, hybrid stacks generally improve loss at a throughput cost, and Muon consistently lowers final validation loss relative to AdamW in the matched architecture settings we evaluate. We introduce and evaluate lightweight cross-layer routing mechanisms for DeltaNet-style memories. The most natural DeltaNet-inspired formulation, forwarding a lower layer's delta-rule write error into the next layer's value target, does not improve over matched baselines. Routing into the aligned hidden stream and forwarding the write value instead yields a modest improvement in the matched runs we report: Cross-Layer Value Routing (CLVR) lowers final validation loss for both DeltaNet and Gated DeltaNet.

</details>


### [184] [KronQ: LLM Quantization via Kronecker-Factored Hessian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07964)
*Donghyun Lee,Yuhang Li,Ruokai Yin,Priyadarshini Panda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KronQ improves post-training quantization (PTQ) by incorporating gradient covariance alongside activation statistics under a Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation, enabling bidirectional incoherence processing and a new mixed-precision sensitivity metric. It notably achieves 7.93 WikiText-2 perplexity for 2-bit weight-only quantization on LLaMA-3-70B, where GPTQ and GPTAQ fail entirely.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing second-order PTQ methods (e.g., GPTQ) construct quantization objectives solely from input activation statistics, implicitly assuming all output channels contribute equally to the reconstruction loss. This assumption is suboptimal. The authors aim to demonstrate that gradient covariance carries complementary, essential information for quantization and that jointly leveraging activation and gradient covariances under a Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation yields significantly better quantization outcomes.

Method: KronQ operates under the Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation, where the quantization loss depends jointly on activation and gradient covariances. The framework exploits this at two levels: (1) Bidirectional incoherence processing — extending input-side random rotation (used in prior work) to the output dimension via gradient covariance, reducing weight magnitude variance across both input and output dimensions; (2) A new inter-layer mixed-precision sensitivity metric driven by traces of the gradient and activation Hessians, enabling more informed bit-width allocation across layers.

Result: On 2-bit weight-only quantization of LLaMA-3-70B, GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge or produce degenerate results (>2000 perplexity on WikiText-2), while KronQ achieves 7.93 perplexity, demonstrating dramatic improvements over existing second-order PTQ methods in extreme low-bit regimes.

Conclusion: Gradient covariance is a critical yet previously overlooked signal for post-training quantization. By jointly modeling activation and gradient covariances via the Kronecker-factored Hessian, KronQ substantially improves quantization quality, particularly in extreme compression settings (e.g., 2-bit) where prior methods collapse.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is a widely adopted technique for compressing large language models (LLMs) without retraining. Existing second-order PTQ methods, including GPTQ, construct quantization objectives exclusively from input activation statistics, effectively assuming that all output channels contribute equally to the layer-wise reconstruction objective. We propose KronQ, a PTQ framework that challenges this assumption by introducing the gradient covariance into the quantization pipeline. Under the Kronecker-factored Hessian approximation, the quantization loss depends jointly on both the activation and gradient covariances, and KronQ exploits this at two complementary levels. (1) KronQ introduces bidirectional incoherence processing, extending the existing input-side random rotation to the output dimension using the gradient covariance, reducing weight magnitude variance across both input and output dimensions. (2) KronQ derives a new sensitivity metric for inter-layer mixed-precision allocation, driven by the gradient and activation Hessian traces. Notably, in the case of 2-bit weight-only quantization on LLaMA-3-70B, while GPTQ and GPTAQ diverge or produce degenerate quantizations (>2000 perplexity on WikiText-2), KronQ achieves 7.93 perplexity.

</details>


### [185] [Provably Optimal Learning Algorithms for Assistance Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08012)
*Nivasini Ananthakrishnan,Mark Bedaywi,Michael I. Jordan,Stuart Russell,Nika Haghtalab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides the first provably efficient online learning algorithms for repeated assistance games, where a human (informed agent) and an assistant (uninformed agent) interact over T timesteps. They introduce assistance regret and present decentralized algorithms achieving (1-1/e)-approximate regret of Õ(T³/⁴), with a hardness result showing (1-1/e) is optimal, and an improved Õ(T¹/²) rate in a pseudo-decentralized setting using shared randomness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assistance games model human-AI collaboration where an informed human observes latent world states and an uninformed AI assistant must learn to help from only the human's actions. Prior work lacked provably efficient online learning algorithms for this repeated interaction setting, leaving open whether near-optimal coordinated policies can be learned with low regret.

Method: The authors formalize assistance regret as the gap between cumulative realized utility and that of the optimal joint policy in hindsight mapping latent states to action pairs. They design decentralized algorithms where the human uses a no-regret bandit strategy over policy recommendations and the assistant runs any no-regret algorithm over its own action space. The analysis uses a reduction to online linear optimization and exploits submodularity of the joint reward function. The improved Õ(T¹/²) rate is achieved via correlated randomization using a shared random string in a pseudo-decentralized setting.

Result: The decentralized algorithms achieve (1-1/e)-approximate assistance regret of Õ(T³/⁴) with polynomial runtime. They prove that no polynomial-time algorithm can achieve an approximation factor better than (1-1/e), establishing computational hardness. In the pseudo-decentralized setting with shared randomness, the regret improves to Õ(T¹/²), which is optimal up to logarithmic factors.

Conclusion: Repeated assistance games admit provably efficient online learning with strong regret guarantees. The (1-1/e) approximation barrier is fundamental (computationally intractable to surpass), but shared randomness enables an optimal Õ(T¹/²) rate, establishing tight bounds for this class of human-AI coordination problems.

Abstract: This paper studies an online variant of the assistance games framework, where an informed agent and an uninformed agent repeatedly interact over $T$ timesteps to optimize a common reward function. While the informed agent (the human) observes a latent state of the world, the uninformed agent (the assistant) observes only the human's actions. We provide the first provably efficient learning algorithms for repeated assistance games. We introduce the notion of assistance regret: the gap between the cumulative utility of interactions and that of the optimal joint policies in hindsight, which map latent states to action pairs. We present decentralized algorithms for both the human and the assistant that achieve a $(1-1/e)$-approximate assistance regret rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{3/4})$, with runtime polynomial in the size of the action and state spaces. These algorithms are general; in particular, they accommodate any no-regret algorithm for the assistant. We prove that achieving a regret approximation factor better than $(1-1/e)$ is computationally intractable. Furthermore, we demonstrate how these generic no-regret algorithms can be tailored to a pseudo-decentralized setting -- using a shared random string -- to achieve a rate of $\widetilde{O}(T^{1/2})$, optimal up to logarithmic factors.

</details>


### [186] [Collate: Collaborative Neural Network Learning for Latency-Critical Edge Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08013)
*Shuo Huai,Di Liu,Hao Kong,Xiangzhong Luo,Weichen Liu,Ravi Subramaniam,Christian Makaya,Qian Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Collate is a federated learning framework that trains heterogeneous models collaboratively to satisfy diverse latency constraints across edge systems. It uses dynamic zeroizing-recovering for per-client architecture adaptation and proto-corrected federated aggregation for unifying heterogeneous local models, achieving up to 3.09% accuracy gains with negligible training overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing FL optimizations focus on training efficiency but neglect inference speed under latency constraints. Device heterogeneity in real-time edge systems causes severe performance degradation, and no prior method addresses simultaneous latency-constrained inference across multiple heterogeneous systems within a single FL training process.

Method: (1) Dynamic zeroizing-recovering method: adapts each local model architecture by pruning (zeroizing) or restoring (recovering) components to meet per-device latency budgets while preserving accuracy. (2) Proto-corrected federated aggregation: aggregates heterogeneous local models from different architectures into a unified global process using prototype-based corrections, enabling a single training run to serve multiple latency constraints.

Result: Compared to state-of-the-art methods under latency constraints: extended models achieve 1.96% average accuracy improvement; shrunk models achieve 3.09% average accuracy improvement. These gains come with almost no extra training overhead.

Conclusion: Collate effectively addresses the joint challenge of device heterogeneity and inference latency constraints in FL by enabling collaborative training of heterogeneous models. It delivers meaningful accuracy improvements for both enlarged and compressed model variants without additional training cost, making it practical for real-time edge deployment.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) empowers multiple clients to collaboratively learn a model, enlarging the training data of each client for high accuracy while protecting data privacy. However, when deploying FL in real-time edge systems, the heterogeneity of devices among systems has a severe impact on the performance of the inferred model. Existing optimizations on FL focus on improving the training efficiency but fail to speed up inference, especially when there is a latency constraint. In this work, we propose Collate, a novel training framework that collaboratively learns heterogeneous models to meet the latency constraints of multiple edge systems simultaneously. We design a dynamic zeroizing-recovering method to adjust each local model architecture for high accuracy under its latency constraint. A proto-corrected federated aggregation scheme is also introduced to aggregate all heterogeneous local models, satisfying the latency constraint of different systems with only one training process and maintaining high accuracy. Extensive experiments indicate that, compared to state-of-the-art methods and under a latency constraint, our extended models can improve the accuracy by 1.96% on average, and our shrunk models can also obtain a 3.09% accuracy improvement on average, with almost no extra training overhead. The related codes and data will be available at https://github.com/ntuliuteam/Collate

</details>


### [187] [PGD-NO: A Neural Operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition for 3D Million-scale Physics Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08025)
*Weiheng Zhong,Jing Bi,Victor Oancea,Hadi Meidani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PGD-NO proposes a neural operator that decouples geometric encoding into a precomputation phase via iterative geometry decomposition, enabling linear memory scalability for learning on meshes exceeding 10 million nodes while maintaining competitive accuracy on industrial benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural PDE solvers suffer from high memory consumption and a single-node bottleneck where mesh resolution is limited by a single GPU's VRAM. The authors aim to overcome this constraint to enable large-scale, high-fidelity industrial simulations.

Method: PGD-NO uses an iterative geometry decomposition algorithm in a deterministic pre-computation phase to extract geometry tokens. This decouples feature extraction from solution querying, relocating geometric encoding overhead away from the main learning pipeline and achieving linear memory scalability.

Result: PGD-NO demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy across diverse industrial benchmarks and successfully handles meshes exceeding 10 million nodes — scales at which existing architectures typically encounter memory exhaustion.

Conclusion: By overcoming mesh-size constraints through precomputed geometry decomposition, PGD-NO provides a robust, efficient, and interpretable solution for next-generation large-scale industrial design applications.

Abstract: While neural PDE solvers have demonstrated significant potential for accelerating engineering simulations, existing architectures remain constrained by high memory consumption and the single node bottleneck, where the maximum processable mesh resolution is strictly limited by the VRAM of a single compute unit. To address these challenges, we propose PGD-NO, a neural operator with Precomputed Geometry Decomposition, that relocates the computational overhead of geometric encoding to a deterministic pre-computation phase. By utilizing an iterative geometry decomposition algorithm to extract geometry tokens, our model decouples feature extraction from solution querying. This architecture enables linear memory scalability, allowing high fidelity learning on meshes exceeding 10 million nodes, a scale where existing architectures typically encounter memory exhaustion. PGD-NO demonstrates competitive predictive accuracy across diverse industrial benchmarks and provides intrinsic interpretability through attention mechanisms. By effectively overcoming traditional mesh-size constraints, PGD-NO offers a robust and efficient solution for the next generation of large-scale, high-fidelity industrial design applications.

</details>


### [188] [Rethinking Small VLM Quantization: From Component-Wise Analysis to Hardware-Aware Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08029)
*Hyeju Shin,Chorwon Kim,Ryangsoo Kim,Hark Yoo,Jaein Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic evaluation of component-wise quantization in small VLMs (<3B params) on Jetson edge devices, validating five hypotheses about quantization sensitivity, encoder-kernel-hardware interactions, INT4 dequantization overhead, composite error additivity, and energy efficiency across different structural paradigms (MoE vs. dense).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite rapid adoption of sub-3B vision-language models for on-device deployment, there lacks a detailed, component-level understanding of how quantization affects each architectural module (vision encoder, projector, LLM backbone), hindering optimal deployment on edge hardware.

Method: A systematic evaluation framework testing five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on Jetson Orin NX and AGX platforms. The approach isolates three architectural components — vision encoder, modality projector, and LLM backbone — and measures latency, VRAM consumption, token generation speed, composite error propagation, and energy efficiency (intelligence-per-joule) under INT4/INT8 regimes, comparing MoE versus dense backbones.

Result: (1) MoE backbones tolerate INT4 quantization better than dense backbones, indicating structural paradigm dominates over scale in determining sensitivity. (2) SigLIP encoders exhibit disproportionate INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere GPUs due to kernel-hardware incompatibility, not an inherent encoder flaw. (3) INT4 LLM quantization reduces VRAM but slows token generation due to dequantization overhead. (4) Composite quantization errors are additive except along the modality-alignment path, where architecture matters. (5) Intelligence-per-joule varies significantly across platforms due to memory bandwidth differences.

Conclusion: Optimal on-device VLM quantization requires component-specific strategies: MoE backbones for aggressive INT4, careful encoder-kernel matching on target hardware, and awareness that INT4 VRAM savings come at a generation-speed cost. Deployment decisions should be guided by per-platform energy-efficiency profiling rather than uniform quantization recipes.

Abstract: The emergence of vision language models with fewer than 3 billion parameters has accelerated the implementation of on-device multimodal intelligence. However, a detailed understanding of component-wise quantization remains a bottleneck for optimal deployment. This paper presents a systematic evaluation framework for empirically validating five hypotheses across six quantization configurations on the Jetson Orin NX and AGX. By separating the vision encoder, projector, and large language model backbone yields the following results: (1) Quantization sensitivity is governed by the structural paradigm (MoE vs. dense) rather than scale alone, with MoE backbones mitigating INT4 noise where dense backbones degrade; (2) SigLIP encoders incur disproportionate INT8 latency on Jetson Ampere--a deployment-specific encoder-kernel-hardware interaction, not a SigLIP flaw; (3) Although INT4 quantization of LLMs greatly reduces VRAM consumption, it also causes slower token generation due to dequantization overhead; (4) Composite quantization errors are largely additive, except along the modality-alignment path, which is architecture-dependent; (5) The intelligence-per-joule profile varies significantly across platforms owing to memory bandwidth constraints.

</details>


### [189] [What to Keep, What to Forget: A Rate--Distortion View of Memory Compaction in LLMs and Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08032)
*Ashwin Gerard Colaco,Nada Lahjouji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper unifies four disparate research areas (KV cache eviction, prompt pruning, architectural state bounding, and agent memory consolidation) under a single rate-distortion framework. It proposes a compaction objective, a seven-axis taxonomy, and reveals that attention magnitude/recency is the universal retention signal — which fails identically across layers by prematurely discarding query-relevant information. The authors identify that repeated compaction in agent settings is almost never measured and propose a benchmark and design principles to address this gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory across the LLM stack — KV caches, prompts, recurrent states, and agent histories — is costly and growing. Four separate communities independently developed compaction methods, but no unified framework exists to compare, transfer, or systematically improve these techniques. The authors aim to bridge these silos by showing they all solve the same fundamental rate-distortion problem.

Method: The authors formalize a single compaction objective based on rate-distortion theory: maximize downstream task utility subject to a resource budget by deciding what context-derived information to retain, at what fidelity. They derive a layer-agnostic lower bound and construct a seven-axis taxonomy (spanning what, when, where, how, how much, with what budget, and measured how) to classify methods uniformly across the stack. They also run a small reference experiment and propose a benchmark design.

Result: Two cross-cutting patterns emerge: (1) at every layer, attention magnitude or recency serves as the retention signal, and it fails universally by discarding information before the query is known, with no undo mechanism; (2) while single-turn long-context compression is carefully measured, the repeated, layered compaction that agents actually perform is almost never evaluated holistically — no benchmark holds a single budget axis across all layers simultaneously.

Conclusion: The rate-distortion lens enables mechanism transfer between previously disconnected layers (e.g., from KV cache management to agent long-term memory). The authors advocate for compaction-aware design principles, highlight open problems around premature discarding and multi-layer budgeting, and call for benchmarks that measure repeated compaction across the full stack under a unified resource constraint.

Abstract: Large language models, and the agents built on them, spend an ever-growing share of their compute and memory on remembering: caching attention keys and values, carrying long prompts, maintaining recurrent state, and storing what happened in previous turns and sessions. Because none of this memory is free, four largely separate research communities have each learned to compact it. They evict or quantize the KV cache, prune or distill prompts, bound architectural state, and consolidate agent memory. We argue that these are instances of one problem: a rate--distortion decision about what context-derived information to retain versus discard, at what fidelity, under a resource budget, so as to preserve downstream task utility. We make this lens precise with a single compaction objective and a layer-agnostic lower bound, use it to build a seven-axis taxonomy that classifies methods from across the stack uniformly, and use it to transfer mechanisms between layers that have never been connected, from serving-stack KV management to agent long-term memory. Two patterns hold across the survey. At every layer the signal that decides what to keep is attention magnitude or recency, and it fails in the same way everywhere, by discarding, before the query is known and with no way to undo it, information the query later needs. And while compression is measured carefully on single-turn long context, the repeated compaction that agents actually perform is almost never measured, and no benchmark holds one budget axis across all the layers at once. We turn both observations into a benchmark proposal, a small reference experiment, and a set of compaction-aware design principles, and we map the open problems.

</details>


### [190] [An exact information theory of generalization phase transitions in Bayesian diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08041)
*Henry Hunt,Mason Kamb,Surya Ganguli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BIRD models explain how diffusion models avoid memorization by restricting information per pixel. The authors identify a phase boundary where memorization occurs when mutual information between noisy observations and training data exceeds log(N), and show diffusion models operate near this boundary by progressively restricting information during generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the fundamental mystery of how diffusion models learn complex high-dimensional distributions from finite data without simply memorizing training samples, a phenomenon that appears to circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

Method: The authors introduce Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models, an analytically tractable framework where each pixel receives restricted information about noisy data. The reverse process uses Bayesian posterior inference to identify which training sample likely produced the current restricted observation. They analyze the information-theoretic conditions governing memorization vs. generalization.

Result: Spatially local BIRD models closely match trained diffusion models (UNets, DiTs) early in training. A phase boundary is identified: memorization occurs when I(restricted noisy observations; training data) > log(N), and generalization otherwise. Experiments confirm this predicted transition, and generation proceeds near the memorization edge, with information restriction increasing over time.

Conclusion: Information restriction plays a fundamental role in enabling diffusion models to circumvent the curse of dimensionality. By operating near the memorization-generalization phase boundary and progressively restricting information, these models balance fidelity and novelty.

Abstract: How diffusion models circumvent the curse of dimensionality to learn complex distributions over high dimensional spaces from a finite training set, instead of memorizing it, remains a fundamental mystery. To address this, we introduce analytically tractable Bayesian information restricted diffusion (BIRD) models, in which each pixel observes restricted information about noisy data. A BIRD model time-reverses diffusion by inferring which past training sample produced its current restricted observation using the Bayesian posterior. This model class generalizes existing analytical diffusion models that use spatially local information restriction. We show that spatially local BIRD models closely approximate trained diffusion models \textit{early in training}, across different architectures such as UNets and DiTs. Under minimal assumptions on the data distribution, we identify an information-theoretic phase boundary between memorization and generalization in the joint space of amount of training data, time in the reverse generative process, and amount of information restriction: a BIRD model memorizes when the mutual information between its restricted noisy observations and the training data exceeds the log number of training points, and it generalizes otherwise. Experiments across a range of datasets confirm our theoretically predicted location for the transition. We find that generation proceeds near the edge of memorization: both spatially local BIRD models and early-training diffusion models track the memorization-generalization phase boundary by increasingly restricting information over time. Overall, our results reveal a fundamental role for information restriction in generative AI to circumvent the curse of dimensionality.

</details>


### [191] [Who Analyses the Analyser? Self-Validating LLM Hazard Analysis with Constitutional Meta-STPA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08054)
*Samuel Tetteh,Udip Shrestha,Joshua R. Waite,Cody Fleming*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents Constitutional Meta-STPA, a framework that applies STPA reflexively to LLM-assisted safety analysis tools themselves, deriving a governance constitution of 29 principles from the tool's own design rather than asserting them externally, and demonstrates that frontier LLMs can self-derive these principles while weaker models cannot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-assisted safety analysis tools are increasingly used in rigorous processes like STPA, yet no one analyzes the safety of these tools themselves. They can hallucinate standards, emit unverifiable constraints, and lack audit trails. The core question—"who analyses the analyser?"—remains unaddressed in the literature.

Method: The authors propose Constitutional Meta-STPA, a closed-loop system where a meta-STPA is performed on the class of AI-assisted safety tools. The loss→hazard→UCA→constraint chain is used to derive (not assert) a governance constitution comprising 21 Tool Principles and 8 Meta-Safety Principles, each with code enforcement points. A constitution-marginal coverage operator over principle set P (|P|=29) is formalized with a soundness lemma isolating coverage from model and scanner.

Result: A frontier ensemble (claude-opus-4.8 + claude-sonnet-4) recovers 18/21 canonical and all 8/8 governance principles from the tool's own design. A weaker model pair recovers only 12/21 and 3/8, showing the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited. The same 8/8 governance principles re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool.

Conclusion: The analyser must itself be analysable. Constitutional Meta-STPA demonstrates that LLM-assisted safety tools can reflexively derive their own governance constraints through meta-STPA, with frontier models capable of near-complete principle recovery. This closes the blind spot in the literature and establishes that self-derivation is feasible but model-dependent.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trusted to draft the artifacts of safety analysis such as, losses, hazards, Unsafe Control Actions (UCAs), and safety constraints, inside rigorous processes such as Systems-Theoretic Process Analysis (STPA). Yet a blind spot runs through this fast-growing literature: every system gets analysed except the LLM-assisted tool doing the analysing, which is itself a safety-relevant system that can hallucinate standards, emit unverifiable constraints, and leave no audit trail from prompt to artifact. We take seriously the question the field has skipped -- {who analyses the analyser?} and answer it by turning STPA on the tool itself. We present \{Constitutional Meta-STPA}, an LLM-assisted STPA tool built around a closed loop: the tool runs a {meta-STPA} of the class of AI-assisted safety tools and {derives} rather than asserts, its governance constitution from the resulting loss$\to$hazard$\to$UCA$\to$constraint chain, yielding a published constitution of $21$ Tool Principles and $8$ Meta-Safety Principles, each bound to a code enforcement point. We formalise the measured object as a constitution-marginal coverage operator over a principle set $P$ ($|P|{=}29$) with a soundness lemma that isolates coverage from model and scanner, and report four findings. {(i)~Self-derivation:} a frontier ensemble ({claude-opus-4.8}${+}${claude-sonnet-4}) recovers $18/21$ canonical and all $8/8$ governance principles from the tool's own design, while a weaker pair recovers $12/21$ and $3/8$, so the meta layer is model-limited, not constitution-limited, and the same $8/8$ re-emerge from a second, independently authored tool.

</details>


### [192] [Reinforcing the Generation Order of Multimodal Masked Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08056)
*Yidong Ouyang,Zhe Wang,Sourav Bhabesh,Dmitriy Bespalov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates optimizing token generation order in Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) for text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding. Since model logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in these domains, the authors introduce a learnable control module trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). The approach achieves 4.08% relative improvement on GenEval (text-to-image alignment) and 4.85% on VLMEvalKit (multimodal understanding).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work showed that adaptive token generation ordering benefits structured reasoning tasks (e.g., Sudoku, code synthesis) in DLMs. However, the authors identify that for text-to-image generation and multimodal understanding, raw model logits fail to reliably indicate optimal generation sequences — unlike in structured language problems. This gap motivates designing a learned mechanism to determine generation order in these less-structured, multimodal contexts.

Method: The authors propose a learnable control module that determines the token generation ordering in DLMs. This module is trained using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), a reinforcement learning approach where the policy is optimized relative to group-based comparisons. The control block augments the standard DLM decoding process, selecting which tokens to generate at each step to improve alignment and comprehension.

Result: The proposed method yields a 4.08% relative improvement on GenEval, a benchmark for object-focused text-to-image alignment, particularly enhancing fine-grained spatial relationship capture. On VLMEvalKit, a multimodal understanding benchmark, the approach achieves 4.85% relative improvement, demonstrating gains in multimodal reasoning and comprehension tasks.

Conclusion: Learning an optimal generation order via a GRPO-trained control module is an effective strategy for improving DLM performance in text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding. The method significantly enhances spatial reasoning in generated images and boosts multimodal comprehension, suggesting broad applicability of adaptive generation ordering beyond purely textual domains.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) have recently achieved substantial progress in natural language generation tasks. Recent research demonstrates that adaptive token generation ordering can significantly improve performance in mathematical reasoning and code synthesis applications. In this work, we investigate the optimization of generation order for both text-to-image synthesis and multimodal understanding. We first establish that, unlike structured problems in language generation such as Sudoku puzzles, model logits alone are insufficient for determining optimal generation sequences in text-to-image generation and multimodal understanding. To address this challenge, we introduce a learnable control module trained via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to determine the generation order. Our results demonstrate that learning this control block substantially improves both text-to-image alignment and multimodal understanding in DLMs. In particular, it enhances the model's ability to capture fine-grained spatial relationships in generated images while also strengthening performance on multimodal reasoning and comprehension tasks. We evaluate our framework on GenEval, an object-focused benchmark for text-to-image alignment, where it achieves 4.08% relative improvements. In addition, experiments on VLMEvalKit confirm 4.85% relative improvements in multimodal understanding, highlighting the broad effectiveness of our approach.

</details>


### [193] [Towards Efficient Large Language Model Serving: A Survey on System-Aware KV Cache Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08057)
*Jiantong Jiang,Peiyu Yang,Rui Zhang,Feng Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A survey that systematizes system-aware KV cache infrastructure (sKis) for LLM serving, organizing existing work into temporal, spatial, and structural dimensions, and analyzing cross-behavior co-design opportunities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM serving systems remain memory-intensive and costly despite model advancements. The KV cache is critical for low-latency, high-throughput inference, yet existing research is fragmented. A systematic perspective on KV cache infrastructure is needed to unify understanding and guide future innovation.

Method: The authors propose the sKis (system-aware KV infrastructure for serving) framework, which revisits recent work through a system behavior lens across three dimensions: (1) execution and scheduling (temporal), (2) placement and migration (spatial), and (3) representation and retention (structural). They further analyze cross-behavior co-design affinity and behavior-objective links.

Result: The survey provides a structured taxonomy of KV cache optimization techniques, revealing cross-dimensional co-design affinities and mapping system behaviors to serving objectives (latency, throughput, memory efficiency). It identifies gaps and future opportunities in the rapidly evolving KV cache design space.

Conclusion: The sKis framework offers a foundational systematization for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in LLM serving infrastructure, highlighting the importance of holistic, cross-dimensional co-design approaches for next-generation serving systems.

Abstract: Despite the rapid advancements of large language models (LLMs), LLM serving systems remain memory-intensive and costly. The key-value (KV) cache, which stores KV tensors during autoregressive decoding, is crucial for enabling low-latency, high-throughput LLM inference serving. In this survey, we focus on system-aware KV infrastructure for serving LLMs (abbreviated as sKis). We revisit recent work from a system behavior perspective, organizing existing efforts into three dimensions: execution and scheduling (temporal), placement and migration (spatial), and representation and retention (structural). Furthermore, we analyze cross-behavior co-design affinity and behavior-objective links, highlighting future opportunities. Our work systematizes a rapidly evolving area, providing a foundation for understanding and innovating KV cache designs in modern LLM serving infrastructure.

</details>


### [194] [When Thinking Hurts: Epistemic Signals in the Reasoning Chains of Visual Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08059)
*Mayank Singal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First empirical characterization of answer entropy behavior across three families of thinking-mode VLMs reveals qualitatively distinct collapse patterns under adversarial POPE samples. Thinking chain entropy consistently outperforms answer entropy for uncertainty quantification, and a practical abstention gate raises accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage with zero additional inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional uncertainty quantification for VLMs targets the answer token distribution, but thinking-mode models generate internal reasoning chains before answering. The authors hypothesize that entropy computed over these chain tokens may provide a more reliable uncertainty signal than answer entropy, especially under distribution shift or adversarial conditions. No prior work systematically characterizes answer entropy behavior or compares chain vs. answer entropy across diverse thinking-mode VLM architectures.

Method: Four thinking-mode VLMs (Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking, GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking, InternVL3-8B, and a second Qwen variant) are evaluated on identical POPE adversarial samples. The study measures both answer entropy (ans H) and thinking chain entropy, computing AUROC for each. Three behavioral families are qualitatively identified: complete collapse, no collapse, and selective thinking. A 300-sample VQAv2 pilot and HallusionBench evaluation extend the analysis. A practical abstention gate is constructed using chain entropy thresholds, measuring accuracy-coverage trade-offs.

Result: Three distinct entropy collapse patterns emerge: Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking collapses (ans H AUROC=0.492), GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking does not (0.716), and InternVL3-8B thinks selectively on only 50% of samples (ans H=0.675 full / 0.602 thinking-only). Chain entropy outperforms answer entropy across all models when chains are present (0.647, 0.759, 0.608 vs. 0.492, 0.716, 0.602). On VQAv2, chain entropy (0.680) beats answer entropy (0.595), with the gap largest for free-form answers (0.733 vs. 0.467). HallusionBench shows moderate signal (~0.64) for Qwen models. Structured abstention affects 12-22% of queries, asymmetrically toward absent-object queries. The abstention gate achieves 93.8% accuracy at 62.7% coverage.

Conclusion: Thinking chain entropy is a more reliable uncertainty signal than answer entropy whenever chains are generated, but model-specific behaviors (collapse, resistance, selective thinking) necessitate architecture-aware UQ strategies. Chain-based abstention provides a practical, cost-free mechanism for improving VLM reliability, particularly for free-form answer settings. Incomplete pre-commitment on difficult reasoning tasks limits but does not eliminate the utility of chain entropy signals.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification for visual language models (VLMs) conventionally targets the answer token distribution. We provide the first three-family empirical characterisation of answer entropy behaviour in thinking-mode VLMs. Running four models on identical POPE adversarial samples, we find three qualitatively distinct patterns: Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking shows complete collapse (ans H AUROC = 0.492); GLM-4.1V-9B-Thinking shows no collapse (0.716); and InternVL3-8B shows selective thinking (chains on only 50% of samples, ans H = 0.675 full / 0.602 thinking-only). Across all three thinking-mode models, thinking chain entropy outperforms answer entropy on the subset where chains are generated (0.647, 0.759, 0.608 vs. 0.492, 0.716, 0.602 respectively), suggesting chain signals are the more reliable predictor whenever chains are present. This holds strongly for Qwen and GLM, but with only marginal and statistically unreliable advantage for InternVL3 (n_FP = 17). A 300-sample VQAv2 pilot confirms chain entropy (0.680) outperforms answer entropy (0.595) on VQAv2 questions, with the gap largest for free-form answers (0.733 vs. 0.467). On harder reasoning tasks (HallusionBench) both Qwen models show moderate signal (approx. 0.64), consistent with incomplete pre-commitment on difficult questions. We additionally document structured abstention affecting 12-22% of queries with asymmetry toward absent-object queries, and a practical abstention gate raising accuracy from 71.0% to 93.8% at 62.7% coverage with no additional inference cost.

</details>


### [195] [Cross-Modal Generative Framework for Signal Translation from Fetal-Maternal Electrocardiograms to Fetal Doppler Waveforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08073)
*Tongli Su,Alireza Rafiei,Marly van Assen,Reza Sameni,Gari D. Clifford,Faezeh Marzbanrad,Nasim Katebi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cross-modal generative model using dilated convolutions and cross-modal attention synthesizes fetal Doppler ultrasound envelopes from dual-lead ECG, achieving 51% lower PSD MSE than baseline and quantifying maternal-fetal coupling contributions via a 39% error reduction from attention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fetal ECG (electrical) and Doppler ultrasound (mechanical hemodynamics) provide complementary cardiovascular information. Understanding which Doppler components are recoverable from ECG — and which are not — can quantify purely mechanical contributions, informing clinical decisions. Clinical evidence of maternal-fetal cardiac coupling further motivates incorporating maternal ECG to improve fetal hemodynamic modeling.

Method: A cross-modal generative framework that combines dilated convolutions with (1) cross-modal attention to selectively incorporate maternal ECG and (2) self-attention to capture long-range temporal dependencies. Trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies.

Result: PSD MSE of 49.9 ± 15.8 dB² (51% lower than two-channel baseline); heart-rate error of 4.71 ± 0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline). Cross-modal attention alone yields a 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation, quantifying maternal-fetal coupling contribution.

Conclusion: The framework advances computational modeling of the maternal-fetal cardiovascular system by enabling Doppler envelope synthesis from dual-lead ECG. Analysis of recoverable vs. residual Doppler components quantifies purely mechanical contributions not recoverable from electrical recordings, facilitating more comprehensive fetal assessment.

Abstract: Fetal electrocardiogram (fECG) and Doppler ultrasound provide complementary views of fetal cardiovascular function: fECG captures electrical activity while Doppler reflects mechanical hemodynamics shaped by factors such as placental resistance and vascular compliance. Understanding the recoverable and unrecoverable Doppler components through reconstruction from fECG offers insight into the relative contributions of electrical versus mechanical factors in fetal circulation, thereby informing clinical decisions. In addition, clinical evidence of maternal-fetal cardiac coupling suggests that maternal cardiovascular dynamics may also inform fetal hemodynamics. To computationally model these relationships, we propose a cross-modal generative framework combining dilated convolutions with cross-modal attention to selectively incorporate maternal ECG and self-attention to capture long-range temporal dependencies. Trained on 885 synchronized fetal/maternal ECG and Doppler envelope segments from 39 pregnancies, our model synthesizes Doppler envelopes with power spectral density mean squared error (PSD MSE) of 49.9 +/- 15.8 dB^2 (51% lower than two-channel baseline) and heart-rate error of 4.71 +/- 0.77 bpm (1.5% better than baseline; negligible relative to the 110-160 bpm physiological range). Cross-modal attention yields a 39% PSD MSE reduction over naive dual-channel concatenation, quantifying the contribution of maternal-fetal coupling. Our proposed framework advances computational modeling of the maternal-fetal cardiovascular system by enabling the synthesis of Doppler envelopes from dual-lead ECG. By analysis of both recoverable and residual Doppler components, this approach enables quantification of the purely mechanical contributions to Doppler waveforms -- those not recoverable from electrical recordings -- ultimately facilitating a more comprehensive fetal assessment.

</details>


### [196] [Modular Pretraining Enables Access Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08077)
*Ethan Roland,Murat Cubuktepe,Erick Martinez,Stijn Servaes,Keenan Pepper,Mike Vaiana,Diogo Schwerz de Lucena,Judd Rosenblatt,Addie Foote,Cem Anil,Alex Cloud*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRAM introduces auxiliary modules into a pretrained model that can be ablated at inference to selectively remove dangerous capabilities, approximating data-filtered models at a fraction of the cost, with scaling analysis showing this approach tracks data filtering up to 5B parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma where the same capabilities can be used for both beneficial and harmful purposes. Current solutions like training separate models per capability profile are prohibitively expensive. A scalable access control mechanism is needed that allows serving models with different capabilities to different users without the cost of multiple full trainings.

Method: GRAM (Gradient-Routed Auxiliary Modules) is a pretraining method that inserts auxiliary modules into a neural network and selectively updates them during training to induce capability specialization. At inference, ablating a specific module removes its associated capability from the network. The method supports multiple capability profiles without additional training cost, as the number of supported profiles does not affect training expense.

Result: Experiments on synthetic stories and realistic dual-use domains (virology, cybersecurity, nuclear physics, specialized code) show GRAM disables targeted capabilities while preserving general performance. It resists capability recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. A Chinchilla-optimal scaling analysis from 50M to 5B parameters demonstrates that GRAM closely tracks data filtering, with the gap between filtered and full-data models widening on removed capabilities but staying small on retained ones. GRAM achieves a 5x cost reduction over data filtering in a 5-profile setting.

Conclusion: GRAM offers a practical and scalable solution to the AI dual-use dilemma by enabling fine-grained capability access control through module ablation, matching the effectiveness of data filtering while dramatically reducing computational cost, and scaling favorably to larger models.

Abstract: AI developers face a dual-use dilemma. An AI capability that helps one user cure a disease can help another synthesize one. This dilemma could be resolved with access control, limiting dual-use AI capabilities to trusted deployments with a legitimate need. A gold standard for access control would be to serve separate models with different capabilities to different users. However, training and deploying multiple models is prohibitively expensive. To address this challenge, we propose gradient-routed auxiliary modules (GRAM), a pre-training method that adds modules to a neural network and selectively updates them to induce specialization. Ablating a module at inference time removes its capability from the network, approximating a model trained on filtered data. We evaluate GRAM on synthetic stories and realistic dual-use data spanning virology, cybersecurity, nuclear physics, and specialized code. These experiments show that GRAM disables targeted capabilities while preserving the rest, and resists their recovery under finetuning better than post-hoc unlearning. Most importantly, a Chinchilla-optimal scaling analysis from 50M to 5B parameters shows that the gap between data-filtered and full-data models widens with scale on removed capabilities but stays small on retained ones, and that GRAM closely tracks data filtering. GRAM's training cost is independent of the number of supported capability profiles, yielding a 5x reduction over data filtering in our 5-profile setting.

</details>


### [197] [Deep Learning Method for Stationary Distribution of Reflected Brownian Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08091)
*Jim Dai,Zhanhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a deep learning framework to learn the Laplace transform of high-dimensional reflected Brownian motion (RBM) using the basic adjoint relationship (BAR), overcoming the lack of closed-form solutions. It achieves near-perfect prediction of tail probabilities via careful design of loss, sampling, and architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed-form solutions for the stationary distribution of high-dimensional RBM are known only in few special cases, and computing tail probabilities is even more intractable despite their practical importance in analyzing stochastic systems like queueing networks.

Method: A deep learning approach that learns the Laplace transform (moment generating function) of high-dimensional RBMs, built on the basic adjoint relationship (BAR). Key components include a carefully designed loss function derived from BAR, a tailored training data sampling procedure, and a specific neural network architecture suited to the problem structure.

Result: The method was evaluated on RBM instances with known ground-truth tail probabilities and demonstrated near-perfect prediction accuracy in high-dimensional settings, significantly outperforming prior tractable approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed framework shows strong potential as a general-purpose tool for analyzing stochastic systems beyond analytically tractable regimes, with code publicly available.

Abstract: The stationary distribution of reflected Brownian motion (RBM) plays an important role in the analysis of high-dimensional stochastic systems, yet closed-form solutions are known only for a few special cases. Computing important performance metrics, such as tail probabilities, is even more intractable, despite their practical relevance. In this paper, we develop a deep learning approach that accurately and efficiently learns the Laplace transform of high-dimensional RBMs based on the basic adjoint relationship (BAR). Our framework combines a careful design of the loss function, training data sampling procedure, and neural network architecture. We evaluate the proposed method on RBM instances with known ground-truth tail probabilities and demonstrate near-perfect prediction in high-dimensional settings, highlighting its potential as a general tool for analyzing stochastic systems beyond analytically tractable regimes. Our code can be found at https://github.com/zhangz73/NN4MGF.

</details>


### [198] [Stochastic Order Learning: An Approach to Rank Estimation Using Noisy Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08103)
*Chaewon Lee,Seon-Ho Lee,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SOL reformulates noisy ordinal label rank estimation as a stochastic ordering problem, using a discriminative loss for instance-centroid structure and a stochastic order loss for probabilistic ranking relations, achieving robust performance under diverse label noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional rank estimation assumes deterministic ordinal labels, but real-world annotations often contain structured uncertainty (e.g., annotator disagreement, boundary ambiguity) rather than simple random corruption. Existing methods fail to model this inherent multi-plausibility in ordinal assignments.

Method: The authors propose Stochastic Order Learning (SOL), which treats each instance as associated with multiple plausible ranks. The framework learns an embedding space via two complementary losses: (1) a discriminative loss that organizes instance--centroid interactions in the latent space, and (2) a stochastic order loss that enforces probabilistic ordering constraints between instances, capturing the stochastic nature of ordinal relationships.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse datasets show that SOL achieves reliable rank estimation under various types and levels of label noise, outperforming methods that assume deterministic ordinal labels.

Conclusion: Modeling ordinal label noise as stochastic ordering rather than label corruption provides a more principled framework for robust rank estimation, and the dual-objective SOL approach effectively operationalizes this insight.

Abstract: Rank estimation under label noise poses a fundamental challenge, as ordinal annotations often exhibit structured uncertainty rather than simple label corruption. In this paper, we reformulate rank estimation with noisy ordinal labels as a stochastic ordering problem, in which each instance is inherently associated with multiple plausible ranks instead of a single deterministic label. Based on this view, we propose stochastic order learning (SOL), a learning framework that captures ordinal label uncertainty and learns an embedding space through two complementary objectives: a discriminative loss that structures instance--centroid interactions and a stochastic order loss that enforces probabilistic ordering relations between instances. Extensive experiments across diverse datasets demonstrate that SOL enables reliable rank estimation under various types and levels of label noise. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/SOL.

</details>


### [199] [Vanilla SGD with Momentum Survives Heavy-Tailed Noise: Convergence Analysis without Gradient Clipping or Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08104)
*Ryusei Yamada,Naoki Sato,Hideaki Iiduka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides the first comprehensive convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum under heavy-tailed noise for strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives, demonstrating that vanilla methods achieve inferior rates compared to clipped/normalized variants, thus revealing inherent limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While specialized modifications like gradient clipping address heavy-tailed noise in SGD, a fundamental gap exists: no rigorous analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum under heavy-tailed noise. The authors aim to fill this gap and determine whether vanilla methods can match the performance of modified variants.

Method: The authors refine existing convergence results for vanilla SGD (without momentum) and derive novel convergence bounds for vanilla SGD with momentum under heavy-tailed noise across three objective classes: strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex functions. No gradient control mechanisms (clipping, normalization) are employed.

Result: The derived convergence rates for vanilla SGD with momentum are strictly inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized SGD variants across all three function classes, establishing an inherent performance gap that cannot be closed without gradient control.

Conclusion: Vanilla SGD with momentum suffers from fundamental limitations under heavy-tailed noise; gradient clipping or normalization are not merely practical heuristics but theoretically necessary for achieving optimal convergence rates. Synthetic experiments corroborate the theoretical findings.

Abstract: Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) is a cornerstone of modern optimization. While its performance under heavy-tailed noise is often addressed through specialized modifications such as gradient clipping or normalization, we investigate a more fundamental question: how does vanilla SGD, particularly with momentum, perform in the presence of heavy-tailed noise? In this paper, we refine existing convergence results for vanilla SGD and, more importantly, provide the first comprehensive convergence analysis of vanilla SGD with momentum for strongly convex, convex, and nonconvex objectives, without employing any gradient control mechanisms. Our results demonstrate that the obtained convergence rates are inferior to the optimal rates achieved by clipped or normalized variants of SGD, thereby revealing inherent limitations of vanilla methods under heavy-tailed noise. The theoretical findings are supported by experiments on synthetic functions.

</details>


### [200] [Contrastive Order Learning: A General Framework for Ordinal Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08109)
*Chaewon Lee,BeomJun Shim,Kwang Pyo Choi,Chang-Su Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ConOrd proposes a contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression that uses soft affinity/disparity weights based on rank differences to capture fine-grained ordinal structure across all sample pairs, achieving state-of-the-art results on age estimation and blind image/video quality assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard contrastive learning ignores label ordering, while order learning relies on local margin-based comparisons and misses global ordinal structure. ConOrd aims to unify the strengths of both paradigms.

Method: A contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights derived from rank differences, applied to all sample pairs in a batch to model fine-grained ordinal relationships globally.

Result: State-of-the-art performance across facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment benchmarks, demonstrating strong generalization.

Conclusion: ConOrd effectively bridges contrastive and order learning, providing a simple yet powerful framework that consistently outperforms existing methods on diverse ordinal regression tasks.

Abstract: We propose contrastive order learning (ConOrd), a contrastive learning framework for ordinal regression that integrates the strengths of contrastive learning and order learning. While contrastive learning effectively leverages all samples in a batch, it typically ignores the inherent ordering among rank labels. Conversely, order learning explicitly models label ordinality but often relies on local, margin-based comparisons, limiting its ability to capture global ordinal structure. ConOrd addresses these limitations by introducing a contrastive order loss with soft affinity and disparity weights based on rank differences, enabling fine-grained modeling of ordinal relationships across all sample pairs within a batch. Extensive experiments on a range of ordinal regression tasks, including facial age estimation, blind image quality assessment, and blind video quality assessment, demonstrate that ConOrd consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance and generalizes well across diverse ordinal regression scenarios. The source code is available at https://github.com/cwlee00/ConOrd.

</details>


### [201] [Workload-Preserving Differentially Private Synthetic Data for Causal Inference via Maximum-Entropy Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08122)
*Amir Asiaee,Kaveh Aryan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes "causal workloads" — differentially private query sets designed around the orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators — to generate DP synthetic data that preserves treatment-arm balance and outcome moments needed for valid causal inference (e.g., ATE estimation). It introduces Causal-AIM for adaptive workload selection and a noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) procedure for confidence intervals, demonstrating that causal workloads excel at strict privacy budgets while generic workloads retain point-RMSE advantages under relaxed privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generic DP synthetic data methods preserve distributional fidelity but fail to maintain the treatment-arm balance and outcome moments critical for causal estimands like ATE. There is a gap between DP synthetic data generation and valid causal inference, since downstream causal estimators require preservation of specific orthogonal moments that generic marginal queries do not guarantee.

Method: The authors propose (1) causal workloads: DP query sets targeting the orthogonal moments needed by doubly robust estimators; (2) a decomposition of ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration components; (3) Causal-AIM, an adaptive workload selection mechanism; and (4) NA+MI, a noise-aware multiple-imputation procedure for constructing valid confidence intervals from DP synthetic data. The workload is released once and supports ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy cost.

Result: Causal workloads prove most useful at strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty quantification. Generic workloads often retain an advantage in point RMSE as privacy budgets relax. The key tradeoff: distributional fidelity aids point accuracy, but valid causal inference demands preserving causal moments and properly propagating DP noise rather than naively treating synthetic rows as real data.

Conclusion: Valid causal inference from DP synthetic data requires a deliberate shift from generic distributional fidelity to targeted preservation of causal moments.
