Causal workloads, combined with noise-aware inference procedures, offer a principled solution that is especially valuable under stringent privacy constraints, revealing a fundamental tradeoff between point accuracy and inferential validity.

Abstract: Workload-based differentially private (DP) synthetic data methods privately measure aggregate queries and post-process the noisy answers into synthetic records. Generic workloads can achieve strong distributional fidelity, but causal estimands such as the average treatment effect (ATE) depend on treatment-arm balance and outcome moments that generic marginals need not preserve. We propose causal workloads: DP query sets designed around the orthogonal moments used by doubly robust causal estimators. The released workload can be used directly by stable moment-map estimators or reconstructed by maximum-entropy calibration into reusable synthetic data; our theory decomposes ATE error into sampling, privacy, workload-approximation, Monte Carlo, and calibration terms. We also introduce Causal-AIM, an adaptive workload selector, and a noise-aware multiple-imputation (NA+MI) procedure for confidence intervals from DP synthetic data. Because the workload is released once, the same DP synthetic table can support ATE, ATT, and subgroup analyses without additional privacy spending. Empirically, causal workloads are most useful at strict privacy budgets and for calibrated uncertainty, while generic workloads often retain an advantage for point RMSE as privacy relaxes. The broader lesson is a tradeoff: distributional fidelity can help point accuracy, but valid causal inference requires preserving causal moments and propagating DP noise rather than treating synthetic rows as real.

</details>


### [202] [DeepPySR -- A Symbolic Regression Framework with Dynamic Pruning, Pareto Selection, and Hierarchical Composition for Real-World Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08150)
*Fuling Chen,Kevin Vinsen,Phillip Melton,Rae-Chi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeepPySR extends symbolic regression with dynamic variable pruning, exponential Pareto selection, and multi-layer hierarchical composition, outperforming PySR and baselines on physics and biomedical/social-science benchmarks while producing interpretable formulas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symbolic regression produces glass-box interpretable models critical for clinical medicine and social science, but existing methods struggle with high-dimensional inputs, principled Pareto-front formula selection, and data irregularities like multicollinearity and class imbalance.

Method: DeepPySR introduces three key innovations: (1) a dynamic variable-pruning schedule that iteratively removes irrelevant features during search; (2) an exponential Pareto selection criterion that eliminates accuracy-complexity trade-off ambiguity; and (3) a multi-layer architecture enabling hierarchical symbolic composition of formulas.

Result: On 4 Feynman physics and 7 biomedical/social-science datasets, DeepPySR surpasses PySR and baselines: body fat (R²: 0.794 vs. 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs. 0.787), student performance (R²: 0.964 vs. 0.948), and Raine BMI (R²: 0.525 vs. 0.370), yielding formulas consistent with domain risk factors.

Conclusion: DeepPySR effectively overcomes key symbolic regression bottlenecks, delivering superior predictive performance and interpretable, domain-aligned analytical expressions across diverse scientific domains.

Abstract: Symbolic regression (SR) discovers analytical equations from data, yielding glass-box models with directly interpretable formulas, unlike black-box methods that rely on unstable post-hoc tools such as SHAP or LIME. This transparency is crucial in clinical medicine and social science, but SR faces three challenges: high-dimensional inputs, principled selection of Pareto-front formulae, and data irregularities such as multicollinearity and class imbalance. We introduce DeepPySR, which addresses these issues with a dynamic variable-pruning schedule to remove irrelevant features during search, an exponential Pareto selection criterion that eliminates trade-offs between accuracy and complexity, and a multi-layer architecture for hierarchical symbolic composition. On four Feynman physics benchmarks and seven biomedical and social-science datasets, DeepPySR outperforms PySR and baselines on body fat (R$^2$: 0.794 vs.\ 0.702), heart disease (F1: 0.898 vs.\ 0.787), student performance (R$^2$: 0.964 vs.\ 0.948), and Raine BMI (R$^2$: 0.525 vs.\ 0.370), producing interpretable formulas aligned with domain risk factors.

</details>


### [203] [Understanding Layer Patching in Model Size Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08170)
*Sara Kangaslahti,Jonathan Geuter,Nihal V. Nayak,Marco Fumero,Francesco Locatello,David Alvarez-Melis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically studies how to select which student layers to replace with teacher layers when interpolating model sizes via patching, framing it as a shortest-path optimization problem and proposing KL divergence-based greedy algorithm (KLPatch) that often outperforms naive sequential strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on boomerang distillation demonstrated that patching teacher layers into a distilled student can create intermediate-sized models, but left open the critical question of *which* student layers should be replaced. No systematic study existed on how layer selection affects interpolation quality.

Method: The authors cast optimal layer subset selection for each target size as a combinatorial optimization problem and prove it reduces to a shortest-path problem in a directed acyclic graph (DAG). They study sequential heuristics (first-to-last, last-to-first patching) and introduce KLPatch, a greedy algorithm that iteratively selects the student layer whose replacement maximizes KL divergence reduction with respect to the teacher's output distribution.

Result: Patching strategy strongly affects interpolation behavior, with effects varying substantially across model families (e.g., Llama vs. Pythia). Simple sequential strategies like last-to-first patching perform surprisingly well, but KLPatch often improves upon them and approximately solves the optimization problem, yielding near-optimal interpolated models.

Conclusion: Layer patching is a powerful and principled mechanism for model size interpolation. The shortest-path formulation provides theoretical grounding, while KLPatch offers a practical, effective algorithm. Together, these contributions deliver both understanding and actionable guidance for constructing interpolated models without retraining.

Abstract: Zero-shot model size interpolation aims to create new models of intermediate target sizes by combining existing models without additional training. Recent work on boomerang distillation [Kangaslahti et al., 2026] shows that a student language model distilled from a larger teacher can be expanded by iteratively patching its layers, replacing student layers with contiguous blocks of teacher layers to obtain models whose size and performance interpolate between the student and the teacher. In this work, we provide the first systematic study of student-layer selection for model size interpolation. We cast finding the optimal layer subset for each model size as an optimization problem and prove it can be viewed as a shortest-path problem in a certain acyclic graph. In experiments, we show that patching strongly shapes interpolation behavior, with effects that vary substantially across model families. We find that simple sequential strategies--patching either from the first layer to the last or from the last to the first--often achieve surprisingly strong performance in practice. We further introduce KLPatch, a greedy patching algorithm based on KL divergence, which often improves over last-to-first patching and approximately solves the optimization problem. Together, our results provide a principled understanding of how layer patching affects model size interpolation and offer practical guidance for constructing near-optimal interpolated models.

</details>


### [204] [Open-ended Multi-agent Autocurricula via Visual Inspection of Policies with Multi-modal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08193)
*Lorenzo Pantè,Andrea Fanti,Roberto Capobianco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces VIP (Visual Inspection of Policies), a method that uses a Video Language Model to analyze recorded episode videos of RL policies and generate curriculum recommendations, outperforming text-based and scalar-score-based curricula on the SMAC benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended curriculum learning in RL requires accurate assessment of task difficulty relative to an agent's learning progress. Existing methods rely on scalar task scores or textual behavior summaries, which can miss nuanced behavioral information. The authors hypothesize that directly inspecting policy behavior through video recordings can provide richer signals for curriculum design.

Method: VIP leverages a Video Language Model (specifically VideoLLaMa2-7B) to watch recorded episode videos of an agent's policy rollout and provide curriculum recommendations. The VLM processes visual behavioral data directly from videos—which can naturally accommodate multi-agent settings—to assess task difficulty and suggest next tasks, without requiring handcrafted scoring functions or textual abstractions.

Result: VIP, even using a lightweight and open-source VLM (VideoLLaMa2-7B), generates more effective curricula compared to a text-only ablation (where the VLM receives textual descriptions instead of videos) and methods relying on scalar task scores, evaluated on the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC).

Conclusion: Direct visual inspection of policy behavior via VLMs is a promising and effective approach for curriculum generation in RL. Video-based assessment captures richer behavioral information than text or scalar summaries, enabling better task sequencing, and the approach scales naturally to multi-agent domains.

Abstract: Open-ended curricula in Reinforcement Learning (RL) aim to train generally-capable agents by identifying tasks that facilitate learning increasingly complex skills. A major challenge when designing such curricula is assessing task difficulty relative to the agent's current learning progress. While previous work has explored using scalar task scores or textual summaries of the agent's behavior, here we study a different approach: directly inspecting policy behavior via recorded episode videos. We introduce a simple yet effective instantiation of this approach which leverages a Video Language Model (VLM) to both process these videos and provide curriculum recommendations, which we call Visual Inspection of Policies (VIP). Since videos can naturally contain any number of controllable agents, we empirically study VIP on the StarCraft Multi-Agent Challenge (SMAC). We show that even with a lightweight and openly accessible VLM (VideoLLaMa2-7B), VIP can use policy videos to generate more effective curricula than both its text-only ablation and methods that rely on scalar task scores.

</details>


### [205] [PIT-SUN: A Deployable Empirical Marginal Transform Framework with Expectation-Consistent Recovery for Regression in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08202)
*Mingyu Zhao,Zhaohan Li,Zhenxiong Miao,Xu Zhang,Dewei Leng,Yanan Niu,Kun Gai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PIT-SUN proposes an empirical marginal recovery framework for unbiased original-space conditional expectation estimation, using probability-integral transformation with multiplicative recovery to handle heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, multimodal targets, avoiding mean collapse and tail shrinkage inherent in direct inverse-transform methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MSE on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, multimodal targets causes gradient instability, mean collapse, and tail shrinkage. Target transformations help scale but lose expectation consistency under nonlinear inverse transforms — only affine transforms preserve it, which cannot provide bounded tail compression. Existing conditionally linear recovery methods leave open which coordinate, inverse lookup, recovery base, and deployment monitor to select.

Method: PIT-SUN uses a single empirical marginal table to define: (1) a bounded normal-score coordinate, (2) an inverse-quantile lookup, (3) a variance-controlled recovery base, and (4) drift monitoring. It applies multiplicative SUN (Standardized Unbiased) recovery to estimate original-space expectations rather than directly inverting transformed predictions.

Result: Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment demonstrate robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

Conclusion: PIT-SUN provides a deployable, expectation-consistent recovery framework that resolves the longstanding tension between scale stabilization via nonlinear transforms and unbiased original-space estimation, with proven industrial efficacy.

Abstract: Estimating original-space conditional expectations is central to value-driven recommender systems, including dwell time, GMV, and LTV forecasting. Standard MSE is expectation-consistent in principle, but its gradients become unstable on heavy-tailed, zero-inflated, and multimodal targets, causing mean collapse and tail shrinkage. Target transformation alleviates this scale conflict, yet any useful nonlinear marginal transform loses expectation consistency under direct inversion. This is not an implementation oversight: a direct inverse-transform estimator is universally expectation-consistent only when the inverse transform is affine, which cannot simultaneously provide bounded tail compression. Existing conditionally linear recovery methods restore expectation consistency, but still leave open which coordinate, inverse lookup, recovery base, and deployment monitor should be selected for sparse complex marginals. We propose \textbf{P}robability-\textbf{I}ntegral-\textbf{TranS}formed \textbf{Un}biased recovery (\textbf{PIT-SUN}), a deployable empirical marginal recovery framework. PIT-SUN uses one empirical marginal table to define a bounded normal-score coordinate, its inverse-quantile lookup, a variance-controlled recovery base, and drift monitoring, then applies multiplicative SUN recovery to estimate the original-space expectation instead of directly inverting transformed predictions. Experiments on synthetic distributions, public benchmarks, large-scale industrial datasets, and online deployment show robust improvements in point accuracy, calibration, and ranking quality with lightweight deployment overhead.

</details>


### [206] [RhyMix: A Lightweight Adaptive Multi-Rhythm Network for Long-Term Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08234)
*Sumit Satishrao Shevtekar,Chandresh Kumar Maurya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RhyMix is a lightweight hybrid forecasting architecture that uses dual-path modeling (cyclic embeddings + multi-scale temporal convolutions) with adaptive gating to capture short-term, seasonal, long-term, and abrupt patterns simultaneously, achieving SOTA on 10/12 long-term forecasting benchmarks with ~40K parameters and <5ms inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time series forecasting architectures suffer from a fundamental trade-off: transformers capture long-range dependencies but oversmooth local variations; convolutions capture local patterns but have limited receptive fields; linear models are efficient but cannot model nonlinear dynamics. Real-world time series simultaneously exhibit multiple temporal patterns (short-term fluctuations, seasonal cycles, long-term trends, abrupt changes) that no single-path architecture can adequately capture.

Method: RhyMix employs a parallel dual-path paradigm: (1) a Cyclic Path with learnable cyclic embeddings providing explicit seasonal inductive bias for rhythmic patterns; (2) a Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention (MSTCN-CA) Path using multi-scale depthwise dilated convolutions for varied receptive fields. Adaptive gating operates at two levels — a path gate dynamically combines four specialized forecasting heads (Direct, Trend-Seasonal Decomposition, Local Convolution, Periodic Fusion) per sample and channel, while a hybrid gate balances the two encoding paths based on input characteristics. The architecture maintains linear complexity in sequence length, channels, and prediction horizon.

Result: RhyMix achieves state-of-the-art forecasting performance on 10 out of 12 real-world long-term forecasting datasets. The model remains lightweight (~40K parameters) with linear computational complexity and low-latency inference (<5ms), making it suitable for edge deployment.

Conclusion: The dual-path design with multi-level adaptive gating effectively resolves the trade-offs inherent in single-path architectures, enabling a single lightweight model to capture diverse temporal dynamics simultaneously. This demonstrates that explicit architectural inductive biases for different temporal patterns, when adaptively combined, outperform monolithic approaches for real-world time series forecasting.

Abstract: Real-world time series exhibit complex dynamics characterized by multiple simultaneous temporal patterns: short-term fluctuations, periodic seasonal cycles, long-term trends, and irregular abrupt changes. However, many existing forecasting architectures rely on single-path temporal modeling--transformers capture long-range dependencies but smooth local variations, convolutions capture local patterns but have limited receptive fields, and linear models are efficient but cannot capture nonlinear dynamics. To address this, we introduce RhyMix (RHYthm MIXture), a hybrid neural architecture designed around a parallel dual-path modeling paradigm with adaptive gating mechanisms. RhyMix integrates two complementary encoding branches: (i) a Cyclic Path that incorporates explicit seasonal inductive bias through learnable cyclic embeddings, capturing predictable rhythmic patterns; and (ii) a lightweight Multi-Scale Temporal Convolutional Network with Channel Attention Path that employs multi-scale depthwise dilated convolutions to capture temporal dependencies across different receptive fields. A key innovation is the use of adaptive gating at multiple levels: a path gate dynamically combines four specialized forecasting heads (Direct, Trend-Seasonal Decomposition, Local Convolution, and Periodic Fusion) per sample and channel, while a hybrid gate adaptively balances the Cyclic and MSTCN-CA Paths based on input characteristics. This design ensures the model adapts to specific temporal patterns while maintaining linear complexity in sequence length, channels, and prediction horizon. Across extensive benchmarks on 12 real-world datasets for long-term forecasting, RhyMix achieves state-of-the-art performance on 10 of 12 datasets. The model remains lightweight (~40K params) with linear complexity and low-latency inference (<5ms),suitable for resource-constrained edge devices and real-time deployment.

</details>


### [207] [Structure Learning on Clustered Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08238)
*Ryan Thompson,Matt P. Wand,Veerabhadran Baladandayuthapani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a mixed-effects DAG structure learning method that estimates a global (fixed-effects) graph while accounting for cluster-specific (random-effects) variations, using a differentiable coupling mechanism to maintain acyclicity, with provable convergence, identifiability, and asymptotic recovery guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DAG structure learning methods assume a homogeneous population, making them unsuitable for clustered data where cluster-specific effects (e.g., patient-level variations) exist. There is a need for methods that can learn causal structures while accommodating both population-level and cluster-level heterogeneity.

Method: The authors extend the fixed- and random-effects framework from classical mixed models to DAG structure learning. They propose a differentiable graph coupling mechanism that ensures the union of fixed- and random-effects graphs remains acyclic. Optimization uses a provably convergent first-order method with efficient batched updates across clusters. They also establish identifiability conditions and prove asymptotic recovery of the true structure.

Result: On both real and synthetic data, the proposed method detects dependencies missed by alternative estimators, demonstrating its effectiveness for structure learning in clustered settings. Theoretical results include identifiability of the model and asymptotic recovery guarantees.

Conclusion: The mixed-effects approach to DAG structure learning fills a gap between causal discovery and clustered data analysis, offering both theoretical guarantees and practical performance improvements over homogeneous methods.

Abstract: Recent algorithmic advances have made directed acyclic graph (DAG) structure learning scalable for causal discovery. Yet, the currently available techniques assume a completely homogeneous population, precluding their application to clustered data where cluster-specific variations (e.g., patient-specific effects) are common. We address this issue by introducing a new approach that estimates a global structure while accounting for local cluster-level effects. The key idea is to extend the fixed- and random-effects framework of classical mixed models to the structure learning setting. Towards this end, we present a differentiable graph coupling mechanism that guarantees the union of the fixed- and random-effects graphs remains acyclic. Computationally, we provide a provably convergent first-order method and leverage efficient batched updates across clusters. Statistically, we establish identifiability of the model and show that our approach recovers the true structure asymptotically. In experiments on real and synthetic data, our proposal detects dependencies missed by alternative estimators, underscoring its value for structure learning in clustered settings.

</details>


### [208] [An interpretable Good--Turing restart criterion for k-means++](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08243)
*Renato Cordeiro de Amorim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GTRC introduces a principled, data-dependent restart criterion for k-means++ that stops when the probability of improvement drops below a tolerance ε, combining a Good-Turing estimator with unconditional and confidence-based bounds, achieving competitive clustering quality while adaptively allocating restarts based on data set difficulty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The number of restarts for k-means++ is typically chosen arbitrarily and applied uniformly across data sets, which wastes computation on easy data sets, potentially underserves hard ones, and undermines fair comparisons between methods.

Method: GTRC combines three components: (1) a Good-Turing estimator to estimate the probability of unseen improved solutions, (2) a proven unconditional bound on that probability, and (3) a confidence-based bound. Restarts stop once the estimated probability of further improvement falls below a user-specified tolerance ε.

Result: Evaluated on 36 data sets, GTRC matched the clustering quality of well-chosen fixed restart counts while adaptively varying the number of restarts according to data set difficulty, guided by an interpretable, data-dependent signal.

Conclusion: GTRC provides a principled, reportable alternative to arbitrarily fixing the number of k-means++ restarts, enabling more efficient and fair use of computational resources across data sets of varying difficulty.

Abstract: The k-means++ algorithm is commonly restarted multiple times to avoid poor local optima, yet the number of restarts is almost always chosen arbitrarily and applied uniformly regardless of data set difficulty. This undermines any comparison relying on such a choice and wastes computation on easy data sets while potentially under-serving hard ones. We introduce GTRC, a restart criterion combining a Good-Turing estimate, a proven unconditional bound, and a confidence-based bound on the probability that a further restart would improve on the current result, stopping once this probability falls below a user-specified tolerance $\varepsilon$. Across 36 data sets, GTRC reached clustering quality competitive with well-chosen fixed restart counts, while the number of restarts used varied considerably and appropriately with data set difficulty, governed by an interpretable, data-dependent signal rather than a fixed rule. GTRC offers a principled and reportable alternative to fixing the number of $k$-means++ restarts in advance. Software:https://github.com/RCdeAmorim/Good-Turing-Restart-Criterion.

</details>


### [209] [CASL-VAE: Learning Structured Latent Variables from Unpaired Data for Semi-supervised Clustering and Paired Sample Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08254)
*Sai Spandana Chintapalli,Pratik Chaudhari,Christos Davatzikos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CASL-VAE is a deep contrastive variational autoencoder that disentangles population variation from unpaired data into shared factors and hierarchical target-specific factors (discrete subtypes + continuous within-subtype variation), outperforming baselines on neuroimaging tasks including Alzheimer's disease subtyping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantifying how a target population (e.g., diseased) varies relative to a reference population (e.g., healthy) is critical in science and medicine. Existing methods struggle without paired data and fail to separate multiple modes of target-specific heterogeneity — they cannot simultaneously capture discrete subtypes and continuous within-subtype variation from unpaired cross-population data.

Method: CASL-VAE is a contrastive latent variable model using variational inference. It factorizes latent representations into: (1) continuous common latent factors shared across populations, and (2) hierarchical salient latent factors that model target-specific variation as a mixture of discrete subtypes with continuous within-subtype variation. The model performs approximate joint likelihood optimization over reference and target domains using only unpaired data, enabling principled paired-sample generation and cross-domain analysis.

Result: On semi-synthetic neuroimaging data, CASL-VAE demonstrated improved subtype recovery and paired-sample generation compared to baseline clustering and generative models. It also revealed biologically plausible heterogeneity patterns in Alzheimer's disease when applied to real clinical data.

Conclusion: CASL-VAE provides a principled framework for modeling structured target-specific variation from unpaired data, effectively separating discrete subtypes from continuous variation while preserving shared population structure, with promising applications in clinical neuroimaging and disease heterogeneity analysis.

Abstract: Quantifying variability in a target population relative to a reference population is central to many scientific and clinical problems (e.g., diseased vs. healthy). Yet, without paired data and in the presence of heterogeneous target variation, existing methods struggle to separate multiple modes of target-specific variation. We propose \textit{CASL-VAE}, a deep contrastive latent variable model that learns structured latent generative factors from unpaired data. CASL-VAE factorizes variation into continuous common latent factors shared across populations and hierarchical salient latent factors that model target-specific heterogeneity as discrete subtypes and continuous within-subtype variation. Using variational inference, we show how approximate joint likelihood optimization over reference and target domains can be performed using unpaired data, providing a principled basis for paired-sample generation and cross-domain analysis. We validate CASL-VAE on semi-synthetic neuroimaging data, demonstrating improved subtype recovery and paired-sample generation compared to baseline clustering and generative models. We also validate its ability to reveal biologically plausible heterogeneity in Alzheimer's disease.

</details>


### [210] [Ensemble Diversity Optimization for Subjective Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08493)
*Xia Cui,Ziyi Huang,N. R. Abeynayake*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EDO is a prediction-space framework that jointly optimizes ensemble weights, size, and calibration via a unified differentiable objective with a signed diversity regularizer. It prevents ensemble collapse and navigates the utility-calibration trade-off, achieving superior probabilistic calibration on subjective NLP benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subjective NLP tasks exhibit systematic annotator disagreement. Existing models tend to collapse this uncertainty rather than faithfully represent it. There is a need for a framework that can preserve or suppress disagreement in a controlled manner while maintaining predictive performance and calibration.

Method: EDO jointly optimizes ensemble weights, effective cardinality, and calibration via Gumbel-Softmax relaxation. It introduces a signed diversity regularizer (tuned on validation data) that steers optimization toward preserving or suppressing annotator disagreement. The framework integrates a soft F1 surrogate, class-weighted cross-entropy for class imbalance, and reliability-weighted diversity to regulate intra-ensemble variability.

Result: On four benchmarks (ArMIS, ConvAbuse, HS-Brexit, MD-Agreement), EDO substantially improves probabilistic calibration: cross-entropy reduced by 40-78%, lower Brier scores versus Soft-CE, Soft-MD, Top-5 Voting, and WEL baselines. It maintains competitive F1 and better alignment with annotator distributions.

Conclusion: Jointly optimizing ensemble structure with a signed diversity regularizer offers an efficient, model-agnostic approach for modeling human subjectivity in supervised learning, enabling controlled navigation of the utility-calibration trade-off.

Abstract: Subjective NLP tasks often exhibit systematic annotator disagreement, requiring models that represent uncertainty rather than collapse it. We introduce Ensemble Diversity Optimization (EDO), a prediction-space framework that jointly optimizes ensemble weights, effective cardinality, and calibration through a unified differentiable objective. EDO learns ensemble composition and size end-to-end via Gumbel-Softmax relaxation and incorporates a signed diversity regularizer, tuned on validation data, to steer optimization toward either preserving or suppressing disagreement. This regularization prevents ensemble collapse and enables controlled navigation of the utility-calibration trade-off. The framework integrates a soft F1 surrogate, class-weighted cross-entropy to address imbalance, and reliability-weighted diversity to regulate intra-ensemble variability. Experiments on four subjective text-classification benchmarks (ArMIS, ConvAbuse, HS-Brexit, MD-Agreement) show that EDO substantially improves probabilistic calibration, reducing cross-entropy (40-78% depending on baseline) and lowering Brier scores relative to Soft-CE, Soft-MD, Top-5 Voting, and WEL, while maintaining competitive F1 and better alignment with annotator distributions. These results demonstrate that jointly optimizing ensemble structure with a signed diversity regularizer provides an efficient, model-agnostic approach for modeling human subjectivity in supervised learning.

</details>


### [211] [Classifier Chain-based Pathological Test Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08299)
*Abu Rafe Md Jamil,Nayan Malakar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multi-label classification system using Classifier Chains (CC) with Logistic Regression achieves 98.83% accuracy in recommending pathological tests from patient symptoms. SHAP-based XAI confirms the model's recommendations align with established medical knowledge, supporting physician decision-making.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diagnostic processes suffer from delayed test recommendations and subjective physician interpretations, which can compromise timely patient care. There is a need for an automated system that can recommend appropriate pathological tests based on patient symptoms before the physician consultation, thereby accelerating the diagnostic workflow and reducing subjectivity.

Method: The authors frame test recommendation as a multi-label classification problem using Classifier Chains (CC) to model dependencies between tests. A custom dataset was built from SOUTHERN.IML pathology data with expert curation. Multiple algorithms were evaluated: Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, Random Forest, and a Majority Voting ensemble. Explainable AI (XAI) via SHAP was applied to provide clinical interpretability by quantifying each symptom's contribution to test recommendations.

Result: Logistic Regression with CC achieved the highest overall accuracy of 98.83%. The Majority Voting ensemble yielded the best balance with precision of 0.93, recall of 0.85, and F1-score of 0.89. SHAP analysis demonstrated that the model's diagnostic reasoning was consistent with established medical knowledge, reinforcing confidence in its reliability.

Conclusion: Classifier Chains can effectively improve traditional algorithms in diagnostic test recommendation. The system provides accurate, interpretable, and medically coherent test suggestions that can assist physicians in logical decision-making during critical scenarios.

Abstract: Accurate and timely diagnoses are essential for quality patient care. However, delayed recommendation of diagnostic tests and physicians' subjective interpretations can hinder effective care. This study introduces a pathological test recommendation system that speeds up the test selection process using patient symptoms before physician consultation. The recommendation task is framed as a multi-label classification problem utilising the Classifier Chain (CC) technique to consider dependencies between tests. We collected data from the SOUTHERN.IML pathology and then created a custom dataset with the help of the expertise. Multiple machine learning algorithms, including Logistic Regression, Decision Tree, and Random Forest, were applied to compare models and identify the best fit for our study context. The Logistic Regression with CC model had the highest overall accuracy at 98.83%, while the Majority Voting ensemble model provided the best balance with a precision of 0.93, recall of 0.85, and F1-score of 0.89. To ensure transparency of the models and clinical interpretability, we used Explainable AI (XAI) techniques utilising SHAP (SHapley Additive Explanations), which identifies how each symptom is contributing to a test recommendation. The diagnostic reasoning revealed by the model was consistent with established medical knowledge of symptoms for the recommended tests, which further adds confidence to the model's reliability for diagnostic purposes. The reasoning could help physicians make logical decisions in critical scenarios. Overall, our findings suggest that CC can improve the efficiency of the traditional algorithms in diagnostic process providing accurate test recommendations.

</details>


### [212] [Learning $\mathsf{AC}^0$ under Locally Sampleable Graphical Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08303)
*Weiming Feng,Xiongxin Yang,Yixiao Yu,Yiyao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a quasipolynomial-time learner for AC⁰ circuits under Gibbs distributions on bounded-degree graphical models that admit efficient local samplers. The key innovation is a new low-degree approximation method based on simulating and truncating Glauber dynamics, which removes the polynomial-growth constraint from prior work. The framework applies to two-spin systems (hard-core model, Ising model) on arbitrary bounded-degree graphs, operating near their sampling thresholds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning AC⁰ circuits under correlated distributions has been a longstanding open problem since Linial-Mansour-Nisan (1993) gave a quasipolynomial learner only for the uniform distribution. Recent work by Chandrasekaran et al. (2026) extended these guarantees to Gibbs distributions on bounded-degree graphs, but required both strong spatial mixing and polynomial growth — the latter being a restrictive condition. This paper aims to remove the polynomial-growth requirement to cover broader classes of graphical models.

Method: The authors develop a new low-degree approximation technique for Gibbs distributions by simulating the classical Glauber dynamics and applying a suitable truncation. This approach replaces the polynomial-growth assumption with the milder condition that the graphical model admits efficient local samplers. The low-degree approximation is then combined with the established low-degree algorithm framework to achieve quasipolynomial-time learning of AC⁰ circuits.

Result: The paper achieves a quasipolynomial-time learner for AC⁰ under graphical models with efficient local samplers, eliminating the polynomial-growth requirement. The framework yields concrete learners for two-spin systems — including the hard-core model and Ising model — on arbitrary bounded-degree graphs, working in parameter regimes that approach the respective sampling thresholds of these models.

Conclusion: By replacing polynomial growth with efficient local sampling, the paper significantly broadens the class of correlated distributions under which AC⁰ circuits can be efficiently learned. The Glauber-dynamics-based low-degree approximation technique may have further applications in computational learning theory and beyond.

Abstract: The problem of learning constant-depth circuits holds profound implications for computational learning theory. In a seminal result, by introducing the low-degree algorithm, Linial, Mansour, and Nisan (J. ACM 1993) presented a quasipolynomial-time learner for $\mathsf{AC}^0$ under the uniform distribution. However, obtaining comparable learning guarantees for broader classes of correlated distributions has remained a longstanding challenge. Recently, Chandrasekaran, Gaitonde, Moitra, and Vasilyan (arXiv 2026) extended these guarantees to Gibbs distributions on bounded-degree graphical models with both strong spatial mixing and polynomial growth.
  In this paper, we give a quasipolynomial-time learner for $\mathsf{AC}^0$ under graphical models that admit efficient local samplers, circumventing the polynomial-growth requirement in prior work. The key ingredient is a new low-degree approximation for Gibbs distributions, established by simulating and suitably truncating the classical Glauber dynamics. As applications, this framework yields learners for two-spin systems, including the hard-core model and Ising model, on arbitrary bounded-degree graphs, in regimes approaching their respective sampling thresholds.

</details>


### [213] [Write-Protected Discrete Bottlenecks for Language-Grounded World Models: A Structural Limitation and Sufficient Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08312)
*Jiayi Fang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that language gradients cannot safely shape discrete symbol representations in Gumbel-softmax bottlenecks due to a structural collapse (2.2/64 symbols used). The authors propose three architectural constraints — gradient detaching, a non-parametric Memory Table for semantic binding via co-occurrence counting, and DP-Means clustering for collision resolution — achieving 97.2% grounding accuracy with zero symbol collapse across all seeds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The dominant paradigm of injecting LLM/VLM features end-to-end into robot world models implicitly assumes language gradients can directly shape physical symbol representations. This paper questions whether that assumption is safe, motivated by the structural failure they identify: Gumbel-softmax discrete bottlenecks collapse under language gradient pressure, losing both symbol diversity and semantic grounding.

Method: The authors systematically characterize the failure mode across a Gumbel-softmax discrete symbol bottleneck, testing the vanilla estimator and five anti-collapse strategies. They then propose three architectural constraints: (1) gradient chain cutting via z.detach() to isolate the symbol bottleneck from language signals; (2) a gradient-free semantic channel using a non-parametric Memory Table (Dict[symbol → Counter[label]]) where co-occurrence counting replaces gradient-based binding; and (3) DP-Means streaming clustering for automatic sub-cluster splitting to handle symbol collisions. Evaluation spans 74 independent runs across three encoder architectures (CNN, V-JEPA 300M, CLIP ViT-L), two environments, and three texture conditions.

Result: The vanilla Gumbel-softmax bottleneck collapses to 2.2/64 symbols (4/5 seeds). All five anti-collapse strategies maintain diversity but achieve ≤9.2% semantic labeling accuracy. The full three-layer solution achieves 97.2% grounding accuracy (vs. 22.2% without Layer 3), zero symbol collapse in all 32 seeds, and 79–100% semantic binding across all tested architectures and conditions, training under 2M parameters with no LLM fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The failure of language gradients in Gumbel-based discrete bottlenecks is structural rather than an optimization issue. The three proposed constraints — gradient isolation, co-occurrence-based semantic binding, and collision-resolving clustering — are sufficient to prevent collapse and enable robust symbol grounding, suggesting that language should interface with discrete world-model symbols through gradient-free channels rather than end-to-end gradient injection.

Abstract: How should language interface with a world model's discrete symbol system? The dominant paradigm -- end-to-end injection of LLM/VLM features into robot world models (RT-2, Octo, PaLM-E) -- implicitly assumes that language gradients can directly shape physical symbol representations. We ask whether this assumption is safe, find that it is not, and characterize the minimal architectural constraint that prevents the failure. Any language gradient entering a Gumbel-softmax-based discrete symbol bottleneck forces a structural trade-off: the vanilla estimator collapses to 2.2/64 symbols (4/5 seeds), while five anti-collapse strategies maintain diversity but fail to learn semantic labels (all <= 9.2% accuracy). No tested GumbelBottleneck variant achieves both objectives simultaneously. Within this family of discrete bottlenecks, the failure is structural rather than a matter of optimization. We characterize a sufficient set of three constraints that prevent the failure: (1) cut the gradient chain (z.detach()), preventing language signals from reaching the symbol bottleneck; (2) provide a gradient-free semantic channel -- a non-parametric Memory Table (Dict[symbol -> Counter[label]], zero parameters, zero gradients) where co-occurrence counting replaces gradient-based binding; (3) handle symbol collisions via DP-Means streaming clustering for automatic sub-cluster splitting. All three layers together achieve 97.2% grounding accuracy vs. 22.2% without Layer 3. Across two experiments spanning 74 independent runs, we demonstrate zero symbol collapse in all 32 seeds, with the blackboard achieving 79-100% semantic binding across three encoder architectures (CNN, V-JEPA 300M, CLIP ViT-L), two environments, and three texture conditions. The fix trains fewer than 2M parameters and requires no LLM fine-tuning.

</details>


### [214] [ArtMine: Discovering and Formalizing Artistic Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08331)
*Kaustubh Kumar,Ashutosh Ranjan,Vivek Srivastava,Blessin Varkey,Shirish Karande*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ArtMine is a framework that reconstructs artistic creation processes from fragmented historical evidence using Peircean abductive reasoning, compositional graphs, and self-reflection-based optimization, moving beyond artifact-centric AI toward process-centered modeling of creative workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current generative AI systems model finished artworks rather than the creative processes behind them. Artistic workflows are documented only through fragmented sources (archives, sketches, correspondence), making computational formalization of process-level understanding difficult. There is a need for systems that can reason about iterative decisions and material operations in art creation.

Method: ArtMine operates in four stages: (1) synthesizing heterogeneous artwork evidence into a structured repository; (2) employing a Peircean abductive agent to infer evidence-grounded production steps; (3) converting these steps into a compositional graph and rendering prompt; (4) optimizing through self-reflection by comparing deviations between generated and reference artworks.

Result: A proof-of-concept case study using open-domain historical sources across multiple artists and artistic movements demonstrates that fragmented documentary evidence can yield coherent, interpretable, and auditable representations of artistic workflows.

Conclusion: By modeling creative processes rather than only final artifacts, ArtMine advances toward process-centered human-AI co-creativity systems, with applications in artistic interpretation, creative education, reflective collaboration, and computational studies of cultural production.

Abstract: Understanding how artworks are created requires reasoning about the iterative decisions, material operations, and contextual influences that shape artistic production. While recent generative AI systems can synthesize artworks with high fidelity, they primarily model distributions over finished artifacts rather than the creative processes underlying their creation. In practice, artistic workflows are only partially documented through fragmented sources such as archival records, preparatory studies, correspondence, etc., making process-level understanding difficult to formalize computationally. In this work, we introduce ArtMine, a framework for discovering and formalizing artistic processes from heterogeneous historical evidence. Our approach synthesizes heterogeneous artwork evidence into a structured repository, from which a Peircean abductive agent infers evidence-grounded production steps. These steps are converted into a compositional graph and rendering prompt, then optimized through self-reflection over deviations between the generated and reference artworks. We provide a preliminary proof-of-concept case study using open-domain historical sources across multiple artists and artistic movements, demonstrating that fragmented documentary evidence can support coherent, interpretable, and auditable representations of artistic workflows. By modeling creative processes rather than only final artifacts, our work moves toward process-centred human-AI co-creativity systems that can support artistic interpretation, creative education, reflective collaboration, and computational studies of cultural production.

</details>


### [215] [AutoAnchor: Stable Diffusion Unlearning Using Cross-Attention as a Manifold Surrogate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08337)
*Siyuan Wen,Jiahao Zeng,Ningning Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes MAnifold-proximal aNchor (MAN) for diffusion unlearning, a two-stage framework that auto-synthesizes manifold-proximal anchors using cross-attention consistency loss as a surrogate, achieving stable, robust, unbiased concept removal without manual anchor selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion unlearning methods are unstable: anchor-based methods rely on hand-picked semantic anchors causing biased unlearning, while anchor-free methods suffer from unconstrained latent updates (normal-space drift) that degrade robustness. A theoretically grounded, automatic anchor selection mechanism is needed.

Method: Formally analyzes diffusion unlearning under the manifold hypothesis, proving that lacking a manifold-proximal anchor causes normal-space drift. Proposes MAN: a two-stage framework that (1) automatically synthesizes manifold-proximal anchors and (2) uses a novel cross-attention consistency loss as a computationally efficient surrogate for geometric manifold proximity, avoiding intractable direct manifold optimization.

Result: MAN achieves up to 31.04% improvement in CLIP score for targeted concept removal and up to 4.18% improvement for non-target utility. When integrated into existing methods, it boosts concept removal by 6.30% and utility by 6.65% on average.

Conclusion: MAN provides a theoretically principled and practically effective solution for stable diffusion unlearning by automatically identifying manifold-proximal anchors, resolving both the bias of anchor-based methods and the instability of anchor-free methods.

Abstract: Diffusion unlearning is essential for mitigating the generation of harmful or copyrighted content in text-to-image models. Current diffusion unlearning techniques determine the model update direction by either using alternatives of the target concept as an anchor or using empty prompts. The anchor-based method relies on manually and semantically-chosen anchors that risk biased unlearning, while the anchor-free method inherently suffers from unrobust unlearning due to unconstrained latent updates. In this work, we theoretically formalize such unstable diffusion unlearning issues under the manifold hypothesis and prove that lacking a manifold-proximal anchor inevitably induces significant normal-space drift that degrades unlearning performance. To achieve stable unlearning, we propose \mysysn, a two-stage framework that automatically synthesizes manifold-proximal anchors. However, direct geometric manifold optimization is computationally intractable. To address this challenge, \mysys introduces a novel cross-attention consistency loss which serves as a highly efficient surrogate of manifold proximity. Experimental results demonstrate that \mysys effectively achieves robust and unbiased unlearning across various state-of-the-art baselines, significantly improving targeted concept removal (by up to 31.04\% in CLIP score) and non-target utility (by up to 4.18\% in CLIP score). Moreover, \mysys can also be easily integrated into existing diffusion unlearning methods to enhance their unlearning performance (by 6.30\% for concept removal and 6.65\% for utility on average).

</details>


### [216] [Spectral Analysis of Dueling Q-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08340)
*Donghwan Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes unregularized, unprojected tabular dueling Q-learning with constant step-size, deriving an exact switching linear system representation for the deterministic case and a finite-time expected error bound for the stochastic case. The analysis reveals how value and advantage updates serve as distinct gains on the action-common and action-differential Q-function components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theoretical guarantees for dueling Q-learning are limited to a regularized formulation, leaving the pure tabular update without regularization incompletely understood. The authors aim to close this gap by providing convergence guarantees for the unregularized, unprojected constant step-size dueling Q-learning recursion.

Method: The authors model deterministic dueling Q-learning as an exact switching linear system and analyze the sampled stochastic version to derive a finite-time error bound in expectation. The analysis decomposes the Q-function into action-common (value function) and action-differential (advantage function) components, interpreting how each update operates with different effective gains on these components.

Result: An exact switching linear system representation is derived for deterministic dueling Q-learning. For the stochastic setting, a finite-time error bound in expectation is established, clarifying how the value and advantage updates act as distinct gains on the action-common and action-differential components of the Q-function.

Conclusion: The paper strengthens the theoretical foundation of pure tabular dueling Q-learning by providing a direct interpretation of the centered decomposition and establishing convergence guarantees without regularization or projection, advancing beyond prior regularized analyses.

Abstract: Q-learning is a fundamental algorithm in reinforcement learning (RL) for solving discounted Markov decision processes (MDPs) when the transition kernel is unknown. The deep Q-network (DQN) extends Q-learning by using a deep neural network for Q-function approximation, which makes Q-learning applicable to more practical high-dimensional problems. Dueling Q-learning decomposes the Q-function into a value function and an advantage function and learns the two components jointly, which can improve learning efficiency. However, the theoretical understanding of dueling Q-learning is still limited. Recent work has initiated an analysis of tabular dueling Q-learning, but existing guarantees focus on a regularized formulation and leave the pure tabular update less completely understood. This paper strengthens that line of analysis by adding a direct interpretation of the centered tabular decomposition and by establishing convergence guarantees for the unregularized, unprojected constant step-size recursion. In particular, we derive an exact switching linear system representation for deterministic dueling Q-learning and a finite-time error bound in expectation for the sampled stochastic version. The analysis clarifies how the value and advantage updates act as different gains on the action-common (value function) and action-differential (advantage function) components of the Q-function.

</details>


### [217] [Certified Interventional Fidelity: Anytime-Valid, Adaptive Evaluation of Causal Claims in Mechanistic Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08349)
*Amir Asiaee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CIF provides confidence intervals and anytime-valid confidence sequences for interventional mechanistic interpretability evaluations, replacing point estimates with statistically rigorous causal claims about model component contributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current interventional interpretability evaluations report point estimates without statistical rigor, making it impossible to distinguish stable causal claims from artifacts of finite sampling, adaptive stopping, or evaluation design choices.

Method: CIF formalizes interventional reports as causal estimands (expectations of bounded scores over stated input/intervention distributions), then constructs Hoeffding-style and variance-adaptive betting confidence sequences. Adaptive intervention sampling is handled via bounded mixture importance weighting, enabling anytime-valid inference even when interventions are chosen adaptively during evaluation.

Result: Variance-adaptive betting sequences reduce certification cost by 10–30× compared to Hoeffding-style baselines. On MNIST abstraction and GPT-2 Small IOI circuit experiments, CIF certifies high-fidelity claims with valid confidence bounds, reveals when apparent method differences lack statistical support, and makes sensitivity to the intervention distribution explicit.

Conclusion: Statistical rigor via confidence sequences transforms interventional interpretability from point-estimate reporting into verifiable causal claims, enabling practitioners to determine whether observed effects are genuine or merely noise.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability often evaluates explanations by intervening on a model: swapping hidden states, patching activations, ablating components, or comparing a compressed model to the original one. These experiments are usually summarized by a point estimate, even though the evaluation may be monitored while it runs or adapted toward suspected failures. This makes it hard to tell whether a reported fidelity or patching effect is a stable causal claim or a consequence of finite sampling and evaluation choices. We introduce Certified Interventional Fidelity (CIF), a statistical layer for interventional interpretability evaluations. CIF first writes the quantity being reported as a causal estimand: an expectation of a bounded score over a stated input distribution and a stated intervention distribution. It then provides confidence intervals and anytime-valid confidence sequences for this estimand, including under adaptive intervention sampling via bounded mixture importance weighting. We instantiate CIF with Hoeffding-style sequences and variance-adaptive betting sequences, the latter reducing certification cost by 10-30x in our experiments. On MNIST abstractions and GPT-2 Small IOI circuits, CIF certifies high-fidelity claims, shows when apparent method differences are not statistically supported, and makes sensitivity to the intervention distribution explicit.

</details>


### [218] [Self-Adaptive Anomaly Detection with Reinforcement Learning and Human Feedback in Connected Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08373)
*Matthias Weiß,Athreya Hosahalli Prakash,Maurice Artelt,Falk Dettinger,Nasser Jazdi,Michael Weyrich*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A self-adaptive anomaly detection framework for connected vehicles that combines deep reinforcement learning for detector selection, statistical drift detection, and human-in-the-loop retraining — achieving sustained performance recovery after software-update-induced concept drift without catastrophic forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Connected vehicles are evolving CPS whose normal behavior shifts over time due to OTA updates, configuration changes, and workload variations, causing static diagnostic methods to silently degrade. Existing solutions address either automated model adaptation or operator integration in isolation, lacking a unified supervisory loop that coordinates both.

Method: The framework integrates three coordinated mechanisms: (1) a factorized deep Q-network with self-attention that dynamically selects the optimal detector per microservice from a candidate pool, leveraging inter-service dependencies; (2) an ensemble of three statistical drift detectors that trigger alarms only under unanimous agreement, prioritizing precision; (3) a human-in-the-loop retraining pipeline using a pending transition buffer and 60/40 prioritized replay to incorporate expert knowledge while preserving prior learned behavior.

Result: Evaluated on an automated valet parking testbed with seven microservices: the attention-augmented agent achieves F1=0.69 vs. ≤0.11 for any static uniform detector. After a real software update inducing concept drift, F1 drops to 0.52; operator-triggered retraining restores F1 to 0.65 on the new distribution while maintaining 0.69 on the prior distribution, demonstrating adaptation without catastrophic forgetting.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully coordinates automated detector selection, drift monitoring, and human-guided retraining in a unified loop, enabling connected-vehicle CPS to sustain anomaly detection accuracy across evolving operational conditions.

Abstract: Connected vehicles are autonomous cyber-physical systems whose behavior must be continuously monitored during operation to detect deviations from normal operation before they propagate into failures. Such evaluation is challenging because the systems themselves evolve: over-the-air updates, configuration changes, and shifting workloads alter the definition of normal behavior, causing static diagnostic methods to degrade silently over time. Existing approaches typically address either automated model adaptation or operator integration in isolation, rather than as a single coordinated supervisory loop.
  This paper presents an online anomaly detection framework for autonomous CPS that integrates three coordinated mechanisms. A factorized deep Q-network with self-attention selects the most suitable detector from a candidate pool for each monitored service, exploiting inter-service dependencies in the microservice topology. An ensemble of three statistical drift detectors monitors the input distribution and raises an alarm only when all three concur, prioritizing precision over recall. A human-in-the-loop retraining mechanism, built around a pending transition buffer and a 60/40 prioritized replay strategy, allows the operator to incorporate expert knowledge while preserving the system's learned response to prior data distributions.
  The framework is evaluated on a connected-vehicle testbed running an automated valet parking application across seven backend microservices. The attention-augmented agent achieves an F1 score of 0.69, compared to at most 0.11 for any single detector applied uniformly. Following a real software update that induces measurable concept drift, F1 drops to 0.52; after operator-triggered retraining, performance recovers to 0.65 on the new distribution while remaining at 0.69 on the prior one, demonstrating sustained adaptation without catastrophic forgetting.

</details>


### [219] [Eigenvalue Calibration for Semantic Embeddings of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08377)
*Sebastian G. Gruber,Nassim Walha,Francis Bach,Florian Buettner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel framework for calibrating eigenvalues of semantic embeddings in LLMs by interpreting them as density matrix predictors and applying temperature scaling to the eigenvalues. The authors establish theoretical results including entropy-risk equivalence and a central calibration inequality, then demonstrate experimentally that LLMs are systematically overconfident and that their method addresses this gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eigenvalues of semantic embeddings are increasingly used for uncertainty quantification in LLMs, but conventional calibration techniques developed for classification probabilities cannot be directly applied to eigenvalues. This creates a gap in the reliable deployment of LLMs, motivating the need for a principled calibration framework tailored to eigenvalue-based uncertainty.

Method: The authors reinterpret LLMs with semantic embeddings as density matrix predictors (a formalism from quantum information theory). They then propose temperature scaling applied directly to eigenvalues for calibration. Theoretically, they prove entropy-risk equivalence under calibration, derive a central calibration inequality specific to eigenvalues, and show that temperature-scaled eigenvalues minimize proper score risks. Experiments are conducted across diverse real-world settings to validate the framework.

Result: Current LLMs exhibit systematic overconfidence when evaluated through eigenvalue-based uncertainty. The proposed temperature scaling of eigenvalues effectively calibrates these density matrix predictors. The theoretical results—entropy-risk equivalence, the calibration inequality, and optimality of temperature scaling under proper score minimization—are all validated empirically.

Conclusion: The work advances both the theoretical foundations and practical methodology for uncertainty quantification with semantic embeddings, providing a principled calibration approach that bridges the gap between classical calibration theory and modern eigenvalue-based methods in LLMs.

Abstract: Uncertainty quantification is central to the reliable deployment of large language models (LLMs), and eigenvalues of semantic embeddings have recently emerged as a key tool in state-of-the-art methods. However, conventional calibration results developed for classification probabilities cannot be directly transferred to eigenvalues. We address this gap by proposing a novel framework for calibrating the eigenvalues of semantic embeddings. We interpret LLMs combined with semantic embeddings of their generated answers as density matrix predictors, and we propose a novel approach to calibrate density matrix predictors by applying temperature scaling to their eigenvalues. We establish entropy-risk equivalence under calibration, derive a central calibration inequality specific to eigenvalues, and prove that temperature-scaled eigenvalues optimize calibration when minimizing proper score risks. Experiments on a variety of real-world settings show that current LLMs are systematically overconfident, and validate our theoretical findings. Together, these results advance the foundations and practice of uncertainty quantification for semantic embeddings.

</details>


### [220] [Dynamics of Gradient Descent with Large Step Size Near a Manifold of Flat Minima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08380)
*Lachlan Ewen MacDonald,René Vidal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends the normal form theory for large-step gradient descent from single-output overparametrized least-squares to vector-valued outputs and manifolds of flat minima, with applications to deep matrix factorization; proves the set of flat minima forms a fiber bundle over a product of spheres and that sharpness is Morse-Bott along this manifold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical GD theory requires step size < 2/sharpness, but this condition is routinely violated in deep network training. Recent work addressed this gap only for single-output least-squares near an isolated flat minimum. The authors aim to generalize to the practically relevant settings of vector-valued outputs and continuous manifolds of flat minima (essential for matrix factorization).

Method: The authors generalize the normal form construction and three convergence theorems from prior work to vector-valued output least-squares and neighborhoods of flat-minima manifolds. Key technical contributions include solving a singular PDE via a novel method. They apply the framework to deep matrix factorization, proving structural results about the geometry of the flat-minima set.

Result: (1) Normal form and three convergence theorems are extended to both vector-valued outputs and manifold settings. (2) For deep matrix factorization under mild assumptions: the set of flat minima constitutes a fiber bundle over a product of spheres, and the sharpness (largest Hessian eigenvalue) is Morse-Bott along this manifold.

Conclusion: The generalized theory significantly broadens the applicability of large-step GD analysis to realistic deep learning architectures, particularly matrix factorization. The fiber bundle structure and Morse-Bott property of the sharpness reveal new geometric understanding of the loss landscape.

Abstract: An important quantity in the theory of gradient descent (GD) is the \emph{sharpness}, defined as the largest eigenvalue of the objective Hessian. Classical analyses typically require the step size to be uniformly smaller than twice the reciprocal of the sharpness, but this condition is frequently violated in the training of deep neural networks. Recent work bridges this gap in the setting of overparametrised least-squares with a \emph{single scalar output}, providing a normal form for large-step GD in a neighbourhood of an \emph{isolated} flat minimum and establishing three corresponding convergence results. In this paper, we extend this theory in two directions: (1) to overparametrised least-squares with \emph{vector-valued outputs} (including regression with arbitrarily many observations), and (2) to a neighbourhood of a \emph{manifold} of flat minima (which we show is essential for applications such as matrix factorisation). We generalise both the normal form and all three convergence theorems of \cite{macdonaldeos} to this broader setting, overcoming several technical challenges, including the solution of a singular partial differential equation via a novel method that may be of independent interest. We further show that our framework applies to deep matrix factorisation under mild assumptions, yielding several new structural results. In particular, we prove that the set of flat minima forms a fibre bundle over a product of spheres, and that the sharpness is Morse-Bott along this manifold.

</details>


### [221] [Beyond Backpropagation: Monte Carlo Method Can Train Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08406)
*Hong Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that a simple random mutation Monte Carlo algorithm—mutate a parameter, keep if loss decreases, else retry—can successfully train deep networks on a single GPU without gradients, batch normalization, or residual connections, and extends to pruning, discrete weights, and unconventional activation functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Backpropagation, while dominant, suffers from vanishing/exploding gradients. The authors aim to show that a gradient-free alternative—specifically the simplest possible Monte Carlo mutation approach—is practically viable for training deep networks, challenging the presumed necessity of gradient-based optimization.

Method: The method is a straightforward random mutation Monte Carlo algorithm: randomly perturb a single parameter, evaluate the loss, accept the mutation if loss decreases, otherwise reject and retry. It operates on a single GPU without requiring batch normalization, residual connections, or any gradient computation. The approach is extended to pure pruning training, discrete weight representations, and unconventional transfer functions (e.g., Gaussian).

Result: The method successfully trains: (1) deep networks with 20+ layers, (2) wide single-hidden-layer networks with up to 16,384 hidden neurons, and (3) a simple Transformer on MNIST image classification and Tiny Shakespeare character-level language modeling. It also reveals substantial redundancy in deep networks.

Conclusion: This gradient-free approach offers a complementary perspective on neural network self-organization and learning mechanisms, and provides an alternative pathway for building physically inspired deep learning systems, suggesting that gradient-based optimization may not be strictly necessary.

Abstract: Backpropagation (BP) dominates deep learning training, but its reliance on gradients brings inherent troubles -- vanishing and exploding gradients. The pursuit of gradient-free methods has long been a goal in the field of artificial intelligence. This paper shows that indeed the simplest Monte Carlo algorithm implemented on a single GPU -- randomly mutate a parameter, keep it if the loss decreases, otherwise retry -- can practically train deep networks. This gradient-free method does not even need common techniques such as batch normalization or residual connections to directly train sufficiently deep networks. More remarkably, its flexibility extends to several nontrivial scenarios: it enables pure pruning training, supports discrete weights, accommodates unconventional transfer functions such as Gaussian, and reveals the substantial redundancy of deep networks. We have demonstrated its feasibility on deep networks with more than 20 layers, single-hidden-layer wide networks with up to 16,384 hidden neurons, and even a simple Transformer architecture trained on both image classification (MNIST) and character-level language modeling (Tiny Shakespeare). This simple gradient-free method may offer a complementary perspective for understanding the self-organization and learning mechanisms of neural networks, and also provides an alternative route for building physically inspired deep learning systems.

</details>


### [222] [Predicting Male Fertility Using Machine Learning: A Semen Parameters Based Analysis with the VISEM Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08429)
*Shahnawaz Qureshi,Raja Khurram Shahzad,Muhammad Fozan,Emal Kawal,Syed Aziz Shah,Sattam Al-Anazi,Syed MuhammadZeeshan Iqbal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Machine learning models, specifically the Nearest Centroid classifier, achieved 94.2% accuracy in classifying male fertility status (Fertile, Sub-Fertile, Infertile) using semen parameters from the VISEM dataset, demonstrating potential for objective clinical decision support in andrology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Male infertility is significantly underdiagnosed, and current semen analysis — the cornerstone of clinical evaluation — may benefit from automated, objective, and accurate classification approaches to support reproductive health decision-making.

Method: The study used the VISEM dataset (85 participants) with semen parameters (sperm concentration, motility, morphology) classified into three WHO-based categories. After preprocessing and feature engineering, over 40 machine learning algorithms were evaluated using the LazyPredict framework, with 5-fold cross-validation and multiclass ROC-AUC analysis for validation.

Result: The Nearest Centroid classifier achieved the highest accuracy at 94.2%, outperforming Support Vector Machines and Quadratic Discriminant Analysis among the 40+ algorithms tested. Robustness was confirmed via cross-validation and ROC-AUC analysis.

Conclusion: Machine learning offers fast, accurate, and objective semen quality assessment, showing strong potential to enhance fertility diagnostics, support clinical decision-making in andrology, and inform patient-specific treatment strategies in assisted reproductive technologies.

Abstract: Male infertility is a significant yet often underdiagnosed aspect of reproductive health, with semen analysis serving as the cornerstone of clinical evaluation. To address this problem, this study investigates the use of machine learning algorithms to classify male fertility status based on key semen parameters, i.e., sperm concentration, motility, and morphology, using the VISEM dataset. This dataset includes semen samples from 85 participants, classified into three categories, i.e., Fertile, Sub-Fertile, and Infertile, according to the World Health Organization's criteria. After pre-processing and feature engineering, the dataset was used to train and assess multiple classification models using the LazyPredict framework. Among the more than 40 algorithms tested, the Nearest Centroid classifier achieved an accuracy of 94.2%, outperforming other models such as Support Vector Machines and Quadratic Discriminant Analysis. The model's robustness was validated using 5-fold cross-validation and multiclass ROC-AUC analysis. This study illustrates that machine learning models can provide fast, accurate, and objective assessments of semen quality, potentially supporting clinical decision-making in andrology and assisted reproductive technologies. These findings emphasize the growing potential of machine learning to enhance fertility diagnostics and inform patient-specific treatment strategies.

</details>


### [223] [MatBind: A Shared Embedding Space for Multimodal Materials Characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08470)
*Le Yang,Anoop K. Chandran,Jona Östreicher,Evgenii Sovetkin,Adrian Mirza,Sebastien Bompas,Bashir Kazimi,Pascal Friederich,Stefan Kesselheim,Kevin Maik Jablonka,Stefan Sandfeld*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MatBind is a contrastive learning framework that aligns four materials modalities—crystal structure, simulated pXRD, DOS, and text—into a unified embedding space using crystal structure as the central anchor, enabling emergent zero-shot cross-modal retrieval and organizing materials by physically meaningful properties without explicit supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crystalline material characterization requires integrating heterogeneous data sources (atomic structures, diffraction patterns, electronic density of states, and natural language), but these modalities are typically stored and analyzed in isolation. This siloing makes cross-modal querying and relational understanding across representational boundaries practically impossible, motivating a unified representation framework.

Method: MatBind employs a contrastive learning framework that aligns four modalities—crystal structure, simulated powder X-ray diffraction (pXRD), density of states (DOS), and text—into a shared embedding space. Crystal structure serves as the central physical anchor. The approach induces alignment between modality pairs that were never explicitly paired during training, enabling emergent zero-shot cross-modal retrieval. Multi-modal query combination further improves retrieval performance.

Result: The learned embedding space organizes materials according to physically meaningful properties without explicit supervision. The framework enables emergent zero-shot cross-modal retrieval between modalities never explicitly paired during training. Retrieval performance improves systematically when multiple modalities are combined at query time.

Conclusion: Treating heterogeneous materials data as complementary projections of a single physical reality—rather than isolated data sources—is not merely a practical choice but is consistent with the underlying physics. The shared representation validates that these modalities are fundamentally interlinked through the physical object they describe.

Abstract: Fully characterizing a crystalline material requires integrating heterogeneous data sources -- atomic structures, diffraction patterns, electronic density of states, and natural language -- each of which captures a different facet of the same physical object. In practice, however, these modalities are stored and analyzed in isolation, making it difficult to relate or query materials across representational boundaries. We present MatBind, a contrastive learning framework that aligns four materials modalities -- crystal structure, powder X-ray diffraction (pXRD) simulated from structures, density of states (DOS), and text -- into a unified embedding space using crystal structure as the central physical anchor. The framework induces alignment between modalities never explicitly paired during training, enabling emergent zero-shot cross-modal retrieval as a direct consequence of the shared representation. The learned embedding space organizes materials according to physically meaningful properties without explicit supervision, and retrieval performance improves systematically when modalities are combined at query time. These results demonstrate that treating heterogeneous materials data as complementary projections of a single physical reality, rather than as isolated data sources, is not a practical choice but is consistent with the underlying physics.

</details>


### [224] [Frequency-Domain Multi-Modality Transportation Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08475)
*Jiewen Deng,Hangchen Liu,Junchen Li,Boyuan Zhang,Renhe Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FreMo is a lightweight, plug-and-play frequency-domain framework for multi-modality transportation forecasting.
