It disentangles modality-wise spectral refinement from cross-modality synergy using a Modality-Wise Frequency Filter (MFF) and a Frequency-Guided Synergy Integrator (FSI), achieving state-of-the-art performance by adaptively and selectively fusing information across modalities in the frequency domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-modality transportation systems (e.g., traffic flow and public transit) share coupled temporal patterns, but different modalities exhibit distinct spectral characteristics and interact unevenly across frequencies. Existing methods operate mainly in the time domain or use coarse feature fusion, failing to capture these frequency-specific interactions, which limits forecasting accuracy.

Method: FreMo is a plug-and-play module compatible with general time series backbones. It consists of two components: (1) Modality-Wise Frequency Filter (MFF), which adaptively refines spectral components within each modality by emphasizing informative frequencies and suppressing noise; and (2) Frequency-Guided Synergy Integrator (FSI), which selectively aggregates cross-modality information based on each modality's relative contribution at each frequency, enabling effective knowledge sharing while mitigating negative transfer.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world multi-modality transportation datasets demonstrate that FreMo consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, showing superior performance and generalization across diverse forecasting scenarios.

Conclusion: Explicit frequency-domain modeling with adaptive, frequency-selective cross-modality synergy is highly effective for multi-modality transportation forecasting. FreMo's lightweight, plug-and-play design makes it a practical enhancement for existing time series forecasting backbones.

Abstract: Multi-modality transportation refers to urban systems composed of multiple transportation modes, such as traffic flow and public transit, whose dynamics are coupled by shared temporal patterns. Accurate multi-modality transportation forecasting remains challenging because (1) different modalities exhibit distinct spectral characteristics and (2) interact unevenly across frequencies, whereas most existing methods operate primarily in the time domain or rely on coarse feature fusion. To address these limitations, we propose a lightweight yet effective Frequency-Domain Multi-Modality modeling (FreMo) that explicitly exploits the frequency domain to enable adaptive and selective cross-modality synergy. FreMo disentangles modality-wise spectral refinement from cross-modality synergy and supports plug-and-play integration with general time series backbones. Specifically, FreMo introduces a Modality-Wise Frequency Filter (MFF) to adaptively refine spectral components within each modality, emphasizing informative frequencies while suppressing noise. FreMo further incorporates a Frequency-Guided Synergy Integrator (FSI) that selectively aggregates information across modalities based on their relative contribution at each frequency, facilitating effective cross-modality knowledge sharing while mitigating negative transfer. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets show that FreMo consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, with superior performance and generalization across diverse forecasting scenarios. The code is available at https://github.com/beginner-sketch/FreMo.

</details>


### [225] [Systematic Evaluation of Learning Rate Scheduling Strategies Across Heterogeneous Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08511)
*Hafsa Mateen,Radu Timofte,Dmitry Ignatov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematically evaluates 25 learning rate scheduler configurations across 30 convolutional and transformer architectures (3,938 model variants) on CIFAR-10, finding that CosineAnnealingWarmRestarts and CyclicLR consistently outperform basic decay strategies, and that optimal scheduler choice is architecture-dependent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning rate scheduling is critical to neural network training, yet manual selection is costly and rarely exhaustive. Classical AutoML approaches treat schedulers as secondary hyperparameters, leaving a gap in systematic understanding of how scheduler choice interacts with architecture type.

Method: The authors used the LEMUR neural network dataset, selecting 30 representative architectures from convolutional and transformer families. Through automated source-code injection, they applied 25 scheduler configurations spanning nine PyTorch scheduler families, evaluating 3,938 model variants on CIFAR-10 for classification accuracy.

Result: The best configuration reached 86.45% top-1 accuracy, with 237 variants exceeding 80%. CosineAnnealingWarmRestarts and CyclicLR consistently outperformed basic decay strategies. Scheduler effectiveness was found to be heavily architecture-dependent, and the resulting accuracy landscape was contributed to the LEMUR nn-dataset.

Conclusion: Scheduler selection should not be treated as a secondary hyperparameter; it significantly interacts with architecture choice. CosineAnnealingWarmRestarts and CyclicLR are recommended as robust default choices. The contributed accuracy landscape serves as a practical reference for principled scheduler selection.

Abstract: Choosing a learning rate scheduling strategy is critical to neural network training, but manual selection is costly and rarely exhaustive. While classical AutoML approaches often treat the scheduler as a secondary hyperparameter, we systematically investigate its impact on classification accuracy across a diverse pool of architectures. We evaluated 30 representative architectures from convolutional and transformer families within the LEMUR neural network dataset. Through automated source-code injection, we applied 25 scheduler configurations across nine PyTorch families, evaluating a total of 3,938 model variants on CIFAR-10. Our best configuration achieved a top-1 accuracy of 86.45%, with 237 variants exceeding 80%. The results show that the choice of scheduler depends heavily on the architecture: CosineAnnealingWarmRestarts and CyclicLR consistently outperform basic decay strategies. The resulting accuracy landscape, contributed to the LEMUR nn-dataset, provides a practical reference for principled scheduler selection.

</details>


### [226] [Stop Guessing When to Stop Testing: Efficient Model Evaluation with Just Enough Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08522)
*Ofir Arviv,Kristjan Greenewald,Yotam Perlitz,Hadar Mulian,Michal Shmueli-Scheuer,Leshem Choshen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fixed-size benchmarks inefficiently serve diverse evaluation needs. This paper proposes an adaptive evaluation framework based on sequential testing with tailored stopping criteria, demonstrating up to 80% computational cost reduction on the Open VLM Leaderboard while maintaining statistical significance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-size benchmarks create a fundamental mismatch: different evaluation objectives (ranking, model selection, development testing) require different levels of statistical power, yet benchmarks use rigid sample sizes. This forces practitioners to choose between excessive computational cost or unreliable results. Sequential testing offers a principled solution to this efficiency-reliability trade-off.

Method: The authors propose an adaptive evaluation framework grounded in sequential testing — an established statistical paradigm. Rather than fixing sample sizes in advance, the framework evaluates models incrementally and applies tailored stopping criteria, including: (1) diminishing returns detection, which halts evaluation when additional samples no longer meaningfully change estimates; and (2) minimum detectable effect size thresholds, which stop testing once sufficient statistical power is achieved for the effect sizes of interest.

Result: On the Open VLM Leaderboard, the framework achieved an 80% reduction in computational cost compared to fixed-size evaluation, allowing a 2.5-point confidence interval width deviation, while preserving statistical significance.

Conclusion: Sequential testing should be adopted as a standard practice in model evaluation. The proposed adaptive framework provides a principled, efficient, and reliable alternative to fixed-size benchmarks, dynamically calibrating evaluation effort to the specific inferential needs of each use case.

Abstract: The inherent rigidity of fixed-size benchmarks makes them an inefficient tool for model evaluation. Diverse evaluation objectives, including model ranking, model selection and testing throughout development, demand varying levels of statistical power. The mismatch between fixed sample sizes and these diverse needs results in either excessive computational cost or compromised reliability - a critical concern for model evaluation. To overcome these limitations, we call for adoption of sequential testing in our field. We provide an adaptive evaluation framework, that provides a principled way to navigate the trade-off between efficiency and reliability in model evaluation. Our framework combines the established statistical paradigm of sequential testing with stopping criteria tailored to common evaluation needs such as diminishing returns detection, and minimum detectable effect size. We demonstrate its ability to adaptively manage the efficiency-reliability trade-off on the Open VLM Leaderboard, including, for example, a 80% reduction in computational cost compared to fixed-size evaluation (with a 2.5-point CI width allowance) while maintaining statistical significance.

</details>


### [227] [CAAD: Causality-Aware Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection via Multi-Scale Alignment and Structural Causal Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08555)
*Xin Wang,Yunshi Wen,Yanan He,Haotian Xu,Youlan Zhao,Michel Ferreira Cardia Haddad,Tengfei Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes CAAD, a novel anomaly detection framework for industrial systems that reframes anomaly detection as continuous verification of Granger causality consistency using exogenous variables, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines on real-world industrial datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing anomaly detection methods focus narrowly on temporal similarity of representations but overlook the disruption of internal causal relationships that characterizes system failures and latent anomalies. There is a need to capture causal relationship breakdowns for more precise detection.

Method: CAAD models exogenous time-series variables as residuals and identifies anomalies as significant deviations caused by external interventions. It uses multi-scale alignment to internalize system dynamics and employs a gradient-based matrix to monitor internal causal relationship breakdowns. Anomaly detection is achieved by quantifying causal deviations in both dynamic evolution and relational topology, framed as continuous Granger causality consistency verification.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world industrial datasets show that CAAD achieves high-precision anomaly detection, outperforming most state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: Reframing anomaly detection through the lens of Granger causality consistency enables superior detection of subtle causal shifts in complex industrial systems, validating the efficacy of the CAAD framework.

Abstract: The operational integrity of complex industrial systems relies on precise anomaly detection and diagnosis. The vast majority of existing methods narrowly focus on capturing temporal similarities of representations, often overlooking the disruption of internal causal relationships, which characterizes system failures and latent anomalies. In this paper, we propose a novel framework (CAAD) that reframes anomaly detection as the continuous verification of Granger causality consistency through exogenous variables.
  Specifically, the CAAD framework models exogenous time-series variables as residuals, identifying anomalies as significant deviations caused by external interventions. The proposed framework leverages multi-scale alignment to internalize system dynamics and utilizes a gradient-based matrix to monitor internal causal relationship breakdowns. By quantifying causal deviations of both dynamic evolution and relational topology, the CAAD is able to capture subtle causal shifts to achieve precise anomaly detection. Extensive experiments on real-world industrial datasets demonstrate that the CAAD achieves high-precision anomaly detection, outperforming most state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [228] [Contravariance Theory: Strong Alignment for Minimal Solutions to Hard Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08561)
*Dan Yamins,Aran Nayebi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: For any two minimal DNN solutions to a sufficiently hard task, weak alignment via affine mappings implies strong alignment of privileged axes, and alignment zippers up the network hierarchy from end-to-end optimization, suggesting that convergent evolution between ANNs and brains is likely inevitable and that metric choice is not highly sensitive under strong task constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses core questions in NeuroAI: how to properly compare DNN representations to brain representations, and to what extent convergent evolution between artificial and biological neural networks should be expected. Prior work lacked formal grounding for these foundational issues.

Method: The authors provide a formal theoretical framework building on the notion of contravariance from Cao and Yamins (2024). They prove two key results mathematically: (i) that weak alignment (based on affine mappings between representations) entails strong alignment of privileged axes, and (ii) that alignment "zippers" upward through the network hierarchy as a consequence of end-to-end task optimization.

Result: Two main theoretical results: (1) weak alignment of network representations via affine mappings guarantees strong alignment of privileged axes; (2) alignment propagates ("zippers") up the network hierarchy, causing privileged axes to emerge naturally from end-to-end task optimization.

Conclusion: Under sufficiently strong task constraints, the specific choice of comparison metric between networks is not highly sensitive, and convergent evolution between DNNs and biological brains is probably inevitable.

Abstract: A series of results from the NeuroAI over the past fifteen years have raised core questions both about how to compare Deep Neural Network (DNN) models to the brain, and about how much convergent evolution to expect between artificial networks and real brain networks. Here, we show that for any two minimal DNN solutions to a sufficiently hard task: (i) "weak" alignment of network representations based on affine mappings guarantees "strong" alignment of privileged axes, and (ii) alignment "zippers" up the network hierarchy, causing the emergence of privileged axes from end-to-end task optimization. These results formalize the notion of contravariance from Cao and Yamins [2024], and illustrate important consequences for the theory of NeuroAI: with sufficiently strong tasks, choice of metric for inter-network comparison is not all that sensitive, and that convergent evolution is probably inevitable.

</details>


### [229] [Spectral Stability of Pseudoinverse-Based Extreme Learning Machine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08581)
*Bich Van Nguyen,Ngoc Anh Khong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes ELM training stability through the spectral properties of the hidden layer matrix, showing that the smallest singular value and condition number govern numerical robustness, and that SVD-based pseudoinverse methods outperform iterative alternatives under ill-conditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although ELM is fast due to analytical output weight computation via the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse, its numerical stability is sensitive to the conditioning of the hidden layer matrix — a problem that lacks a rigorous spectral understanding.

Method: The authors adopt a spectral analysis framework: (1) they quantify how the smallest singular value amplifies perturbations in output weights, (2) use the condition number as a measure of hidden-layer instability, (3) compare SVD-based pseudoinverse with iterative hyperpower methods, and (4) interpret width-dependent conditioning through a random feature lens. Validation uses synthetic matrices and standard ELM benchmarks.

Result: SVD-based pseudoinverse computation consistently proves more reliable under ill-conditioned settings. Iterative hyperpower methods exhibit greater sensitivity to the singular value structure. Width-dependent conditioning trends align with predictions from random feature theory.

Conclusion: ELM stability is fundamentally dictated by the singular value distribution of the hidden layer matrix. Practitioners should favor SVD-based pseudoinverse methods, especially when the hidden layer is wide or poorly conditioned.

Abstract: Extreme Learning Machine (ELM) computes output weights analytically using the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse. Although this leads to fast training, its numerical stability depends strongly on the conditioning of the hidden layer matrix. This paper studies pseudoinverse-based ELM from a spectral perspective. We show that the smallest singular value governs perturbation amplification in the output weights, while the condition number provides a quantitative measure of hidden-layer instability. We compare SVD-based pseudoinverse computation with iterative hyperpower methods and discuss width-dependent conditioning through a random feature interpretation. Experiments on synthetic matrices and ELM benchmarks show that SVD-based methods remain the most reliable under ill conditioning, while iterative methods are more sensitive to spectral properties. The results suggest that ELM stability is fundamentally governed by the singular value structure of the hidden layer matrix.

</details>


### [230] [Robust Bayesian Decision Making under Adversarial Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08590)
*Haripriya Harikumar,Sammie Katt,Yasir Zubayr Barlas,Samuel Kaski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Bayesian experimental design framework that incorporates adversarial robustness, targeting decision stability under worst-case hidden perturbations rather than nominal decision optimality, and shows it yields significantly more reliable decisions than conventional decision-aware design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing decision-aware experimental design methods assume well-specified outcome models and rely on the stability of optimal decisions under real-world perturbations. In practice, hidden or weakly modeled effects frequently influence outcomes, altering decision optimality and producing misleading conclusions. This motivates designing experiments that are robust to such adversarial variation.

Method: Building on Bayesian decision theory, the authors formalize an adversarially robust optimal decision by modeling worst-case variation through adversarial variables. They derive a principled Bayesian experimental design criterion that explicitly targets decision stability rather than nominal optimality, guiding sequential data acquisition to maximize information gain against plausible adversarial perturbations.

Result: Experiments on synthetic and real-world scientific datasets demonstrate that conventional decision-aware design converges rapidly to high-confidence but fragile decisions, whereas the proposed robustness-aware approach consistently yields decisions that are significantly more stable and reliable under adversarial variation.

Conclusion: Targeting decision stability through adversarially robust experimental design provides a principled safeguard against hidden confounding effects, producing more trustworthy decisions for downstream scientific and applied settings where model misspecification is a realistic concern.

Abstract: Scientific experiments are often designed to maximize information gain, yet in many applications the primary objective is to support reliable downstream decision-making. Existing decision-aware experimental design and active learning methods typically assume well-specified outcome models and implicitly rely on the stability of the optimal decision under real-world perturbations. In practice, however, experimental outcomes are frequently influenced by hidden or weakly modeled effects, which can substantially alter decision optimality and lead to misleading conclusions. We study sequential adversarially robust decision-aware experimental design, where data acquisition has to take into account information gain against plausible worst-case unexpected effects, modeled here as variation in adversarial variables. Building on Bayesian decision theory, we formalize an adversarially robust optimal decision under this setting and derive a principled Bayesian experimental design criterion. The criterion explicitly targets decision stability rather than nominal optimality. Experiments on synthetic and real-world scientific datasets show that conventional decision-aware design can converge rapidly to high confidence yet fragile decisions, while our robustness-aware approach yields decisions that are significantly more stable and reliable under adversarial variation.

</details>


### [231] [Steering Neural Network Training through Interpretable Constraints Based on Partial Dependence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08641)
*Yann Claes,Pierre Geurts,Vân Anh Huynh-Thu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a novel explanation-guided learning method for regression problems that steers neural networks using partial dependence, ensuring model predictions align with domain knowledge. The approach yields better performance, improved data efficiency, and faithful explanations compared to unconstrained models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While interpretability techniques have advanced, most work focuses on post-hoc explanation of classification models rather than actively guiding training with prior knowledge. Existing explanation-guided learning methods are predominantly classification-oriented and assume prior knowledge about important input features/regions, leaving a gap for regression problems and functional domain knowledge about how features should influence predictions.

Method: The authors propose steering neural network training using partial dependence — specifically constraining the average model response to certain features to align with functional domain knowledge (e.g., monotonicity, known physical relationships). This is achieved through a regularization approach that penalizes deviations between the model's partial dependence and the user-specified target functions during training.

Result: On regression tasks including dynamical systems forecasting, the proposed method consistently outperforms unconstrained models in predictive accuracy and demonstrates superior data efficiency. Critically, post-hoc interpretations from the constrained models genuinely reflect the injected domain knowledge, whereas unconstrained models fail to exhibit these expected relationships despite potentially competitive accuracy.

Conclusion: Explanation-guided learning via partial dependence constraints is an effective strategy for incorporating functional domain knowledge into neural network training for regression. It simultaneously improves model performance, sample efficiency, and the faithfulness of resulting interpretations, bridging the gap between model accuracy and alignment with established domain understanding.

Abstract: Over the last few years, there has been an increased interest in making machine learning models more interpretable. Although a great deal of effort goes into developing techniques for interpreting the interactions learned by a given model, fewer studies focus on assessing the quality of such explanations. Even fewer focus on how to adjust the model to produce explanations faithful to prior knowledge, a process known as explanation-guided learning. Furthermore, most approaches in this area focus on classification problems and usually assume prior knowledge about which input features or regions are most important. In this work, we introduce a new approach to steering neural networks based on partial dependence, such that their average response to certain features aligns with specific functional domain knowledge about the problem. We empirically demonstrate on a range of regression problems, including dynamical systems forecasting, that models whose training has been controlled using our method perform better than unconstrained models and are more data-efficient. Moreover, we highlight that interpretations obtained from the former actually align with the user-provided knowledge, whereas those obtained from the latter do not.

</details>


### [232] [BiSCo-LLM: Lookup-Free Binary Spherical Coding for Extreme Low-Bit Large Language Model Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08643)
*Yuantian Shao,Peisong Wang,Zhilei Liu,Chuangyi Li,Yuanteng Chen,Pengcheng Xie,Yiwu Yao,Zhihui Wei,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BiSCo-LLM introduces a codebook-free binary spherical coding framework for compressing LLM weights to extreme low-bit regimes (~2 bits). It uses hyperspherical binarization, residual coding, and category-wise recovery distillation, achieving competitive rate-distortion performance without explicit vector-quantization codebooks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs face memory, bandwidth, and storage bottlenecks during deployment. Existing low-bit methods either use scalar/group-wise quantization (simple but limited near 2 bits) or vector quantization (richer representation but incurs codebook/index overhead). The goal is to achieve extreme compression without the storage penalty of explicit codebooks.

Method: BiSCo-LLM has three core components: (1) **Binary Spherical Quantization (BSQ)** — local weight chunks are mapped onto a unit hypersphere and binarized into compact spherical codes, yielding a bit-packed sign stream as the main payload; (2) **Residual BSQ** — encodes reconstruction error from the base codec for an explicit rate-distortion path, with neural decoders replacing stored codebooks; (3) **Category-wise Recovery Distillation** — applied after replacing each Transformer module category (e.g., attention, FFN) to align local weight reconstruction with global model behavior. An 8-bit protected-channel path stabilizes sensitive channels and is accounted separately in the storage budget, which also includes binary codes, neural decoders, LoRA adapters, and metadata.

Result: Not explicitly stated in the abstract; the abstract focuses on the architectural description and claims of competitive performance at extreme low-bit regimes without codebook overhead.

Conclusion: BiSCo-LLM provides a viable path to extreme LLM weight compression by combining spherical binarization with residual coding and recovery distillation, eliminating the codebook/index storage burden of traditional VQ methods while maintaining reconstruction quality.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly constrained by memory capacity, weight bandwidth, and checkpoint storage during deployment. Existing low-bit compression methods mainly follow two directions. Scalar or group-wise quantization is simple and compatible with efficient low-precision kernels, but its representation capacity becomes limited when the target budget approaches 2 bits per weight. Vector-quantized weight compression provides a richer block-level representation, but usually introduces explicit codebooks, index lookup, and additional storage accounting. This paper presents BiSCo-LLM, a codebook-free binary spherical coding framework for extreme low-bit LLM weight compression. The core pipeline is built on three components. First, local weight chunks are mapped onto a unit hypersphere and binarized into compact spherical codes, so that the main payload is a bit-packed sign stream rather than explicit VQ centroids. Second, a residual BSQ stage encodes the reconstruction error left by the base spherical codec, providing an explicit rate-distortion path without stored codebooks. Third, category-wise recovery distillation is performed after replacing each Transformer module category, reducing the mismatch between local weight reconstruction and assembled model behavior. A small 8-bit protected-channel path is used as an auxiliary stabilization mechanism for sensitive channels and is counted separately from the BSQ payload. The reported storage budget includes binary codes, neural decoders, protected-channel payloads, LoRA adapters, and metadata.

</details>


### [233] [Multi-Modal, Multi-Environment Machine Teaching for Robust Reward Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08647)
*Ali Larian,Qian Lin,Chang Zong Wu,Daniel S. Brown*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of learning reward functions that generalize across multiple environments. It first theoretically proves that comparisons impose strictly stronger constraints than other feedback modalities in the unlimited-data regime. Building on this, the authors propose a hierarchical machine teaching algorithm that greedily selects informative environments and then strategically queries low-cost feedback within those environments, achieving better generalization and lower regret than uniform baselines under fixed feedback budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward functions learned via IRL from demonstrations in a single MDP often fail to generalize to new environments because the reward information is entangled with the specific dynamics of the training MDP. Existing optimal teaching analyses for IRL are limited to single-environment, demonstration-only settings, leaving open the question of how heterogeneous feedback modalities (comparisons, demonstrations, etc.) and multi-environment dynamics jointly constrain generalizable reward functions.

Method: The authors first conduct a theoretical analysis comparing how different feedback modalities (demonstrations, comparisons, trajectory rankings, etc.) constrain the reward hypothesis space, proving that comparisons are strictly strongest in the unlimited-data regime. They then propose a hierarchical machine teaching algorithm that operates across multiple MDPs: (1) an outer loop greedily selects environments that expose complementary reward constraints, and (2) an inner loop strategically queries low-cost, high-information feedback within each selected environment. The approach aims to maximize reward identifiability under a fixed feedback budget.

Result: Empirically, the proposed method achieves substantially lower regret and stronger generalization to held-out environments compared to uniform teaching baselines under identical feedback budgets, demonstrating the value of jointly optimizing environment selection and feedback modality choice for learning dynamics-robust reward functions.

Conclusion: Multi-environment, multi-modal teaching is essential for learning reward functions that generalize across diverse deployment contexts. Comparisons provide the strongest per-datum constraints, and strategic environment selection combined with cost-aware feedback querying significantly outperforms uniform approaches.

Abstract: As autonomous agents are increasingly deployed across diverse operational contexts, aligning their behavior with human intent demands reward functions that remain robust to such changes rather than overfitting to any single environment. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) provides a principled way to infer such objectives from human feedback. However, existing analyses of optimal teaching approaches for IRL focus on single-environment, demonstration-only settings, leaving underexplored how heterogeneous feedback modalities and environment dynamics jointly constrain reward functions that generalize across multiple environments. Because demonstrations in one MDP entangle reward information with that environments specific structure, the resulting rewards frequently fail to generalize when the agent is deployed in a new setting. We first analyze how different feedback modalities constrain rewards, showing that, in the unlimited-data regime, comparisons impose strictly stronger global constraints than other modalities. Beyond this theoretical analysis, we introduce a hierarchical machine teaching algorithm for reward learning that operates across multiple MDPs. The algorithm first greedily selects informative environments that expose complementary reward constraints, then strategically queries low-cost feedback within those environments. Empirically, our method achieves substantially lower regret and stronger generalization to held-out environments than uniform teaching baselines under identical feedback budgets, demonstrating the importance of multi-environment, multi-modal teaching for learning dynamics-robust reward functions.

</details>


### [234] [Secure Decentralized Federated Learning via Gossip and Virtual Voting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08651)
*Amirhossein Taherpour,Xiaodong Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: gspDAG-FL is a secure decentralized federated learning framework that uses a gossip-derived DAG and Hashgraph-style virtual voting to achieve provenance finality and Byzantine resilience without centralized coordination, matching ledger-based FL accuracy while improving throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing decentralized FL relies on gossip but lacks provenance finality and robustness to Byzantine or lazy nodes. Ledger-based FL adds auditability but reintroduces global coordination overhead that contradicts the locality goals of DFL. A solution is needed that delivers both security guarantees and decentralization.

Method: gspDAG-FL combines three mechanisms: (1) nodes gossip model payloads only with neighbors; (2) full nodes collect event certificates and receiver-endorsed accepted gossip proofs to reconstruct a Topology DAG, then run Hashgraph-style virtual voting with compact full-node certificates, achieving finality over unique model-origin tuples; (3) payload validation, accepted-proof validation, and private semantic audit before aggregation. Safety and conditional liveness are formally proved, with a convergence guarantee under time-varying effective mixing.

Result: On MNIST classification and Penn Treebank language modeling with up to N=100 nodes under mixed Byzantine and lazy participation, gspDAG-FL achieves learning quality close to validation-based ledger FL while reducing coordination bottlenecks, improving throughput, and maintaining high invalid-origin detection rates.

Conclusion: gspDAG-FL demonstrates that consensus can be derived directly from the gossip substrate used for model dissemination, achieving ledger-comparable security and accuracy without sacrificing the locality and scalability benefits of DFL.

Abstract: Decentralized federated learning (DFL) removes the central server by letting nodes exchange model updates through peer-to-peer gossip, but existing gossip-based methods often lack provenance finality and resilience to Byzantine or lazy participants. Ledger-assisted federated learning (FL) improves auditability, yet blockchains, shards, or settlement committees can reintroduce global coordination costs that conflict with DFL locality. This paper proposes \emph{gspDAG-FL}, a secure DFL framework that derives consensus from the same gossip history used to disseminate models. Nodes exchange model payloads only with neighbors, while full nodes collect event certificates and receiver-endorsed accepted gossip proofs, reconstruct a compact Topology directed acyclic graph (DAG), and run Hashgraph-style virtual voting followed by compact full-node certificates. Finality is over unique model-origin tuples, not identical local parameter states. To improve resilience, gspDAG-FL combines payload validation, accepted-proof validation, and private semantic audit before aggregation. We formalize the adversarial setting, prove safety and conditional liveness of the control plane, and give a convergence guarantee for certified perturbed gossip under time-varying effective mixing. Experiments on MNIST classification and Penn Treebank language modeling, using fair held-out validation/audit data and networks up to \(N=100\), show that gspDAG-FL achieves learning quality close to validation-based ledger FL while reducing coordination bottlenecks, improving throughput, and maintaining high invalid-origin detection under mixed Byzantine and lazy participation.

</details>


### [235] [EdgeRefine: Privacy-Utility Balance for Graphs via Jaccard Sampling under Edge Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08659)
*Wenxiu Ding,Muzhi Liu,Zheng Yan,Mingjun Wang,Yifan Zhao,Qiao Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EdgeRefine is a local differential privacy framework for GNNs that improves the privacy-utility trade-off via adaptive edge refinement. It estimates edge probabilities using Jaccard similarity, ranks edges, and uses the privacy budget ε to balance true/false edges while controlling total edges with a sampling rate k. It achieves near-baseline accuracy and strong resilience against graph reconstruction attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNNs struggle in privacy-sensitive settings because graph structures leak sensitive link information. Existing edge-level DP solutions inject noise into the full adjacency matrix, but stronger privacy demands more noise, severely degrading utility. The privacy-utility trade-off remains a major barrier to practical privacy-preserving graph learning.

Method: EdgeRefine operates under local differential privacy with three key steps: (1) estimating edge-existence probabilities via Jaccard similarity and ranking edges accordingly; (2) using the privacy budget ε to determine the ratio of true to false edges, then sampling them based on the probability ranking; (3) controlling the total number of edges through a separate sampling rate k to maintain sparsity and reliability of the final graph.

Result: At ε=2.5, EdgeRefine improves node classification accuracy over SOTA by 17.8% on ACM (GAT) and 19.7% on Cora (GCN). In graph classification, average accuracy degradation is ~5% versus the noise-free baseline. Under reconstruction attacks, it maintains relative absolute error >1 across all ε budgets, averaging 1.962 on Cora and 1.472 on AMAP.

Conclusion: EdgeRefine substantially outperforms existing privacy-preserving graph learning methods, achieving accuracy comparable to noise-free baselines while providing strong resilience against privacy leakage from graph reconstruction attacks.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have shown considerable success in learning from graph-structured data, but their use in privacy-sensitive areas remains difficult because graph structure can leak sensitive link information. To satisfy edge-level differential privacy, a common approach is to inject noise into all elements of the graph's adjacency matrix, thereby obfuscating the existence of any single edge. However, stronger privacy requires more noise, and excessive noise reduces utility, making the privacy-utility balance a major barrier to practical privacy-preserving graph learning.
  To address this issue, we propose EdgeRefine, a local differential privacy framework that improves this trade-off through adaptive edge refinement. EdgeRefine first estimates edge-existence probabilities using Jaccard similarity and ranks edges for noisy edge removal. To ensure the sparsity and reliability of the final graph, it uses the privacy budget $ε$ to determine the ratio of true to false edges, samples them separately based on this probability ranking, and controls the total number of edges with a separate sampling rate $k$. Extensive experiments show that EdgeRefine achieves accuracy comparable to the noise-free baseline and substantially outperforms other privacy-preserving methods across datasets and GNN architectures. Under privacy budget $ε= 2.5$, EdgeRefine improves node classification accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines by 17.8\% on ACM under GAT and 19.7\% on Cora under GCN. In graph classification, it achieves an average accuracy degradation of around 5\% compared to the noise-free baseline. Under graph reconstruction attacks, EdgeRefine maintains relative absolute error levels above 1 across all privacy budgets, averaging 1.962 on Cora and 1.472 on AMAP, indicating strong resilience against privacy leakage.

</details>


### [236] [Resample or Reroute? Budget-Aware Test-Time Model Selection for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08665)
*Teng-Ruei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper formulates budget-aware test-time model selection, proposing an online "resample-or-reroute" (RoR) policy that allocates a fixed per-query cost budget between resampling the current model and rerouting to an alternative model, using estimated marginal correctness per unit cost. Experiments show RoR achieves a superior cost-quality Pareto front over multiple baselines across diverse benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows test-time resampling can recover per-instance selection headroom that no single-commit router captures, but this guarantee relies on an idealized oracle with correctness labels and unconstrained budget — conditions absent in real deployments. No existing work treats resampling and rerouting as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget, motivating a unified budget-aware formulation.

Method: The authors propose an online resample-or-reroute (RoR) allocation policy that, given a per-query budget and an imperfect verifier, dynamically allocates budget units between resampling the committed model and rerouting to an alternative model. Decisions are driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost, grounded in the recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling. Evaluation uses replay experiments on multi-draw correctness tensors from an eleven-model open-weight pool across four benchmarks of varying difficulty.

Result: RoR attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front relative to single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines, with the largest gains on the most heterogeneous benchmark. An ablation shows gains are verifier-gated, shrinking as verifier quality degrades. Robustness replays under a provider price vector and a label-free agreement verifier delineate where conclusions carry over.

Conclusion: Budget-aware test-time model selection with a unified resample-or-reroute policy yields meaningful quality improvements under realistic deployment constraints. The approach's effectiveness depends on verifier quality and model pool heterogeneity, with clear boundaries identified for when gains materialize.

Abstract: Routing among large language models (LLMs) trades response quality against serving cost, motivated by the reported gap between deployed routers and a per-instance oracle. Recent analysis shows that test-time resampling can recover per-instance selection headroom that no single-commit router captures; however, that guarantee holds only under an idealized oracle equipped with correctness labels and an unconstrained budget, neither of which a deployed system has. To the best of our knowledge, no previous work treats resampling the committed model and rerouting to an alternative model as competing uses of a single per-query cost budget. Therefore, this work formulates budget-aware test-time model selection: given a per-query budget and an imperfect verifier, allocate each unit of budget between resampling and rerouting so that expected correctness is maximized. An online resample-or-reroute (RoR) allocation policy driven by estimated marginal correctness per unit cost is proposed, and its behavior is grounded in the recoverability asymmetry between selection and sampling. Replay experiments on newly regenerated multi-draw correctness tensors from an eleven-model open-weight pool over four benchmarks of differing difficulty show that the proposed RoR policy attains a favorable cost-quality Pareto front relative to single-route, one-commit-router, budget-aware best-of-K, cascade, and random-allocation baselines for the tested pools, with the largest gains on the most heterogeneous benchmark; an ablation further shows the gains are verifier-gated, shrinking as verifier quality degrades, and robustness replays under a provider price vector and a label-free agreement verifier delineate where the conclusions carry over.

</details>


### [237] [A Practical Investigation of Training-free Relaxed Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08690)
*Guoxuan Xia,Luka Ribar,Paul Balanca*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An empirical investigation of training-free relaxed speculative decoding methods, unifying them in a shared framework, benchmarking them on modern setups, and distilling practical takeaways — notably that relaxation demands careful capability evaluation and many relaxed methods are incompatible with lightweight multi-token-prediction drafters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard speculative decoding is lossless, guaranteeing exact preservation of the LLM's sampling distribution. Recent work suggests relaxing this guarantee can yield further speed-ups, controlled capability-speed trade-offs, or even capability gains. However, the practical landscape of these relaxed methods is underexplored, motivating a systematic empirical investigation.

Method: The authors unify existing training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques within a shared conceptual framework. They then benchmark these methods across contemporary settings, evaluating both speed and generation quality/capability trade-offs.

Result: Key empirical findings: (1) relaxed speculative decoding requires substantial capability evaluation, unlike lossless methods where correctness is guaranteed; (2) many relaxed approaches depend on a drafter that is itself a good language model, rendering them unsuitable for lightweight, dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters.

Conclusion: Practitioners should be aware that relaxed speculative decoding involves non-trivial capability-speed trade-offs requiring careful evaluation, and that the choice of drafter architecture critically affects which relaxed methods are viable.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates sampling from an autoregressive LLM by using a faster auxiliary model to draft tokens which are then verified in parallel by the LLM. Standard speculative decoding is lossless: its rejection and resampling steps exactly preserve the LLM's sampling distribution. Recent work argues that relaxing this strict guarantee can yield further speed-ups, controlled capability-speed trade-offs, or even capability gains. We practically investigate training-free relaxed speculative decoding techniques, unify existing approaches within a shared framework, benchmark them on contemporary settings, and distil takeaways and empirical findings for practitioners. Important takeaways include: relaxation can require considerable capability evaluation unlike lossless speculative decoding, and many relaxed approaches rely on a drafter that is a good language model, making them unsuited for lightweight dedicated multi-token-prediction drafters.

</details>


### [238] [MPFlow: Learning Budgeted Max-Flow Optimization on the Lightning Network with Deep Graph Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08703)
*Harrison Rush,Vincent Davis,Simone Antonelli,Vikash Singh,Jesse Shrader,Emanuele Rossi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph reinforcement learning agent solves budget-constrained channel placement in the Lightning Network to maximize max-flow routing capacity, outperforming heuristics and deployed in production managing $16M+ across 30 nodes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Node operators in the Bitcoin Lightning Network face a critical resource-allocation problem: given a fixed capital budget, which channels should be opened to maximize the node's routing capacity and fee revenue? Existing approaches rely on heuristic rules (e.g., connecting to high-degree hubs) that fail to account for global network topology and capacity constraints.

Method: The problem is formalized as selecting k edge additions to maximize s-t max-flow under a budget constraint. The solution uses a message-passing neural network policy trained with proximal policy optimization (PPO) and action masking. A key innovation is the hub-exclusion curriculum: top hubs are progressively removed from training subgraphs, forcing the agent to learn capacity-aware placement strategies rather than degenerate hub-attachment policies.

Result: The RL agent consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across multiple random seeds and unseen Lightning Network snapshots. In production deployment, the agent executed 4,640 channel-open decisions, cumulatively allocating 267.3 BTC (over $16 million) across 30 managed nodes.

Conclusion: Graph reinforcement learning with hub-exclusion curriculum training is an effective and practical approach for liquidity placement in the Lightning Network, capable of learning capacity-aware strategies that generalize to unseen network topologies and outperforming heuristic methods in both experimental and real-world production settings.

Abstract: We address liquidity placement in the Bitcoin Lightning Network (LN): given a fixed budget, which channels should a node open to maximize its routing capacity? We cast this as a budget-constrained combinatorial optimization problem on graphs, selecting $k$ edge additions that maximize $s$--$t$ max-flow, a theory-grounded measure of routing capacity, and solve it with graph reinforcement learning. Our lightweight agent combines a message-passing policy network with proximal policy optimization (PPO) and action masking, and is trained under a hub-exclusion curriculum: the network's top hubs are removed from training subgraphs, forcing the policy to learn capacity-aware placement rather than hub attachment. In extensive experiments on real Lightning Network snapshots, our method consistently outperforms strong heuristic baselines on the max-flow objective across multiple seeds and unseen graphs. The agent has been deployed in production for peer recommendations, executing 4640 channel-open decisions that cumulatively allocate 267.3 BTC over $16 million across 30 managed nodes.

</details>


### [239] [Deep Learning for Joint Narrowband Interference Cancellation and Soft Demodulation in OFDM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08717)
*Emmanouil Kavvousanos,Francky Catthoor,Vassilis Paliouras*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified deep learning framework (NBI-CNet + LLR-CNet) replaces conventional compressed-sensing and Gaussian demapping to jointly cancel narrowband interference and produce robust soft demodulation metrics, eliminating error floors and achieving near-optimal BLER performance at drastically reduced complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional compressed-sensing NBI mitigation suffers from high sequential latency and leaves structured, non-Gaussian residuals. These residuals cause LLR unreliability, decoder saturation, and severe error floors when fed into classical Gaussian demappers, creating a critical pipeline mismatch between interference cancellation and soft demodulation in OFDM systems.

Method: A two-component deep learning pipeline: (1) NBI-CNet — a physics-informed convolutional network that estimates NBI parameters and removes multi-tone interference in a single feed-forward pass without prior knowledge of interferer count; (2) LLR-CNet — a structural whitener that maps non-Gaussian post-mitigation residuals onto well-calibrated soft LLR metrics. The design is scale-invariant, generalizing across arbitrary FFT sizes without retraining.

Result: NBI-CNet reduces computational complexity by up to 60% vs. EOMP-IDS (N=2048, Q=64). The joint framework eliminates error floors (including a 2×10⁻⁴ floor from interferer-estimation errors). At SIR=-10 dB, it operates within 0.2–0.5 dB SNR of the optimal iterative baseline at BLER=10⁻⁴. At SIR=10 dB with heavy spectral overlap (Q=12), it avoids signal-peak confusion and delivers >3 dB coding gain over classical greedy algorithms.

Conclusion: The proposed unified deep learning framework effectively resolves the pipeline mismatch between NBI cancellation and soft demodulation, eliminating error floors, approaching optimal iterative performance, and offering robust, scale-invariant generalization across FFT sizes without retraining.

Abstract: Narrowband interference (NBI) severely degrades orthogonal frequency-division multiplexing (OFDM) systems by corrupting subcarriers and rendering classical soft demodulation ineffective. Conventional compressed-sensing (CS) mitigation exhibits high sequential latency and leaves structured, non-Gaussian residuals that cause log-likelihood ratio (LLR) unreliability, decoder saturation, and severe error floors when employing classical Gaussian demappers. We resolve this pipeline mismatch using a unified deep learning framework for joint NBI cancellation and robust soft demodulation. First, NBI-CNet employs a physics-informed convolutional architecture to estimate NBI parameters and remove multi-tone interference in a single forward pass. Without requiring prior knowledge of the active interferer count, NBI-CNet reduces computational complexity by up to 60% ($N{=}2048, Q{=}64$) compared to the state-of-the-art EOMP-IDS algorithm. Second, LLR-CNet acts as a structural whitener by mapping non-Gaussian post-mitigation residuals onto well-calibrated soft metrics. Simulations demonstrate that this joint framework eliminates the error floors inherent to traditional baselines across dense grids. Under severe interference ($\text{SIR}{=}{-}10$ dB), the pipeline operates within a $0.2$ to $0.5$ dB SNR margin of the optimal iterative baseline at a target block error rate (BLER) of $10^{-4}$. Under mild interference ($\text{SIR}{=}10$ dB) with heavy spectral overlap ($Q{=}12$), where classical greedy algorithms erroneously subtract valid data components and corrupt the payload, NBI-CNet avoids signal-peak confusion to deliver a coding gain exceeding $3$ dB. Finally, the architecture circumvents the $2{\times}10^{-4}$ error floor triggered by interferer-estimation errors, while its scale-invariant design enables robust generalization across arbitrary FFT sizes without retraining.

</details>


### [240] [Latent Memory Palace: Reasoning for Control as Autoregressive Variational Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08724)
*Chuning Zhu,Eva Xu,Jose Barreiros,Krishnan Srinivasan,Paarth Shah,Abhishek Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LMP introduces an autoregressive latent space for control policies that enables adaptive reasoning akin to a "memory palace," achieving strong performance in simulation and real-world tasks while yielding an improved variable-length action tokenizer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human decision-making exhibits flexible, adaptive reasoning — some actions are immediate, others require deliberation. While language models have developed similar adaptive reasoning, transferring this to continuous control is difficult because reasoning in raw language space lacks the spatial granularity needed for precise motions.

Method: LMP frames reasoning as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution. The method organizes information in a latent "memory palace" where retrieval is iterative and adaptive. A latent-space reinforcement learning technique is derived to tractably optimize the variational lower bound. Two instantiations are presented: LMP-π (a policy) and LMP-tok (a variable-length action tokenizer for downstream autoregressive policies).

Result: LMP-π achieves strong empirical performance in both simulation and real-world domains, with interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute. LMP-tok significantly improves the performance of downstream autoregressive policies compared to standard tokenization approaches.

Conclusion: The work provides a new perspective on latent reasoning for control through variational inference, demonstrating that adaptive, interpretable reasoning can emerge in continuous control policies without relying on explicit language-space deliberation.

Abstract: Human decision-making is highly flexible -- some actions are taken immediately; others require longer deliberation. Language models have exhibited a similar capacity for adaptive "reasoning." However, transferring this capability to continuous control policies has been challenging, as directly reasoning in language space may lack the granularity for spatial understanding and precise motions. In this work, we show that reasoning for control policies can emerge by organizing information in an autoregressive latent space reminiscent of a memory palace, where retrieval is iterative and adaptive. Our method, Latent Memory Palace (LMP), formulates reasoning as variational inference with an autoregressive latent distribution. We derive a latent-space reinforcement learning technique to tractably optimize its variational lower bound. The resulting policy, LMP-$π$, achieves strong empirical performance in simulation and real-world domains while exhibiting interpretable, adaptive allocation of test-time compute. We further show that the same framework yields a variable-length action tokenizer, LMP-$\texttt{tok}$, which significantly improves the performance of downstream autoregressive policies. Together, these results present a new perspective on latent reasoning for control through the lens of variational inference.

</details>


### [241] [Super Weights in LLMs and the Failure of Selective Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08733)
*Shreyas Subramanian,Adewale Akinfaderin,Akarsha Sehwag*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Super Weights — individually critical parameters — cannot be effectively fine-tuned in isolation despite their importance. Training them alone or with local neighborhoods collapses accuracy to random guessing, while LoRA with the same parameter budget succeeds. Parameter importance does not imply parameter trainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work identified Super Weights as parameters whose removal catastrophically degrades LLM performance. The natural assumption would be that such important parameters should be prime targets for efficient fine-tuning. This paper challenges that assumption, investigating whether parameter importance translates to parameter trainability in isolation.

Method: The authors conduct a series of empirical studies on OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B: (1) training Super Weights in isolation (100–8,192 parameters), (2) expanding to local neighborhoods of up to 36K parameters around Super Weights, (3) training equal numbers of randomly chosen parameters in the same down_proj layers, (4) comparing against vanilla LoRA on attention weights and down_proj layers, and (5) a 10-seed ablation constraining LoRA updates exclusively at Super Weight coordinates.

Result: Training Super Weights in isolation drops accuracy to random-guessing levels on both model sizes. Expanding to local neighborhoods provides no improvement. Training randomly chosen positions in the same layers improves over baseline, confirming the collapse is specific to Super Weight targeting. Vanilla LoRA succeeds with only 0.16% of parameters. Constraining LoRA updates to Super Weight coordinates yields statistically indistinguishable results from full LoRA.

Conclusion: Parameter importance does not imply parameter trainability in isolation. Effective fine-tuning requires structured, layer-wide decompositions (like LoRA) rather than targeting individually important weights. Super Weights' importance manifests in inference, not as privileged loci for adaptation.

Abstract: Recent work identified Super Weights, individual parameters whose removal degrades model performance by orders of magnitude. We show that this degradation due to pruning Super Weights does not universally apply to all LLMs. Furthermore, if these parameters are so important, Super Weight-aware training should be effective. We show the opposite. Training Super Weights in isolation (100 to 8,192 parameters) drops accuracy to random-guessing levels on both OLMo-1B and OLMo-7B, and expanding to local neighborhoods of up to 36K parameters provides no improvement. The failure is specific to Super Weight coordinates: training an equal number of randomly chosen positions in the same down_proj layers instead improves over the baseline, so the collapse comes from targeting Super Weights, not from sparsity itself. Vanilla LoRA, updating every position in attention weight matrices through low-rank structure, succeeds with only 0.16% of parameters, and applying the same low-rank update to down_proj succeeds as well. A 10-seed ablation confirms that constraining LoRA updates at positions corresponding to Super Weight coordinates yields statistically indistinguishable results. These findings establish that parameter importance does not imply parameter trainability in isolation, and that effective fine-tuning relies on structured decompositions over entire layers rather than targeting individually important weights.

</details>


### [242] [SLORR: Simple and Efficient In-Training Low-Rank Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08754)
*David González-Martínez,Shiwei Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLORR is a stateless, architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization that uses GPU-friendly approximations (Hoyer sparsity and nuclear norm) to induce compressibility in neural networks, achieving strong results on vision models and LLMs with minimal training overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern neural networks are not naturally amenable to aggressive low-rank factorization without significant accuracy loss. Existing training-time low-rank regularizers suffer from drawbacks: requiring SVDs of large weight matrices, modifying model architecture with extra trainable parameters, or relying on stateful cached quantities.

Method: SLORR is a framework that directly regularizes original weight matrices using GPU-friendly approximations for forward and backward passes. It offers two variants: SLORR-Hoyer (based on the Hoyer sparsity metric) and SLORR-Nuclear (based on the nuclear norm). Both avoid SVDs, architectural modifications, and stateful caches, and come with approximation guarantees.

Result: On ImageNet-1K, across continued training of ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, ViT-L/16 and pretraining of ResNet-18, SLORR induces compressibility with under 8% training overhead. In LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales, SLORR-Hoyer preserves performance substantially better than unregularized models with less than 1% average training overhead.

Conclusion: SLORR provides a practical, low-overhead method for inducing low-rank structure during training without altering model architecture, enabling effective compression across both vision and language domains, including large-scale LLM pretraining.

Abstract: Low-rank factorization is widely used to compress neural networks, but modern models are often not naturally amenable to aggressive factorization without significant accuracy loss. Existing training-time low-rank regularizers can improve compressibility, but they often require SVDs of large weight matrices, modify the model architecture (introducing additional trainable parameters), or rely on stateful cached quantities. To address these limitations, we introduce SLORR, a simple, stateless, and architecture-preserving framework for in-training low-rank regularization, instantiated with two main variants based on the Hoyer sparsity metric and the nuclear norm. SLORR directly regularizes the original weight matrices using GPU-friendly approximations for the forward and backward passes of the regularizers, for which we provide approximation guarantees. We first evaluate SLORR on ImageNet-1K across short-horizon continued training of ResNet-50, ViT-B/16, and ViT-L/16, and pretraining of ResNet-18, where SLORR induces compressibility while introducing less than 8% training overhead. We further evaluate SLORR-Hoyer in LLM pretraining at 135M and 560M scales: SLORR-trained compressed models preserve performance substantially better than unregularized models while adding less than 1% average training overhead.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [243] [Collective Intelligence with Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07729)
*J. de Curtò,I. de Zarzà*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework with solver, critic, and aggregator agents is evaluated across four configurations. Results show that model heterogeneity—not just framework architecture or redundant sampling—is the critical driver of substantial performance gains, achieving 2.3× improvement over homogeneous setups and superior step-wise reasoning accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As foundation models scale, coordinating multiple models into cooperative reasoning systems can yield safer, more reliable AI. The study aims to disentangle whether performance gains stem from the multi-agent framework structure itself, redundant sampling, or genuine model diversity.

Method: A three-role agent architecture: solver models generate independent drafts, a critic agent performs structured critique and revision, and an aggregator synthesizes a final consensus. A scoring module evaluates semantic, numerical, and procedural dimensions. Four configurations are compared via ablation: (1) Individual Baseline, (2) Homogeneous Framework (one shared model), (3) Redundant Homogeneous Solvers (multiple instances of same model), and (4) Heterogeneous Framework with diverse specialized models. Benchmarks span calculus, physics, chemistry, biology, economics, optimization, statistics, and mathematics.

Result: The heterogeneous configuration achieved step-wise accuracy of 0.64 vs. 0.54 for individual baselines, representing a 2.3× improvement over homogeneous configurations. Framework structure and redundant sampling provided only modest gains. Model diversity dramatically improved step-wise reasoning quality and reduced variance across categories and difficulty levels. Complementary error detection and reasoning refinement emerged only with heterogeneous agents.

Conclusion: Model heterogeneity is the critical factor for substantial performance improvements in multi-agent reasoning systems, enabling complementary error detection and enhanced step-wise reasoning quality. Heterogeneous multi-agent coordination supports transparent, auditable, high-confidence decision-making for scientific and industrial applications under the Global Applied AI paradigm.

Abstract: As foundation models grow in scale and diversity, coordinating multiple models into cooperative reasoning systems offers a path toward safer, more reliable AI. This chapter presents a multi-agent framework where solver models generate independent drafts, each undergoes structured critique and revision by a critic agent, and an aggregator agent synthesizes a final consensus solution. A scoring module provides semantic, numerical, and procedural evaluation across all agents. Through ablation studies on a benchmark spanning calculus, physics, chemistry, biology, economics, optimization, statistics, and mathematics, we isolate the contributions of framework architecture versus model diversity. We compare four configurations: (1) Individual Baseline, (2) Homogeneous Framework using one shared model, (3) Redundant Homogeneous Solvers using multiple instances of the same model, and (4) Heterogeneous Framework with diverse specialized models. Results show that while framework structure and redundant sampling yield modest gains, model heterogeneity is the critical factor driving substantial performance improvements. The heterogeneous configuration achieves superior step-wise accuracy (0.64 vs. 0.54 for individual models; 2.3x improvement over homogeneous configurations) with reduced variance across categories and difficulty levels. Step-wise reasoning quality (correctness of intermediate steps, not just final answers) improves dramatically only with model diversity, showing that heterogeneous agents provide complementary error detection and reasoning refinement essential for explainability and auditability. We discuss architectural principles, evaluation methodology, and implications for Global Applied AI, showing how heterogeneous multi-agent coordination supports transparent, auditable, high-confidence decision-making across scientific and industrial domains.

</details>


### [244] [From Triggers to Emotions: A CPM-Grounded Appraisal Multi-Agent for Dynamic Emotional Evolution in Persona-Based Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07824)
*Jingyao Cai,Shuaijun Liu,Abdul Rehman,Yutong Guo,Qin Tian,Thomas Dolby,Sue Green,Chantel Cox,Xiaosong Yang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: CPM-MultiAgent introduces a psychologically-grounded multi-agent framework that models a character's emotion as a dynamically evolving latent state—continuously reshaped by dialogue triggers via CPM-based appraisal—rather than as a static trait, enabling more emotionally consistent persona-based role simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing persona-based dialogue systems treat emotions as static traits or surface stylistic cues, while affective dialogue research focuses on empathetic responses to users. Neither approach models how a character's own emotional state evolves dynamically in response to conversational triggers—a gap that limits emotionally consistent, multi-turn role simulation.

Method: The authors propose CPM-MultiAgent, a framework grounded in the Component Process Model (CPM) from psychology. The framework comprises three stages: (1) affective trigger extraction from dialogue, (2) CPM-based collaborative appraisal (where multiple agents evaluate events along CPM dimensions such as novelty, pleasantness, goal conduciveness, and coping potential), and (3) emotion state updating, which continuously revises the persona's latent emotional state across multi-turn interactions.

Result: Experiments—including baseline comparisons, ablation studies, human evaluation, and case analyses—demonstrate that CPM-MultiAgent effectively models dynamic emotional evolution in emotionally sensitive role-simulation settings.

Conclusion: Grounding emotion modeling in the CPM appraisal framework enables more psychologically plausible and consistent emotional dynamics for persona-based dialogue agents, addressing a key underexplored dimension in conversational AI.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have substantially advanced persona-based dialogue agents for emotion-sensitive role simulation in healthcare, education, counseling, customer service, and interactive storytelling. However, two related lines of work leave a key gap. Persona-based dialogue systems often encode emotions as static traits or surface-level stylistic cues, and affective dialogue research has largely focused on empathetic response generation toward users rather than modeling the agent persona's own evolving emotional state. As a result, trigger-driven emotional evolution within a character remains underexplored. To address this limitation, we draw inspiration from the Component Process Model (CPM), a psychological theory that views emotion as a dynamic process shaped by the appraisal of external events. We propose CPM-MultiAgent, a CPM-grounded emotion evolution multi-agent framework for supporting emotional changes in persona-based dialogue. Instead of treating a character's emotion as a fixed attribute, CPM-MultiAgent represents it as a latent state that is continuously reshaped by dialogue triggers. Through affective trigger extraction, CPM-based collaborative appraisal, and emotion state updating, the framework enables more emotionally consistent role simulation in multi-turn interactions.Experiments with baseline comparisons, ablation studies, human evaluation, and case analyses demonstrate that CPM-MultiAgent effectively models dynamic emotional evolution in emotionally sensitive role-simulation settings.

</details>


### [245] [Early to Share, Late to Save: Synchronisation-Driven Communication Gating in Bandwidth-Constrained Cooperative VLN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08504)
*Arav Gupta,Nivedan Yakolli,Avinash Gautam*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper introduces bandwidth-constrained cooperative Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) and proposes a hindsight gating mechanism that learns when agents should communicate under limited bandwidth. Counter-intuitively, optimal gating fires early and when agents are confident, not uncertain. The authors explain this via recurrent hidden-state alignment: early communication injects grounded representations that compound through GRU updates, achieving near-unconstrained performance with far fewer transmissions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cooperative VLN methods assume unlimited communication between agents, which is unrealistic for bandwidth-limited real-world deployments. The paper addresses the underexplored problem of deciding when to communicate under strict transmission budgets, where information efficiency becomes critical.

Method: The authors propose hindsight gating, a lightweight supervised gate that post-hoc labels communication-critical timesteps by analyzing navigation failures. Unlike REINFORCE-based approaches that suffer from high variance, this method uses supervised signals derived from when communication could have prevented errors. Experiments are conducted across budget levels B ∈ {1,3,5}.

Result: Trained gates consistently fire in early episode steps and when agents are confident, contradicting the uncertainty-based communication intuition. With B=3 transmissions, hindsight gating achieves +0.072 cumulative alignment gain, approaching unconstrained communication (+0.078). This represents 260% greater alignment efficiency than random gating (+0.020) and 320% greater than entropy-based gating (+0.017).

Conclusion: The paper establishes a new communication regime for bandwidth-limited embodied agents: synchronize representations early, then navigate independently. Early communication provides persistent benefits through recurrent hidden-state alignment in GRU-based architectures.

Abstract: Most cooperative Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) methods assume unlimited communication, not considering real-world applications where bandwidth is restricted and information efficiency is critical. We introduce \textbf{bandwidth-constrained cooperative VLN} and propose \textbf{hindsight gating}: a lightweight supervised gate that labels communication-critical steps post-hoc from navigation failures, avoiding the high variance of REINFORCE. Contrary to the intuition that agents should communicate when uncertain, we observe a consistent counter-intuitive pattern: trained gates fire predominantly in early episode steps and more often when agents are confident, across all budget levels ($B \in \{1,3,5\}$). We explain this through \textbf{recurrent hidden-state alignment}: early communication injects grounded trajectory representations that persist and compound through subsequent Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) updates, achieving $+0.072$ cumulative alignment gain with $B{=}3$ transmissions, approaching unconstrained communication ($+0.078$) at 260\% greater alignment efficiency than random gating ($+0.020$) and 320\% greater efficiency than entropy-based gating ($+0.017$). Our results establish a new communication regime for bandwidth-limited embodied agents: synchronise representations early, navigate independently later. Our codebase is available at: https://github.com/AravG13/bandwidth-constrained-cooperative-vln

</details>
